Cover: The Rhetoricity of Philosophy: Audience in Perelman and Ricoeur after the Badiou-Cassin Debate, Blake D. Scott, published by Routledge, Taylor and Francis Group, London, New York.


“This is a book that needed to be written at this particular moment in the devel­
opment of the relationship between philosophy and rhetoric in the twenty-first
century. It puts in conversation four key thinkers in those fields who have not
been dealt with together in such detail and with such insight. Its argument that
an expanded notion of rhetorical audience will prove fruitful for contemporary
philosophy is persuasively presented and should provoke productive discussion
within philosophy and between philosophers and rhetoricians.”
Steven Mailloux, Loyola Marymount University, USA
“The Rhetoricity of Philosophy is significant for philosophy and rhetoric alike,
but not simply because it uncovers rhetoric as the point of commonality in the
Cassin-Badiou debate, nor because it discerns the limitations of the conceptions
of rhetoric in both Perelman’s New Rhetoric Project and Ricoeur’s hermeneu­
tics. Instead, and more importantly, by offering an expanded notion of audience,
Blake Scott elegantly demonstrates rhetoricity as inherent to human discourse
and action, and thereby provides a guide for how philosophical practice may
respond effectively to polarized discourses on contemporary social issues.”
Michelle Bolduc, University of Exeter, UK
﻿


﻿Logo: Taylor and Francis, Taylor and Francis Group, http://taylorandfrancis.com.


This book aims to recast the way that philosophers understand rhetoric.
Rather than follow most philosophers in conceiving rhetoric as a specific
way of speaking or writing, it shows that rhetoric is better understood as
a dimension of all human discourse and action—what the author calls
“rhetoricity”.
This book provides the first philosophical treatment of rhetoricity. It is
motivated by two ongoing developments. The first is the debate between
Alain Badiou and Barbara Cassin about philosophy’s relation to rhetoric.
Both Badiou and Cassin are critical of rhetoric, albeit for different rea­
sons. Second, there has been a growing resurgence of interest in rhetoric
considering the recent rise in authoritarian politics as well as new forms
of propaganda driven by “persuasive technologies”. This book identifies
the common target of Badiou’s and Cassin’s otherwise incompatible cri­
tiques: rhetoric’s conception of audience. It offers a fresh take on the “new
rhetoric” project of Chaïm Perelman and Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca, putting
their work into conversation with the Badiou-Cassin debate. The book
then turns to the hermeneutic philosophy of Paul Ricoeur in search of
an expanded conception of audience. It shows that Ricoeur’s hermeneutic
philosophy allows us to extend Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s psycho­
logical notion of audience to texts themselves and to argue that human
beings have a rhetorical capacity to reflect on audiences in search of what
is potentially persuasive.
The Rhetoricity of Philosophy will be of interest to scholars and
advanced students working in contemporary European philosophy, rheto­
ric, argumentation studies, and social theory.
Blake D. Scott is Postdoctoral Research Associate at KU Leuven’s Institute
of Philosophy. His articles have appeared in journals including Philosophy
& Rhetoric, Informal Logic, Argumentation, Études Ricoeuriennes/
Ricoeur Studies, Analecta Hermeneutica, and Sartre Studies International.
The Rhetoricity of Philosophy

When studying the site of an ancient civilization, archaeologists look to the
midden—that is, deposits of refuse and other waste—for crucial evidence
about the lives of our ancestors. The idea is straightforward: by studying
what a society chose to discard, we learn something about the site’s for­
mer inhabitants that would not otherwise be accessible. The present study,
although of a rather different nature, relies upon the same principle. It is
concerned with something that philosophy, time and time again, has cho­
sen to discard and disavow—rhetoric. There is one important difference,
however: where waste can actually be discarded and placed beyond the
city walls, we shall defend the view that rhetoric cannot be separated from
philosophy. Rhetoric is always to be found within philosophy’s walls,
inseparable from it as a human practice.
Yet, there are two ways to read this metaphor, and emphasizing their
difference is a good way to clarify the stakes of what follows. The first
is to view philosophy and rhetoric as externally related. On this view,
philosophy and rhetoric are seen as different sorts of conduct, however
harmoniously that relation is understood. In broad strokes, this is the
“conventional view” philosophers take of rhetoric, and it is the view that
we shall reject. The second way of reading it—which is the view we shall
defend—is that philosophy and rhetoric are internally related. On this
view, rhetoric is inseparable from philosophy because it is constitutive of
what philosophers do. Though rhetoric certainly does not exhaust phi­
losophy, we intend to show that dismissing this important dimension of
philosophical practice is no less misguided than simply equating the two.
The aim of this book is thus to recast the way that philosophers usu­
ally understand rhetoric. Most philosophers tend to think of it as some­
thing foreign, something other than what they do. For some it is a specific
way of speaking or writing. Others see it as a certain kind or genre of
discourse. And others yet see it as a distinct discipline in its own right,
whether as a rival or supplement to philosophy. Rather than dispute the
relative merits of these definitions, we want to focus on clarifying their
Introduction
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-1


2  Introduction
common phenomenological basis. In other words, if we attend carefully
to the phenomenon in question—in this case human action—what exactly
do terms like “rhetoric” and “rhetorical” describe? Which kinds of action
or aspects of human conduct do they refer to? By drawing attention back
to “things themselves” in this way, we intend to show that the concern
with audiences historically associated with “rhetoric” is as important for
philosophers as for everyday social actors.
Whatever else one might mean by the term, then, our central claim is
that the object of rhetoric—what rhetoric is about—is what we shall call
“rhetoricity”, the rhetorical dimension of all discourse. Where most phi­
losophers who engage with rhetoric end up typologizing discourse, that is,
making a distinction in kind between rhetorical and non-rhetorical ways
of acting, we argue that there is a rhetorical dimension to every instance
of discourse. To adopt a rhetorical perspective on discourse is thus to rec­
ognize that all discourse has a determinate addressive character; it is to
recognize that discourse, regardless of medium, constructs its audience in
some particular way before being taken up by any particular addressee.
Responsible for this rhetoricity, as we shall see, is the exercise of what we
call our “rhetorical capacity”, that capacity to reflect on audiences in view
of what is potentially persuasive. While not equally prominent in every
instance of action, this capacity is basic to human beings (and perhaps
other forms of life too) and can be seen at work in everyday social interac­
tion as well as highly specialized contexts like philosophy.
To avoid any misunderstanding, our emphasis on rhetoricity should not
be taken to mean that this dimension is always the most important. Indeed,
we do not deny the weight of other sorts of considerations, whether they
be of an ethical, epistemological, or aesthetic variety. Yet, however one
might want to coordinate these different concerns in a given context, the
rhetorical is the one that philosophers most often feel at liberty to over­
look. The chapters that follow explore some of the reasons why this is so.
But, as we shall argue, they are insufficient to justify the continued neglect
of philosophy’s own rhetoricity—especially when it is precisely due to this
neglect that other considerations often fail to achieve their intended effect.
For this reason, our strategy in this study shall be to suspend normative
concerns as much as possible for the sake of emphasizing rhetoricity as
a descriptive feature of philosophical practice. By taking this descriptive
detour, our wager is that philosophers will be better equipped to pose nor­
mative questions and engage with the world around them.
*
These are the broad strokes of our argument. Yet the main challenge
faced by a study like ours is the patent difficulty of pinning down just
what is meant by “rhetoric”. While commonly understood as “the art of


Introduction  3
persuasion”, the contested terrain of the term prevents anyone from sim­
ply picking their preferred historical definition off the shelf as much as
concocting their own. Let no one enter the discussion claiming to stand
on neutral terminological ground: there can be no other way to begin than
from the very conflict between philosophy and rhetoric—a conflict as old
as philosophy itself.
To avoid conceptual and terminological confusion, then, we refrain
from giving “philosophy” and “rhetoric” any fixed meaning from the out­
set. Instead, at each step of the argument we start from the definitions and
meanings attributed to these terms by our chosen interlocutors. This her­
meneutic approach ensures that the reader can measure for themselves the
strength of the conclusions we draw against those of the authors we engage
with. Potential ambiguity is further mitigated by the fact that our aim is
not to provide a history of the conflict. Others have already trodded this
ground, and our study has greatly benefitted from their results.1 Instead,
we take our point of departure from the most recent manifestation of this
conflict, visible in both public discourse and contemporary philosophy.
This resurgence of interest in rhetoric can be observed as much in the
catalogues of academic publishers as on the shelves of local bookstores.
One telling example is that in recent years Aristotle’s Rhetoric—the found­
ing text of rhetorical theory in the Western tradition—has been retranslated
into English no less than three times by major presses, to say nothing of
numerous reprints of older translations.2 It is not by chance that a renewed
interest in rhetoric comes at a time of great anxiety about the future. In

4  Introduction
part, it is connected to the rise of authoritarian politics around the world
and the perceived degradation of political discourse often metonymized
by the name Donald Trump.3 At the same time, it is no longer possible to
ignore the disturbing ubiquity of “persuasive technologies” in our everyday
lives and the new forms of propaganda they make possible.4 Moreover, the
introduction of the term “post-truth” into the lexicon has sedimented the
feeling that science and reason are engaged in a winner-take-all struggle
against those who prefer to use words only as weapons.5 This way of fram­
ing things was quickly put to the test with the arrival of the COVID-19
pandemic. Unsurprisingly, debates about the safety and efficacy of vaccines
and other public health measures were quickly divided along the familiar
lines of the rational and the rhetorical, the scientific and the sophistic.
The irreconcilability of philosophy and rhetoric is thus so deeply
entrenched in the Western philosophical tradition and popular conscious­
ness that the many iterations of this ancient quarrel seem to have had little
effect in redrawing the battle lines. This is all the more surprising given
that 20th-century European philosophy saw a great “renaissance” of rhet­
oric in the postwar period.6 While many factors contributed to this “rhe­
torical turn”,7 its emergence was inextricably linked to what is arguably

Introduction  5
the central philosophical development of the 20th century: the widespread
recognition of language as both a medium and object of philosophical reflec­
tion.8 As Samuel IJsseling captures this development in the 1970s: “No mat­
ter how divergent and varied contemporary philosophy may be, there is one
common feature of all philosophers today, namely, the attention given to lan­
guage. Whoever embarks upon reflection on language necessarily confronts
the problem of rhetoric.”9 While the studies of IJsseling and others shed light
on the latent rhetorical content in recent European philosophy, the roots of
philosophy’s traditional hostility towards rhetoric are deep and continue to
prevent philosophers from reassessing its value for their own practice.
One of the motivations for this study is a worry about the comforting
allure of this old opposition. The worry is this: by continuing to typologize
discourse along rhetorical lines, that is, by continuing to think of philoso­
phy and rhetoric like oil and water, we foreclose access to a dimension of
philosophical practice that desperately needs our attention. If philosophers
are to play any role in meeting the challenges posed by the sorts of societal
issues mentioned above, we must rethink what we mean by “rhetorical”
and stop using it as a label for things we do not like. Instead, we would do
better to recognize that every discourse, indeed every human action, has a
rhetorical dimension in virtue of our capacity for reflecting on the expec­
tations, values, and preferences of those around us. Rhetoric is thus not
something we can opt out of, but it is something we can do better or worse.

*
This brings us to the most recent manifestation of the conflict between
philosophy and rhetoric with which our study begins. Nowhere in con­
temporary European philosophy is the symptomatic character of rheto­
ric more striking than in the ongoing debate between Alain Badiou and
Barbara Cassin. Indeed, as Slavoj Žižek aptly characterizes it, the Badiou-
Cassin debate is “a new version of the ancient dialogue between Plato and


6  Introduction
the sophists”.10 However, there is one curious feature of the debate that
neither the participants themselves nor commentators have drawn atten­
tion to. Despite taking opposed sides in this ancient dialogue, Badiou and
Cassin surprisingly both regard rhetoric as an enemy to their respective
projects. For Badiou, the proud Platonist, rhetoric’s concern with audi­
ences and their opinions leaves it entirely divorced from truths, which he
takes to be the main concern of the philosopher. For Cassin, the avowed
sophist, rhetoric was never anything more than a philosophical ruse—
a Platonic invention designed to suppress the creative power of genuine
sophistic discourse. In either case, then, rhetoric is seen as a dead end for
contemporary thought.
Motivated by the incompatibility of their respective interpretations, our
study begins by asking whether rhetoric has more to offer than Badiou and
Cassin suggest. Responding to their criticisms, in the following chapters
we shall see why rhetoric remains an indispensable area of concern regard­
less of where one stands on the debate. Importantly, our aim is not to
provide a critique of Badiou and Cassin’s respective projects. This would
take us well beyond the limits of our study, and there is certainly much
more to their works than a critique of rhetoric. Rather, our aim is to use
their critiques as motivation for rethinking the way that philosophers tend
to think about rhetoric and its relation to their own practice.
In support of our more positive assessment of rhetoric, we draw on the
pioneering “new rhetoric” of Chaïm Perelman (1912–1984), developed
with long-time collaborator Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca (1899–1987), as well
as the hermeneutic philosophy of Paul Ricoeur (1913–2005). To different
degrees, Perelman and Ricoeur both manage to suspend philosophy’s long-
standing prejudice against rhetoric and reconsider the significance of the
deceptively simple fact that discourse is always oriented towards an audi­
ence. After clarifying the terms of their own debate, Ricoeur’s hermeneutic
philosophy allows us to expand Perelman’s technical notion of “audience”
from the specific way that a speaker constructs their addressee to the way
that a “text” itself does so. Where Perelman’s definition suffers from cer­
tain psychological limitations, Ricoeur allows us to identify what we call
the “audience of the text”, the audience construction embedded in the text
distanciated from the original intentions of the author. Rather than con­
tinue to oppose philosophy and rhetoric as different kinds of discourse or
competing disciplines, we use this expanded notion of audience to expose
the “rhetoricity” of discourse, the rhetorical dimension of human action
obscured by philosophy’s deep-rooted mistrust of its ancient rival.
10  Slavoj Žižek, Less than Nothing: Hegel and the Shadow of Dialectical Materialism (Lon­
don: Verso, 2012), 77.


Introduction  7
Far from an exegetical curiosity, then, we turn to Perelman and Ricoeur
because the conceptual resources they provide enable a better description
of what philosophers actually do. While at times having to read them
against the grain, Perelman and Ricoeur both help to remind philosophy
of its own rhetorical shadow, of the various audiences which sit both
upstream and downstream from the event of thinking. Without an aware­
ness of these audiences, that is, without recognizing its place in the field of
action, philosophy risks becoming detached from the actual. Rhetoric is
therefore as consequential for the most theoretical and seemingly abstract
forms of reflection as the most applied and seemingly practical interven­
tions. If rhetoric is constitutive of philosophy, as we argue, then a greater
sensitivity for rhetorical concerns will not only clarify the practice of doing
philosophy, but it can also help us do it better; it can help us become more
aware of our many audiences, become less insular, and become better
equipped to engage with other disciplines and the world that conditions us.
*
We begin our argument in Chapter 1 with an examination of the Badiou-
Cassin Debate. Our aim here is to understand Badiou and Cassin’s reasons
for rejecting rhetoric while at the same time highlighting the incompat­
ibility of their assessments. After analysing Badiou and Cassin’s respective
positions, we show how both their critiques converge on rhetoric’s concern
with audiences. For different reasons, both find rhetoric’s conceptualiza­
tion of addressivity too narrow and unable to deal with the driving con­
cerns of their respective projects.
Before addressing Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms, Chapter 2 turns to the
“new rhetoric” project of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca. Our aim here is
to see how the idea of the rhetorical “audience” has been understood on
its own terms, prior to the criticisms of Badiou and Cassin. To this end, we
provide a general interpretation of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s most
important work, The New Rhetoric: A Treatise on Argumentation (1958).
Here we highlight the central methodological role of the audience in their
theory of argumentation—the very concept that Badiou and Cassin take to
be most problematic about rhetoric. We then survey the reception of their
work across various disciplines, drawing attention to its relative neglect
by philosophers. This not only helps to contextualize Badiou and Cassin’s
criticisms but also those of Paul Ricoeur which we discuss in Chapter 5.
Building on our interpretation of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca,
Chapters 3 and 4 reintroduce Badiou and Cassin into the discussion.
This time, however, we examine their criticisms of rhetoric by staging a
trial with Perelman. Our aim in these chapters is to assess whether there
remains something of value for contemporary philosophy in Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s conception of audience. While Badiou and


8  Introduction
Cassin identify certain gaps and limitations in Perelman’s position, we
argue that these problems are remediable within the original ambit of
the new rhetoric project. Thus, rather than do away with rhetoric and
discard the concept altogether, we conclude that Badiou and Cassin’s
criticisms should instead motivate the search for an expanded conception
of audience.
Chapter 5 begins our search for an expanded conception of audience
by turning to Ricoeur. Given that Ricoeur does not himself understand his
work in rhetorical terms, we first examine several key texts from the 1950s
to the 1970s that reveal his latent concern with rhetoric. To fully justify
this connection, we then reconstruct the “missed encounter” between
Perelman and Ricoeur in The Rule of Metaphor (1975). By bracketing
Ricoeur’s own (often borrowed) conception of rhetoric, we argue that he
shares a similar concern with audiences as Perelman, despite approach­
ing the question from the perspective of hermeneutics. This overlapping
concern brings us to Ricoeur’s contribution to an expanded conception
of the rhetorical audience. By analogy with Ricoeur’s critique of “roman­
tic” hermeneutics, we argue that it is possible to extend Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s conception of audience in the direction of what we call
the “audience of the text”. Significantly, this extension does not require
us to abandon rhetoric for hermeneutics but reframe the way rhetoric is
understood. It is here that we introduce our conception of “rhetoricity” as
a particular dimension of all discourse.
Having justified the possibility and fruitfulness of using Ricoeur to
expand Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work, Chapter 6 examines three
threads in Ricoeur’s philosophy that come into focus when viewed through
the lens of rhetoric. Pace Cassin and Badiou, each thread illustrates the
rhetoricity of philosophy itself. First, to Cassin’s charge that rhetoric is too
philosophical, we argue that Ricoeur’s notion of “refiguration” reconciles
the strong opposition Cassin erects between her sophistic conception of
performance and Perelman’s rhetorical conception of audience. Second,
to Badiou’s charge that rhetoric is too sophistical, we argue that Ricoeur’s
account of “phronetic judgment” illustrates the constitutive rhetoricity of
philosophical practice—even for a philosophy that rejects Perelman’s plu­
ralism. Finally, taking a step beyond the Badiou-Cassin debate we argue
that rhetoric is best understood as a basic human capacity and as such can
be incorporated into Ricoeur’s philosophical anthropology. While most
visible in the production and reception of arguments, our rhetorical capac­
ity can be seen across the full spectrum of human action. We conclude by
suggesting that those who stand to gain most from taking the rhetoricity
of philosophy seriously are those who understand philosophy not only as
a way of interpreting the world but also as a way of changing it.


1
Long-time friends, co-editors, and co-authors, Alain Badiou (1937–) and
Barbara Cassin (1947–) are also long-time enemies.1 Over the past several
decades, Badiou and Cassin have been engaged in a friendly but spirited
debate that has in recent years come to a head in the form of several books
which now permit a more direct examination of their respective positions.
Yet, there is one aspect of their debate that neither Badiou and Cassin nor
their commentators have drawn attention to—both present equally force­
ful, yet mutually incompatible answers to the question of philosophy’s
relation to rhetoric.2
While Badiou and Cassin disagree fundamentally about sophistry and
its relation to philosophy, they both side against rhetoric and its defenders,
albeit for different reasons. Where Badiou views philosophy’s rapproche­
ment with rhetoric in the 20th century as the regrettable triumph of soph­
istry over philosophy, Cassin sees only the enduring success of a Platonic
ruse, the philosophical eclipse of a more genuine “sophistics”. Faced with
these diverging interpretations, in this chapter we unpack Badiou and
1  In 1991 Badiou and Cassin replaced Paul Ricoeur and Francois Wahl as editors of the
series “L’ordre philosophique” for Seuil (François Dosse, Paul Ricœur. Les sens d’une vie
(Paris: La Découverte, 2001), 459). After resigning in 2007 over a disagreement with the
publisher, they have since co-edited the collection “Ouvertures” with Fayard (Alain Beuve-
Méry & Jean Birnbaum, “Divorce philosophique: Barbara Cassin et Alain Badiou quitte le
Seuil pour Fayard”, Le Monde, 4 October 2007).
2  Mladen Dolar, for example, focuses on how Badiou and Cassin base their arguments on
two different strands of Lacanian theory and play the one off the other (Mladen Dolar,
“Sophist’s Choice”, Crisis & Critique, Vol. 6, No. 1 (2019), 67–84). For his part, Slavoj
Žižek briefly discusses the debate in Less than Nothing, but does so primarily to distinguish
Badiou and Cassin’s respective ontological positions from his own (Slavoj Žižek, Less than
Nothing: Hegel and the Shadow of Dialectical Materialism (London: Verso, 2012), 77–78).
Building on Žižek’s argument, Alexander Stagnell argues for a kind of synthesis between
Badiou and Cassin which he calls “dialectical sophistics” (Alexander Stagnell, “Slavoj
Žižek and Dialectical Sophistics: On the Relationship between Dialectical Philosophical
and Philosophical Rhetoric”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 53, No. 2 (2020), 134–156).
The Enemy of My Enemy
Philosophy, Sophistics, and Rhetoric in the
Badiou-Cassin Debate
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-2


10  The Enemy of My Enemy
Cassin’s reasons for rejecting rhetoric and identify the common target of
their critiques: rhetoric’s conception of “audience”.
We begin by looking at Badiou’s critique of the postwar philosophi­
cal landscape, his definition of philosophy, and consequent definition
of sophistry. We then turn our attention to Cassin and her very differ­
ent approach to “sophistics”. Unlike Badiou, Cassin attempts to rescue
sophistics from notoriety by challenging a number of deeply engrained
philosophical assumptions. From this overview of their respective posi­
tions, we then look closely at one aspect of their debate, their disagreement
over universalism and relativism. This disagreement allows us to identify
rhetoric’s conception of audience as the common source of their otherwise
incompatible critiques.
1  Badiou and the Figure of the Sophist
While the interpretation of any living philosopher faces certain limitations,
our case presents additional difficulties. Alain Badiou is both a prolific
writer and a systematic thinker. We must first therefore justify how we
propose to approach Badiou’s sizeable corpus. Concerned as we are with
the way he understands and distinguishes philosophy from sophistry and
his related conception of rhetoric, we focus primarily on his critique of con­
temporary philosophy, rather than his own systematic project (expounded
in a trilogy of systematic works written over the span of 30 years).3 This
distinction allows us to narrow our discussion to a number of key texts.
First, we review Badiou’s basic philosophical position in Manifesto for
Philosophy [Manifeste de la philosophie] (1989). Published one year after
Being and Event [L’être et l’événement] (1988), Manifesto for Philosophy
serves as an entry point into Badiou’s thought and allows us to establish
the basic terms of his critique of postwar philosophy. In a second step, we
look more closely at this critique in Conditions [Conditions] (1992) where
Badiou most clearly articulates and defends his definition of philosophy.
This definition is of particular interest in relation to Cassin as Badiou does
not simply define philosophy over and against sophistry, but rather builds
the conflict into the definition itself. In a final step, we examine Badiou’s
critique of the “linguistic turn” and his arguments against conceiving phi­
losophy as a “general rhetoric”.
3  The three books of the “Being and Event” trilogy are: Being and Event (1988), Logics of
Worlds (2006), and The Immanence of Truths (2018). For an overview of the systematic
relation between these three “big philosophical books”, see Badiou, The Immanence of
Truths: Being and Event III, trans. Susan Spitzer and Kenneth Reinhard (London: Blooms­
bury, 2022), 22-25/L’Immanence des vérités. L’être et l’événement, 3 (Paris: Fayard, 2018),
16–20.


The Enemy of My Enemy  11
Return to Philosophy
True to its title, Badiou’s Manifesto for Philosophy is less a comprehensive
treatment of his own philosophical system than a programme for over­
coming a deadlock in the state of philosophy towards the end of the 20th
century. The main targets of Badiou’s polemic are several of his contempo­
raries, whom he argues have abandoned the idea that philosophy is even
possible.4 Indeed, unlike other disciplines Badiou observes that this resig­
nation is unique to philosophy:
Only philosophers have interiorized the notion that thought, their
thought, encountered the historic and political crimes of this century
and all those leading up to it, both as the obstacle to all continuation
and as the tribunal of a collective and historic intellectual forfeiture.5
For Badiou, philosophers appear to have taken on an unbearable burden,
assuming responsibility for “the sublime or repugnant avatars of the politi­
cal” in the 20th century.6 Despite the apparent humility of this gesture,
Badiou points out that it actually imputes considerable power and impor­
tance to the philosopher—much more than the very same “modest parti­
sans of the impasse of philosophy” would want to admit.7 This modesty
thus harbours a hidden potency: philosophy becomes the historical agent
responsible for the great political calamities of the 20th century. Rejecting
any false modesty, Badiou’s programme thus calls for an end to this “end
of philosophy” and proposes to diagnose, criticize, and surpass the sources
of philosophy’s recent self-abnegation.8
To appreciate the force of Badiou’s critique, we first need to come to
grips with three key concepts in his philosophy: “condition”, “truth”, and
“event”. Deploying these interrelated concepts, he invites his reader to
break with the impasses of historicism and relativism and recover the pos­
sibility of philosophy once again.9 By “condition” Badiou understands a
4  Here Badiou is referring primarily to Philippe Lacoue-Labarthe, Jean-François Lyotard,
Gilles Deleuze, and Jacques Derrida.
5  Badiou, Manifesto for Philosophy, trans. Norman Madarasz (Albany, NY: State University
of New York Press, 1999), 28-29/Manifeste pour la Philosophie (Paris: Éditions du Seuil,
1989), 8.
6  Badiou, MP, 29/MPP, 9.
7  Badiou, MP, 29/MPP, 9.
8  For a more recent discussion of this theme of “the end” of philosophy, see Badiou’s dia­
logue with Giovanbattista Tusa in Alain Badiou and Giovanbattista Tusa, The End: A Con­
versation, trans. Robin Mackay (Cambridge: Polity, [2017] 2019).
9  This emphasis on the possibility of philosophy is exemplary of a certain kind of Platonic
pedagogy pervading all of Badiou’s work. In one episode of Dina Dreyfus’s television
series, Le temps des philosophes, from 1965 entitled “Philosophy and Truth”, Badiou


12  The Enemy of My Enemy
domain of human experience which, prior to and irrespective of any philo­
sophical activity, is capable of producing its own kind of truth. As we shall
see below, Badiou identifies four such conditions of philosophy: science,
politics, love, and art. But to understand the full meaning of this concept,
we must also see how he recasts the notion of “truth”.
Unlike many other philosophers, Badiou makes a firm distinction
between truth [verité] and knowledge [savoir].10 Knowledge, or what is
merely “veridical”, refers to what can be identified and classified in terms
of “what there is” in a given situation or world, i.e. in terms of properties
that can be indicated by explicit phrases or sets of phrases within a given
language.11 Truth, on the other hand, is “indiscernible” in relation to what
there is; truth is, from the perspective of knowledge, an immanent “excep­
tion” to the world.12 Understood this way, truth does not refer to the cor­
respondence between propositions and things, but rather a procedure of
something new.13
The newness of such a procedure, of a truth, is the result of what Badiou
calls an “event”. At its most basic level, an event can be understood as an
unpredictable and incalculable supplement to a given situation that stands
in excess of what could have been known previously. An event is thus the
appearance of novelty; it interrupts the conventional laws through which
a given situation (or “world”) had previously been known. We can think
distinguishes “dogmatic” teaching, which seeks to impart to students the views and doc­
trines of particular authors and schools, from teaching in the “rigorous sense of the term”,
which Badiou defines as “the teaching of the possibility of philosophy” (Tho, Tzuchien and
Bianco, Giuseppe, eds. Badiou and the Philosophers: Interrogating 1960s French Philoso­
phy, trans. Tzuchien Tho (London: Bloomsbury, 2013), 96).

et l’événement (Paris: Seuil, 1988), 361. See also Badiou, Infinite Thought: Truth and
the Return to Philosophy, trans. ed., Oliver Feltham and Justin Clemens (London: Con­
tinuum, 1998), 61. Here Badiou compares this distinction to Kant’s distinction between
reason and understanding and Heidegger’s distinction between aletheia and technê.
11  Badiou, Being and Event, 328/L’être et l’événement, 362.
12  Badiou, Being and Event, 327/L’être et l’événement, 361. Badiou introduces the language
of truth as an “exception” in Logics of Worlds and its companion manifesto; see Badiou,
Logics of World: Being and Event, 2, trans. Alberto Toscano (London: Continuum, 2009),

Badiou, Second Manifesto for Philosophy, trans. Louise Burchill (Cambridge: Polity C,
2011), 20-22/Seconde manifeste pour la philosophie (Paris: Flammarion, [2009] 2010),
28–31.

de l’universalisme (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, [1997] 1998), 7.


The Enemy of My Enemy  13
of Marx’s conception of a revolution as an example of an event, a moment
of novelty giving rise to a new procedure of truth—in this case a political
truth. Badiou describes Marx’s conception of revolution as “the affirma­
tive revelation of the hidden laws of society: class struggle, contradictions,
economic power”, but also as “the destructive transgression of all these
laws: collective economy, dictatorship of the proletariat”.14 While this
is but one example, pertaining to the political condition of philosophy,
events within all four conditions induce the appearance of a “subject” who
may or may not be faithful to it. The subject of an event is thus faced with
a choice to either support the incorporation of the novel truth into the
world or betray it and stubbornly insist on the prior frame of reference.15
Events, and the truth procedures they make possible, only occur in a
number of specific “conditions” or domains of human experience. As we
mentioned above, Badiou identifies four such domains—science, love, art,
and politics—all of which are necessary conditions of philosophy’s appear­
ance. But why these four domains and not others? Indeed, why only four?
As Peter Hallward explains, Badiou’s reason is that it is only in these four
domains that a subject (whether individual or collective) can emerge; or,
more precisely, it is only within these four fields or dimensions of experi­
ence that a subject can be “fully subtracted from the operations of knowl­
edge and re-presentation”. This is why, Hallward continues, there can be
no “subject of athletics, of agriculture, of charity, of education. The ordi­
nary ‘service of goods’ precludes all subjectivation”.16 It is thus only in the
evental domains of science, love, art, and politics that mundane human
existence can become a “subject to truth”.
Not to be confused with any kind of historicism, Badiou understands
these conditions and the truths they engender to be historically “trans­
versal”. Truths, in other words, are enduring points of reference for the
philosopher:
[The conditions of philosophy] are uniform procedures recognizable
from afar, whose relation to thought is relatively invariant. The name
of this invariance is clear: it is the name ‘truth’. The procedures that
condition philosophy are truth procedures, identifiable as such in their
recurrence. We can no longer believe the narratives by which a human
group invokes its origin or destiny. We know that Olympia is only a hill
14  Badiou, “The Three Negations”, Cardozo Law Review, Vol. 29, No. 5 (2008), 1878.
15 “Examples: the appearance, with Aeschylus, of theatrical Tragedy; the irruption, with
Galileo, of mathematical physics; an amorous encounter which changes a whole life; the
French Revolution of 1792” (Badiou, “Philosophy and truth”, Infinite Thought, 62).
16  Peter Hallward, Badiou: A Subject to Truth (Minneapolis, MN: University of Minnesota
Press, 2003), 181–182.


14  The Enemy of My Enemy
and the Sky is filled only with hydrogen or helium. But that the series
of prime numbers is unlimited may be demonstrated today exactly as
in Euclid’s Elements, that Phidias was a great sculptor is not in doubt,
that Athenian democracy was a political invention whose theme still
occupies us and that love designates the occurrence of a Two in which
the subject is transfixed—this we understand when reading Sappho or
Plato just as when reading Corneille or Beckett.17
Conditions should thus not be confused with anything like factual cir­
cumstances, but invariant anchoring points out of which philosophy con­
stitutes itself across time. The four conditions—science, art, politics, and
love—are thus not strictly speaking philosophical in and of themselves but
rather sites where truth procedures—or “generic procedures” in Badiou’s
more technical vocabulary—can exist for thought beyond the historical
contexts from which they emerge. What this affords Badiou is a way to
reinvigorate a philosophical concern with truth by side-stepping the his­
toricist paradigm.
If recognizing that the existence of truths proper to each of the four
domains is necessary for philosophy, what exactly does philosophy do
with these truths? What operation does it perform? Since Badiou defines
truths as being in excess of what materially binds them to their historical
situation, something is needed to bring them together, something is needed
to render them contemporaneous to one another in thought. This is the
task that Badiou assigns to philosophy:
The specific role of philosophy is to propose a unified conceptual space
where the naming of events which serve as the point of departure for
truth procedures take place. Philosophy seeks to gather together all the
additional names (noms-en-plus). It deals, in thought, with the compos­
sible character of the procedures which condition it. It does not estab­
lish any truth, but it sets a locus of truths.18
It is not therefore the role of philosophy to produce truths; philosophy
plays no role in their production. Although essentially concerned with
truth—indeed this concern, as we will see, is what for Badiou distinguishes
it from sophistry—philosophy is not itself a truth procedure. Rather, phi­
losophy constructs a “locus” or conceptual space for the truths that have
been produced within the domains that condition it. By thinking the truths
proper to the conditions of science, love, art, and politics, by “making a
hole” in knowledge from which truths are subtracted, philosophy weaves
17  Badiou, MP, 33-34/MPP, 13–14.
18  Badiou, MP, 37, translation modified/MPP, 18.


The Enemy of My Enemy  15
“a general space in which thought accedes to time, to its time, so long as
the truth procedures of this time find shelter for their compossibility within
it”.19
By orienting his own philosophical project in relation to truths, Badiou
believes he is in a good position to mount a principled critique of any
philosophy that fails to think all four of its conditions. As he argues in
many of his polemical writings, much of what has claimed the title of
philosophy for itself since the 19th century fails to think all four of its
conditions.20 In Badiou’s Lacanian vocabulary, such a “philosophy” is said
to be “sutured”21 to one of its conditions. A sutured philosophy is one
that “delegates its functions to one or other of its conditions, handing
over the whole of thought to one generic procedure”.22 In other words,
a sutured philosophy takes some of its conditions for granted, allowing
itself to be guided by unthought premises smuggled in from the neglected
condition(s). Much of what is attractive in Badiou’s philosophy derives
from his ability to position himself over and against his interlocutors’s
failure to “de-suture” their thinking from philosophy’s four conditions.
Yet, this general picture of Badiou’s conception of the task of philoso­
phy in relation to its conditions has left out one important consideration:
philosophy does not operate in isolation. For Badiou, philosophy is always
engaged in a hegemonic struggle against sophistry. Indeed, Manifesto for
Philosophy itself is exemplary in this respect. Under Plato’s banner, Badiou
charges his contemporaries with betraying philosophy for sophistry. Here
and elsewhere, Badiou identifies contemporary sophistry with a certain
line of thought exemplified by, but certainly not limited to, Wittgenstein.23

20  Badiou, MP, 62/MPP, 42. This is not to say, however, that Badiou offers a wholesale rejec­
tion of recent philosophical history. Rather, this criticism is part of his effort to restore the
systematic aspirations that Badiou maintains are essential to philosophy.

first presented in the session of 24 February 1965 in Lacan’s twelfth seminar and later
published in Cahiers pour l’Analyse in 1965; see Peter Hallward and Knox Peden, eds.,

2012), 91–102.
22  Badiou, MP, 61/MPP, 41.
23  Badiou, MP, 97/MPP, 79. Although not specified here, Badiou is clearly thinking of the
later Wittgenstein. For a general overview of Badiou’s interpretation of Wittgenstein, as
well as his later distinction between sophistry and antiphilosophy, see Bruno Bosteels’s
introduction to Badiou, Wittgenstein’s Antiphilosophy (London: Verso, 2011), 1–66.
For a discussion of the sometimes blurry line between sophistry and antiphilosophy in
Badiou, see Justin Clements and Adam J. Bartlett, “‘The Greatest of Our Dead’: Badiou


16  The Enemy of My Enemy
What worries Badiou beyond any proper name, however, are the features
that this pervasive line of thought shares with the ancient Greek Sophists:
“[t]he decisive importance of language and its variability in heterogenous
games, doubt as to the pertinence of the concept of truth, rhetorical prox­
imity to the effects of art, pragmatic and open politics”.24 These features,
Badiou worries, “common to the Greek sophists and to any number of
contemporary orientations … explain why studies and references devoted
to Gorgias or Protagoras have recently been multiplied”.25 It is thus in
response to this revival of sophistry that Badiou’s “Platonic gesture” of (re)
defining philosophy in relation to truth must be understood.
Sophistry and the Definition of Philosophy
To look closer at the role of sophistry in Badiou’s philosophy we now turn
to Conditions, focusing in particular on the first two essays: “The (Re)turn
of Philosophy Itself” and “Definition of Philosophy”.26
“The (Re)turn of Philosophy Itself” elaborates five theses—a style which
pays homage to Badiou’s “masters”, specifically Louis Althusser.27 Badiou
opens with his first thesis: “Philosophy today is paralyzed by its relation
to its own history”.28 Here the critique of historicism is taken up in more
general terms, at a distance from the polemical context of Manifesto for
Philosophy. Unpacking this first thesis Badiou observes that contemporary
philosophy finds itself stuck between two equally insufficient tendencies.
The first tendency proceeds by means of a suture, whereby philosophy
“strives to graft itself onto established activities”—namely, one or more
of philosophy’s conditions. The second tendency proceeds by turning
inwards; philosophy becomes nothing but its own historiography, “a
museum of itself”.29
Preferring not to choose between these alternatives, Badiou affirms in
his second thesis that: “Philosophy must break, from within itself, with
and Lacan”, Badiou and Philosophy, ed. Sean Bowden and Simon Duffy (Edinburgh:
Edinburgh University Press, 2012), 190–193.

1995), Jean-François Lyotard and Jean-Loup Thébaud’s Au juste (Paris: Christian Bour­
geois, 1979), or the “Protagoras Notice” and “Gorgias Notice” in Lyotard’s Le Différend
(Paris: Minuit, 1983) (Badiou, Wittgenstein’s Antiphilosophy, n. 11, 14).

27  Badiou, CN, 3/CS, 57.
28  Badiou, CN, 3/CS, 57.
29  Badiou, CN, 3/CS, 57.


The Enemy of My Enemy  17
historicism”.30 To avoid both tendencies he exhorts philosophy to “initially
determine itself in the absence of any reference to history”.31 As he is care­
ful to explain, this is not to suggest that the history of philosophy should
be ignored. The point, rather, is more a question of method. What matters
is that philosophy should once again make “decisions of thought without
turning back towards a presupposed historical meaning that has been set
for these decisions”.32 Breaking with historicism thus involves accepting
at some level that philosophy is capable of legitimating its own discourse.
It is only after deciding upon its axioms and drawing their consequences,
he continues, that philosophy should then “summon its history”.33 As he
already argued in Manifesto for Philosophy, this means that philosophy
ought to “determine itself in such a way that it is philosophy itself that
judges its history, and not its history that judges it”.34
To accept this claim is to commit oneself, in one way or another, to
defining philosophy in non-historical terms. Enter Badiou’s third thesis:
“A definition of philosophy exists”.35 Without the compulsion to respond
in historical terms, Badiou can begin to speak more concretely about the
definition of philosophy. Like any good definition it should enable phi­
losophy to be distinguished from what it is not. This becomes all the more
important (and all the more difficult) when philosophical discourse is chal­
lenged by skilful imitators. It is here that Badiou first introduces the figure
of the sophist.
“Every definition of philosophy must distinguish itself from sophistry”.36
Badiou’s fourth thesis ties the future of philosophy to its ability to distin­
guish itself from sophistry, “philosophy’s enemy brother, its implacable
twin”.37 Given the stakes, it is important to know precisely how one is
to distinguish between the copy and the real thing, the imitator and the
imitated. The conventional way of responding to this question is to differ­
entiate between their respective kinds of discourse. Where the philosopher
deals only with what can be demonstrated through valid argumentation,
the sophist traffics in a deceptive and opportunistic rhetoric. Yet, Badiou
recognizes the insufficiency of any strategy that draws the line between phi­
losophy and sophistry solely on the basis of discourse, on how they speak.
For Badiou, this strategy always cheats: the problem is precisely that the
30  Badiou, CN, 4/CS, 58.
31  Badiou, CN, 4/CS, 58.
32  Badiou, CN, 5/CS, 59.
33  Badiou, CN, 5/CS, 59.
34  Badiou, CN, 5/CS, 59.
35  Badiou, CN, 5/CS, 59.
36  Badiou, CN, 8/CS, 62.
37  Badiou, CN, 5/CS, 60.


18  The Enemy of My Enemy
sophist, whether ancient or modern, is “externally (or discursively) indis­
cernible from the philosopher”.38
Who then are these sophists and how does Badiou propose to distin­
guish sophistry from philosophy? As he explains:
Modern sophists are those who maintain, in the school of the great
Wittgenstein, that thought is caught in the following alternative: that
it either consists in effects of discourse, in language games, or that it
consists in silent indication, in the pure “showing” of that which is
subtracted from language’s grasp. Sophists are those for whom the fun­
damental opposition is not between truth and error, or errancy, but
between speech and silence, that is, between that which can be said and
that which it is impossible to say. Or again: between meaningful and
meaningless statements.39
While there are noticeable shifts in Badiou’s targets throughout his writ­
ings, the general profile of the sophist remains consistent.40 Leaving aside
the reference to Wittgenstein, what Badiou draws attention to here is the
sophist’s exclusive emphasis on language, discourse, and the realm of
meaning (sens) more generally, at the expense of truth. Where sophistry
dwells within the endless proliferation of sense and meaning, “philosophy
is subtractive in that it makes holes in sense, or causes an interruption
in the circulation of sense”.41 Above all, “what the sophist professes to
show, ancient and modern alike”, is that the concept of truth is “use­
less and uncertain”, owing to an ontology recognizing that “conventions,
rules, genres of discourse and language games are all that exist”.42 Thus,
despite being “armed with the same rhetoric” and “drawing from the same
references”, what ultimately distinguishes sophistry from philosophy for
Badiou is that sophistry is organized around the affirmation, “there is no
truth” [il n’existe aucun verité].43

tard is one of Badiou’s major but less-often recognized “sophistic” interlocutors.

calls “democratic materialism” in Logics of Worlds, according to which all that exists
are “bodies and languages”; see Logics of Worlds, 1/Logiques des mondes, 9; SM, 19/
SMP, 28.

Éditions Albin Michel, 1991), 136, our translation. The text from which these passages

The Enemy of My Enemy  19
With the question of who is who turning on such a subtle difference, it
is not always easy to put this criterion to use. This is why Badiou is careful
to warn philosophy against any “anti-sophistical extremism”:
[Philosophy] loses itself whenever it feeds the dark desire to do away
with the sophist once and for all. This is precisely, in my view, what
defines dogmatism: the claim that the sophist, because he is like the
perverse double of the philosopher, ought not to exist. No, the sophist
must instead be assigned to his place [assignée à sa place].44
The need to retain the sophist as an adversary constitutes a philosophical
ethics for Badiou. The philosopher must refute the sophist, but nothing
more. For all his guile and deception, the sophist serves to remind the phi­
losopher that the category of truth is ultimately empty; it serves to remind
her that philosophy is not itself a truth procedure.45
While the sophist is construed as philosophy’s trans-historical rival,
Badiou observes that this rivalry is particularly acute in 20th-century
thought as a result of the “great linguistic turn”. Beneath the idea of “the
End of philosophy” and “the end of Truth” lies an “inherently sophistic
assessment of the century”, Badiou argues.46 On his view, philosophy must
reassert itself here; it must redeploy the category of truth and refute the
sophistic position, it must assign the sophist “to his place”. On the basis
of this critique Badiou can thus confidently close with his fifth and final
thesis: “philosophy is possible”.47
Truths, Not Language
Before turning to Cassin, we now want to concretize our discussion by
examining Badiou’s critique of “democratic materialism”, his term for
the dominant ideology of the contemporary world. According to Badiou,
the spontaneous philosophy of this worldview is that of “linguistic phi­
losophy”, which he argues is nothing but sophistry masquerading as phi­
losophy. According to Badiou, democratic materialism is defined by the
ontological commitment that there are “only bodies and languages”.48 As
the defining characteristic of sophistry, ancient and modern alike, is the
the chapter “Antiphilosophy: Lacan and Plato” in Badiou, CN, 228-247/CS, 306–326; see
Bosteels’s introduction in Badiou, Wittgenstein’s Antiphilosophy, n. 23, 23.
44  Badiou, CN, 18, translation modified/CS, 73.
45  Badiou, CN, 19/CS, 75.
46  Badiou, CN, 20/CS, 75.
47  Badiou, CN, 21/CS, 76.
48  Badiou, Logics of Worlds, 1/Logiques des mondes, 9.


20  The Enemy of My Enemy
affirmation “there is no truth”, Badiou’s critique will mobilize the cat­
egory of truth to distinguish philosophy (as he defines it) from democratic
materialism.
To distinguish itself from the sophistic worldview of democratic materi­
alism Badiou warns that philosophy should not overcompensate by defin­
ing truths as something ontologically more than bodies and languages—as
this would involve taking on theological or transcendental commitments,
which he also wants to avoid. Rather, for Badiou the philosopher’s claim
is that truths exist as exceptions to “what there is” (i.e. bodies and lan­
guages). As he puts it: “There are only bodies and languages, except that
there are truths”.49 Defining truths as “exceptional” with respect to what
there is in a particular world is Badiou’s way of confronting the same
dilemma Kant encountered in the Critique of Pure Reason: a critical path
is needed that takes us beyond scepticism and dogmatism. For Badiou,
truths cannot be defined either as a subjective form of judgment (scepti­
cism) or as having any ontological transcendence (dogmatism). Instead,
they must be rigorously defined in immanent terms.50 Truths for Badiou
are thus immanent exceptions to the world from which they emerge.
Badiou thus agrees with democratic materialism that truths are materi­
ally composed of bodies and languages. Where he differs is his claim that
truths also exist. However, to avoid falling back into a dogmatic posi­
tion, he defines truths, and the procedures which bring them about, as
exceptions to what is rather than something ontologically more or other.
Paradoxically, it is the very immanence of truths that bestows them with
universality—in the sense that their value exceeds the particularity of any
given world.51 Thus, while Badiou readily admits that truths are always
produced by determinate individuals in determinate historical and cultural
contexts, he insists that truths are nonetheless irreducible to the mate­
rial totality of bodies and languages in those contexts. Truths are thus
“transworldly” in the sense that they can be “understood and used in indi­
vidual and symbolic contexts that are as vastly distant as they are different,
in space as in time”.52 It is this “exceptional” status of truths that Badiou
deploys to differentiate truth from opinion and, by extension, philosophy
from the sophistic position of democratic materialism.
By attempting to restore philosophy’s concern with truths, Badiou is
pushing back against the “linguistic turn” so prominent in 20th-century
thought. The stakes of philosophy’s relation to language are most clearly
expressed in “The Desire of Philosophy and the Contemporary World”
49  Badiou, Logics of Worlds, 4/Logiques des mondes, 12.
50  Badiou, The Immanence of Truths, 27/L’immanence des vérites, 21–22.
51  Badiou, The Immanence of Truths, 27/L’immanence des vérites, 21–22.
52  Badiou, SM, 21/SMP, 29.


The Enemy of My Enemy  21
(1999).53 As he sees it, contemporary philosophy finds itself in a world
“subordinated to the merchandising of money and information”, “an anar­
chy of more or less regulated, more or less coded fluxes, wherein money,
products and images are exchanged”.54 If philosophy is to sustain itself in
such a world, he argues, it must be able to propose a “principle of inter­
ruption” capable of withstanding this “endless regime of circulation”.55 If
philosophy ignores the category of truth and deprives itself of the point of
interruption truth provides; if philosophy is content to remain within the
democratic materialist consensus, in other words, it resigns itself to “what
there is”—namely, the bodies and languages that make up “the general cir­
culation of knowledge, information, merchandise, money and images”.56
Faced with such obstacles, Badiou worries that the linguistic turn has left
contemporary philosophy with no way of extracting itself from the world
as it is.
Continuing his diagnosis, Badiou analyses contemporary philosophy
in terms of three broad orientations which, in spite of their inner diver­
sity, can be identified by two principal features: (1) the abandonment of
metaphysics and of the search for truth and (2) the subsequent adoption
of language as the “crucial site for thought”.57 The three orientations that
Badiou identifies by means of these two features are the “hermeneutic”,
“analytic”, and “postmodern” currents, all three of which replace the clas­
sical question of truth with the question of meaning.58 In their commitment
to “the polyvalence of meaning and the plurality of language”, Badiou
notes the close resemblance of each orientation to the world as it is. As a
result, he argues, they are unable to sustain the appropriate distance from
the world that philosophy requires.59 This critique is similar to Badiou’s
rejection of historicism in Manifesto for Philosophy: in both cases thought
is determined by what is rather than what is exceptional—truths. Without
truth, philosophy concedes to the “sophistic thesis” and loses the ability
to carry out its essential task—thinking the compossibility of the truths
proper to its four conditions.

Desire”, IN, n. 1, 57.

56  Badiou, IN, 49.
57  Badiou, IN, 47.

59  Badiou, IN, 50.


22  The Enemy of My Enemy
In Happiness [Métaphysique du bonheur reel] (2015), a text closely
related to “The Desire for Philosophy”,60 Badiou further unpacks his criti­
cism of sophistry and relation to the linguistic turn. Importantly, it is here
that Badiou explicitly equates sophistry and rhetoric:
The linguistic turn of the twentieth century basically resulted in a type
of doctrine that assimilates philosophy to a general rhetoric. This can
go as far as Barbara Cassin’s thesis: there is no ontology, only a logol­
ogy. It is language that cuts out and constitutes all that is proposed as
the form of being. The twentieth century has known a tendency, at once
academic, critical, anti-dogmatic, which progressively centred on the
creative power of language … In my view, this makes philosophy into
a general rhetoric, an inventive, modern rhetoric, all that you could
want.61
By equating sophistry and rhetoric, Badiou suggests that rhetoric is not an
aspect or dimension of philosophy (as we shall argue), but rather some­
thing in competition with it, similar to the way he understands religion.62
Yet, for all this, Badiou is left to explain the obvious fact—a fact
embraced by Cassin—that philosophy nonetheless never takes place out­
side of or beyond language. Without denying this fact, Badiou explains
how his philosophy relates to language:
language is not the absolute horizon of thought. The great linguistic
turn of philosophy, or the absorption of philosophy into the medita­
tion on language must be reversed… I am for philosophy’s revivify­
ing the idea that it does not take as its point of departure words,
but things. Needless to say, it must be acknowledged that a language
always constitutes what can be called the historical matter of truth
and of philosophy. A language always gives what I would call the

revised version of the earlier essay. As it was published in French in 2015 Badiou presum­
ably maintains his general diagnosis of the contemporary world and the state of philoso­
phy.
61  Badiou, Happiness, trans. A.J. Bartlett and Justin Clemens (London: Bloomsbury, 2019),
102/Métaphysique du bonheur reel (Paris: Presses Universitaires de Frances, 2015), 67.
62  When asked in an interview why he does not consider religion as a fifth condition of

rivalry with science, art, love, and politics, I would rather consider it a rival of philosophy
itself. We could say in dialectic with philosophy itself. I conceive of it as a domain that
has a desire, perhaps the exaggerated wish, to assume all conditions of human existence”

The Enemy of My Enemy  23
colour of philosophy, its tonality and its inflexion. All these singular
figures are proposed to us by language. But I would also maintain
that this is not the essential principle of the organization of thought.
The principle that philosophy cannot renounce is that of its uni­
versal transmissibility, whatever the prescription of style or colour,
whatever its connection to such or such a language. Philosophy can­
not renounce that its address is directed to everyone, in principle if
not in fact, and that it does not exclude from this address linguistic,
national, religious, or racial communities. Philosophy privileges no
language, not even the one it is written in. Philosophy is not enclosed
within the pure formal ideal of scientific language. Its natural element
is language, but, within that natural element, it institutes a universal
address.63
There is thus no dispute that language constitutes philosophy’s “natural
element”. Rather, what Badiou disputes is that language is the final hori­
zon of philosophy and could thus serve as its principle of organization.
Yet, what is of most interest in this passage is Badiou’s recourse to the
concept of addressivity—the recognition that the philosopher too has an
audience. Indeed, in spite of Badiou’s rejection of sophistry and all things
rhetorical, he cannot avoid defining philosophy in terms of its particular
form of address (its universal address). For now, we simply draw attention
to this, as we return to the tension between universality and addressivity
below and in later chapters.
2  Cassin and the Origins of (Philosophical) Rhetoric
We now turn to Barbara Cassin whose work radically challenges the
kind of philosophy Badiou advocates—for what could be a more radical
challenge to his Platonism than a rigorous defence of its oldest and most
cunning rival? Sophistry or, as she prefers to call it, “sophistics” [la sophis­
tique], is Cassin’s alternative to what she sees as the dominant “ontologi­
cal” way of thinking about logos in philosophy. Ontology, according to
Cassin, restricts the task of logos to “saying what is”. Against ontology,
Cassin proposes a sophistic countermodel, a “logology”, which is a term
she borrows from Novalis.64 If ontology is concerned with “saying what
63  Badiou, IN, 50–51, our emphases.
64  Surprisingly, Cassin makes no mention of Kenneth Burke’s influential use of the term “log­
ology”. See, for example, Kenneth Burke, The Rhetoric of Religion: Studies in Logology
(Berkeley, CA: University of California Press, [1961] 1970).


24  The Enemy of My Enemy
is”, logology, by contrast, is concerned with “making what one says be”.65
Cassin’s logology thus seeks to underscore the performative character of
discourse.
To show the contrast with Badiou, this section is devoted to unpack­
ing Cassin’s sophistic position and her reconfiguration of the opposition
between philosophy and sophistry. Importantly, we emphasize how this
reconfiguration involves distinguishing sophistics from rhetoric, which
Cassin regards as nothing but a “philosophical invention”. Our discus­
sion revolves around two of Cassin’s most important theoretical books.
The first, L’effet sophistique [The Sophistic Effect] (1995), is her most
systematic elaboration of sophistics—it is, even according to Badiou, noth­
ing less than a “major book” [un grand livre].66 The second is Quand
dire, c’est vraiment faire [How to Really Do Things with Words] (2018),
which Cassin envisioned as a sequel to The Sophistic Effect. Here, Cassin
further develops her conception of sophistic discourse as epideixis or
performance.67
The Sophistic Effect
While always accompanied by a rigorous textual and philological exami­
nation of ancient and classical texts, the aim of Cassin’s work is not pri­
marily historical. As she explains, her interest in the sophists has as little to
do with an “antiquarian zeal for ill-known texts” as with any “rehabilita­
tion” for its own sake of those “accursed thinkers” that exist beyond the
margins of traditional philosophy.68 In our view, Cassin’s use of the soph­
ists can be understood as playing on what Michel Foucault calls “the rule
of the tactical polyvalence of discourses”, according to which the circula­
tion of power in a given discourse renders the distribution of terms within
it “neither uniform nor stable”; where terms are not simply arranged in
a hierarchy divided between what is accepted and excluded. It is due to
this distribution that there can be “shifts and reutilizations of identical
65  Cassin, “Introduction: Toward a New Topology of Philosophy”, Sophistical Practice:
Toward a Consistent Relativism (New York, NY: Fordham University Press, 2014), 2. See
also Cassin, “Ontologie/Logologie”, L’archipel des idées de Barbara Cassin (Paris: Édi­
tions de la maison des sciences de l’homme, 2014), 23–28.
66  Badiou, “Logology Against Ontology” in The Adventure of French Philosophy, trans.
Bruno Bosteels (London: Verso, 2012), 309/L’aventure de la philosophie française. Depuis
les années 1960 (Paris: La fabrique éditions, 2012), 194.
67  A number of Cassin’s articles and sections from her books have been translated into Eng­
lish in Sophistical Practice: Toward a Consistent Relativism (2014). Where possible we
cite both the translations in this volume and the original texts.
68  Cassin, “Who’s Afraid of the Sophists? Against Ethical Correctness”, SP, 28–29.


The Enemy of My Enemy  25
formulas for contrary objectives”.69 Nowhere is Foucault’s idea more
applicable within philosophical discourse than in relation to the deceptive
instability of the distinction between philosophy and sophistry, between
the one who stands within or beyond the reach of logos.
Cassin’s choice of the phrase “sophistic effect” as the title of her first
major work is significant in this regard; it intends to capture something of
this unstable duality:
Sophistry is an effect, on the one hand, because it is an artefact, an effect
of philosophy. But sophistry, a fiction of philosophy, is also an effect
because it produces a recoil effect [un choc en retour] on philosophy
and never ceases to have an effect on it.70
Sophistry is thus paradoxically both (1) an effect of philosophy, a product
of its own making as well as (2) responsible for certain effects on the very
thing which gave rise to it. It is therefore not because sophistry falls outside
of philosophy that Cassin is interested in it but because its exclusion is a
“structural effect”;71 what interests her are sophistry’s effects on philoso­
phy which—despite its apparently fictional status—are a consequence of
its exclusion.72
This way of conceptualizing sophistry and its relation to philosophy
stands in stark contrast to more conventional philosophical narratives.73
We have already seen this narrative at work in our discussion of Badiou’s
definition of philosophy. While admittedly more nuanced, we saw that
Badiou defines the philosopher, in part, by her opposition to the sophist,
to the imposter who discursively imitates philosophy. Although warned
never to give in to “anti-sophistic extremism”, the philosopher must strive
to refute the sophist, to put him in his place by deploying the power of
truths.74 Although Cassin recognizes that Badiou insists upon regulating
this conflict with a “philosophical ethics”, she worries that the difference

Vintage, [1976] 1980), 100.
70  Cassin, L’effet sophistique (Paris: Gallimard, 1995), 11. Unless accompanied by a cor­
responding reference to an English translation, all translations are ours.
71  Cassin, “Who’s Afraid of the Sophists? Against Ethical Correctness”, SP, 29.
72  A short summary of Cassin’s argument can be found in Cassin, “Sophistique, histoire
sophistique de la philosophie”, ABC, 9–16. For a more detailed analysis of the “sophistic
effect” and its relation to the history of philosophy, see Andrew Goffey, “If ontology, then
politics: The sophist effect”, Radical Philosophy, 107 (2001), 11–20.
73  Two representative studies of this more conventional philosophical view are those of

74  Badiou, CN, 18/CS, 73.


26  The Enemy of My Enemy
between refuting and destroying the sophist is “perilously slim”.75 After
all, the traditional image of the sophist that Badiou reproduces is a difficult
character to sympathize with. In The Sophistic Effect Cassin ventriloquizes
the “traditional figure of sophistry” as it emerges from Plato’s dialogues
and repeated by Badiou:
ontologically, the sophist is not concerned with being, but takes refuge
in non-being and what is accidental; logically, he does not seek truth or
dialectical rigour but only opinion, apparent coherence, persuasion, and
victory in the oratorical joust; ethically, pedagogically, and politically:
his goal is not wisdom and virtue for the individual or for the city but
rather personal power and money; even on literary grounds, the figures
of his style are only the flourishes of an encyclopaedic vacuity. When
measured by the standard of being and truth, sophistry must be con­
demned as pseudo-philosophy.76
What troubles Cassin most about this all-too familiar view of sophistry is
the way it frames the problem. At work in the initial self-evidence of this
description is the “sophistic effect”, the structural exclusion of the sophist
from debates over physis as much as the polis.
To understand Cassin’s rejection of the conventional view of sophistry,
as well as how her conception of sophistics tries to sidestep philosophical
ontology, we need to start from the beginning: Where does the negative
image of sophistry come from? Who were these sophists and how can we
understand “rhetoric”, that elusive technique with which they are reputed
to have made weak arguments stronger and strong arguments weaker?
If we are to keep in mind the difficulties of the “sophistic effect”, one
way of answering this question must be avoided at all costs: to presup­
pose that there is a determinate and historically continuous set of practices
or doctrines called “rhetoric” that, identifiable in advance of the inquiry,
would enable us to locate a precise moment of origin. The complex strug­
gle for hegemony between philosophy and sophistry, as well as the conse­
quent reception of this struggle, mediates any direct access to the object of
investigation.
A more fruitful approach, Cassin suggests, would instead be to investi­
gate the very term “rhetoric”, rather than the doctrine that it supposedly
names. Given that the network of meanings associated with sophistry and
rhetoric derive their negative valence from their opposition to the philo­
sophical, Cassin wants to challenge the assumption which the opposition
75  Cassin, “Who’s Afraid of the Sophists?”, SP, 42.
76  Cassin, ES, 10/SP, 30, translation modified.


The Enemy of My Enemy  27
rests upon in the first place. The central claim Cassin defends is the
following:
My claim is that rhetoric is a philosophical invention, an attempt to
tame logos, in particular, the Sophist’s logos and its effects. The crea­
tion of rhetoric by philosophy is itself the very first “rhetorical turn” …
[This] matters because the strength of logos has been made to vanish,
caught and subsumed under rhetoric.77
There are thus two steps to Cassin’s argument: first, Cassin will show that
rhetoric, as understood by philosophers and rhetoricians alike, is an inven­
tion of the dominant ontological tradition in philosophy; second, that this
invention has served to occlude a more genuine sophistic conception of
logos, a “logology” concerned with its effects, with what it can do.
Plato’s Invention of Rhetoric
The first part of Cassin’s argument draws support from the work of Edward
Schiappa. In 1990 Schiappa published an influential article entitled “Did
Plato Coin Rhêtorikê?”, later published in Protagoras and Logos: A Study
in Greek Philosophy and Rhetoric (2003).78 Against the conventional wis­
dom that the sophists self-consciously practised and taught a particular
technique called “rhetoric”, Schiappa argues that it was in fact Plato him­
self who coined the term. While the term “rhêtôr” can be found in writings
from the fifth  and fourth  centuries, referring to someone who gave public
speeches, the term “rhêtôr-ikê”, referring to the technê of oratory, does
not. Rather, its first appearance seems to be in Plato’s Gorgias, composed
around 385 BCE.79
Beyond indirect philological evidence, Schiappa also offers some induc­
tive evidence internal to Plato’s dialogues. As Schiappa explains, Plato’s
dialogues reveal a striking proclivity for coining words ending in “-ikê”,
77  Cassin, “Rhetorical Turns in Ancient Greece”, SP, 76.
78  Edward Schiappa, “Did Plato Coin Rhêtorikê?”, American Journal of Philology, Vol.
111 (1990), 460–73; Schiappa, Protagoras and Logos: A Study in Greek Philosophy and
Rhetoric, 2nd ed. (Columbia, SC: University of South Carolina Press, 2003).
79  Schiappa, Protagoras and Logos, 40. Schiappa’s thesis should be contrasted with the more
conservative interpretation of Laurent Pernot: “The large number of texts that have disap­
peared—notably the texts of the sophists—imposes, moreover, the most extreme caution.
We will confine ourselves, therefore, to concluding that the word rhētorikē is attested in
the preserved sources from about 390, and that it appears in a way that leads one to think
that it was already in existence” (Laurent Pernot, Rhetoric in Antiquity, trans. W.E. Hig­
gins (Washington, D.C.: The Catholic University of America Press, [2000] 2005), 22–23).


28  The Enemy of My Enemy
denoting “art of”.80 This interpretation thus fits with Plato’s general strat­
egy of distinguishing philosophy and its characterizing pursuit of epistêmê
from various sorts of technê. This distinction is especially clear in the fol­
lowing passage from Plato’s Gorgias where the term first begins to appear:
Socrates: but no one asked you [Polus] what qualities Gorgias’ area of
expertise [technê] has. The question was what it is, and what Gorgias
should be called … Gorgias, won’t you tell us yourself what your area
of expertise [technê] is, and so what to call you?
Gorgias: It’s rhetoric [rhêtôrikê], Socrates.
Socrates: We’d better call you a rhetorician, then?
Gorgias: A good one, Socrates, if you want to call me what (as Homer
puts it) “I avow I am.”81
By provoking Gorgias to name the technê he possesses—and by having
Gorgias say it himself—Plato opposes the truth-seeking enterprise of phi­
losophy from the success-seeking enterprise of the rhetorician. As Schiappa
explains, in doing so, Plato presents “rhetoric” as something already avail­
able in the cultural stock of meanings and thereby as having a conceptual
constancy it appears not to have really had.82
Yet Schiappa only sets out to show that Plato invented the term, empha­
sizing that the implications of this remain unclear.83 Cassin, however,
wants to push the point further. She argues that this invention, what she
calls Plato’s “trick”, came to cement the “philosophical” conception of
logos as normative:
So there we are. It is as if the word “rhetoric” had always been available
and that it was, in fact, the true name for sophistry … the strength of
logos has been made to vanish, caught and subsumed under rhetoric.
And rhetoric itself has also been made to vanish as soon as it is born.
That is why it is such a clever and quasi-diabolic rhetorical turn—a
philosophical trick.84

anapolis, IN: Hackett, 1997), 791–869.
82  Schiappa, Protagoras and Logos, 44.
83  Schiappa, Protagoras and Logos, 40. In a recent paper Schiappa revisits the implications

The Enemy of My Enemy  29
Cassin’s analysis thus wants to emphasize what philosophy has been able to
accomplish by equating sophistics and rhetoric. This equation, she argues,
holds out a “double strategic interest” for philosophy. The first equation,
exploited throughout Plato’s Gorgias, consists of “excluding the sophist-
orator from philosophy and its history”.85 The second is that anyone who
seeks to defend rhetoric is immediately caught up in “sophistic rhetoric”,
which, from this perspective can only be thought of as epistemologically
deficient in some respect.86 With this trick Plato succeeds in installing the
philosopher as the gatekeeper of the legitimate usage of logos.
Aristotle and the Decision on Meaning
Yet for all this Cassin argues that it is really Aristotle who opens the
door for the kind of “anti-sophistical extremism” that even Badiou had
warned against. Despite the “philosophical ethics” he proposes to keep
this in check, what interests her is the “original place” where the ethical
enters into language.87 For Cassin, it is Aristotle who provides philoso­
phy’s canonical answer to this question in the form of an ethics governing
the basic conditions of meaning-making or signification, which, by defini­
tion, sophistic discourse violates. Unlike Plato (and Badiou), not only does
Aristotle look to refute the sophist but to exclude him and his use of logos
from the sphere of what is properly human.88 This Aristotelian gesture is
well captured by the title of Cassin’s 1989 book, La decision du sens [The
Decision on Meaning], coauthored with Michel Narcy. This “decision on
meaning” refers the way in which Aristotle circumscribes the basic require­
ments of signification in Book IV of the Metaphysics, which effectively
forecloses sophistic discourse from the sphere of rationality.89 Let us briefly
rehearse Aristotle’s argument.
Shortly after introducing the first principle of the science of being qua
being, the principle of non-contradiction, Aristotle anticipates objections
from those who would seek to deny it as the “most certain of all” principles:
