86  Cassin, ES, 412–413, our translation.
87  Barbara Cassin and Michel Narcy, La décision du sens: Le livre Gamma de la Métaphy­
sique d’Aristote, introduction, texte, traduction et commentaire (Paris: Vrin, 1989), 11/
Cassin, “Speak If You Are a Man, or the Transcendental Exclusion”, SP, 44. See also Cas­
sin, “Sens”, ABC, 29–34.
88  Cassin, “Introduction: Toward a New Typology of Philosophy”, SP, 6.
89  For an extended discussion of Cassin and Narcy’s La décision du sens and their interpre­
tation of Aristotle, see Stanislas Breton, “La Décision du sens”, L’Annuaire philosophique
1989–1990 (Paris: Seuil, 1991); “Sophistique et ontologie”, Revue philosophique de Lou­
vain, Vol. 90, No. 87 (1992), 279–296.


30  The Enemy of My Enemy
There are ways to demonstrate that [their views are] impossible by
means of refutation, if only the one disputing it says something; if he
says nothing, it is absurd to seek an argument to meet someone who
has no argument, insofar as he has none, for such a person, insofar
as he is such, is from that point on like a plant. I say that demonstra­
tion by means of refutation is different from demonstrating because the
one demonstrating would seem to require from the outset the thing to
be shown, while if someone else is responsible for such a requirement,
there would be a refutation rather than a demonstration. And the start­
ing point for all such arguments is not the demand that one say some­
thing either to be or not to be (for perhaps one might suppose that this
would require from the outset the thing to be shown), but that what he
says must mean something to both himself and someone else; for this is
necessary if he is going to say anything. For if this is not the case, there
would be no argument with such a person, neither by himself in relation
to himself, nor with anyone else.90
In her analysis of this passage, Cassin draws attention to Aristotle’s argu­
mentative strategy and the way he characterizes those who would object.
Since he cannot positively demonstrate the principle, as the argument goes,
Aristotle’s strategy consists of showing that, in any possible objection, the
principle is already at work. Yet, what Cassin focuses on is Aristotle’s
qualification that the principle is found to be already at work if only an
objector would “say something”—or, more precisely, if they were to
“mean” something, that is, to say something significant.
Cassin’s interpretation thus draws attention to the series of equivalences
in Aristotle’s refutation that take us from “saying something” (legein ti) to
“saying something significant” (sêmainein ti). This series of equivalences
can be summarized as follows:
	(1)	 Human beings are animals who speak (legei)—without speech human
beings behave as plants;
	(2)	 to speak is to say something (legei ti);
	(3)	 to say something is to signify something (semainei ti);
	(4)	 to signify something is to signify one and only one thing—if not, one
does not actually signify;
	(5)	 to not signify is to not say anything;
	(6)	 to not say anything is to not speak
90  Aristotle, Aristotle’s Metaphysics, trans. Joe Sachs (Santa Fe, NM: Green Lion Press,
1999), 1006a, 60.


The Enemy of My Enemy  31
	(7)	 to not speak is to behave like a plant.91
If the condition of saying anything is to signify one and only one meaning,
any kind of equivocal signification is equivalent to saying nothing at all.
As Aristotle’s dialectical method already assumes the principle in question,
Cassin calls this strategy a “transcendental refutation”, in the sense that
it prescribes the normative conditions of all dialogue and language use.92
Under the jurisdiction of Aristotle’s principle of non-contradiction,
the sophist is thus confronted with a forced choice (“your money or your
life!”, to borrow Lacan’s example):93 if the sophist accepts the terms and
says something, i.e. signifies in accordance with the principle of non-con­
tradiction, they submit to Aristotle’s prohibition of homonymy and forfeit
any other conception of logos. If the sophist does not accept, whether by
refusing to speak or by signifying nothing—both of which amount to the
same thing—they are excluded from the sphere of meaning and rational
activity altogether. By transgressing the principle and thus failing to sig­
nify, the sophist has accomplished nothing more than if they had simply
remained silent—in both cases the sophist behaves like a plant:
Not content to reduce sophistry to the harmful shadow cast by philoso­
phy, Aristotle develops a genuine strategy of exclusion. For if the soph­
ist perseveres in his so-called phenomenology, he condemns himself to
silence; and if he still insists on speaking, he then speaks logou kharin,
“to say nothing” or “for the pleasure of speaking”, by simply making
noise with his mouth. By equating the requirement of non-contradiction
with the requirement of signification, Aristotle is able to marginalise
those who resist and relegate these “speaking plants” to the confines not
only of philosophy but of humanity.94

York, NY: Fordham University Press, 2020), 61-62/Jacques le sophiste. Lacan, logos et
psychanalyse (Paris: Epel, 2012), 119–121; Le bonheur, sa dent, douce à la mort: Autobi­
ographie philosophique (Paris: Librairie Arthème Fayard, 2020), 73.
92  Cassin, “Speak If You Are a Man, or the Transcendental Exclusion”, SP, 46/“Comment
l’éthique vient au langage, d’Aristote à Habermas”, Aristote et le logos: Contes de la phé­
noménologie ordinaire (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 1997), 13. In this essay
Cassin traces this argument from Aristotle to the work of Karl-Otto Apel, Jürgen Haber­
mas, and Richard Rorty.
93  Lacan, The Seminar of Jacques Lacan, Book XI, 212.
94  Cassin, ES, 12.


32  The Enemy of My Enemy
With this examination of Cassin’s readings of Plato and Aristotle we arrive
at the first step of her larger argument: rather than any one stable, self-
same doctrine competing with philosophy, rhetorikê and sophistikê are
distinguished from philosophy by the very gesture that brings them into
being. In Plato, the invention of rhetoric distinguishes the art of persuasion
from the search for truth, and, in Aristotle, sophistry becomes the deliber­
ate violation of the conditions of meaningful speech.
Epideixis: Sophistic Performance
After recognizing philosophy’s hand in producing rhetoric and sophistry
the question now is how else sophistics might be understood. If rhetoric
and sophistry are simply by-products of philosophy, what exactly does
Cassin mean by “sophistics”? “To try to understand sophistics”, Cassin
advises, “one must at least be willing to consider, beyond the oppositions
between philosophy and rhetoric, meaning and nonsense, its discursive per­
formances as a number of sagacious standpoints against ontology”.95 In a
first approach, then, sophistics is “an evasion of metaphysics and an alter­
native, as early as the Presocratics, to the classical lineage of philosophy”.96
In other words, sophistics for Cassin is a kind of “strategic identity” that
responds to the terrain established by philosophy.97
If ontology is the means by which philosophy establishes this terrain,
“logology” is the strategic response of Cassin’s sophistics.98 By “ontology”,
Cassin is referring to the paradigm of thought she traces from Parmenides,
Plato, and Aristotle through to Kant, Heidegger, and Habermas—right
up to Perelman.99 The defining feature of this ontological paradigm is the
idea that the task of language is to express an already present reality given
prior to speaking. For ontology, logos is thus first and foremost logos apo­
phantikos, which, to paraphrase Heidegger, is discourse that lets what is
being spoken of manifest itself as what it really is.100 In short, ontology
95  Cassin, ES, 12.
96  Cassin, ES, 12.
97  Alain Badiou and Barbara Cassin, Homme, femme, philosophie (Paris: Librairie Arthème
Fayard, 2019), 73.
98  For a short summary of the argument, see Cassin, “Performance, performatif”, ABC,
123–132.
99  Cassin, ES, 19.
100 “In discourse (ἀπόφανσις) [apophansis], insofar as it is genuine, what is said should be
derived from what is being talked about. In this way spoken communication, in what
it says, makes manifest what it is talking about and thus makes it accessible to another.
Such is the structure of λόγος ἀπόφανσις [logos apophansis]” (Martin Heidegger, Being
and Time, trans. Joan Stambaugh (Albany, NY: State University of New York Press,
[1927] 2010), 31).


The Enemy of My Enemy  33
privileges the assertive power of language to the exclusion of others. For
logology, by contrast, logos is understood as a kind of performance (logos
epideiktikos).101 Attempting to subvert the hegemony of philosophical
ontology, Cassin’s sophistics adopts a new stance towards the relation of
saying and being: if ontology is concerned with saying what is, logology is
concerned with “making what one says be”.102
While this formulation is a good place to start, it runs the risk of sim­
plifying what Cassin herself claims she is trying to do. Rather than simply
invert the existing hierarchy (established by philosophy) between the phil­
osophical and the sophistic, between truth and persuasion, Cassin is really
trying, like J.L. Austin, to “play Old Harry” with such distinctions. By
focusing on the notion of performance (epideixis), she wants to shift away
from these received oppositions and approach this ancient conflict from
an entirely different perspective. What is important for Cassin about the
notion of performance is that it confronts philosophy with a “third dimen­
sion” of language hidden by the philosophical privileging of apophansis:
Philosophy has habituated us into counting to two. When one speaks,
one can either “speak of” or “speak to” … “To speak of,” to unveil,
to describe, to demonstrate, is of the major register of philosophy, con­
sidered as ontology and phenomenology. “To speak to,” to persuade,
to have an effect on the other, is of the register of rhetoric. From the
point of view of philosophia perennis, there is no third dimension of
language.103
From the hegemonic perspective of philosophical ontology, then, language
use can only fall along one of two opposed axes (see Figure 1.1). Where
the first axis is concerned with the relation between what a speaker says
and what is (philosophy), the second axis is concerned with the relation
between a speaker and their audience (rhetoric).
What Cassin opposes in this two-dimensional framing of language is
that it tacitly privileges the “ontological model” of logos. Once philosophy
has laid claim to the speaker-world axis—the traditional locus of truth—
anything concerned with the speaker-audience axis cannot but appear
suspicious. By framing the problem in this way, philosophy sees rhetoric
101  Cassin’s “sophistic” treatment of epideixis, which we unpack below, can be read along­
side Walker’s “revisionary” history of rhetoric, which similarly reconstructs rhetoric in a
more sophistic direction (cf. Jeffrey Walker, Rhetoric and Poetics in Antiquity (Oxford:
Oxford University Press, 2000)).
102  Cassin, “Introduction: Towards a New Typology of Philosophy”, SP, 2.
103  Cassin, “How to Really Do Things with Words: Performance Before the Performative”,
SP, 194, translation modified/Cassin, Quand dire, c’est vraiment faire. Homère, Gorgias
et le people arc-en-ciel (Paris: Fayard, 2018), 85.


34  The Enemy of My Enemy
and sophistry as having a dangerous preoccupation with audiences, some­
thing completely extraneous to its normative way of conceiving language.
Unhappy with this framing, sophistics then “does not want to understand
rhetoric as arising from the general discursive regime of apophantics”, it
challenges the assumption “that ‘speaking to’ [axis 2] is and must be sub­
mitted to ‘speaking of’ [axis 1]”.104 For it is precisely this submission, she
writes, that serves as the crucial point in determining “the philosophical
anchoring of rhetoric, grosso modo right up to Perelman”.105
Without stepping outside of this framing, then, sophistic discourse can
only be reduced to the rhetorical axis, the register of persuasion. Against
the perennial opposition of these two axes, Cassin insists that the genu­
inely sophistic notion of epideixis must be understood prior to the way
that it has been interpreted philosophically:
[Epideixis is] the rhetorical genre par excellence but in excess of rhetoric
philosophically assigned to its place [assignée à sa place]: an epideixis
does not describe in terms of truth or simply produce an effect of per­
suasion, but it carries out [effectue] with felicity what I call a world
effect [effet-monde].106
104  Cassin, SP, 195, translation modified/QD, 87.
105  Cassin, SP, 195, translation modified; QD, 88.
106  Cassin, QD, 96, our translation, our emphasis.

Speaking to:

The Enemy of My Enemy  35
In this passage Cassin alludes to Badiou’s remark that philosophy must
perpetually “assign sophistry to its place”. In an attempt to undercut this
argument, Cassin argues that sophistic performance is not about either
axis: epideixis and the “world effect”107 it brings about concerns neither
the disclosure of what is (ontology) nor the persuasion of an audience
(rhetoric); prior to any such consequences it is first and foremost a doing.
To clarify this difference, Cassin’s position can be contrasted with
Austin’s in How to Do Things with Words, whom she praises for “waking
philosophy from its apophantic slumber”.108 By calling into question that
“age-old assumption in philosophy”, that “to say something, at least in
all cases worth considering … is always and simply to state something”,
Austin found himself in need of a new vocabulary.109 After reworking his
initial distinction between “performative” and “constative” utterances in
the great “sea change” that gave birth to the theory of speech-acts, Austin
introduces the triad of “locutionary”, “illocutionary”, and “perlocution­
ary” acts at the beginning of Lecture VIII.110 The first thing to be avoided
here is to equate Cassin’s notion of performance with perlocution. Though
they seem to share the same prefix, Cassin points out that the “per” in
Austin’s “perlocution” refers to the “by means” of saying (as opposed
to the “in saying” characteristic of illocutionary acts). The “per” in per­
formance or performative, by contrast, “denotes the accomplishment of
a ‘right to the end’ … In the performative, the utterance is the means of
acting and of producing an effect”.111 If Austin’s notion of a locutionary
act corresponds to the traditional philosophical register of apophansis or
assertion, i.e. what Cassin calls the axis of “speaking of” (axis 1), then the
perlocutionary corresponds to the axis of “speaking to” (axis 2), the axis
of rhetoric philosophically conceived: assertion speaks of what is, whereas
persuasion, speaking to, is an effect produced by saying.
This leaves us with Austin’s notion of illocution. Although Cassin’s
notion of performance is in some ways closest to illocution, this too is
107  In his review of L’effet sophistique Pierre-Joseph Salazar insists that the term “effet-
monde” should not be translated as “world effects”, but “effects of worlds” (Pierre-
Joseph Salazar, L’effet Sophistique, by Barbara Cassin (Book Review), Rhetorica, Vol.
15, No. 2 (1997), 216). In a more recent paper, Oliver Feltham translates the term as
“effect-world” (Oliver Feltham, “‘Lying is a Bloodsport’: Barbara Cassin and Theatrical­
ity”, S: Journal of the Circle for Lacanian Ideology Critique, Vol. 13 (2022), 98–118. We
simply flag this issue and retain the translation used in Sophistical Practice.
108  Cassin, SP, 198, translation modified/QD, 93.
109  J.L. Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 2nd ed. (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University
Press, [1955] 1997), 12.
110  Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 150.
111  Cassin, SP, 201/QD, 97.


36  The Enemy of My Enemy
somewhat misleading.112 Unlike Austin, Cassin insists that sophistic per­
formance must be thought prior to the performative, that is, prior to its
opposition to constative, which would bring us back to the framework of
the two axes sketched above.
How then are we to understand sophistic performance if not in opposi­
tion to assertion or apophansis? Cassin’s preferred way of answering this
question is by way of Gorgias’s Encomium of Helen. Here it is Gorgias’s
discursive performance itself which casts a new Helen into the world, a
Helen worthy of praise. For Cassin, it is through the performance itself
that Gorgias “succeeds in turning the phenomenon into its contrary: the
phenomenon becomes the effect of the all-powerful logos”.113 What this
example brings to the fore is the undeniable creative or inventive power
of logos, that remarkable capacity of “turning the phenomenon into its
contrary” or “making what one says be”. Where philosophers have gone
to great lengths to show that speech has always already consented to ontol­
ogy, that saying is always bound to what already is, Cassin’s sophistics
wagers on the inverse:
There is a moment in any act of praising when language takes the upper
hand with respect to the object, when language crafts the objects, when
description and the commonplace (topos) are exposed. From ontology
to logology, from phusis to polis, from description to performance, it is
the moment of creation and of the creation of values: new koina which
allow us to start again, new common places, new common thoughts.
We are now in politics, manipulating evidence in order not to deal
with preexistent proofs but to contrive new types of obviousness. Such
is the power of logos that Plato had to tame and to challenge with
philosophy.114
It is this creative power of epideixis, of sophistic performance, as common
to Gorgias’s invention of a new Helen as it was to Plato’s invention of
rhetoric itself, that serves as the basis of Cassin’s thought and what moti­
vates her objection to philosophical ontology in the style of an Aristotle, a
Kant, a Habermas, a Perelman, or a Badiou.
3  Rhetoric in the Badiou-Cassin Debate
In print, the debate between Badiou and Cassin is less a series of isolated
confrontations than a protracted and often implicit dialogue interspersed
112  We discuss Cassin’s reading of Austin at length in Chapter 4, pp. 173–177.
113  Cassin, SP, 216/QD, 132.
114  Cassin, “Rhetorical Turns in Ancient Greece”, SP, 79.


The Enemy of My Enemy  37
with the occasional thrust and parry. For his part, Badiou’s first substan­
tive engagement with Cassin’s work is “Logologie contre ontologie”
[“Logology Against Ontology”], a book review of The Sophistic Effect
from 1996.115 A decade later, in Logics of Worlds, Badiou again makes
reference to Cassin in a brief appendix that further specifies his reasons
for opposing sophistry.116 In 2012, at a colloquium in Cerisy dedicated
to Cassin’s work, Badiou delivered an address entitled “Éloge de Barbara
Cassin”.117 For her part, Cassin’s responses are less frequent and less
explicit. Although she does reference Badiou in a few footnotes in The
Sophistic Effect, it is not until their later collaborations that a more com­
plete response begins to take shape.
Recently, however, a total of three co-authored books have appeared.
The first, Il n’y a pas de rapport sexuel. Deux leçons sur “L’Étourdit” de
Lacan [There is No Sexual Relationship: Two Lessons on Lacan] (2010), is
a dual reading of Lacan’s notoriously opaque 1972 essay “L’Étourdit”.118
The second, also from 2010, is a small book on Heidegger, entitled
Heidegger: Le Nazisme, les femmes, et les philosophes [Heidegger: His
Life and His Philosophy] (2010).119 In this text, Badiou and Cassin exam­
ine their respective ways of relating to Heidegger’s thought, his relation to
Nazism, and politics more generally. Finally, their most recent and most
pertinent collaboration for our purposes is Homme, femme, philosophie
(2019).120 This book is a dialogue comprised of a series of letters exchanged
by Badiou and Cassin between 2015 and 2017, a transcription of a discus­
sion that took place at Johns Hopkins University in 2015, as well as some
short essays in which they distinguish their positions on philosophy, the

38  The Enemy of My Enemy
role of gender in the philosophy/sophistry opposition,121 and Parmenides,
among other topics.
Taking Homme, femme, philosophie as our main point of reference,
we begin this final section by examining a fragment of their debate on the
question of universality and relativism in philosophy. This confrontation
not only foregrounds their disagreement about philosophy and sophistry;
it also brings out why they are both critical of rhetoric. In the last two
sections, we discuss Badiou and Cassin’s respective conceptions of rheto­
ric. These discussions allow us to bring out the incompatibility of their
interpretations.
Universalism vs. Relativism
In Badiou and Cassin’s discussion of Parmenides in Homme, femme, phi­
losophie, Badiou summarizes what he takes to be at stake for philosophy
in their debate:
The negative part of my work is, as is very often the case in philosophy,
to fight the dominant philosophical current at the time that I am writ­
ing. This current displays a decided relativism, of which my very dear
friend Barbara Cassin is one of the greatest representatives, a cult of dif­
ferences and identities, in particular linguistic and cultural, a propensity
to make the individual the supreme value, all of which in fact amounts
to eliminating the fundamental triad, which for me, in its a-religious
and preferably anti-religious instance, has always defined philosophy
proper: singularity, universality, absoluteness. This is in fact what the
word “truth” sums up, for all truth has nevertheless been recognised,
gradually, as having universal value; and could only be so as long as it
bore witness to a form of absoluteness […] The crucial question is: are
there truths, in the form of created works recognised as universal, that
bear witness to a form of absoluteness accessible to human practice?122
What for Badiou distinguishes philosophy from the “decided relativism”
[relativisme décidé] of Cassin’s sophistics is the triad of singularity, uni­
versality, and absoluteness summed up by the word “truth”.123 In this
121  For her take on this opposition, see Cassin, “Femme-philosophe”, ABC, 145–154.
122  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 101–102.
123  Badiou’s use of these three terms—universality, singularity, absoluteness—roughly cor­
respond to the perspective taken on truth in Being and Event, Logics of Worlds, and The
Immanence of Truths respectively (Badiou, The Immanence of Truths, 589/L’immanence
des vérites, 681).


The Enemy of My Enemy  39
particular dialogue, however, it is universality and its relation to truth that
occupies most of the discussion.
We have already touched upon the important role that universality
plays both in Badiou’s critique of contemporary philosophy and his own
reworking of the concept of truth. Here we should clarify that there are
at least two senses in which truths are universal for Badiou. The first is
ontological. As we have seen, Badiou wants to avoid a dogmatic position
by denying truths any transcendent ontological status. Instead, truths are
ontologically universal for Badiou in the sense that they “can be under­
stood and used in individual and symbolic contexts that are as vastly dis­
tant as they are different, in space as in time”.124 Truths are thus universal
because they exceed—in the form of an “exception”—the materiality of
bodies and languages that make up a given situation or world. Yet, truths
are also universal in a second sense, which we have also touched upon in
Badiou’s critique of language. In this second sense, truths are universal in
terms of their address; truths are “directed to everyone, in principle if not
in fact”.125 It is in virtue of their “exceptional” existence and universal
address that truths can be transmitted and appropriated in other times and
places.126 The task of philosophy is therefore not only about thinking the
compossibility of truths across its four conditions but also about ensur­
ing their transmission, that is, “sharing” them.127 This double movement,
immortalized in Plato’s image of the philosopher departing and returning
to the cave, is a consistent theme throughout Badiou’s work, which we will
return to below.
For her part, Cassin is highly sceptical of Badiou’s reliance on the con­
cept of universality. “The Universal”, she writes, “has only one way of
functioning, exclusion. My universal or nothing. My pure reason or chaos.
Only its sharing validates it … I will make you universal like me”.128 In spite
of the irony, we saw above that Cassin has several arguments as to why we
should be suspicious of philosophy’s universalizing tendency—even with­
out mentioning its clear political abuses. On the one hand, universality
was deployed by Plato to delimit genuine philosophical discourse from
its crafty imitators. On the other, it was used by Aristotle to exclude the
sophists from the sphere of humanity altogether. Cassin’s project is thus in
some ways the inverse of Badiou’s. Instead of constructing universality, the
124  Badiou, SM, 21/SMP, 29.
125  Badiou, “Philosophy and Desire”, IN, 50–51, our emphasis.
126  We discuss Badiou’s use of the terms “address”, “transmission”, and “inscription” in
Chapter 3, pp. 140-144.
127  Badiou, HA, 96/MBR, 61–62.
128  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 55.


40  The Enemy of My Enemy
point is rather to “complicate the universal with languages”.129 As Cassin
emphatically puts it:
There is no philosophy of Barbara Cassin. What interests me is the
pleasure of penetrating a language. I think there’s something extraor­
dinary about it. You [Badiou] may find it extraordinary to construct a
universal proposition, a complex and complicated universal proposi­
tion, and to propose a truth. Not me, I don’t feel the need for a truth. I
prefer strange discoveries, surprises, which shape a new perspective—as
long as I have a perspective and it can be called new. This is what hap­
pens when you philosophize in words, in tongues, in languages, in order
to construct.130
Cassin is thus also interested in constructing—not constructing universals
per se but constructing points of contact between languages, cultures, and
traditions through the work of translation. What she means by this is best
exemplified by the Dictionary of Untranslatables: A Philosophical Lexicon
[Vocabulaire Européen des Philosophies. Dictionnaire des intraduisibles]
(2004),131 a work she describes as “sophistic logology immersed in the
plurality of languages”.132
If Cassin’s project thus takes its cue from the factual multiplicity of
languages, Badiou’s cuts against it. For him, what Cassin describes as
the “pleasure” of language is insufficient to serve as an intellectual norm
for philosophy. By contrast, he argues that philosophy involves “forcing
language to establish itself in a regime, often austere in its effect, whose
essential norm is not to please, but to convince, even against the tastes of
the person who reads or listens to the philosophical discourse”.133 While
Badiou does not explain what he means here by “convincing”—surprised,
Cassin even interjects: “to convince, you say!”—he goes on to clarify how
he understands the ideal of philosophical language:
One of the essential aims of philosophical language is to combat equiv­
ocity … I do not particularly like the multiplicity of languages, which is
only the result of a dispersion originating in the human animal. More

130  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 116–117.
131  Cassin, ed. Dictionary of Untranslatables: A Philosophical Lexicon, trans. Steven Rand­
sal et al., trans. eds. Emily Apter et al. (Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2014)/
Vocabulaire Européen des Philosophies. Dictionnaire des intraduisibles (Paris: Le Rob­
ert/Seuil, 2004). For a discussion of this project in relation to her own work, see Cassin,
“Intraduisibles”, ABC, 189–206.
132  Cassin, “Philosophizing in Tongues”, SP, 249.
133  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 118, our emphasis.


The Enemy of My Enemy  41
generally, the philosopher must not accept language as it is, nor be
enchanted by the multiplicity of its forms. Rather, he must orient him­
self according to the ideal compass of a single, fundamental language.
This was Leibniz’s dream, in the form of a mathesis universalis. And it
is true, this can be seen as early as Parmenides, and became clear with
Plato, that mathematics is philosophically the most interesting language
available, the closest to the aims of philosophy.134
Rather than dispute this point, Cassin agrees with Badiou’s characteri­
zation of philosophy, conceding that “univocity has always been the job
of philosophy, from Aristotle to Leibniz, and up until today, up until ...
you [Badiou]”.135 Rather than objecting, Cassin simply points out that
the dream for a perfectly univocal discourse has never been realized, that
mathematics is not in fact the language of everyday life.136 Later on Cassin
unpacks her criticism further:
I reject your definition of philosophy. You give me here a beautiful defi­
nition of the mathematical ideal, of a certain Platonism in the manner
of the universal characteristic of Leibniz … A beautiful ideal, but not
mine … Moreover, it is a fact that equivocity and not univocity will be
the rule, inventive and unexpected—for yes, language, each language,
each interaction, is of course inventive. The philology-philosophy link
indicates that it would be a pity to do without this type of inventive­
ness. What am I doing? I don’t care about producing something true,
that is, something True. I indicate by my reticence that I don’t believe in
Truth (but you would tell me that you are interested in truths, plural),
and that, in my eyes at least, this does not amount to falling into what
you call doxa.137
Cassin thus takes her point of departure from the existence of the plurality
of languages—it is not enough to appeal to the univocity of mathematics
to escape from this condition.138

135  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 120.
136  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 121.
137  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 155.

42  The Enemy of My Enemy
To the extent that she is willing to accept anything like “the universal”
it is only on the condition that it helps us understand “why and how it is
relative”.139 Indeed, the only kind of universality she is willing to accept
is what she calls a “dedicated universal”, such as “the truth performed
by the Truth and Reconciliation Commission in South Africa—‘enough
truth for’ making the Rainbow People exist”.140 In contrast to Badiou’s
philosophical universalism, then, Cassin’s sophistic relativism is thor­
oughly “comparative”.141 It rejects the idea of eternal values as much as
the idea that everything is equal. Cassin draws from Protagoras to navigate
between the two horns of this well-worn opposition:
Protagoras radically changes the game, moving from the binary true/
false opposition to the comparative: “better”. We understand that there
is no Truth with a capital T, … but a “truer”. Not an absolute, but a
comparative; and more precisely still, a “dedicated comparative”: the
“truer” is a “better for”; for the best is defined as “the most useful”,
the most suitable for (the person, the situation, all the components of
that moment which the Greeks call kairos, “opportunity”) […] In this
perspective, there is no being to be sought below the appearing, … no
One to be sought below or above the multiple. There is no God’s point
of view, as in Leibniz, to unify all the perceptions of monads. Yet, and
this is what all our contemporaries who vilify relativism fail to see, not
all opinions are the same. This is why it is necessary, pedagogically and
politically, for individuals as well as for cities, to make people capable
of preferring the better, i.e. the better for. Politics does not consist in
universally imposing Truth (or imposing universal truth). It consists in
differentially helping to choose the best. Such is the very great subtlety,
but also the very great objectivity of relativism.142
By rejecting the superlative in favour of the comparative, Cassin’s position
highlights the situatedness of discourse: there is no “truth”, only what is
Lacan in Public: Psychoanalysis and the Science of Rhetoric (Tuscaloosa, AL: The Uni­
versity of Alabama Press, 2012).
139  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 170.

141  Cassin’s position here is remarkably similar to Perelman’s, which we discuss in more

142  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 174–175. Cassin discusses this further in “Genres and Gen­

The Enemy of My Enemy  43
“truer”; no best, only what is “better”, etc.143 In this way, Cassin’s sophis­
tics is equally concerned about philosophy’s address, or what Badiou calls
“transmission”. The difference, however, is that for Cassin transmission is
always dependent upon the various components of kairos, i.e. the persons,
the places, and values that make up any discursive situation.
What Is Philosophical Argumentation?
Badiou and Cassin clearly take opposing sides on the question of universal­
ity or relativism. What remains to be seen is how these radically opposed
positions nonetheless take rhetoric as a common enemy. To recall, where
Badiou argued that reducing philosophy to the most “general rhetoric”
among language games is to deprive philosophy of any critical potential,
Cassin argued that the Platonic invention of rhetoric erects a hierarchy
between two axes of language which privileges the ontological discourse
of the philosopher.
Returning to the same discussion of Parmenides in Homme, femme,
philosophie, we find Badiou arguing that Parmenides should not be seen as
the first philosopher. While Parmenides does speak about “being”, “non-
being”, and the “one and the multiple”, Badiou argues, there are texts from
many different cultures which speak of similar ideas, many of which long
pre-date Parmenides.144 According to Badiou, the content of Parmenides’s
Poem is not sufficient to be considered philosophy in the strict sense. The
reason for this, he explains, has to do with the form of its transmission.
Although Parmenides indeed uses what will become standard philosophi­
cal categories, formally speaking the Poem relies on elements of a “sacred”
or “religious” nature in the transmission of its contents. As Badiou points
out, the Poem and other such texts derive their validity from “the status of
those who pronounce them”—prophets, priests, rulers, etc.—rather than
by the “argumentative force, the will to prove, [and] the exposure of all that
is said to public criticism” characteristic of philosophy.145 The difference,
then, is that these texts contain “fabulous stories, not argumentation”:
Philosophy is precisely a break with narratives of a religious nature.
Philosophy is a secularization of thought, the birth of which is con­
temporary, in fact, with the first purely demonstrative science, namely
mathematics, and not related to a fable. […] In philosophy, you assume
that what you say is true, not because you are a God, a King, a Goddess,
a Priest, a Poet or even a Philosopher, but because your arguments,
143  See also Cassin, “Relativisme consequent”, ABC, 155–161.
144  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 106.
145  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 107.


44  The Enemy of My Enemy
your proofs, subjected to meticulous, even hostile criticism, prove to be
convincing. The true essence of philosophy is rationally standardized
discussion [la discussion rationnellement normée].146
This passage is of particular interest as it brings together a number of
points relevant to our question.
In breaking with what he calls religious narratives, Badiou characterizes
philosophical discourse in terms of the following interrelated features:
	(1)	 The validity of philosophical argumentation does not derive from the
social status of the speaker.
	(2)	 For an argument or proof to be considered valid, it must be exposed to
and withstand criticism from others—it must prove to be convincing.
	(3)	 The ideal of philosophical discourse is an ordered discussion adhering
to rational norms.
These three points not only clarify Badiou’s own position; they also give
us a clear picture of Badiou’s problem with conceiving of philosophy as a
“general rhetoric”. Using Aristotle’s triad of the three pisteis or “means of
persuasion”—logos, ethos, and pathos—as a frame of reference, Badiou’s
reasons for opposing rhetoric become clear.147 Unlike sacred or religious
narratives, the validity of philosophical argumentation (modelled on the
precision of mathematical reasoning) does not depend on the status of
the speaker—the speaker’s ethos or character is irrelevant. Philosophical
arguments must in some sense prove themselves. Moreover, the validity
and conviction they obtain through criticism is not won by any manipula­
tion of pathos but by the self-evidence of the matter itself—by the force of
logos alone. It is in this sense that, like Plato, mathematics for Badiou “is
what allows us to break free from the sophistical dictatorship of linguistic
immediacy”.148
By taking language as the absolute horizon of philosophical thought,
those who take philosophy only as a “general rhetoric” neglect its scientific
146  Badiou and Cassin, HFP, 107–108.
147  The phrase “means of persuasion” is a common translation of Aristotle’s term “pisteis”.
As Aristotle explains in On Rhetoric: “Of the pisteis provided though speech there are
three species; for some are in the character [ēthos] of the speaker, and some in disposing
the listener in some way, and some in the speech [logos] itself, by showing or seeming to
show something” (Aristotle, On Rhetoric: A Theory of Civic Discourse, 2nd ed., trans.
George A. Kennedy (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2007, 1356a, 38, additions are
Kennedy’s).
148  Badiou, “Mathematics and Philosophy: The Grand Style and the Little Style”, Theoreti­
cal Writings, ed., trans. Ray Brassier and Alberto Toscano (London: Continuum, 2006),
17.


The Enemy of My Enemy  45
condition. For Badiou, this means neglecting the mathematical “event”
of Cantorian set theory and its consequences for the standards of rigour
which philosophers must now hold themselves to.149 Without the resources
of all four of philosophy’s conditions, Badiou fears that even an honest and
well-intentioned rhetoric (let alone sophistry) will inevitably succumb to
what “originally taints argumentation” from the beginning—namely, the
fact that one is always arguing with someone.150 Thus even the philoso­
pher must deal with the fact that one’s own argumentation is vitiated from
within; that when I argue, “I am necessarily on that someone’s ground
since it is he whom I want to convince”.151 Indeed, as Badiou points out,
all argumentation presupposes a certain “equality” between arguer and
audience.
Yet, if argumentation presupposes the equality of interlocutors, if its
point of departure is what Badiou calls the sophistic “symmetry of opin­
ion”, what can attest that an argument “is really an argument for truth
against an erroneous opinion? What will extract it, visibly and assertively,
from a debate between different opinions?”152 At bottom, this is Badiou’s
problem with rhetoric. Mired in the plurality of language games and with­
out recourse to truths, rhetoric appears incapable of surmounting this
obstacle on its own.
Ambiguities of Philosophical Rhetoric
We have already seen that, for Cassin, Badiou’s way of framing the oppo­
sition between philosophy, on the one hand, and sophistry and rhetoric,
on the other, is skewed in favour of philosophy. This is why Cassin was
critical of rhetoric (philosophically conceived) in the first place. To recall,
her claim was that “rhetoric is a philosophical invention, an attempt to
tame logos, in particular the Sophist’s logos and its effects”.153 By erect­
ing an opposition between two axes of discourse, one between speaker
and being, and the other between speaker and audience, philosophers have
always been able to submit audience concerns to the rules of ontological
discourse. In doing so, all uses of language that are not first and foremost
ontological, namely those which speak of what is, are relegated to the
lesser axis of persuasion. Since truth only pertains to the ontological axis,
149  Badiou explains the relation of mathematics to his philosophy in “Mathematics and
Philosophy and “Philosophy and Mathematics” included in Theoretical Writings.
150  Badiou, Le Séminaire: Pour aujourd’hui: Platon !: 2007–2010 (Paris: Fayard, 2019),
691, our translation.
151  Badiou, Le Séminaire: Pour aujourd’hui: Platon !, 691, our translation.
152  Badiou, Le Séminaire: Pour aujourd’hui: Platon !, 691, our translation.
153  Cassin, “Rhetorical Turns in Ancient Greece”, SP, 76.


46  The Enemy of My Enemy
the rhetorical axis is deficient by default. For Cassin, this submission was
“the crucial point that determines the philosophical anchoring of rheto­
ric, grosso modo right up to Perelman”. The basic premise of sophistics,
therefore, is the rejection of this submission, of this way of framing things,
however else discursive performance might be understood.
Cassin’s argument is grounded in the ambiguities of Plato’s depiction
of rhetoric. As she explains, “Plato is at the origin of the philosophically
fundamental question, no doubt the only properly philosophical question
that arises regarding rhetoric: that of the ambiguity, or even homonymy,
of rhetoric”.154 Cassin’s interpretation focuses on two main ambiguities in
Plato’s dialogues. The first concerns the ambiguity between a “good” and
“bad” rhetoric. The second ambiguity arises from thinking about rhetoric
as a neutral practice. In this second case the ambiguity arises in relation to
the ends for which an otherwise neutral rhetoric might be used.
The first ambiguity is a result of what Cassin calls Plato’s “double strat­
egy”. Not only does Plato invent rhetoric; he also makes it disappear. This
double strategy effectively corresponds to the different gestures made by
Plato in the Gorgias and the Phaedrus. As she explains, Plato’s strategy in
the Gorgias is to invent rhetoric and, by equating it with sophistry, devalue
it in one and the same stroke.155 This equation can be observed in the fol­
lowing passage from the Gorgias:
Socrates: I want to say (at the risk of sounding like a mathematician)
that as ornamentation is to exercise, so cookery is to medicine—or
rather, as ornamentation is to exercise, so sophistry is to the legislative
process, and as cookery is to medicine, so rhetoric is to the administra­
tion of justice.
Although sophistry and rhetoric are essentially different, in the way
I’ve described, there are, as I say, enough similarities for their practi­
tioners to find themselves all jumbled together in the same province and
with the same concerns, until they don’t know what to make of one
another and no one else does either.156
If Plato’s strategy in the Gorgias is to equate rhetoric with sophistry, his
strategy in the Phaedrus, by contrast, is to portray it as a kind of second
coming—rhetoric is put to the cross only to return as philosophy itself. In
both cases, however, the point of departure is the same. As Cassin observes,
Plato defines “rhetoric” in both texts as atekhnos tribê, which Waterfield
154  Cassin, ES, 414.
155  Cassin, “Rhetorical Turns in Ancient Greece”, SP, 79-80/ES, 415.
156  Plato, Gorgias, trans. Robin Waterfield, 465c, 33.


The Enemy of My Enemy  47
renders as “unsystematic” or “experiential knack”.157 Unlike the Gorgias,
however, the Phaedrus goes further. Here we find the possibility of a genu­
inely philosophical rhetoric that, unlike sophistic rhetoric, is capable of
being defined and known by its practitioners. This genuine rhetoric, how­
ever, arises from the submission of this “knack” to philosophical dialectic:
Socrates: And what do we think would happen if “honey-tongued
Adastrus” or Pericles were to hear of any of the wonderful techniques
we were just discussing …? we were just discussing coarse to the peo­
ple who have written books on these techniques and teach them as if
they constituted rhetorical expertise? Or, since they’re cleverer than
us, would they tell us off too and say: “Phaedrus and Socrates, rather
than getting cross you should feel sorry for people who prove inca­
pable of defining rhetoric because they are ignorant of dialectic. As a
result of this ignorance they thought they had discovered what rhetoric
was when they had learnt only the necessary prerequisites to rhetorical
expertise”.158
Faced with the ambiguity between sophistry (the “bad” rhetoric of the
Gorgias) and philosophy (the “good” rhetoric of the Phaedrus), Cassin
argues that the two accounts effectively cancel out the very concept of
rhetoric. If bad rhetoric is sophistry and good rhetoric is philosophy, then
there is really no rhetoric at all; “one plus one equals zero”.159
This brings us to the second source of ambiguity surrounding rhetoric,
which can also be found in Plato’s Gorgias. In this case, the ambiguity
is not between two kinds of rhetoric but between two uses of “a single
axiologically neutral or indifferent technique”, one good and one bad.160
“Just like any competitive skill”, Gorgias explains to Socrates, “rhetoric
should be used when morally appropriate”.161 Just as before, Cassin argues
that this conception of rhetoric still remains trapped within a philosophical
frame:
It must therefore be noted that, if rhetoric as such is indifferently capa­
ble of the best and the worst, then, for the sophist as for Socrates, it
157  Cassin, SP, 81/ES, 417; Plato, Phaedrus, trans. Robin Waterfield, 260e, 48; Plato, Gor­
gias, 462c, 29.
158  Plato, Phaedrus, 269b, 60.
159  Cassin, ES, 414/SP, 80.
160  Cassin, ES, 419.
161  Plato, Gorgias, 47a, 20.


48  The Enemy of My Enemy
needs to be subjected to an extrinsic finality, to an ethics decided by an
authority that bears a strong resemblance to philosophy.162
Defining rhetoric instrumentally thus only defers the problem. If the scope
of rhetoric is limited to choosing the means of persuasion, the question of
whether those means are being put to appropriate ends is pushed beyond
its purview—and into the hands of philosophy. In short, the problem of
rhetoric is converted into an ethical problem or, more specifically, a prob­
lem of intention.
Cassin is thus looking for a conception that goes beyond the opposi­
tion of a good and bad rhetoric and the opposition between a good and
bad finality. To avoid falling back into any philosophical framing, and to
disqualify any ethics, Cassin argues that a “problematic of the effect” must
be adopted against a “problematic of intention”.163 The reason for this, she
explains, is that:
only the effect is index sui, not susceptible to pseudos, and allows rheto­
ric to evacuate ethics like a foreign body: it is the intention, playing on
the indiscernible, that makes the usurpation possible and forces one to
accept the mask of the sophist as soon as one does not loudly assume
the face of the philosopher. Rhetoric as such is, on the contrary, as
Aristotle suggests, compatible with both sophistry and philosophy. But,
from the Platonic-Perelmanian point of view of an ethics of intention
that always pre-exists and endures, a rhetoric thus undecidable can only
be sophistical or, in any case, bad.164
The resulting inability to imagine rhetoric any other way is ultimately
why Cassin wants to abandon rhetoric altogether and start again with
“sophistics”, a conception of logos that goes beyond “the general discur­
sive regime of apophantics”.
Against this discursive regime, Cassin’s sophistic logology instead takes
epideixis or the performance of discourse as primary. In this way, she side­
steps Badiou’s characterization of the sophist as the one who says “there is
no truth”. In her view, sophistics is more about the way that discourse is
conceived. This is why the sophist is not a sceptic: “The sophist’s position
162  Cassin, ES, 420.
163  Cassin, “Bonnes et mauvaises rhétoriques. de Platon à Perelman”, Figures et conflits rhé­
toriques, eds. Michel Meyer and Alain Lempereur (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de
Bruxelles, 1990), 25 our translation. Although the contents of this article are nearly iden­
tical to the chapter of L‘effet sophistique from which we have been citing, this particular
passage only appears in the article. A similar passage can be found in Cassin, ES, 435.
164  Cassin, ES, 435.


The Enemy of My Enemy  49
is not in relation to truth but with respect to discourse: Being, and truth
if one holds to it, is an effect of saying”.165 Cassin’s wager with sophistics
is that this reversal will avoid the ruse of what philosophy calls “rheto­
ric” and permit us to see, finally, that ontology is in fact a “subset of
logology”.166
4  Conclusion
Despite taking opposed sides in the ancient quarrel between philosophy
and sophistry, our discussion has revealed that Badiou and Cassin find
themselves united against rhetoric as something of a common enemy. While
their critiques appear quite different, what they both object to, above all
else, is rhetoric’s preoccupation with audiences. For Badiou this preoccu­
pation means an inability to grasp the universality of truths which alone
can break the endless circulation of opinion. For Cassin, rhetoric’s narrow
focus on audiences means an inability to appreciate the political salience
of world-effects, the performative power of language “in excess” of their
perlocutionary effect on any particular audience. In this one respect, then,
Badiou and Cassin’s enmity is belied by their agreement that rhetoric is a
dead end for contemporary thought.
In the following chapter we begin to challenge these overlapping nega­
tive assessments by examining a much more favourable view of the rhetori­
cal audience. For this positive account we turn to the “new rhetoric” of
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca. This will prepare us for our reconsidera­
tion of Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms in Chapters 3 and 4, where we ask
whether their reasons should lead us to reject a rhetorical conception of
audience altogether or expand and extend it.
165  Cassin, Jacques the Sophist, 37/Jacques le sophiste, 76.
166  Cassin, Jacques the Sophist, 95/Jacques le sophiste, 181.


2
The “new rhetoric” of Chaïm Perelman (1912–1984), developed together
with long-time collaborator Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca (1899–1987), is the
most ambitious attempt to recover rhetoric’s value for philosophy in the
20th century.1 While many philosophers might be associated with the so-
called “rhetorical turn” in 20th-century thought, Perelman is uniquely
explicit about his attempt to reconcile the long-standing conflict between
philosophy and rhetoric. Indeed, while the broader “linguistic turn”
would bring many philosophers into contact with rhetorical themes, few
recognized this connection as early as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca (c.
1948)—and fewer still were willing to stake their reputation on rhetoric’s
revival. Yet, as one might have expected, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
insistence on taking rhetoric seriously struck many philosophers, especially
those committed to a more traditional view of logic, as something of a
betrayal, even landing their works, as Johan van Benthem recounts, on the
list of “forbidden books” for students.2
1  While there is some disagreement about Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s respective contri­
butions to the new rhetoric project, it is generally recognized that Perelman was responsible
for its philosophical aspects. Olbrechts-Tyteca’s contribution, on the other hand, owing to
her social scientific training, “took the form of systematic and fine-grained description of
discourse at work” (Barbara Warnick, “Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca’s Contribution to the New
Rhetoric”, Listening to Their Voices: The Rhetorical Activities of Historical Women, ed.
Molly Meijer Wertheimer (Columbia, SC: University of South Carolina, 1997), 70). The
most extensive study of Olbrechts-Tyteca’s role in the project is that of David A. Frank and
Michelle Bolduc, “Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric”, Quarterly Journal of Speech,
Vol. 96, No. 2 (2010): 141–163. In the interpretation that follows, we emphasize the sig­
nificance of both aspects of the project. When discussing the project as a whole, we refer
to both authors. When discussing the philosophical stakes of the project, we refer only to
Perelman.
2  Johan van Benthem, “One Logician’s Perspective on Argumentation”, Cogency, Vol. 1, No.
2 (2009), 14.
The Audience in Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-3


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  51
Alongside these imposing credentials, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
“new rhetoric project” [hereafter NRP]3 is of particular interest to us
because it revolves around the very concept that Badiou and Cassin take
to be most problematic about rhetoric—the audience. As we shall see, this
concept is essential to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s recovery of rheto­
ric and the methodology at work in their theory of argumentation. In this
way, their project is already implicated in the stakes of the Badiou-Cassin
debate. While Perelman is never specifically targeted by Badiou, his theo­
rization and use of a rhetorical conception of audience brings him neatly
within the scope of Badiou’s critique. Cassin, on the other hand, frequently
refers to Perelman. In fact, she explicitly identifies him as the philosopher
of rhetoric par excellence, the contemporary inheritor of Plato’s ambigu­
ous conception of rhetoric. In this chapter we thus provide an interpreta­
tion of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work with a particular focus on
their distinctively rhetorical conception of audience.
We begin with a reading of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s most
important work, The New Rhetoric: A Treatise on Argumentation [Traité
de l’argumentation: La nouvelle rhétorique] (1958). More than any of the
other concepts introduced in this text, our concern with their key notion
of audience obliges us to address general interpretative questions about
the work as a whole and how it fits into the NRP more generally. As we
shall see, this is because the concept of audience is central to Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s methodology. Our interpretation thus focuses
on the way they use the concept of audience to expose the social prac­
tice of arguing to a quasi-phenomenological description. On our reading,
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca regard this descriptive detour as the first
step towards a general theory of argumentation which they see as a prereq­
uisite to the normative assessment of human action and thought.
In a second step we survey the reception of the NRP across several dis­
ciplines. This section provides some context for the criticisms of Badiou
and Cassin, as well as those of Ricoeur which we discuss in Chapters 5
and 6. As we shall see here, while some scholars working in the human and
social sciences were receptive to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s ideas,
this was much less so for philosophers. Ironically, it is among philoso­
phers, their intended audience, that they faced the most resistance. After
discussing some of their difficulties in finding a philosophical audience,
we briefly discuss the work of one prominent exception, Michel Meyer,
whose own philosophical approach, “problematology”, seeks to extend
3  Here we follow the convention used by Michelle Bolduc and David A. Frank in The Intel­
lectual and Cultural Origins of Chaïm Perelman and Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rheto­
ric Project: Commentaries on and Translations of Seven Foundational Articles, 1933–1958
(Leiden: Brill, 2023), 1.


52  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
Perelman’s conception of rhetoric in a more systematic fashion. In doing
so, however, we argue that Meyer overlooks the methodological role of
the audience that our interpretation emphasizes. Consequently, his work
does not defend rhetoric precisely where it is most contested by the likes
of Badiou and Cassin.
1  The New Rhetoric: A Theory of Argumentation
In this first section we turn our attention to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
most important work, The New Rhetoric: A Treatise on Argumentation
[hereafter Treatise], published in 1958. Not only is this text the most com­
prehensive expression of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s thinking about
rhetoric, but it has also been the primary (and in many cases the only)
point of reference for many of their readers. For these reasons, it serves
as our main reference. When necessary, we also draw upon Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s other writings to shed light on aspects of the Treatise
that require further elaboration. While many of the concepts introduced in
this text have been subject to considerable analysis and debate, commenta­
tors have tended to shy away from more general interpretative questions
about how the Treatise is to be read as a whole. In our reading, therefore,
we draw attention to the aim, scope, and methodology of their study. The
purpose of this approach is to isolate Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
understanding of the audience, a concept we shall continue to unpack and
ultimately expand in subsequent chapters.
Interpreting the Treatise
Finding a clear entry point into the Treatise is notoriously puzzling.
Indeed, as Alan Gross and Ray Dearin warn the reader, “there is no get­
ting away from the fact that [the Treatise] is difficult to read”.4 Or, as
Roland Schmetz puts it, the Treatise is “an easy book to read but difficult
to understand”.5 Either way, Perelman himself admits in the Foreword
to the 1969 English translation that the work is “compactly written and
demands close study from the very outset”.6 In our view, the source of the
difficulty is not so much the material or the style of writing, but the nature
of the inquiry itself. Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s study challenges a
4  Alan G. Gross and Ray D. Dearin, Chaim Perelman (Albany, NY: State University of New
York Press, 2003), ix.
5  Roland Schmetz, L’argumentation selon Perelman. Pour une raison au cœur de la rhéto­
rique (Namur: Presses Universitaires de Namur, 2000), 345.
6  Chaïm Perelman and Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca, The New Rhetoric: A Treatise on Argumen­
tation, trans. John Wilkinson & Purcell Weaver (Notre Dame, IN: University of Notre
Dame Press, 1969), vii.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  53
number of deeply engrained beliefs about reason and argumentation that
pervade Western thought. Matters are further complicated by the fact
that, as Michel Meyer points out in his Preface to the sixth French edition,
“nowhere in the Treatise is the specificity of the New Rhetoric’s approach
clearly stated”.7 Indeed, of the nearly 700-page text, the Introduction and
Part One combined—which establish the aim, scope, and methodology of
the study—total a mere 80 pages.8 While this would normally be sufficient,
it is hardly enough to do justice to the radical ambitions of the broader
project, let alone the numerous topics introduced in Part One. This distri­
bution is undoubtedly the source of many difficulties that have long frus­
trated readers, as crucial interpretative choices must often be made on the
basis of a single passage. One reason for this may be due to space limi­
tations imposed by the publisher.9 As Michelle Bolduc and David Frank
explain, according to Noemi Perelman Mattis, Perelman’s daughter, the
original manuscript was over 2000 pages long.10 To meet the publisher’s
demands Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca may have opted to minimize
explanatory material in favour of what appears to be most central to the
book, their study of argumentative techniques in Part Three—which totals
over 400 pages.
Whatever the cause, we should not allow what might have been included
to overshadow what the Treatise actually contains. While Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca do not walk us through their methodology, they do
clearly define the goal of their study. The following definition provides the
most secure point of entry for any interpretation: “the object of the theory
of argumentation is the study of the discursive techniques allowing us to
induce or to increase the mind’s adherence to the theses presented for its
assent”.11 This statement of purpose, which will appear with little varia­
tion throughout many of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s writings, will
require further unpacking as we proceed. In a first approach, however,
it is sufficiently clear: the Treatise is first and foremost a study of argu­
ments, that is, a study of the “discursive techniques” by means of which
  7  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, Traité de l’argumentation. La nouvelle rhétorique, 6e éd.
(Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de Bruxelles, [1958] 2008), vii, our translation.
  8  Based on the sixth edition of the Traité de l’argumentation (2008) cited above.
  9  The Treatise was originally published in two volumes by Presses Universitaires de France
as part of the series “Logos: Introduction aux Études philosophiques”, then edited by
Gaston Berger.
10  Bolduc and Frank, ICO, 6.
11  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 4, emphasis modified/TA, 5. See also, Perelman,
“Les cadres sociaux de l’argumentation” (1959), Rhétoriques, 2e éd. (Bruxelles: Édi­
tions de l’Université de Bruxelles, [1989] 2012), 308; “Recherches interdisciplinaires
sur l’argumentation” (1966), RH, 328; “Une théorie philosophique de l’argumentation”
(1968), RH, 211.


54  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
social actors seek to modify the “adherence” of others (or even oneself).
Unlike those of logical demonstration, the theses or conclusions for which
we argue do not enjoy the self-evident character of strict logical necessity.
Instead, arguers must earn the assent of their audience by providing justifi­
catory reasons of various kinds that build upon the available facts, values,
and preferences in one’s cognitive environment.12 In doing so, arguers can
deploy a range of argumentative techniques, some of which will be more
effective than others depending on the particularities of the audience and
the argumentative situation. It is to the study of these various techniques
that the Treatise is dedicated.
While the goal of their study is clearly defined, Meyer’s point still stands.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca fail to clearly specify their method of iden­
tifying, describing, and classifying these discursive techniques, or what
they also call “argumentative schemes” [schèmes argumentatifs]. This is
both surprising and unfortunate given the central place of these techniques
in the work. It also goes a long way in explaining their relative neglect
in the literature in comparison to theoretical discussions of concepts.13
Fortunately, however, there are other places we can turn for assistance.
Of particular help in this regard is Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca’s Le comique
du discours [The Comedy of Discourse] (1974), an underappreciated work
that constitutes one of the most interesting extensions of the NRP. For our
purposes, what is significant about this text is that Olbrechts-Tyteca draws
attention to the approach she and Perelman had adopted in the Treatise,
explaining to what extent it will be retained in her own study:
Let us recall that the Treatise adopted a kind of regressive approach ...
It seems to us today that this regressive approach … was a response,
perhaps without being fully aware of it at the time, to the need felt by
all the sciences at a certain moment to adopt a genetic classification—
meaning by this, in the broadest sense, a classification in which the dif­
ferent types of objects are in some way derived from one another. Our
chapters [by contrast] correspond to a successive deepening, starting
from what is given.14
12  Here we rely on Christopher W. Tindale’s account of the “cognitive environment” in,
among other places, The Philosophy of Argument and Audience Reception (Cambridge:
Cambridge University Press, 2015), 44–46.
13  Barbara Warnick and Susan L. Kline discuss this neglect in “The New Rhetoric’s Argu­
ment Schemes: A Rhetorical View of Practical Reasoning”, Argumentation and Advocacy,
Vol. 29 (1992), 1–2.
14  Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca, Le comique du discours (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de
Bruxelles, 1974), 22–23.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  55
This passage from Olbrechts-Tyteca helps to guide our interpretation of
the Treatise in the right direction, identifying the approach that she and
Perelman took as “regressive” rather than “genetic”.15 The choice of the
term “regressive” here recalls Perelman’s 1949 essay “First Philosophies
and Regressive Philosophy” [“Philosophies premières et philosophie
regressive”].16 Unlike the principles of “first philosophies”, whose absolute
value is determined by their self-evidence and indisputability, the principles
of a “regressive philosophy” derive their value from “the consequences
that can be drawn from them” and are “none other than the facts that
serve as a concrete starting point to all philosophical research”.17
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s clarification thus sheds light on the nature of their
quasi-pragmatist methodology.18 It is with a regressive method, which
refuses any strict separation of theory and practice, that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca approach the documents that serve as the raw material
for the Treatise—“arguments put forward by advertisers in newspapers,
politicians in speeches, lawyers in pleadings, judges in decisions, and phi­
losophers in treatises”.19 Thus, rather than “taking their cue from the mod­
els provided by the deductive or experimental sciences”, they begin their
15  In the Treatise Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca indicate that they had considered different
kinds of distinctions to systematize their study of argumentative techniques, but note that
they avoided distinctions which “have a genetic aspect foreign to our concerns” (NR, §44,
191/TA, 257). They even consider the possibility that all argumentative schemes might be
reducible to one fundamental, underlying scheme, but ultimately resist this temptation,
noting that “this would distort the first results of our analysis for the benefit of a precon­
ceived idea” (NR, §44, 192, translation modified/TA, 258).
16  This essay first appeared in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, Rhétorique et philosophie.
Pour une théorie de l’argumentation en philosophie (Paris: Presses Universitaires de
France, 1952).
17  Perelman, “First Philosophies and Regressive Philosophy”, ICO, 105/RH, 143–145.
18  Michel Meyer speculates that, “Had Perelman lived longer … he would surely have
continued his theorization of rhetoric by linking it to a certain form of pragmatism”
(Michel Meyer, What Is Rhetoric? (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2017), 53). Benoît
Frydman explicitly interprets the new rhetoric along pragmatist lines in Le sens des
lois. Histoire de l’interprétation et de la raison juridique, 3e éd. (Bruxelles: Bruylant,
2011), as does Guillaume Vannier in Argumentation et droit. Introduction à la Nouvelle
Rhétorique de Perelman (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 2001), 68. While there
are undoubtedly similarities, we would be more cautious. For example, in a letter to
American political scientist Harvey Wheeler, Perelman explains: “It is true that my ideas
are near to those of the pragmatists because I see from a rhetorical point of view strong
connections between thought and action, but I am not establishing any theory of truth.
I am connecting rhetorical reasoning to decision making. Should there be some similari­
ties between my ideas and those of American philosophers, surely Dewey and Peirce are
the nearest to me, but I feel nearer to Peirce” (Perelman, Letter to Harvey Wheeler, 20
September 1969, Fonds Chaïm Perelman, Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles,
BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 27.7).
19  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 10/TA, 13.


56  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
study of arguments with nothing less than precisely “what is given” [ce qui
est donné], as Olbrechts-Tyteca puts it, the full complexity of argumenta­
tive experience. They then proceed to interrogate this material, digging
below the surface for regressive principles of intelligibility enabling them
to organize and classify the various means by which arguers seek to modify
“the mind’s adherence”.
The result of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s efforts is a vast but sys­
tematic catalogue of “argumentative schemes”, which, indeed, is the pri­
mary task of the book. Rather than identify which sets of propositions are
valid or truth-preserving in the manner of formal logic—or even stipulate
what strong arguments ought to look like in the manner of a more norma­
tive theory—the theory of argumentation Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
present in the Treatise provides the conceptual tools for interpreting and
describing how arguers modify argumentative situations discursively. The
argumentative schemes they analyse thus aim to capture what arguments
do rather than what they ought to do.20
Although some may see this as an endorsement of argumentative rela­
tivism, in our view the chief virtue of the Treatise is precisely its attempt at
neutrality on normative questions. Rather than impose or prescribe what
is “rational” in advance, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s approach oper­
ates on the assumption that argumentative situations are already imbued
with reason, however inchoate; that argumentative situations are already
charged with normative expectations and commitments by virtue of the
arguers and audiences that populate them. Argumentation is thus con­
strued not as a rational order unto itself, but as a reflexive social practice
that erupts from and flows back into the social world. While Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca are not indifferent to normative questions, as far as the
Treatise is concerned, then, normative questions are better posed after a
detour through a descriptive study of argumentative techniques.
To avoid misunderstanding, our claim is not that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own scholarly activity is neutral in the sense of being
unguided by any normative commitments whatsoever. Indeed, there would
be no need for the NRP in the first place if Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
did not think it could contribute to the development of a human community
built more on reasonable agreement than violence and domination. Our
claim is rather that the theory of argumentation presented in the Treatise is
neutral about argumentation. In our view, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
understand the standpoint they take as argumentation theorists along the
lines of what sociologist Luc Boltanski calls “simple exteriority”, which
20  Vannier makes a similar point, noting that our authors do not attempt to “impose exclu­
sive rules of legitimate reasoning” (Vannier, Argumentation et droit, 97).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  57
refers to the “basic sociological operation of describing society”.21 Unlike
“complex exteriority”—which, building on the former, goes further by
making an explicit value judgment about what is being described—simple
exteriority suspends questions of normative justification for the purpose
of rigorous description.22 As Ernst Wolff puts it, “One assumes a position
of simple exteriority by taking a spectator’s position, outside the normal
unfolding of social events, in order to subject the phenomena to careful
study and reporting”.23 In the context of argumentation, this means that
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca first want to go out and look at the ways
people argue without having a fixed view of what makes arguments good
or bad, strong or weak, in advance.
To summarize thus far, Olbrechts-Tyteca’s remarks in The Comedy
of Discourse have, in part, helped us to overcome the difficulty observed
by Meyer. Not only does she draw attention to the nature of her and
Perelman’s method, but she also redeploys it in her own study, which
retains the same basic structure and section titles as the Treatise. Thus,
even without a complete explanation of their methodology, it is clear that
the structure and order of presentation in the Treatise is anything but arbi­
trary—it is part and parcel of their regressive rhetorical method.24
In our reading of the Treatise, we shall therefore pay particular atten­
tion to the order and structure of the work. Our approach is thus closer to
that of Schmetz than Gross and Dearin. Where Gross and Dearin choose
not to follow the order of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s presentation,
arguing that it “tends to obscure their most interesting ideas in a mass of
detail”,25 Schmetz opts for a slower and more patient approach, emphasiz­
ing the internal coherence of the Treatise.26 Similarly, we emphasize the
importance of locating their terms and concepts within the development
of the work as a whole. As we cannot discuss every aspect of the Treatise,
our exposition will try and follow Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own
advice that the order of topics in a discourse should be adapted to the needs
21  Luc Boltanski, On Critique: A Sociology of Emancipation, trans. Gregory Elliot (Cam­
bridge: Polity, 2011), 6.
22  Boltanski, On Critique, 6–7; Ernst Wolff, Between Daily Routine and Violent Protest:
Interpreting the Technicity of Action (Berlin: De Gruyter, 2021), 17.
23  Wolff, Between Daily Routine and Violent Protest, 160.
24  The same general structure and order of presentation is retained in nearly all of Perel­
man’s later summaries, including Logique juridique: Nouvelle rhétorique, 2e éd. (Paris:
Dalloz, [1976] 1999) and The Realm of Rhetoric, trans. William Kluback (Notre Dame,
IN: Notre Dame University Press, 1982)/L’empire rhétorique: Argumentation et rhéto­
rique, 2e éd. (Paris: Vrin, [1977] 2012).
25  Gross and Dearin, Chaim Perelman, xi.
26  Schmetz, L’argumentation selon Perelman, 345.


58  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
of one’s audience.27 While Gross and Dearin’s strategy was perhaps well-
suited to the needs of their audience—to introduce Perelman’s thought to
a primarily American, English-speaking audience largely unfamiliar with
his work—it is less suitable for ours. Where our reading departs from
Schmetz, then, is not so much in approach as in emphasis.28 His study is
of a much more general nature than our own. By contrast, the aim of our
exposition in this chapter is to expose the methodological role of the rhe­
torical audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work. On our read­
ing, the “audience” is the most innovative philosophical idea of the NRP.
In order to assess the merits of Badiou and Cassin’s respective criticisms, it
is therefore around this idea that our interpretation must revolve.
As we proceed, however, we must confront an interpretive difficulty
that has beset the reception of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work: the
conflation of what we shall call two distinct attitudes which are, admit­
tedly, not always easy to disentangle. These attitudes are (1) the theoreti­
cal attitude that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca themselves adopt in their
study and (2) the practical attitude of those arguers and audiences actu­
ally engaged in argumentation. In other words, our interpretation will
insist upon the distinction between (1) the primarily descriptive task of the
Treatise’s theory of argumentation and (2) Perelman’s own philosophical
position about what is at stake for persons engaged in argument, responsi­
ble to others for his or her judgments. While never absolute in practice, this
methodological distinction will allow us to argue later that the rhetorical
concept of audience at the core of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work
can withstand Badiou and Cassin’s respective criticisms of rhetoric.
The Cartesian Error
Published in 1958, the Treatise appeared alongside a number of impor­
tant studies associated with what some scholars call the “rhetorical turn”
in Western thought.29 As David A. Frank points out, 1958 in particular
27  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 508, translation modified/TA, 672.
28  As Schmetz explains, the aim of his study is “to investigate precisely what image Perel­
man and Olbrechts-Tyteca propose of the discursive practice of the philosopher and of
all those who argue more generally” (Schmetz, L’argumentation selon Perelman, 3, our
translation).
29  Alongside the works of Kenneth Burke and Walter J. Ong, Dilip Parameshwar Goankar
identifies the Treatise as part of the “explicit” rhetorical turn in “Rhetoric and Its Double:
Reflections on the Rhetorical Turn in the Human Sciences”, The Rhetorical Turn: Inven­
tion and Persuasion in the Conduct of Inquiry, ed. Herbert W. Simons (Chicago, IL: The
University of Chicago Press, 1990), 352–353.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  59
was something of a watershed moment for rhetorical studies.30 It was in
this year that Hannah Arendt’s The Human Condition, Michael Polanyi’s
Personal Knowledge, Walter J. Ong’s Ramus, Method, and the Decay of
Dialogue, and Stephen E. Toulmin’s The Uses of Argument were pub­
lished. Yet, at least as far as rhetoric is concerned, the Treatise stands in a
class of its own. None of these other works recognize to the same degree
the significance of recovering rhetoric’s lost connection to philosophy.
From its opening pages, it is clear that the Treatise intends to intervene in
a debate that lies at the heart of Western philosophical thinking: the very
meaning of reason.
In this section we examine the first part of the Treatise’s Introduction,
where Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca situate their work within the his­
tory of Western philosophy. Their objective here is to dissociate the dis­
tinctive rationality of argumentation from the “demonstrative paradigm”
of Western philosophy. This objective is clear from the first line of the
Introduction:
The publication of a treatise devoted to argumentation and its connec­
tion to an ancient tradition, that of Greek rhetoric and dialectic, con­
stitutes a break with a conception of reason and reasoning which, from
Descartes, has marked western philosophy for the last three centuries.31
Following their “discovery” of rhetoric and its connection to argumen­
tation in the late 1940s, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca now boldly
announce a break with the classical conception of reason associated with
the name of Descartes.32
According to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca the Cartesian paradigm
of reason is both monological and absolute. The temptation of a mono­
logical conception of reason already lurks in Plato’s dialogues. Take, for
example, Socrates’s warning against misology in the Phaedo. At a certain
moment in the dialogue Socrates begins to detect that, “like those who are
quite uneducated”, he is beginning to succumb to the temptation to get the
better of his interlocutors rather than pursue “the truth about the subject
of discussion”.33 While Socrates’s dialectical method indeed requires the
agreement of his interlocutors, he is careful to stipulate what distinguishes
30  David A. Frank, “1958 and the Rhetorical Turn in 20th-Century Thought”, The Review
of Communication, Vol. 11, No. 4 (2011), 239.
31  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 1, translation modified/TA, 1.
32  For Olbrechts-Tyteca's own detailed account of their discovery, see Olbrechts-Tyteca,
"Rencontre avec la rhétorique", in Centre National Belge de Recherches de Logiques, La
théorie de l’argumentation: Perspectives et applications (Louvain: Éditions Nauwelaerts,
1963): 3–18.
33  Plato, Complete Works, 91a, 79.
