60  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
his approach from mere agreement: “I shall not be eager to get the agree­
ment of those present that what I say is truth, except incidentally, but
I shall be very eager that I should myself be thoroughly convinced that
things are so”.34 Thus, what is most important is that Socrates agrees with
himself. By shifting the agreement he requires away from his interlocutors,
he tacitly establishes a value hierarchy between the two audiences: Socrates
becomes an audience to himself. Although reasoning with oneself is rarely
thought of as having an audience, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca extend
rhetoric’s reach even to the lone self-deliberator. Although not concerned
specifically with Plato, it is this conception of reason, where conviction
finds refuge in self-transparency, that defines the insular, and ultimately
solipsistic conception of rationality they rally against.
As we might have guessed, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s most fre­
quent target is Descartes. Yet, in the Introduction to the Treatise Descartes
functions primarily as a synecdoche for a wider strain of thought, one that
views the hallmark of reason in terms of self-evidence.35 As Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca explain, the idea of self-evidence has long been associ­
ated with the classical concept of “demonstration” that runs from Aristotle
all the way to modern logic. In Aristotle, a logical demonstration is gov­
erned by analytical rationality, that kind of reasoning concerned with the
necessary or what “cannot be otherwise”.36 Here, analytic reason finds
secure footing in what is eternal and fixed, allowing it to be subject to the
mechanical rigour of the syllogism.
Unlike Descartes, however, Aristotle also recognizes the importance of
dialectical reasoning. By contrast, dialectical reasoning is concerned with
the much broader field of all that can be otherwise, namely, all that is con­
cerned with human action.37 Here, the high standard of the “necessary”
holds little sway, as claims about human action can aspire only to what
is “probable” (eikos).38 Descartes, however, makes no such distinction.
In the Meditations on First Philosophy he explains that he will put aside
“everything that admits of the least doubt, as if I had discovered it to be
completely false”.39 To his credit, Descartes clearly recognizes the limits of
34  Plato, Complete Works, 91a, 79.
35  In the Introduction, the names of Descartes, Leibniz, Mill, Pascal, Kant, and Bergson are
all mentioned and included among those who, in some cases despite themselves, “carry on
the Cartesian tradition by the limitation they impose on the concept of reason” (Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 3/TA, 4).
36  Aristotle, The Complete Works of Aristotle, 1139a11–14, 1798.
37  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 2nd ed., trans. George A. Kennedy (Oxford: Oxford University
Press, 2007), 1357a25, 42.
38  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1357a35, 42.
39  René Descartes, Philosophical Essays and Correspondence, ed. Roger Ariew (Indianapo­
lis, IN: Hackett, 2000), 108.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  61
this method, pointing out that he is “concentrating only on knowledge”,
admitting that it may even lead to “danger or error” were he to apply this
rule to action.40 Yet, it was precisely this concern with knowledge that
would occupy modern philosophy for several centuries.
This neglect of reasoning about action is one of the major concerns
permeating Perelman’s philosophy and the NRP more generally. By unduly
narrowing the scope of reason, the Cartesian paradigm abandons the
sphere of action to irrationality. Where questions of knowledge are con­
cerned with our reasons for holding certain beliefs, questions of action are
characterized by the need for decision-making and justifying those deci­
sions to others in an acceptable manner. When decisions are made, the
deciding agency assumes responsibility for justifying their decision should
it draw criticism from others. Criticism, for Perelman, arises when an
action upsets the (often implicit) background of norms and customs.41 By
looking to resolve questions of criticism and disagreement in terms of self-
evidence, Cartesian rationality renders the idea of “reasonable disagree­
ment” impossible. As Descartes himself explains in Discourse on Method:
“each time the judgments of two people diverge on a single point, it is
certain that at least one of them is wrong”.42 For Descartes, if either party
were really in possession of knowledge, the clarity and distinctness of their
ideas should enable their interlocutor to see its truth.
The problem with taking self-evidence as the standard of rationality
is visible within Descartes’s own writings. Take for example, Thomas
Hobbes’s objection to Descartes’s apparent demonstration that the mind
has an innate idea of God. Frustrated, Descartes ends his final reply
with the following: “But I have repeated ad nauseam how it is we have
an idea of God. And nothing at all is asserted here that weakens my
demonstrations”.43 Now, if we take Descartes’s point about disagreement
seriously either one of two things is true: either Descartes has failed to live
40  Descartes, Philosophical Essays and Correspondence, 107.
41 “A question of justification ordinarily arises only in a situation that has given rise to
criticism: no one is called upon to justify behavior that is beyond reproach. Such criti­
cism, however, would be meaningless unless some accepted norm, end, or value had been
infringed upon or violated. A decision or an action is criticized on the ground that it is
immoral, illegal, unreasonable, or inefficient—that is, it fails to respect certain accepted
rules or values. It always occurs within a social context; it is always ‘situated.’ Criticism
and justification are two forms of argumentation that call for the giving of reasons for
or against, and it is these reasons that ultimately enable us to call the action or decision
reasonable or unreasonable” (Perelman, “The New Rhetoric: A Theory of Practical Rea­
soning”, The New Rhetoric and the Humanities: Essays on Rhetoric and Its Applications
(Dordrecht: D. Reidel Publishing Company, 1979), 33).
42  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 2/TA, 2; Descartes, Philosophical Essays and Cor­
respondence, 3.
43  Descartes, Philosophical Essays and Correspondence, 176.


62  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
up to his own method or the great Hobbes is somehow unable to see what
is self-evidently true. It is precisely this way of framing disagreements that
Perelman’s entire philosophy rallies against; when self-evidence becomes
the hallmark of reason, the idea of a reasonable disagreement becomes
unthinkable.
While Perelman often uses Descartes as an example, we should not
think that Cartesians are his only target. Indeed, Perelman often seems
to be using Descartes as an object lesson for a more proximate audience,
as a way to draw attention to the shortcomings of a conception of reason
that continues to retain a great deal of currency. Perelman’s criticism is
directed equally towards any philosophy that seeks to resolve disagree­
ment of a theoretical or practical nature by means of “intuition”. In “Une
théorie philosophique de l’argumentation” [A Philosophical Theory of
Argumentation] (1968), Perelman elaborates on his criticism of self-evi­
dence and the Cartesian paradigm of reason more generally:
Descartes’s error was to believe that there are clear and distinct notions
whose relations give rise to self-evident propositions, and that these
notions can be grasped through an infallible intuition, which relates to
a simple nature, about which error is impossible. But this assertion of
the existence of simple natures, which we could know perfectly, inde­
pendently of any context and any relation with anything else, corre­
sponds to an atomized vision of reality, the insufficiency of which we
have come to know. The notions of line or space do not have the same
meaning in Riemann’s geometry and in Euclid’s. A fortiori, the funda­
mental notions of law, morality and philosophy, which can be inserted
in contexts that vary in an unforeseeable way, will have meanings that
correspond neither to simple natures nor to a unique combination of
such natures.44
Whether what is given to intuition is of a rational or a sensible nature,
in rationalist and empiricist philosophies respectively, Perelman argues
that overemphasizing intuitive self-evidence downplays the importance of
argumentation in philosophy.45 If the idea of self-evidence is conceived as
an intuition that is both necessarily true and immediately recognizable as
such, what cognitive advantage could argumentation possibly provide? If
44  Perelman, “Une théorie philosophique de l’argumentation”, RH, 218, our translation.
45  Perelman, RH, 215, our translation. Interestingly, Theodor W. Adorno raises a similar
charge against Edmund Husserl, claiming that the logical outcome of his phenomenology
and its reliance on intuition amounts to the “withering away of argument” (Theodor W.
Adorno, Against Epistemology: A Metacritique: Studies in Husserl and the Phenomeno­
logical Antinomies, trans. Willis Domingo (Cambridge: Polity, [1970] 2013), 209–210).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  63
such an intuition were possible, would argumentation not prove to be a
distraction from seeing things as they really are?46
At its core, then, Perelman’s problem with the Cartesian paradigm of
reason is its inability to deal with problems of human action. By refusing
to look for reason elsewhere, beyond what can be self-evidently intuited,
this way of thinking amounts to “a perfectly unjustified and unwarranted
limitation of the domain in which our faculty of reasoning and proving
intervene”.47 Thus, rather than conclude that reason is incompetent out­
side those areas where demonstrative rationality hold sway, they thematize
argumentation in search of an extended conception of reason, tailored to
those spheres where, owing to their connection to human action, probabil­
ity rather than certainty is the rule.
While Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca do not deny the place of demon­
strative reason—Perelman was after all trained as a logician—the idea of
a new rhetoric is motivated by the simple observation that argumentation
is the far more pervasive practice in human life.48 Although philosophers
spend a great deal of time engaging in argument, it has rarely been thema­
tized as a subject in its own right. Indeed, even when philosophers do speak
of argument, the precise meaning of the term is often taken for granted. In
46  Jean-Paul Sartre defends such a view in Being and Nothingess: “The only kind of knowl­
edge is intuitive. Deduction and discourse, which are incorrectly labelled as ‘knowledge’,
are only instruments leading to intuition” (Jean-Paul Sartre, Being and Nothingness:
An Essay in Phenomenological Ontology, trans. Sarah Richmond (London: Routledge,
[1943] 2018), 246).
47  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 3, translation modified/TA, 3–4. Although their use
of the term “faculty” is a regrettable choice, the authors do clarify here and elsewhere
that they are opposed to differentiating between “human faculties” in a way that is “com­
pletely artificial and contrary to the real processes of our thought” (NR, 3/TA, 4). Perhaps
this is most explicit in The Realm of Rhetoric, where Perelman clarifies that arguers “do
not address what we call ‘faculties,’ such as intellect, emotion, or will; they address the
whole person [l’homme complet]” (Perelman, RR, 13/ER, 31).
48  This is not to suggest, however, that the motivations for the NRP were purely intellec­
tual. Indeed, as David Frank’s work admirably demonstrates, the rhetorical turn of which
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work is an important part should be understood as an
attempt “to work through traumas of the holocaust and the destruction wrought by total­
itarian governments” (Frank, “1958 and the Rhetorical Turn in 20th-Century Thought”,
240). In Perelman’s case, he was forced to resign from his post at the Université libre de
Bruxelles after the Nazi invasion and occupation of Belgium in 1940 (Ibid., 246). Accord­
ing to Gross and Dearin, however, Perelman and three other Jewish faculty members
refused to resign as a matter of conscience, agreeing not to teach but retaining their con­
nection with the university (Gross and Dearin, Chaim Perelman, 3; Jules Gérard-Libois
and José Gotovitch, L’An 40: la Belgique occupée (Bruxelles: CRISP, 1971), 462). Perel­
man was thus able to use his position to facilitate underground resistance activities (Gross
and Dearin, Chaim Perelman, 4).


64  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
Perelman’s view, however, argumentation must be given a systematic place
within philosophy itself.
The Audience
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s “break” with Cartesian rationality was
a fairly radical gesture in the 1950s, but it is one that many other philoso­
phers would make throughout the 20th century. In broad strokes, 20th-
century philosophy largely abandoned Cartesian rationality in favour of a
more relational or dialogical conception. Yet, what makes Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work stand out is their discovery and deployment of a
rhetorical conception of “audience” in their methodology. In this section
we explore this aspect of their work in the Treatise’s Introduction.
No discussion of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s methodology can
neglect the importance of their choice to call their project a “new rheto­
ric”. In the Introduction to the Treatise Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
first explain that they chose “rhetoric” over “dialectic” because they
wanted to avoid terminological confusion with the “dialectical” philoso­
phies associated with Hegel and Marx. “Rhetoric”, they continue, had
fallen into such disrepute that it was less likely to invite misunderstanding.
The second and more important reason is that these ancient arts were more
concerned with “probable” opinions than necessary truths. While many
philosophers in the 20th century also draw attention to the probable, fal­
lible, and defeasible nature of human reasoning, what is distinctive about
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s approach is the further recognition that
the very distinction between opinion and truth is “relative to the minds
which adhere to them”.49
As Perelman would recount later, recognizing that this distinction is
always relative to an audience allowed him to escape the false dichotomy
imposed by the “fact-value problem”.50 Indeed, rather than view state­
ments of fact and statements of value as involving different kinds of judg­
ments, Perelman suggests that the distinction itself rests upon the beliefs
and attitudes of a particular audience. Note that Perelman is not saying
here that values, unlike facts, require additional ornamentation for others
to find them persuasive; he is saying that the distinction between facts and
values is a function of how one views the world. What is accepted as fac­
tual by one audience may be considered a value judgment or an expression
of preference by another.
49  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 5/TA, 6.
50  Perelman recounts his encounter with this problem in the Foreword to L’empire rhéto­
rique (1977). This short text was regrettably not included in William Kluback’s English
translation, The Realm of Rhetoric (1982).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  65
In our view, this recognition marks the key insight of the Treatise. As
they themselves put it: “Our rapprochement with [rhetoric] seeks to empha­
size the fact that all argumentation develops with a view to an audience”.51
What makes their rhetoric “new”, then, is that they extend the rhetorical
audience to all forms of discourse. While rhetoric is often understood nar­
rowly as the art of public speaking (i.e. speaking to an audience), Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca discovered that the aim of oratory—“the adherence
of the minds addressed”52—is in fact the aim of all argumentation, how­
ever dissimulated the audience might be through various technologies and
media. As they explain:
What we preserve of traditional rhetoric is the idea of the audience,
an idea immediately evoked by the mere thought of discourse. All dis­
course is addressed to an audience and it is frequently forgotten that
this applies to everything written as well. Whereas a speech is conceived
in terms of the audience, the physical absence of his readers can lead a
writer to believe that he is alone in the world, though his text is always
conditioned, whether consciously or unconsciously, by those persons he
wishes to address.53
Although the Treatise is primarily concerned with written texts,54 their
fundamental insight is that audiences and their conditioning effects are not
a unique feature of public speaking but an essential structure of discourse
as such and, therefore, of all argumentation.
The discovery that argumentation is an essentially audience-oriented
practice holds important consequences for the way that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca theorize. Chief among these is the recognition that even
philosophers and social scientists like themselves address their arguments
to an audience. Most radical about this simple fact is the relativizing effect
it has on the idea that there is any kind of philosophically neutral way of
approaching argumentation as an object of study. While this problem can,
to a certain extent, be put to one side within particular subfields where
there is sufficient methodological agreement, it will again rear its head
when that agreement is inevitably contested.
This seems to rule out the possibility of according any privileged sta­
tus to philosophy. Indeed, why should the way that philosophers argue
51  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 5, translation modified/TA, 6.
52  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 6/TA, 7.
53  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 5/TA, 6–7, translation modified.
54  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 6/TA, 8.


66  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
serve as the ideal for all argumentation? As Perelman points out, philoso­
phers themselves strongly disagree about the value of different kinds of
arguments:
Each philosopher is situated in a certain line of thought, which has the
effect of giving preference to a certain type of argument. For think­
ers who follow Aristotle, the argument that takes essences and essen­
tial characteristics into account will be considered strong, whereas for
Bentham and the utilitarians, the only argument in favour of a course of
action will be the good or bad consequences that result from it … Each
philosopher could thus characterize themselves by the kind of argu­
ments to which they deny any value. This is a new way of looking at
the history of philosophy, which would correlate a thinker’s ontology
with their methodological precepts, and shed new light on the history
of thought.55
There is thus no single argumentative framework that all philosophers use
to advance their positions. Rather, at any given time there are always mul­
tiple philosophical positions that each conceive argumentation in a specific
way. As Perelman puts it in Logique juridique [Juridical Logic] (1976),
“each type of philosophy, each methodology, valorizes this or that kind
of argument, while challenging the value of others”.56 In part, it is this
pluralism of incommensurable conceptions of argumentation that makes
the history of philosophy what Kant called that “battlefield” of endless
controversy.57
When asked by Jean Wahl about the possible dangers that this argumen­
tative pluralism might invite, Perelman responds sarcastically by saying
that “people understand one another much better than philosophers”.58
Yet, beneath this remark there is a serious point. Here, Perelman turns the
problem around, noting that it is precisely the narrow, monolithic way of
thinking about philosophical debate that has prevented philosophers from
seeing what their own argumentation has in common with argumentation
55  Perelman, “L’idéal de rationalité et la règle de justice” (1961), Éthique et droit, 2e éd.
(Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de Bruxelles, 2012), 145–146, our translation. For
similar remarks, see “L’argument pragmatique”, (1958), RH, 24/“The Pragmatic Argu­
ment”, The Idea of Justice and the Problem of Argument, trans. John Petrie (London:
Routledge & Kegan Paul, 1963), 201.
56  Perelman, Logique juridique, 126, our translation.
57  Immanuel Kant, Critique of Pure Reason, trans. Paul Guyer and Allen W. Wood (Cam­
bridge: Cambridge University Press, [1781] 1998), Aviii, 99.
58  Perelman, “L’idéal de rationalité et la règle de justice”, ED, 146, our translation.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  67
that occurs in everyday life.59 In the Treatise, Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca want to correct this narrowness by showing that philosophers are
not in any privileged position, as they rely upon the same range of argu­
mentative techniques as everyone else:
We shall show the same techniques of argumentation can be found at all
levels, at that of a discussion around the family table as well as that of
a debate in a highly specialized environment. If the quality of the minds
which adhere to certain arguments, in highly speculative domains, is a
guarantee of their value, the commonality of structure between these
arguments and those used in everyday discussions explains why and
how we understand them.60
Once we recognize the de facto pluralism of argumentative techniques,
philosophy can no longer serve as a privileged model of argumentation but
only one instance of it, an instance that itself needs to be theorized.
This recognition brings us to the heart of the philosophical problem
raised by Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work: If every philosophical
position prescribes in advance a hierarchy among different kinds of argu­
ments, how is it possible to study argumentation without presupposing
a particular philosophical position from the outset? Or, put differently,
how is it possible to study argumentation philosophically without making
prior ontological and epistemological commitments that would prescribe
in advance what should count as (good) argumentation?
Philosophical Epoché and Rhetorical Reduction
To explain how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca address this problem, we
propose to borrow from the phenomenological tradition in calling the way
that they attempt to abstain from such judgments a kind of “philosophi­
cal epoché”. Here, this gesture refers to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
(temporary) methodological bracketing or suspension of ontological and
epistemological presuppositions for the sake of identifying and describing
the essential features of argumentation.61
59 “[W]e should note that people recognize all types of arguments and accord them value
depending on the context and the field of application. What characterizes philosophers
is that they neglect certain types of arguments and disregard them in their philosophical
vision” (Perelman, “L’idéal de rationalité et la règle de justice”, ED, 146).
60  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 7–8, translation modified, our emphasis/TA, 10.
61  In general, the epoché is for Husserl a certain withholding of judgment that suspends or
puts out of play the presuppositions of the “natural attitude”. More specifically, our use
of Husserl here draws from the “philosophical ἐποχή” first mentioned in §18 of Ideas I
which, he explains, “consists in the fact that we completely withhold judgment regard­


68  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
While neither Perelman nor Olbrechts-Tyteca draws attention to this
aspect of their method, the attempt to abstain from making ontological
and epistemological commitments when theorizing argumentation can
be seen at various points throughout the Treatise. In the second chap­
ter of Part Three, for example, when discussing “arguments based on the
structure of reality” [les arguments basés sur la structure du réel], they are
careful to remind the reader that they “are not assuming any ontologi­
cal position”. This is an important qualification, as what interests them
there “is not an objective description of reality, but the manner in which
opinions concerning [reality] are presented”.62 In The Realm of Rhetoric
Perelman is even more direct, stating that the “theory of argumentation
need not take a position in an ontological debate”.63 On our reading, this
is not because Perelman thinks that it is possible in a sustained way to
avoid such commitments when one is actually arguing. Nor is it that he,
as a philosopher, is trying to have it both ways on the question of realism.
Rather, this preliminary methodological neutrality, or “simple exterior­
ity” as we called it earlier, is but a necessary first step in the development
of a general theory of argumentation not predetermined by any particular
philosophical position.64
The textual evidence for interpreting the Treatise as operating under
some kind of philosophical epoché is most striking in a 1958 corre­
spondence between Perelman and Raymond Ruyer. After congratulating
Perelman on the publication of the Treatise, Ruyer questions him on his
seemingly “anti-realist” conception of values. Perelman’s response is most
instructive:
I assume that you only wrote your letter after reading the first volume
of the Treatise. In the second volume, and especially in the study of
dissociations, you will see how close the link between value and reality
is. When you speak of my anti-realism of values, for me it is simply a
ing the doctrinal content of all previous philosophy” (Edmund Husserl, Ideas for a Pure
Phenomenology and Phenomenological Philosophy: First Book: General Introduction
to Pure Phenomenology, trans. Daniel O. Dahlstrom (Indianapolis, IN: Hackett, [1913]
2014), §18, 33).
62  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §60, 262, our emphasis/TA, 352–3.
63  Perelman, RR, 90/ER, 119.
64  In correspondence with French philosopher Léon Husson, Perelman clarifies his personal
position on the question of realism: “I believe in the existence of a mind-independent real­
ity. I am therefore not an idealist. But when it comes to describing that reality, one must
use a language that is inseparable from an explicit or forgotten argument. It is in this
sense that, in [the Treatise], I define facts and truths insofar as they are accepted without
any dispute” (Perelman, Letter to Léon Husson, 13 June 1975, Fonds Chaïm Perelman,
Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 23.2, our translation).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  69
question, not of denying the reality of this or that value, but of insisting
on the triple status of our judgements in argumentation; sometimes they
are presented as an objective statement, the expression of a fact or a
truth, sometimes as a pure subjective attitude, and sometimes, finally, as
the basis of an agreement which, without being universal, goes beyond
the individual ... As to what should be included in each of these three
categories depends on a determinate philosophical point of view.
My aim was therefore less to develop an axiology than a phenome-
nology of our judgements, essentially taking into account their dynamic
aspect linked to action.65
While we do not take this as evidence that Perelman identifies with the
phenomenological movement or would even accept our application of the
term epoché—indeed, all available evidence points to the contrary66—it
does help to orient our interpretation of the Treatise.67 Just as the phenom-
enologist is concerned not with the objective existence of this or that object
but with the specific way it is given to consciousness (e.g. qua existing,
imagined, remembered), the argumentation theorist is concerned not with
evaluating arguments but the with way argumentative techniques present
their subject matter to an audience—whether as an “objective statement”,
a “pure subjective attitude”, or as the “basis of an agreement” as he puts
it in his letter to Ruyer. The Treatise is thus primarily concerned not with
the relative merits of this or that argument, but with a systematic descrip-
tion of various argumentative techniques irrespective of their “objective”
validity or “factual” correspondence with the world.
65  Perelman, Letter to Raymond Ruyer, 12 July 1958, Fonds Chaïm Perelman, Archives de
l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 25.10, our translation and emphasis.
The division of the two volumes of the first edition of the Treatise occurs between §59
and §60 of Part Three.
66  Perelman only ever mentions the epoché in Husserl to criticize it: “The idea of an ‘epoché’
would have been a mere naivety, if it were not to play an essential role in phenomenologi-
cal systems, that of separating the subjective from the objective, and of validating what is
posited as being free from the influence of any contestable opinion” (Perelman, “Le réel
commun et le réel philosophique” (1964), RH, 294, our translation). Suffice it to say that
we do not share Perelman’s interpretation of Husserlian phenomenology.
67  Others have noted the phenomenological resonances of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
work. Todd Oakley, for example, remarks in passing that “the theory of argumentation
outlined by Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca is a phenomenological theory in that it is a
sorting out of the different manifestations of intentionality and the means of making them
public” (Todd Oakley, From Attention to Meaning: Explorations in Semiotics, Linguistics,
and Rhetoric (Bern: Peter Lang, 2009), 198). For his part, Robert E. Tucker attempts to
“breathe new life” into Perelman’s notion of “presence”, which we will discuss below, by
approaching it from a phenomenological perspective (Robert E. Tucker, “Figure, Ground
and Presence: A Phenomenology of Meaning in Rhetoric”, Quarterly Journal of Speech,
Vol. 87, No.4 (2001), 397).


70  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
Having methodologically suspended all philosophical commitments,
how then do Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca proceed to study actual argu­
ments? Without an undisputed reality as a guide, what possible anchoring
point could they rely upon as theorists? For this it is best to return to our
original point of departure: Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s definition of
the goal of their study. We now quote the passage in full, this time drawing
attention to the notion of “adherence” [adhésion]:
the object of the theory of argumentation is the study of the discursive
techniques allowing us to induce or to increase the mind’s adherence to
the theses presented for its assent. What is characteristic of the adher­
ence of minds is its variable intensity: nothing constrains us to limit our
study to a particular degree of adherence characterized by self-evidence,
and nothing permits us to consider a priori the degrees of adherence to
a thesis as proportional to its probability, and to identify self-evidence
with truth. The right method is to not confuse from the start those
aspects of reasoning relative to truth and those relative to adherence,
but to study them separately, even though we might have to examine
later their possible interference or correspondence. Only on this condi­
tion is it possible to develop a theory of argumentation that has any
philosophical significance.68
Let us unpack this important passage in three points:
	(1)	 Unlike the binary character of self-evidence, “the mind’s adherence”
is characterized by its variable intensity.69 Once freed from the arti­
ficial limitations of Cartesian rationality, there is no need to restrict
the study of argumentation to those patterns of reasoning that pre­
serve the truth of self-evident propositions. Indeed most (if not all) of
our personal commitments fail to meet this standard of certainty. And
yet these commitments remain essential to our practical engagements.
“Adherence” is thus a much more accommodating notion, refusing the
artificial separation of human faculties.70
	(2)	 Rather than view adherence as deficient with respect to truth, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca turn things around: they view self-evidence as
but one very particular degree of adherence. From this perspective,
there is no need to limit their study to the small subset of adherences
that meet the high standard of self-evidence. Similarly, they see no
68  Perelman, NR, 4, translation modified/TA, 5.
69  Although one might quarrel with the choice of “mind” here, Perelman insists that argu­
mentation is always directed toward “the whole person” (Perelman, RR, 13/ER, 31).
70  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 3/TA, 4.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  71
reason (under their epoché) to correlate the degree of one’s adherence
to its “objective” probability, or even to identify self-evidence with
truth.
	(3)	 Instead of approaching the study of argumentation with a notion of
truth already in hand, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca will proceed
by studying the distinctive rationality of argumentation through the
prism of adherence. While they leave open the question of how their
work might converge with modern developments in logic and epis­
temology, it is only by first refusing this methodological conflation,
they argue, that a theory of argumentation can have any philosophical
import down the road.
Focusing on the notion of adherence thus allows us to provide a more com­
plete picture of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s methodology and, fur­
ther, what makes it distinctively rhetorical. Without a pre-argumentative
“reality” to serve as a point of reference (bracketed by the philosophical
epoché), their study of argumentative techniques will instead anchor itself
to the audience—an arguer’s construction (whether conscious or uncon­
scious) of their intended addressee(s). As a construction, externalized in
speech or writing, the audience for Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca is an
argumentation theorist’s only reliable touchstone for determining how
an arguer depicts their addressees and what they take them to adhere to.
Unlike “objective reality” or “other minds”, the audience is purely imma­
nent to argumentation. In this regard, it is a more stable criterion for iden­
tifying and classifying the wide anthropological breadth of argumentative
techniques that interest Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca than an “objec­
tive” criterion that has already settled on a “determinate philosophical
point of view”, as Perelman put it in his letter to Ruyer.
Borrowing again from phenomenology, we propose to call the way
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca thematize the correlation between arguer
and audience their “rhetorical reduction”.71 Not to be confused with
reductionism or simplification, “reduction” in the phenomenological sense
(drawing on the Latin sense of reducere) refers to the act of “question­
ing back”, that is, guiding or tracing one’s attention back to a particular
71  As Dan Zahavi explains, in Husserl the epoché is “the term for our abrupt suspension of a
naïve metaphysical attitude and can be likened to a philosophical gate of entry”, whereas
“the reduction is the term for our thematization of the correlation between subjectivity
and world” (Dan Zahavi, Husserl’s Phenomenology (Stanford, CA: Stanford University
Press, 2003), 46). Instead of thematizing the subject-world correlation, our repurposing
of the reduction thematizes the arguer-audience correlation.


72  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
phenomenon.72 To avoid presupposing from the start the very thing they
are trying to investigate, it is in this sense that Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca “reduce” or “trace back” the various aspects of argumentation to
this essential feature, namely, the addressive structure of argumentation.73
By adopting this methodological shift in perspective, the question is no
longer whether an argument is formally valid or corresponds to reality,
but how an argument seeks to act on the audience’s adherence. By neutral­
izing one’s spontaneous evaluative attitude toward arguments and instead
focusing on an argument’s internal reference to an audience, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca are able to examine argumentation from a new perspec­
tive. What this change in perspective affords them is a point of relative
stability from which the argument can be described rather than evalu­
ated. Instead of asking whether the premises of the argument are true, or
whether the conclusion really follows from its premises, they now ask how
the argument attempts to induce or increase the audience’s adherence. In
this way, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca require no reference to any “first
principles” or Archimedean point; their rhetorical reduction provides their
study with a point of relative fixity—an argument’s internal reference to
an audience.
It is thus by staying within these methodological parameters—what
we have characterized as their (1) philosophical epoché and (2) rhetorical
reduction—that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca avoid succumbing to the
fundamental philosophical problem facing any theory of argumentation:
namely, how to theorize argumentation without presupposing a determi­
nate philosophical position from the outset.
Describing Arguments
Thus far we have focused on reconstructing the rhetorical methodology
that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca use to develop their theory of argu­
mentation. We now look beyond the Introduction to the remainder of the
Treatise to flesh out and substantiate our interpretation. We begin with a
schematic overview of the work as a whole and then zoom in on several
72  Should one still find this language unhelpful or unpalatable for any reason, “reduction”
can be substituted with “regression”, in Perelman’s own sense of a “regressive” philoso­
phy. Although this term has its own undesirable baggage.
73  There are of course other essential features to argumentation. For example, arguments
are also always about something. However, it seems that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
focus on argumentation’s addressive structure because they believe that all argumentative
choices are made on the basis of an arguer’s construction of their audience. Focusing on
the audience thus allows their theory to detect and capture (at least in principle) the full
range of argumentative techniques deployed by social actors.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  73
examples that show Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical method­
ology at work.
Aside from the Introduction and the Conclusion the Treatise is com­
prised of three main parts. Part One, entitled “The Framework of
Argumentation” [Les cadres de l’argumentation], is undoubtedly the
source of many interpretative disagreements over the Treatise, as it is here
that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca establish the bounds, range, or scope
of the object of their theory of argumentation.74 In Part One the authors
introduce the fundamental distinction between demonstration and argu­
mentation (§1), the minimal pre-conditions of argumentation (§2), the
definition of audience as a construction of the arguer (§3–5), the distinc­
tion between persuading and convincing (§6), definitions of three kinds of
audiences (§7–9), the effects of argumentation within the argumentative
situation (§10), the underappreciated significance of epideictic discourse
for argumentation (§11,12), the relation between argumentation and vio­
lence, and (§13) the role of personal engagement in argumentation (§14).
Although each of the ideas introduced in these sections merits its own
discussion (and we shall examine several of them in Chapters 3 and 4),
many of them have been discussed at length by various commentators.
We shall thus not wade into these debates but push forward while simply
noting the role of Part One within the regressive structure of the work
as a whole. As the authors explain, the role of Part One is to introduce
the “special characteristics of argumentation and the problems inherent
to its study” that follow from its dissociation with the classical concept of
demonstration.75 In short, Part One presents a series of interrelated prob­
lems that explore the bounds or scope of a theory of argumentation. It is
between Part One and Part Two that we would locate the break between
the more philosophical aspects of the Treatise and the theory of argumen­
tation proper, i.e. the “technical analysis of arguments as they actually
occur [telles qu’elles se présentent]”.76
If Part One establishes the bounds of the study, the three chapters that
comprise Part Two provide a technical vocabulary for describing “The
Starting Point of Argumentation” [Le point de depart de l’argumentation].
This technical vocabulary aims to describe “the types of objects of agree­
ment [objets d’accord] that play different roles in the arguing process”.77
In more conventional terms, these features are the “premises” held in
74  James Crosswhite suggests “bounds” as an alternative to “framework” in translating
“cadres”, see James Crosswhite, Deep Rhetoric: Philosophy, Reason, Violence, Justice,
Wisdom (Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 2013), 60.
75  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 13/TA, 17.
76  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §15, 66, translation modified/TA, 88.
77  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §15, 66/TA, 88.


74  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
common or available between arguers and audiences—although the mean­
ing of this term is much broader than its formal counterpart. While the
starting points of argumentation are presented here as static and synchro­
nous, in practice they are always temporal and dynamic. These various
points of contact, or “objects of agreement”, may pre-exist argumentation
or be established by it. Among these objects discussed in Chapter One are
the “facts”, “truths”, and “presumptions” that both the speaker and audi­
ence take to be real (§16, 17), as well as the “values”, “value hierarchies”,
and the various “loci” (or topics) they take to be preferable (§18–25). The
first chapter concludes with some more general reflections on agreement as
it pertains to specialized contexts (§26–28). Chapter Two details the many
choices arguers face in adapting to their audience, whether they are made
consciously or not. In terms of the content of argumentation this includes
everything from the selection of “data” [les données]78 and “information”
to the very terms and concepts that arguers choose to make “present” for
their audience (§29–35). Chapter Three discusses the choices that confront
arguers in the presentation of those materials. In terms of the form of argu­
mentation, this includes everything from temporal considerations owing to
particular institutional contexts, to minute linguistic decisions, and choices
of argumentative figures (§36–41).
It is worth repeating that although some of the elements discussed in
Part Two are presented as a set of static considerations, the processes to
which they refer are essentially temporal or dynamic, forming a synthetic
whole when observed in their full, living diachrony.79 It is for this rea­
son that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca will later say that their rhetori­
cal approach to argumentation makes no absolute distinction between the
form and content of discourse.80 In their view, this very separation is “at
the source of the degeneration, sterility, and verbalism of rhetoric, as well
as the contempt one generally feels for it”.81
Furthermore, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are careful to note that
the way they have divided up the materials in Part Two was chosen for
expository purposes and should thus not be misunderstood. On our
78  As Jeanne Fahnestock suggests, “les données” would probably be better translated as the
“givens” in an argument, rather than “data”, which suggests that the term applies only to
facts. See Jeanne Fahnestock, “‘No Neutral Choices’: The Art of Style in The New Rheto­
ric”, The Promise of Reason: Studies in The New Rhetoric, ed. John T. Gage (Carbondale,
IL: Southern Illinois University Press, 2011), 32.
79  For a more extensive discussion of temporality in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work,
see Blake D. Scott, “Argumentation and the Challenge of Time: Perelman, Temporality,
and the Future of Argument”, Argumentation, Vol. 43, No. 1 (2020): 25–37.
80 “We refuse to separate the form of a discourse from its content” (Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca, NR, §36, 142, translation modified/TA, 192).
81  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §36, 142/TA, 191–192.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  75
reading, what guides their exposition is what we have called their “rhe­
torical reduction”, which in this case means that “from start to finish,
the analysis of argumentation is concerned with what is supposed to be
accepted by the audience”.82 Understood in this way, the rather minimalis­
tic technical definitions they provide of what would otherwise appear to be
classical philosophical terms begins to make more sense. In our terms, the
definitions presented in Part Two are introduced within (1) the “brackets”
of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s philosophical epoché and (2) guided
by the thematic orientation of their rhetorical reduction to be squarely
focused on the arguer-audience correlation.
To substantiate this reading further let us now turn to several examples.
First, here is how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca introduce their defini­
tion of “fact” insofar as their theory of argumentation is concerned:
It is not possible—nor would it be consistent with our purpose—to
define “fact” in a way that would enable us, in all times and in all
places, to classify this or that concrete datum as a fact. On the contrary,
we must stress that, in argumentation, the notion of “fact” is uniquely
characterized by the idea that is held of agreement of a certain type
relating to certain data, those which refer to an objective reality.83
There is thus no attempt here to take an “objective” stance towards real­
ity; they do not, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca continue, “hope to find
a criterion that enables us, under all circumstances and independently of
the audience’s attitude, to affirm that something is a fact”.84 Instead, facts
are considered only as objects of “precise, limited agreement” about what
arguers and audience both take as real or objective.
It is in this same way that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca define the
notion of “value”. Unlike facts, values are defined as “objects of agreement
which do not claim the adherence of the universal audience”.85 Without
wading into the meaning of the “universal audience” (which we shall dis­
cuss in detail in Chapter 4), in argumentation “values” are understood
simply as adherences which are not taken to be shared by everyone. Again,
for the purposes of their theory of argumentation, values (and the various
hierarchies in which they are always arranged) are not defined “objec­
tively” but are always conceived as relative to the adherence of the audi­
ence in an argumentative situation.
82  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §15, 65, translation modified/TA, 87.
83  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §16, 67/TA, 89.
84  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §16, 68/TA, 90.
85  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §18, 75/TA, 89.


76  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
This same methodological approach is also visible in the discussion of
the important notion of rhetorical “presence” introduced in Part Two,
Chapter Two. This notion captures the argumentative choice of having to
render certain things salient to the audience, a choice that sets in motion
an inescapable play of presence and absence. As Perelman will later sum­
marize the idea, “Every argument implies a preliminary selection of facts
and values, their specific description in a given language, and an emphasis
which varies with the importance given them”.86
As “presence” has been the subject of considerable debate among com­
mentators, it is a useful place to highlight some advantages of our inter­
pretation. The source of the controversy lies in the way that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca attempt to limit the scope of the concept:
This notion of presence … is not a philosophically elaborated notion.
A philosophy that considers presence as a cornerstone of its structure—
that of Buber or Sartre, for example—would connect it with an ontol­
ogy or an anthropology. That is not our purpose. We are interested only
in the technical aspect of this notion.87
From this passage, we can see that the notion of presence is meant to be
considered only in its “technical” aspect, that is, not derived from nor
dependent upon any particular philosophical position. Yet, as commenta­
tors such as Louise Karon argue, this limitation seems to contradict the
rest of their theory. The problem according to Karon is that this limitation
of presence to its technical aspect prevents it from playing any role in the
theory of knowledge she takes Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca to be devel­
oping.88 Where Karon attempts to resolve the contradiction, our interpre­
tation avoids the problem altogether: we reject the view that the Treatise
is concerned with defending any “theory of knowledge” whatsoever.89 On
86  Perelman, RR, 34/ER, 56.
87  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §29, 119, translation modified, our emphasis/TA,
159–160.
88 “This emphasis on ‘the technical aspect of the notion’ leaves us with a fragmental account
of presence in relation to the rest of their theory. On one hand, we should limit our under­
standing of presence to its place in the general conduct of argument, while, on the other
hand, we should apprehend the authors’ theory of rhetoric or argumentation as genera­
tive of a theory of knowledge. Herein lies the key to assessing the notion: if presence is an
inevitable element of any argumentation, then presence will indeed be an equally impor­
tant and inevitable component of the authors’ theory of knowledge” (Louise A. Karon,
“Presence in The New Rhetoric”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 9, No. 2 (1976), 97–98).
89  This is not to say that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are not interested in epistemology.
To the contrary, Perelman insists that epistemological concerns can be clarified by plac­
ing them within “the more general framework of a theory of argumentation” (Perelman,
“Value Judgements, Justifications and Argumentation”. Philosophy Today, Vol. 6, No. 1


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  77
our reading, it is not only the notion of “presence” that is limited to its
technical aspect, but all of the notions introduced in Part Two and Part
Three of the Treatise, as we have already seen with “facts” and “values”.
For their part Gross and Dearin also take issue with the above passage.
While noting that they are sympathetic to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
“desire to shun philosophical speculation”, they insist that “presence”
retains certain “existential implications” despite the attempt to limit the
notion to a “matter of technique”.90 Gross and Dearin thus imply that one
must choose: one or the other, technical or existential. Our reading how­
ever allows us to have it both ways, provided that we keep things clear: we
argue that the Treatise proceeds under precise methodological constraints
for the purpose of developing a general theory of argumentation. While
such a theory undoubtedly has implications for epistemology, as Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca take every opportunity to emphasize, these implica­
tions are not the primary concern in the Treatise. Thus understood, there
is no contradiction in their attempt to develop a technical vocabulary for
theorizing argumentation and saying that argumentation—the social prac­
tical their theory aims to describe—has “existential” implications.
To summarize, what we have called Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
“philosophical epoché” and “rhetorical reduction” intend to capture the
operative methodological constraints of their theory of argumentation
when describing the way arguments work. In other words, these terms
explain why they so frequently remind the reader that they are emphasiz­
ing the neglected technical aspect of argumentation. As we shall continue
to see, the rhetorical methodology Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca pro­
pose is what enables the argumentation theorist to adopt the theoretical
attitude required for their descriptive work; it does not yet say anything
about the way that arguments should be evaluated from within the practi­
cal attitude of those engaged in argumentation—including the philosopher
arguing about existential or ontological questions.
Association and Dissociation
Although Part Two is nearly twice the length of Part One, the unequal
distribution of the text in favour of Part Three should serve as some
indication of its importance. With its own introduction (§44) and five
(1962), 50). Within the NRP, then, a “general theory of argumentation” and a “theory of
knowledge” are conceived as separate tasks, with the former being a “precondition” [pré­
alable] to the latter (Ibid., translation modified). In a philosophical dispute, for example,
a first-order disagreement might be the result of an underlying epistemological disagree­
ment.
90  Gross and Dearin, Chaim Perelman, 150.


78  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
chapters, Part Three—entitled “Techniques of Argumentation” [Les tech­
niques argumentative]—is a rich catalogue of the discursive techniques by
which arguers seek to modify the adherence of audiences. Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca call these various techniques “argumentative schemes”
[hereafter “argument schemes”].91 Faithful to their regressive rhetorical
method, Part Three first identifies several argument structures on their
own before examining their synthetic interaction. Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca are clearly conscious of the difficulties of this approach, noting that:
the analysis of one link of argumentation out of context and indepen­
dently of the situation to which it belongs involves undeniable dangers.
These are due not only to the equivocal character of language, but also
to the fact that the mainspring [les ressorts] of an argument is almost
never entirely explicit.92
Outside of their context, without the meaningful ties that bind arguments
to their situation, the argument schemes they discuss are intended only as
approximations and could, in principle, be classified differently. As the
classification they present does not intend to be definitive but only their
“first results”, the details of the argument schemes are less relevant for
our purposes. What matters for us here is that Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s aim in introducing these argument schemes is to capture the full
range of discursive techniques that speakers may deploy, in a variety of
contexts, “to induce or increase the mind’s adherence to theses presented
for its assent”.
As it would overburden our discussion to list every technique they
identify, we restrict ourselves to the way they are systematically arranged.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca organize their argument schemes by dis­
tinguishing between what they call processes of “association” [liaison] and
“dissociation”.93 As they explain:
By processes of association we understand schemes which bring sepa­
rate elements together and allow us to establish a unity among them,
which aims either at organizing them or at evaluating them, positively
or negatively, by means of one another. By processes of dissociation, we
mean techniques of separation which have the purpose of dissociating,
91  The most extensive study of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s argument schemes is that of
Warnick and Kline. See Barbara Warnick and Susan L. Kline, “The New Rhetoric’s Argu­
ment Schemes: A Rhetorical View of Practical Reasoning”, Argumentation and Advocacy,
Vol. 29 (1992), 1–15.
92  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §44, 187, translation modified/TA, 251.
93  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §44, 190/TA, 255.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  79
separating, disuniting elements which are regarded as forming a whole
or at least a unified group within some system of thought.94
Associative schemes are those which aim to synthesize, bring together,
or—in the culinary sense of liaison—“thicken” the bonds between ele­
ments that had previously been unrelated in the mind of the audience.
Conversely, those based on dissociation aim to separate elements from an
already constituted whole. To avoid any reductive interpretation of this
distinction, they are quick to point out the complementarity of these two
processes:
all association implies dissociation, and, conversely: the same form
which unites various elements into a well-structured whole dissociates
them from the neutral background which it separates them from. The
two techniques are complementary and are always at work at the same
time; but the argumentation through which a datum is modified can
stress the association or the dissociation which it is promoting without
making explicit the complementary aspect which will result from the
desired transformation.95
Like many of their distinctions in the Treatise, the difference between
these two “processes” should be understood phenomenologically—recall­
ing Perelman’s own use of that term in his letter to Ruyer.96 Argument
schemes are thus neither to be taken as psychological processes in the
mind nor transcendent logical forms. Rather, argument schemes are better
understood as Weberian “ideal types” or phenomenological “essences”—
provided that we “remain constantly on guard against any Platonizing
interpretation of essences and consider them simply as meanings or prin­
ciples of intelligibility”, to borrow from Paul Ricoeur in a somewhat
different context.97 As we shall see in more detail below, the attempt to
ontologize Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s vocabulary has been a con­
sistent source of misunderstanding.
Among the techniques of association, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
introduce three sub-classes, which each receive a dedicated chapter. In
Chapter One we are introduced to “quasi-logical” arguments, which most
94  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §44, 190, translation modified/TA, 255–256.
95  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §44, 190, translation modified/TA, 256.
96  Perelman, Letter to Raymond Ruyer, 12 July 1958, Fonds Chaïm Perelman, Archives de
l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 25.10.
97  Paul Ricoeur, Freedom and Nature: The Voluntary and the Involuntary, trans. Ezrahim V.
Kohák (Evanston, IL: Northwestern University Press, [1950] 1966), 4/Philosophie de la
volonté. 1. Le volontaire et l’involontaire (Paris: Éditions Points, [1950] 2009), 20.


80  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
closely resemble the patterns used in formal reasoning (§45–59). After
some general considerations (§60) Chapter Two then discusses “arguments
based upon the structure of reality”. These techniques are characterized by
the fact that they aim to connect things from an already established concep­
tion of what is real through associations based upon temporal succession
(§61–67) or coexistence (§68–77). Conversely, Chapter Three introduces
“arguments establishing the structure of reality”. These techniques seek
to establish what is real either through a connection to particular cases
(§78–81) or by analogy (§82–88).
Chapter Four turns to those techniques that rely upon processes of disso­
ciation. Here Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca discuss the ways that existing
systems of thought, or already associated notions can be separated using
argumentative techniques (§89–96). Distinguishing between the breaking of
an association and dissociation proper, this chapter forefronts the phenom­
enological subtlety of Perelman’s and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s analysis. Where
breaking an association consists in “affirming that elements which should
remain separate and independent have been improperly associated”, dis­
sociation proper “assumes the original unity of elements comprised within
a single conception and designated by a single notion”.98 Consequently,
dissociation proper “brings about a more or less profound restructuring
of the conceptual data that are used as the basis of argument”.99 While
Chapter Four is abound with such distinctions, Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca consistently warn against hypostasizing them:
In the final analysis, the factors that will show that we are in the presence
of a dissociation of notions and not of a mere rejection of an association
are the argumentative situation in its totality and, more particularly, the
notions on which the argumentation rests, the changes to which it leads,
and the techniques that make the changes possible.100
The importance of this constant reference to the “argumentative situation
in its totality” is significant. It is the need for such a reference that allows
their theory to be applied and adapted to different cultural and institu­
tional contexts.101
98  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §89, 411-412/TA, 550–551.
99  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §89, 412/TA, 551.
100  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §89, 412, emphasis added/TA, 552.
101  In 1979 Olbrechts-Tyteca suggests that the theory of philosophical pairs (discussed
below), the basis of the distinction between associative and dissociative techniques,
should be tested outside of the Western tradition—specifically beyond the “Indo-
European tradition” (Olbrechts-Tyteca, “Les couples philosophiques. Une nouvelle
approche”, Revue Internationale de Philosophie, Vol. 33, No. 127/8 (1979), 98, our
translation).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  81
Owing to their significance in philosophical discourse Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca place a particular emphasis on processes of dissocia­
tion. Since dissociation seeks to interrupt an existing system of thought by
“modifying certain concepts which make up its essential parts”, they claim
that these argumentative techniques “are characteristic of all original phil­
osophical thought”.102 The most interesting use of dissociation is found
in their theory of “philosophical pairs” [les couples philosophiques]. In
essence, the theory of philosophical pairs aims to capture argumentation’s
innovative function at the level of concepts or meaning.103 Conceptual
innovation is required when arguers are confronted with the inevitable
incompatibility that arises between existing notions.104
To exemplify this, they introduce the dissociation that they call the “appear­
ance-reality” pair, which they consider to be “the prototype of all concep­
tual dissociation because of its widespread use and its basic importance
in philosophy”.105 The idea is straightforward: everyday life frequently
provokes interpretative difficulties, disputes, and disagreements. Whether
within one’s own perception or at the level of language, the immediate
givenness of things gives rise to incompatibility: although it looked alit
from afar, the café turns out to be closed; the headline in the newspaper
enrages a friend whereas I find it amusing. In either case the incompat­
ibility invites a dissociation between the “appearance” of things and their
“reality”. My sequence of perceptions of the café are incompatible: to the
former I assign “appearance”, to the latter I assign “reality”. To my friend,
the amusement I find in the headline is but an appearance, while in reality
it is enraging, and so on. To capture this philosophical pair, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca introduce the following diagram:
appearance
reality
102  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §44, 190/TA, 256.
103  David Frank suggests that dissociation should serve rhetorical scholars as the 21st cen­
tury’s “canon of invention” (David A. Frank, “The Origins of and Possible Futures for
Chaïm Perelman and Lucie Olbrechts-Tyteca’s Dissociation of Concepts”, Philosophy &
Rhetoric, Vol. 53, No. 4 (2020), 396).
104  Since argumentation operates within an open, rather than a closed system, and since
argumentative discourse is characterized by polysemy rather than univocity, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca prefer to speak of “incompatibility” rather than “contradiction”.
Where the formal notion of contradiction—the assertion of a univocal proposition and
its negation—renders a system internally inconsistent, incompatibility imposes a choice
to deny or resolve the incompatibility in some way. See Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca,
NR, §46, 195-197/TA, 262–264.
105  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §90, 415/TA, 556.


82  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
Although they regard this philosophical pair as the “prototype” of all con­
ceptual dissociation, the purpose of their discussion is not to focus on the
content of this particular pair.106 Recall, their Treatise aims to provide the
means of describing and analysing argumentative discourse. The theory of
philosophical pairs is no exception. With respect to this particular pair,
they repeat that this “preference for what is real appears not only in philo­
sophical discussion but is also expressed in everyday thinking in a wide
variety of circumstances”.107 They do, however, recognize the influence
of philosophical elaborations on “ordinary thinking”, noting that the fre­
quency of certain pairs is likely due to “the residue of a dominant cultural
tradition”.108 Yet, the purpose of their discussion is not to elaborate a par­
ticular philosophical perspective.109 It remains subordinate to their aim of
developing a general theory of argumentation. It is worth repeating this
point as it is a frequent source of misunderstanding: “It is not a question of
constructing a particular philosophy, but only of observing what is being
done in various systematizations of thought and in various philosophies,
irrespective of their tendency”.110
To this end, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca introduce a more general dia­
gram, allowing the contents of any philosophical pair to be substituted for
“term I” and “term II”:
appearance
reality
term I
term II
→
Where term I refers to the more direct and immediate notion, term II can
only be grasped comparatively, in relation to term I. Term II thus results
from:
106  Our interpretation of the Treatise helps to acquit Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca of
the charge that, by choosing the “appearance-reality” pair as the prototypical dissocia­
tive technique, they fall back into “epistemological authoritarianism”, as Emmanuelle
Danblon argues in Rhétorique et rationalité. Essai sur l’émergence de la critique et de la
persuasion (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de Bruxelles, 2002), 122, our translation.
107  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §90, 417/TA, 558.
108  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §90, 420/TA, 562.
109  As Olbrechts-Tyteca will later put it: “Nothing authorizes us to say—unless one adopts
a determinate philosophy—that there are fundamental pairs” (Olbrechts-Tyteca, “Les
couples philosophiques”, 92).
110  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §91, 423, translation modified, emphasis added/
TA, 562.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  83
a dissociation effected within term I with the purpose of getting rid of
the incompatibilities that may appear between different aspects of term
I. Term II provides a criterion, a norm which allows us to distinguish
those aspects of term I which are of value from those which are not; it is
not simply a datum, it is a construction which, during the dissociation
of term I, establishes a rule that makes it possible to classify the multiple
aspects of term I in a hierarchy … In relation to term I, term II is both
normative and explanatory. After the dissociation has been made, term
II makes it possible to retain or to disqualify the various aspects under
which term I is presented.111
In the case of the appearance-reality pair, the incompatibility among the
various appearances motivated a dissociation within the appearances
themselves. By introducing “reality” as a norm to distinguish those appear­
ances that are of more value than others, it is possible to cast some of the
appearances as mere appearances (term I) and some of them as real (term
II). In this case, dissociation erects a “value hierarchy” between “reality”
and “appearance” in which the former is esteemed above the latter. In
other contexts, dissociation can also be used to undermine or even reverse
existing value hierarchies, such as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own
attempt to restore rhetoric’s philosophical reputation vis-à-vis logic (under­
stood narrowly as demonstration). It can also be used to frame seemingly
irreconcilable commitments in new and unforeseeable ways, primarily
by facilitating the “back and forth” movement between “concrete” and
“abstract” values.112 In all cases, dissociative techniques play an essential
role in generating and fuelling that “intense activity in the realm of values”
whereby values are “constantly being recast and remolded”.113
Though we have not been able to examine each and every argument
scheme introduced in the Treatise, we have seen the two basic processes
by which Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca identify and eventually organize
them: association and dissociation. The result of their efforts is a vast, but
systematic catalogue of the ways in which arguments can act on the adher­
ence of audiences.
Effectivity or Validity?
Chapters Five and Six mark an important shift within Part Three and
the Treatise as a whole. Where the first three chapters examine argument
schemes in a relatively static way, the final two chapters of Part Three start
111  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §90, 416/TA, 557.
112  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §19, 78/TA, 104.
113  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §19, 79/TA, 107.


84  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
to consider them in motion. In the introduction to Part Three (§44), we
will recall, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca indicated that they would first
analyse isolated argument structures before looking at them synthetically.
Over 350 pages later, it is to these synthetic aspects of argumentation that
they now turn.
Entitled “The Interaction of Arguments”, the final chapter of Part Three
(Chapter Five) discusses the phenomenon of argument strength (§97–98),
the convergence of arguments (§99), the amplitude of arguments (§100–
102), and the order in which arguments are presented (§103–105). No
longer concerned with static elements in isolation, here is how Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca describe these phenomena of “argument interac­
tion” in the opening paragraphs of this chapter:
At the outset of our analytical study of arguments, we drew attention
to its schematic and arbitrary character. In reality, the elements isolated
for purposes of study form a whole. They are in constant interaction
at more than one level: [1] interaction between various arguments put
forward, [2] interaction between the arguments and the overall argu­
mentative situation, [3] between the arguments and their conclusion,
and, finally, [4] between the arguments occurring in the discourse and
those that are about the discourse.114
What the introduction of these four levels highlights is that any description
of argumentation will be confronted with an enormously complex set of
variables. An analysis can always go deeper into any one of these levels or
broaden its scope, extending its frame to include data from other levels.
This complexity can of course be reduced by institutional contexts (e.g.
court of law) which impose constraints on variables like relevance, permis­
sibility, duration, style of presentation, etc.
At this synthetic level of analysis one might expect Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca to finally take an “objective” stance and discuss argu­
mentation from a more normative perspective. Yet here too they continue
to discuss “argument interaction” descriptively, just as we have already
seen with “facts”, “values”, and “presence”.
In addition to providing further evidence of their rhetorical methodology
at work, this way of approaching argument interaction also highlights the
importance of interpreting the Treatise in terms of our distinction between
(1) the theoretical and primarily descriptive attitude of the argumentation
theorist and (2) the practical attitude of the engaged arguer or audience. In
our view, adopting the theoretical attitude involves a temporary suspension
114  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, §97, NR, 460/TA, 610.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  85
of evaluation. The purpose of adopting this attitude is not so that the argu­
mentation theorist can judge whether an argument is strong or weak but to
describe the way it intends to act on the audience it addresses. Of course,
this temporary suspension of evaluation must eventually give way to a
value judgment, as the argumentation theorist cannot sustain the theoreti­
cal attitude indefinitely. Even when informed by theoretical insights, this
value judgment can only be made from within the practical attitude. From
within this attitude—that is, as an engaged social actor confronted with
practical choices—assessment and evaluation cannot be avoided.
This brings us to the most compelling illustration of Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical methodology in the Treatise, namely, their
discussion of the phenomenon of “argument strength” [la force des argu­
ments] in §97. Coming to grips with this section is essential to avoid mis­
understanding the nature of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s project and
thus serves as the strongest textual evidence in support of our interpreta­
tion. Given that many readers of the Treatise have been concerned with
evaluating arguments, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work has often
been criticized for advocating a rather naive form of audience-based rela­
tivism. In brief, critics argue that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca confuse
validity with effectiveness, reducing all truth claims to their empirical suc­
cess in persuading audiences.115 Although we have already laid the ground­
work for rejecting this interpretation, it is not until Chapter Five that we
begin to see how their account actually deals with normative questions.
The first thing to note about the discussion of argument strength is
where it appears within the work as a whole. Note that it is not included in
the more philosophical discussions of the Introduction or Part One or the
Conclusion, but well into Part Three’s examination of argumentative tech­
niques. Here, argument strength is examined primarily as a phenomenon
of argument interaction alongside contributing factors such as “conver­
gence”, “amplitude”, and “order”. In §97 Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
are not interested in the question of when arguments should be consid­
ered strong or weak. Rather, argument strength—the fact that arguments
always appear as strong or weak to audiences—is understood as a kind of
emergent phenomenon, generated by the interaction of various elements
within an argumentative situation. These elements include: the intensity
of the audience’s adherence to the premises, the associative links between
premises, the relevance of the arguments to the particular discussion,
115  One of the most influential expressions of this criticism is that of Frans van Eemeren
and Rob Grootendorst, “Perelman and the Fallacies”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 28,
No. 2 (1995), 124.


86  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
as well as other arguments that might be at play in the argumentative
situation.116
Argument strength is thus understood not as a property of arguments
on their own, that is, of arguments as isolated products, but rather as
a comparative or contrastive property, generated by the tension between
competing arguments in particular situations. A strong argument is thus
always “stronger than”; a strong argument stands out against the hori­
zon of a weaker argument, however implicit. Importantly, this means that
the phenomenon of argument strength remains relative to audience adher­
ence, as it is ultimately an argument’s “capacity for resisting objections”
that determines how strong it will appear to them in a given situation.117
“The strength of an argument”, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca explain,
“shows itself as much by the difficulty there is in refuting it as by its inher­
ent qualities”.118
If we are careful to note where this discussion occurs in the Treatise, it
becomes clear that this analysis of argument strength is purely descriptive
in its approach. Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are not providing us with
practical norms for evaluating arguments but identifying, in theory, the
conditions for arguments to appear strong or weak to audiences in the first
place.
However, such an approach invites a question that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca had already raised in the Introduction: can argument
strength be determined only by studying the empirical effects of vari­
ous argumentative techniques? Here it is important to proceed carefully
as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are quick to anticipate a potential
misunderstanding:
Knowledge of individual reactions alone, and the most thoroughgoing
investigations of differential psychology, are insufficient to measure
[argument] strength, for it includes a normative element that may be
regarded as one of the premises of argumentation or is at least insepa­
rable from the concept of strength. Is a strong argument an effective
argument which gains the adherence of the audience, or is it a valid
argument, which ought to gain it? Is the strength of an argument a
descriptive or a normative quality? Does its study fall under individual
and social psychology, or rather under that of logic?119
116  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 461/TA, 611.
117  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 461/TA, 612.
118  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 461/TA, 611.
119  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 463, translation modified/TA, 613.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  87
The authors thus clearly recognize the work of a “normative element” in
the phenomenon of argument strength. Yet, rather than commit to either
side of this dichotomy—that is, rather than approach the problem of argu­
ment strength from a “determinate philosophical point of view” that has
already settled the issue of what ought to count as effective or valid—they
approach the normal-norm opposition as a “philosophical pair” like any
other; they approach the distinction between what is “normally” effective
and the “norm” of validity as a dissociation internal to argumentation
itself:
normal
norm
This way of treating the question of argument strength corroborates the
two aspects of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical methodology
that our interpretation has tried to reconstruct. Although Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s reasons for studying argumentation in the first place
are undoubtedly motivated by a normative interest in improving the qual­
ity of argumentative practices in society as a whole, the theory of argu­
mentation found in the Treatise is descriptive in approach and strives for
philosophical neutrality about arguments themselves. This is why the nor­
mal/norm pair (like all the pairs they discuss) cannot be conceived in abso­
