lute terms: “the norm”, as they explain, “is definable only in relation to
an audience whose reactions provide the measure of what is normal and
whose adherence founds the norms of value”.120 This very dissociation is
thus itself a phenomenon of interaction for their theory to describe and
interpret in various contexts.
For Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca to take a stand on a particular hier­
archy would be to adopt a “determinate philosophical point of view”.
In other words, it would be to adopt a practical attitude towards argu­
mentation, which would violate the methodological limits established by
what we are calling their philosophical epoché and rhetorical reduction.
It is therefore not within the scope of the Treatise to advance any “the­
ory of knowledge”, which Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca here recognize
is what usually steps in to distinguish the norm from the normal121—let
alone advance one that would establish once and for all what makes an
argument strong and what criteria ought to be adopted to determine this.
Indeed, this point applies equally to the “universal audience”, which crit­
ics often mistake for a substantive argumentative norm. Yet, it too is a
120  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 463, our translation and emphasis/TA, 614.
121  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 464/TA, 615.


88  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
technical notion used to describe one way that arguers (typically, but not
exclusively, philosophers) can construct an audience.122
This brings us to the last point we want to emphasize, namely, the reason
why Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca stop short of prescribing normative
criteria for assessing or evaluating the strength of arguments. To answer
this question, let us return to the final section of Part One, “Argumentation
and Commitment” (§14). Here is how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
introduce the nature of commitment or engagement in argumentation:
The impossibility of regarding argumentation as an intellectual exercise
entirely divorced from all preoccupations of a practical nature compels
us to transpose certain concepts pertaining to knowledge that have been
developed in a quite different philosophical perspective, such as the
opposition between objective and subjective. Objectivity, as it relates
to argumentation, must be rethought and reinterpreted if it is to have
meaning in a conception that does not allow the separation of an asser­
tion from the person who makes it.123
Although the personal character of argumentation is not developed at
great length in the Treatise (for reasons that our interpretation should help
to clarify), the use of “the person” here is important for understanding the
limits of a theory of argumentation, as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
conceive it.
It is one thing for the argumentation theorist to describe the technical
workings of argumentation, to identify an argument as an instance of a
particular argumentative technique. But it is quite another to evaluate an
argument’s strength. To evaluate an argument is to take a stand or make a
judgment on what the argument is about; it involves a degree of personal
commitment for which one is ultimately responsible.124 Evaluation, there­
fore, requires adopting a practical attitude towards the argument as well
122  While Perelman occasionally compares the universal audience to Kant’s categorial
imperative, this is not at all how it is introduced in the Treatise. Here it is introduced
to provide a better description of what philosophers actually do when they argue philo­
sophically: namely, constructing their discourse in such a way that any reasonable per­
son (as they conceive of them) could follow their reasoning and willingly assent to the
same conclusion. Of course, the philosopher may be perfectly mistaken in their concep­
tion—as the entire history of philosophy undoubtedly attests. But this does not challenge
the utility of having a term for describing this way of constructing one’s audience. We
discuss the universal audience in detail in Chapter 4, pp. 161-169.
123  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §14, 59, translation modified, our emphasis/TA,
78.
124 “Since rhetorical proof is never a completely necessary proof, the mind which gives his
adherence to the conclusion of an argument does so by an act that commits him and for
which he is responsible” (Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §14, 62/TA, 82).


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  89
as the person making it. Ultimately, the difference between the theoreti­
cal attitude of the argumentation theorist and the practical attitude of the
engaged arguer boils down to a difference in audience.
To adopt the theoretical attitude is to attempt to occupy the position
of a neutral spectator to an argument (simple exteriority)—a relatively
detached audience not yet concerned with its quality or consequences.125
To adopt the practical attitude, on the other hand, is to self-identify as
an audience of the argument. This happens primarily when something is
at stake “for me”, so to speak. Whether encountered while on the street,
scrolling through the comments of a video, or working through a dense
philosophical book, arguments have a way of finding us. Arguments,
we might say, interpellate us as audiences. Once we find ourselves “in
audience” in this way, our assessment of the argument’s strengths and
weaknesses—whether spontaneous and unrefined or slow and methodi­
cal—inescapably involves the mobilization of our own personal configura­
tion of values, preferences, and sense of reality in relation to what has been
argued and by whom the argument was made.
In a certain sense, then, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s critics are
correct in alleging that the Treatise’s approach to argumentation is fun­
damentally audience-relative. What they neglect, however, is the differ­
ence between the theoretical and practical attitudes we have identified.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca never sought to provide a normative the­
ory of argument strength in the style of more contemporary theories of
argument such as Pragma-dialectics or many of the approaches associated
with the “informal logic” movement. It is thus not the case that Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca decline to address normative concerns; it is just that
they approach the phenomenon of argumentation from a different per­
spective. Their theory of argumentation aims to describe the workings of
the already normative practice of argumentation from a (defeasibly) neu­
tral, technical perspective.
A Theory of Argumentation and Its Philosophical Significance
Having traversed the Introduction and its three main parts we now come
to the Conclusion of the Treatise. The Conclusion is itself divided into
two main parts. In the first part, the authors provide a brief overview of
the book’s main themes and express their hope that others will take up
125  To avoid misunderstanding, we are not saying that the theoretical attitude is a guarantee
of impartiality, neutrality, or objectivity. Theoretical claims are just as liable to critique
and revision as practical ones. The essential difference lies in their respective scope. For
example, it is one thing to claim that an argument makes an appeal to authority and
quite another to claim that we should therefore act accordingly.


90  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
the research they have started. As Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are the
first to admit, “Far from exhausting the subject, we have barely scratched
its surface and, at times, have done no more than point to its richness”.126
Above all, they hope that they have been able to “prevent the reduction of
all the techniques of proof to formal logic and the habit of seeing nothing
in reason except the faculty to calculate”.127 In other words, they remind
us that Western philosophy’s narrow conceptions of proof and logic have
led to a constricted conception of reason. Were these limitations reversed,
however, as they propose, it would pave the way for a more expansive
conception of human rationality.
It is in pursuit of such an expanded rationality that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca introduce the second part of the Conclusion. Having
already summarized the main results of the book, they explain that the
purpose of their remaining remarks is to present “some considerations that
are too general to fall within a theory of argumentation, but provide it
with a framework that emphasizes its philosophical significance”.128 Here,
our interpretation finds further support: the authors themselves draw
attention to the difference between “a theory of argumentation” and its
“philosophical significance”. Unlike those who would conflate the theory
of argumentation with Perelman’s philosophy tout court, we have empha­
sized the importance of keeping these two aspects of the NRP distinct.129
In the remaining lines of the first part of the Conclusion, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca deploy an analogy with Descartes, spelling out how the
subsequent philosophical considerations of the Conclusion should be read
in relation to their theory of argumentation:
126  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 509/TA, 675.
127  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510/TA, 676.
128  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510, our emphasis/TA, 676.
129  Of course, this separation can also be taken too far. As James Crosswhite reminds us,
the Treatise was written in the shadow of the Second World War: “One will never under­
stand the deep rhetorical dimensions of the project unless one reads the [Treatise] in
this light. It is a philosophical treatise with a profoundly concrete aim: establishing the
possibility of a just peace, a possibility that might ground hope and the practical efforts
that follow from it. It was written in a context in which this hope had been shattered. If
we read it from relatively comfortable cocoons as a merely speculative effort to develop
a theory of argumentation, then we fail to comprehend it altogether” (Crosswhite, Deep
Rhetoric, 270). While this context is important, Crosswhite’s reading runs the risk of
conflating Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s philosophical position and the theory of
argumentation—a point which they themselves emphasize. Insisting on this difference
preserves the independent value of their theory of argumentation and the methodologi­
cal role of the rhetorical audience even if one rejects their philosophical position. This
will be important in defending the new rhetoric’s recovery of rhetoric from the criticisms
of Badiou and Cassin.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  91
Just as the Discourse on Method, though not a work on mathematics,
secures the widest sphere of application for the “geometrical” method
(though there is nothing to prevent one from being a geometrician with­
out begin a follower of Descartes), so the views we shall advance—
though the theory and practice of argumentation are not necessarily
bound up with them—accord argumentation a place and importance
that they in no way possess in a more dogmatic vision of the universe.130
In this telling passage, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are clear that the
theory and practice of argumentation, as they conceive it, are not “nec­
essarily bound up” with the philosophical conclusions that they draw
from them.131 At most, it seems that for them the theory of argumentation
underdetermines the adoption of particular philosophical positions. This
is to say that while their theory does not depend on adopting any particu­
lar philosophical position, it is fundamentally incompatible with certain
philosophies—which is clear from Perelman’s many remarks against scep­
ticisms and absolutisms of various kinds, which he argues systematically
exclude argumentation from the sphere of reason.
In the second part of the Conclusion, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
stake out a series of philosophical positions drawn from their study of
argumentation. It is here that the philosophical brackets of their epoché
are finally removed; here the theoretical attitude of the argumentation the­
orist and the requirement of descriptive neutrality are set aside. Now we
see Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca adopting the practical attitude of the
arguer, advocating a “determinate philosophical point of view” of their
own for which they assume personal responsibility.
First, given the contingent and malleable nature of notions, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca take a stand against any “uncompromising and irre­
ducible philosophical oppositions presented by all kinds of absolutism”.132
Thus, many of the familiar dualisms in Western thought, such as truth and
opinion, reason and emotion, knowledge and opinion, subjectivity and
objectivity, etc., are refused as a matter of principle. This is where the “prag­
matist” interpretation of the new rhetoric finds a certain confirmation.
130  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510, translation modified and our emphasis/TA,
676.
131  When asked in 1960 whether he bases his entire system on argumentation, Perelman
replies: “I would never claim that I base my whole system on argumentation: I find that
great harm has been done to the understanding of [human] thought by the exclusion of
argumentation, but I would not want to see it as the whole of a philosophy” (Perelman,
ED, 182, our translation).
132  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510, translation modified/TA, 676.


92  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
Second, following from the first, they reject the very idea of “defini­
tive, unalterable revelations, whatever their nature or their origin”.133 Not
only does this include spiritual or religious revelation but also “all immedi­
ate, absolute data, be they termed sensations, [or] rational self-evidence”,
which they regard as nothing but the obverse side of religious mysticism.134
In either case, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca regard as futile any attempt
to sequester certain facts or truths from the “full time” [temps plein] of
meaningful human life and hold them to be “identical in all normally con­
stituted minds, independently of social and historical contingencies”.135
They regard this manoeuvre as a philosophical sleight of hand: the phi­
losopher withdraws certain elements from his or her total set of opinions,
elevating them to the status of indisputable facts or truths, and safeguards
them from the realm of discussion and argumentation. For Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca, this foundationalist approach to philosophical method
needlessly abandons the sphere of human action to arbitrariness, solip­
sism, irrationality, and, ultimately, violence.
Third, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca reject the absolutist epistemol­
ogy underpinning the distinction between judgments of reality and judg­
ments of value. This distinction, they argue, can never be neatly drawn, as
it takes for granted “precarious agreements of varying intensity”, agree­
ments which in most cases are never made fully explicit.136 Owing to the
important role played by agreement [accord], Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca find both realism and nominalism too static to account for the
dynamism of language, which, they remind us, is not only a means of
communicating but also “an instrument for acting on minds, a means of
persuasion”.137 Language is thus neither a carbon copy of the reality it rep­
resents nor the arbitrary invention of individuals. Rather, what they will
elsewhere call a “living language” [une langue vivante]138 is a reservoir of
available meanings, containing notions of varying degrees of clarity, with
a capacity for innovation as well as a certain inertia:
All language is the language of a community, be this a community
bound by biological ties, or by the practice of a common discipline or
133  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510, translation modified/TA, 676.
134  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 510, translation modified/TA, 676.
135  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, “On Temporality as a Characteristic of Argumenta­
tion”, ICO, 191/RH, 388; NR, 510/TA, 677. For a further discussion of the idea of “full
time” and its opposition to “empty time”, see Scott, “Argumentation and the Challenge
of Time”, 27–28.
136  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 513/TA, 680.
137  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 132/TA, 177.
138  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, “Les notions et l’argumentation” (1955), RH, 113, our
translation.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  93
technique. The terms used, their meaning, their definition, can only be
understood in the context of the habits, ways of thinking, methods,
external circumstances, and traditions known to the users of those
terms. […] Adherence to particular linguistic usages normally expresses
the explicit or implicit adoption of certain standpoints [prises de posi­
tion] which are neither the reflection of an objective reality nor the man­
ifestation of individual arbitrariness. Language is part of the tradition of
a community, and like the others, it only undergoes revolutionary modi­
fication where there is a radical failure to adapt to a new situation; oth­
erwise its transformation is slow and imperceptible. But an agreement
on the use of terms, no less than an agreement about the conception of
reality and the vision of the world, even though it may not be disputed,
is not indisputable: it is linked to a social and historical situation which
fundamentally conditions any distinction that one might wish to draw
between judgments of reality and value judgments.139
This and similar passages place Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca among
the earliest European philosophers to adopt an approach to language
comparable to what is now called “pragmatics” in linguistics (not to be
confused with “pragmatism” in the philosophical sense). From this per­
spective, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca find themselves equally opposed
to any kind of linguistic individualism, which neglects the importantly
intersubjective dimension of language, as well as any strong form of lin­
guistic structuralism, which neglects the capacity of individuals to innovate
language through use.
Yet, what is it then that allows us to transcend the social and historical
conditioning of our language and our knowledge? Put differently, what can
transform certain de facto agreements into agreements de jure? Without
elaborating here, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca simply note that, for
them, it is only after a process of argumentation that a choice or a decision
for a particular course of action deserves to be called “rational”.140 They
thus call their philosophical position a “critical rationalism”, which they
introduce in the following way:
The theory and practice of argumentation are, in our view, correlative
with a critical rationalism that transcends the duality “judgments of
reality-value judgments”, and makes both judgments of reality and value
judgments dependent on the personality of the scientist or philosopher,
139  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 513/TA, 681.
140  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 514, translation modified/TA, 681. Perelman will
later dissociate between the term “rational” and “reasonable” (le raisonnable). We dis­
cuss this distinction in Chapter 3, pp. 121-128.


94  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
who is responsible for his decisions in the field of knowledge as well as
in the field of action.141
Here again, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are clear to remind us that
their philosophical position, critical rationalism, is only “correlative” with
the theory and practice of argumentation. In other words, they do not
regard their own philosophical position as the only one capable of sys­
tematically incorporating a theory of argumentation. At the same time,
however, they stand firmly behind their position, arguing that only “the
existence of an argumentation that is neither compelling nor arbitrary can
give meaning to human freedom, a state in which a reasonable choice can
be exercised”.142 Indeed, the notion of freedom would be meaningless if it
meant nothing more than conforming to a pre-given order. And, without
recourse to argumentation, that is, without being based on reasons, the
exercise of freedom would be irrational, “reduced to an arbitrary decision
operating in an intellectual void”.143 It is therefore only through something
akin to what they call argumentation that this dilemma can be overcome.
This point brings us to the ultimate philosophical aim of the Treatise, as
well as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own expressed motivations for
pursuing the project: the recognition that only a theory of argumentation
could accomplish what a “logic of value judgments” had tried in vain to
accomplish, “namely the justification of the possibility of a human com­
munity in the sphere of action when this justification cannot be based on a
reality or objective truth”.144
With this we come to the end of the Treatise. While it is tempting to
think that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s theory of argumentation is
derived from, or leads directly to, the “critical rationalism” they pro­
pose in the Conclusion, it is important to recall the various clues we have
drawn attention to throughout our reading. Whether or not Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s theory ultimately succeeds in attaining the kind of neu­
trality they pursue, we maintain that their regressive methodology, which
centres around their rhetorical conception of audience, is essential to their
legacy and ought not to be neglected. Moreover, the concept of audience
is also essential for extending their work and forming new philosophical
allegiances with other authors who may not at first glance appear sympa­
thetic to rhetoric.
141  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 514/TA, 681–682.
142  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 514/TA, 682.
143  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 514/TA, 682.
144  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 514/TA, 682.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  95
2  Reception of the New Rhetoric Project
Having explained how we interpret the aim, scope, and methodology of
the Treatise, we now turn to the reception of the NRP more broadly. The
purpose of this section is to contextualize Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
work within the landscape of 20th-century thought as well as Badiou and
Cassin’s respective criticisms of rhetoric, which are part of a broader and,
we shall argue, ultimately misplaced hostility towards rhetoric. As we will
see here, those working in the human and social sciences proved much
more receptive to the NRP than philosophers. After discussing some of the
difficulties Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca faced in finding a philosophical
audience for their ideas, we then briefly discuss Belgian philosopher Michel
Meyer, whose “problematology” is one of the few philosophical attempts
to extend Perelman’s vision of rhetoric.
Human and Social Sciences
Although Perelman primarily understood the NRP as a philosophical
intervention, he was always aware that its success would depend on the
uptake and application of the theory of argumentation in other fields
and disciplines.145 Among these it was primarily those working in the
human and social sciences that would prove most receptive to Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s ideas. In part, this broad reception is the result of
decades of argumentative effort throughout Europe and the Americas as
well as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s efforts in securing and overseeing
the translation of their work.146 According to Michelle Bolduc, the signifi­
cance of translation in this regard has been underappreciated, particularly
among Anglo-American scholars.147 As Bolduc explains:
145  In a letter to Swiss philosopher, logician, and founder of “natural logic”, Jean-Blaise
Grize, Perelman explains that “argumentation theory can only become operative in par­
ticular disciplines” (Perelman, Letter to Jean-Blaise Grize, 11 May 1976, Fonds Chaïm
Perelman, Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 22.5, our
translation).
146  For an extensive and detailed account of the importance of translation for the NRP see
Michelle Bolduc, Translation and the Rediscovery of Rhetoric (Toronto: Pontifical Insti­
tute of Medieval Studies, 2020), 266–330.
147 “That Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca were sensitive to issues of translation is a natural
outcome of their multilingual background and their European—specifically, Belgian—
context. Perelman, who was born in Poland and emigrated to Belgium as a teenager,
spoke numerous languages, including Polish, Yiddish, French, Flemish, German, Italian,
and English; we know that Olbrechts-Tyteca read widely in French, German, Italian, and
Spanish” (Bolduc, Translation and the Rediscovery of Rhetoric, 267–268).


96  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
both Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca were very involved in the transla­
tion of the works of the [new rhetoric project]. Perelman’s correspond­
ence shows him actively seeking to have translations made of his work
into numerous languages, including German, Italian, English, Flemish,
Japanese, Romanian, and Hebrew. Furthermore, Perelman not only
corresponded directly with his translators, offering to be a resource for
their practice of translating his work, but also actively assessed the qual­
ity of their translations.148
Translation, she continues, was not only central to the dissemination
of their new rhetoric, but also to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own
recovery of rhetoric in the first place, first through Brunetto Latini and
then Cicero by way of Jean Paulhan.149
Among the various disciplinary worlds of the NRP’s reception, there
are a few that stand out. The most proximate world is undoubtedly the
field of law. Perelman was not only reputed as a philosopher—for a time
the “master philosopher of Brussels”150—but more specifically as a legal
philosopher.151 As H.L.A Hart would write in his Introduction to a col­
lection of Perelman’s articles, Perelman was indeed “a master of two
disciplines: philosophy and law”.152 It is thus no surprise that his work
would draw the attention of legal scholars. Indeed, after the publication
of the Treatise, Perelman began to take a more serious interest in law and

ing paragraph containing this passage was excluded from the Lectures 2 version of this
text, cf. Ricoeur, Lectures 2. La contrée des philosophes (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1999),
481–495.

l’argumentation (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de Bruxelles, 1970), Droit, morale et

The essays contained in these volumes have more recently been collected in Éthique
et droit (2012), now in its second edition. Some of these essays have been translated

The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  97
juridical reasoning, marking a decisive turn in his work.153 However, as
Benoît Frydman reminds us, Perelman did not accomplish this task alone.
Perelman’s work on juridical argumentation was the result of a truly col­
laborative effort with his colleagues and jurist friends at the Université libre
de Bruxelles.154 The comparatively greater uptake of Perelman’s work by
European scholars within the fields of law, legal theory, and jurisprudence
can in large part be explained in terms of this institutional context.155 This
trend has continued into the 21st century, with many of the monographs
devoted to Perelman’s work, and the NRP more generally, approaching it
from a legal perspective.156

98  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
The reception of the NRP has not only been confined to law. It has also
had a considerable influence in linguistics and other fields concerned with
the use of language.157 Although neither Perelman nor Olbrechts-Tyteca
were linguists, their understanding of argumentation as a form of discur­
sive action clearly dovetails with, and in some ways anticipates, paral­
lel developments in speech-act theory and pragmatics. Among European
scholars especially, the NRP has come to serve as an important point of
reference for the study of argumentation from a linguistic perspective.158
The reception of the NRP in linguistics and related fields such as dis­
course analysis and rhetoric has been equally influential in North America.
In the United States, the first to pick up on the significance of Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work were primarily scholars in departments of speech
and communications, English, and rhetoric.159 The NRP’s “first contact”
with these previously isolated academic cultures can be traced back to
the fall of 1962, when, at the suggestion of American philosopher Henry
Johnstone Jr., Perelman was appointed Distinguished Visiting Professor
at what was then called the Department of Speech and Philosophy at The
Pennsylvania State University.160 This is how Robert T. Oliver recounts
this meeting of minds:
157  Within Belgium, this influence continues in the ongoing activities of the Groupe de

libre de Bruxelles (https://gral​.ulb​.ac​.be/).
158  Notable examples include Jean-Blaise Grize, De la logique à l’argumentation (Genève:

à l’étude linguistique de la parole argumentative (Paris: Kimé, 1990); Ruth Amossy,
L’argumentation dans le discours, 4e éd. (Paris: Armand Colin, [2000] 2021); Ruth
Amossy, et al., éds., Après Perelman: Quelles politiques pour les nouvelles rhétoriques ?
L’argumentation dans les sciences du langage (Paris: L’Harmattan, 2002); Emmanuelle
Danblon, La fonction persuasive: Anthropologie du discours rhétorique: Origines et
actualité (Paris: Armand Colin, 2005); Marc Angenot, Dialogues de sourds: Traité
de rhétorique antilogiques (Paris: Mille et une nuits, 2008). Recent studies in linguis­
tics that build upon the NRP include Ruth Amossy, “The New Rhetoric’s Inheritence.
Argumentation and Discourse Analysis”, Argumentation, Vol. 23 (2009), 313–324;
Marc Angenot, et al., éds., Renaissances de la rhétorique: Perelman aujourd’hui (Paris:
Éditions Nota bene, 2016); Jean-Claude Guerrini, Les Valeurs dans l’argumentation:
L’héritage de Chaïm Perelman (Paris: Classiques Garnier, 2019).
159  For Perelman’s own recounting of his contact with scholars in the United States, see the
Foreword to the English translation of the Treatise (NR, v-vi); Ray D. Dearin, ed., The
New Rhetoric of Chaim Perelman: Statement & Response (Lanham, MD: University
Press of America, 1989). This volume includes a number of critical essays about Perel­
man’s work by scholars at universities in the United States, as well as a response from
Perelman.
160  Robert. T. Oliver, “Philosophy And/Or Rhetoric”, 571.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  99
We Speech professors listened with the delight a traveller feels who
encounters a stranger who turns out to be a fellow countryman. Without
hesitation, he had struck at once at the heart of the philosophy upon
which our profession is based. More clearly and surely than many of
our own colleagues in other departments of our own universities, he
had stated precisely the central core of our own discipline. Still we could
not quite believe that this professor from Europe, where the Speech
profession is practically unknown, could possibly understand the full
implications of what he himself had so cogently stated.161
Yet the benefits of this encounter did not only extend in one direction. In
his own version of these events, Perelman admits that at the time of writing
the Treatise he “had entirely ignored the works which speech professionals
have published since 1915”.162 Indeed, after meeting these fellow travel­
lers he admits that some of the statements made in the Treatise extolling
its novelty now appear overstated or even “ridiculous”.163 Although ini­
tially separated by disciplinary and linguistic barriers, the NRP nonetheless
found common cause with these rhetoricians. The success of this and other
encounters is well attested by Perelman’s place, among the likes of Kenneth
Burke, as an enduring figure in rhetorical curricula in the United States.
In Canada the NRP seems to have first drawn the attention of scholars in
Quebec.164 On several occasions Perelman was invited to lecture at McGill
University in Montreal throughout the 1960s and 1970s. One member
of Perelman’s audience during one of these visits in the early 1960s was
philosopher J. Anthony Blair, then a young student at McGill.165 Some
years later at the University of Windsor in southwestern Ontario, Blair
and fellow philosopher Ralph H. Johnson would pioneer the “informal
logic” movement in the late 1970s.166 Although not initially sympathetic
to the use of “rhetoric”, informal logic shared Perelman’s interest in the
161  Robert. T. Oliver, “Philosophy And/Or Rhetoric”, 572.
162  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and The Rhetoricians: Remembrances and Comments”,
The New Rhetoric of Chaim Perelman, 241.
163  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and The Rhetoricians”, 241.
164  For a detailed account of the reception of Perelman’s thought in francophone Québec, see
Marcel Côté, “Postface. Perelman au Québec”, Renaissances de la rhétorique, 345–363.
165  J. Anthony Blair, personal correspondence with the author, 22 April 2021.
166  For an account of the Informal Logic movement and its relation to the development of
argumentation studies in Canada, see Blair, “Pioneering Informal Logic and Argumen­
tation Studies” in Informal Logic: A “Canadian” Approach to Argument, ed. Federico
Puppo (Windsor: Windsor Studies in Argumentation, 2019), 35–60; Takuzo Konishi,
“Toward a History of Argumentation: Canadian informal logic”, OSSA Conference
Archive, 92 (2009), 1–26.


100  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
study of argumentation and reasoning as it occurs in real-life contexts.167
In 1983 Johnson and Blair even wrote to Perelman hoping that he might
join the editorial committee of their new journal, Informal Logic, which
was then called the “Informal Logic Newsletter”.168 However, Perelman
ultimately declined mistakenly believing that the effort was more “peda­
gogical than philosophical”.169 Despite Perelman’s initial assessment, the
Treatise would come to serve as a major point of reference in bridging
the various schools of thought within the informal logic movement which
shared a common interest in the study of argumentation.170
The field of argumentation theory, and argumentation studies more
broadly, has thus begun to break down some of the disciplinary barriers
that constrained the early reception of the NRP.171 Now an interdiscipli­
nary research field in its own right, argumentation theory continues to
engage with many of the ideas that first found expression in Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s writings.172 To this extent, the Treatise has come
to enjoy a second life as a classic text that scholars from various disci­
plines will continue to draw from and engage with as the field continues
to develop.
Philosophy
Moving past the NRP’s considerable influence in the fields of law, lin­
guistics, rhetoric, and argumentation theory, we now turn to Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s reception among philosophers, the audience that
most concerns us here. Although Perelman was already a well-established

mal Logic and the Historicity of Reason”, Informal Logic, Vol. 26, No. 3 (2006), 341–
357.

Perelman, Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 23.6.
169  Perelman, Letter to Ralph Johnson and Anthony J. Blair, n.d, Fonds Chaïm Perelman,
Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 23.6.

chts-Tyteca for informal logic”, Rhetoric and Argumentation in the Beginning of the
XXIst Century, ed. Henrique Jales Ribeiro (Coimbra: Coimbra University Press, 2009),
17–32.

Springer, 2014). For a more concise overview in French, see Philippe Breton and Gilles

The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  101
philosophical figure in Europe, it was only with the publication of the
Treatise in 1958 that he and Olbrechts-Tyteca would achieve international
recognition. As Thomas M. Conley describes:
The book had an immediate impact on the Continent, where it was
reviewed favorably in almost every journal devoted to philosophy.
Surprisingly, it was praised even in Britain, in a review by Peter Strawson
in Mind, a journal dominated by the “ordinary language philosophy”
current at Cambridge and Oxford. But the most enthusiastic welcome
was offered in the United States, where, Perelman was to say later, he
least expected it.173
However enthusiastic their reception in the United States, that audience, as
we have seen, was composed primarily of professors of speech and rheto­
ric rather than philosophy. Yet, as Perelman had long maintained, for the
NRP to be further developed, “the theory of argumentation must awaken
the interest of philosophers and not merely that of lawyers and members
of the speech profession”.174
But it is precisely here that the new rhetoric fell short. Favourable
reviews by European philosophers notwithstanding, the long-term uptake
of the NRP proved somewhat of a disappointment. Despite its bold claim
that a theory of argumentation provided the means of completing the
advancements of modern logic in the 20th century,175 the new rhetoric
hardly achieved the same status as the projects of some of Perelman’s more
well-known contemporaries. Indeed, as Christopher W. Tindale points
out, there is a regrettable irony to this outcome: if the success of argu­
mentation can in part be measured by the responses of the audience for
which it is intended—a distinctive feature of Perelman’s theory of argu­
mentation—then by that standard “the project of the new rhetoric appears
unsuccessful, because the audience for whom Perelman (and Olbrechts-
Tyteca) expressly wrote was the audience least captivated by his ideas”.176
In the following decades after the publication of the Treatise Perelman
expresses frustration at his fellow philosophers for their stubborn resist­
ance to all matters rhetorical. This frustration is particularly acute in the
Foreword to L’empire rhétorique [The Realm of Rhetoric] (1977), regret­
tably not included in the English translation. In this text Perelman laments
173  Thomas M. Conley, Rhetoric in the European Tradition (Chicago, IL: The University of
Chicago Press, 1990), 297.
174  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric: A Theory of Practical Reasoning”, NRH, 35.
175  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 10/TA, 13.
176  Tindale, “Ways of Being Reasonable: Perelman and the Philosophers”, Philosophy &
Rhetoric, Vol. 43, No. 4 (2010), 338.


102  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
the fact that many of his French contemporaries had either neglected or
seriously misunderstood his work in thinking that “the rehabilitation of
rhetoric, that a new rhetoric could be limited to updating the rhetoric of
figures”.177 Here Perelman specifically targets Paul Ricoeur for unjustly
placing his and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work under the same category as
Groupe µ’s A General Rhetoric (1970) in Ricoeur’s then recently published
The Rule of Metaphor (1975).178 In this work, Ricoeur takes Groupe µ’s
structuralist rhetoric to be representative of all “new rhetorics”, which he
describes as having the common aim of trying “to renovate the essentially
taxonomic enterprise of classical rhetoric”.179 As we shall see in Chapter
5, Perelman was right to feel neglected by Ricoeur and others. Indeed, not
only do Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca not share this “common aim”, but
their work also anticipates some of the responses that would be mobilized
against structuralism in subsequent decades. It would therefore seem that
even many of Perelman’s natural philosophical allies were not immune to
their profession’s long-standing prejudice against rhetoric.
Despite Strawson’s positive assessment of the Treatise the NRP would
meet a similar fate on the other side of the channel.180 Indeed, Perelman
himself recognized the affinity of certain aspects of his thought with the
“ordinary language philosophers”.181 At a meeting of the Sociétés de phi­
losophie de langue française in Geneva in 1966, for example, Perelman
notes that his “suggestion to use law to clarify and enrich moral reflection
… can be compared with the technique of analysing language practiced by
the Oxford School”.182 Yet, with few exceptions, the appreciation of this
compatibility moved in only one direction.183 As Tindale observes, while

The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  103
more sympathetic readers like Hart found aspects of Perelman’s philoso­
phy attractive, it seems that it was the importance he placed on rhetoric
that prevented a more felicitous convergence.184
Within the philosophical landscape of postwar 20th-century thought,
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca found themselves in an awkward position;
their ideas were caught between the rapidly stagnating “analytic-continen­
tal” divide, without falling neatly in either camp.185 Even though Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca balanced their interest in the workings of language
with an appreciation of the mind’s contribution to the social world and
its many layers of meaning, their work proved too analytic for continen­
tals and too continental for analytics. It is perhaps no surprise then that
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s ideas would resonate most forcefully in
more proximate quarters, the tradition which Perelman himself had helped
to consolidate—the École de Bruxelles [Brussels School].186
Problematology
The most prominent and original philosophical figure of the “Brussels
School” is undoubtedly Michel Meyer (1950–2022), a student of Perelman
who would later succeed him as Professor of Philosophy and Rhetoric at
et philosophie: Perelman et la philosophie analytique”, Chaïm Perelman (1912-2012),
161–185.
184  Tindale “Ways of Being Reasonable: Perelman and the Philosophers”, 342, 345.
185  The difficulty of placing Perelman along these lines is noted by Charles Taylor, who was
also present at the Royaumont conference. In his review of the proceedings Taylor writes:
“This is the record of a dialogue that didn’t come off, a dialogue de sourds. This Royau­
mont conference was an attempt to make contact between two philosophical worlds
that touch today at very few points, and which one can name for short Anglo-Saxon and
Continental. The former was represented mainly by philosophers from Oxford, although
America was represented in Professor Quine and one or two others; the ‘Continentals’
for their part were mostly interested in or adepts of the methods of phenomenology,
although some—for example, Professor Perelman from Brussels—stood closer to the
empiricist tradition” (Charles Taylor, “Review of La Philosophie Analytique”, The Philo­
sophical Review, Vol. 73, No. 1 (1964), 132–133).
186  The most comprehensive account of Perelman’s place in the “Brussels School” is Marc

L’Homme et la rhétorique. L’École de Bruxelles (Paris: Méridiens Klinckseick, 1990)
as well as Michel Meyer’s “The Brussels School of Rhetoric: From the New Rhetoric

discussion of Perelman’s place in the “Brussels School” in the context of law, see Benoît

104  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
the Université libre de Bruxelles.187 Meyer’s own philosophy, what he calls
“problematology”, attempts to provide the missing philosophical ground
to the NRP and extend it in a more systematic fashion. From the per­
spective of problematology, Perelman’s view of rhetoric suffers from what
Meyer calls “propositionalism”—a position he regards as endemic to a
large part of the Western philosophical tradition. As Meyer explains, prop­
ositionalism is the view that “the judgment or the proposition is the most
basic unit of thought”, a view which has imposed itself as the only possible
conception of reason since Aristotle.188 What this view neglects, he argues,
is the (often implicit) problematicity of propositions. Meyer’s problematol­
ogy thus seeks to rectify this problem:
Problematology explicitly anchors thought in questioning and, as a con­
sequence, thematizes the question-answer couple on which it is based.
The basic unit of thinking is no longer a judgement or proposition but
the problematological difference, from which judgments only emerge to
be considered as answers.189
In other words, propositions always presuppose a problem or question to
which they serve as a response or answer. Meyer’s problematology there­
fore attempts to recover the “problematological difference” between ques­
tions and answers suppressed by the propositionalist conception of reason.
By neglecting the interrogative foundation of reason, Meyer argues that
Perelman’s view of rhetoric ultimately suffers from a narrowness com­
mon to a number of historical and more recent conceptions of rhetoric.
According to Meyer, there are “three possible and traditional anchor
points of rhetoric”: logos, ethos, and pathos.190 For example, Meyer argues
that Plato’s conception of rhetoric is pathos-centred, Aristotle’s is logos-
centred, and that of Cicero is ethos-centred.191 As for Perelman, Meyer
argues that his conception of rhetoric is ultimately concerned more with
logos than ethos or pathos—a criticism made in slightly different ways by
187  Nick Turnbull, Michel Meyer’s Problematology: Questioning and Society (London:
Bloomsbury, 2014), 22. Turnbull’s book is the only English-language monograph on
Meyer’s philosophy. For an introduction to Meyer’s work in French, see Angèle Kre­
mer-Marietti, Michel Meyer et la problématologie (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de
Bruxelles, 2008).
188  Michel Meyer, What Is Rhetoric? (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2017), 18; Principia
rhetorica: Une théorie générale de l’argumentation (Paris: Fayard, 2008), 32.
189  Meyer, What Is Rhetoric?, 34.
190  Meyer, What Is Rhetoric?, 7.
191  Meyer, Principia rhetorica, 19.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  105
Gross and Dearin, Marc Angenot, as well as Michael Leff.192 As Meyer
formulates this objection:
When Perelman invokes the audience, it is nevertheless, like the speaker,
subordinate to the consideration of arguments, which are subject to
detailed analysis. Logos is the anchoring point of his approach. There is
no passionate or impassioned pathos; no speaker whose character [i.e.
ethos] would be more important than the arguments themselves, to the
point of being studied in its own right.193
Meyer thus wants to unlock the full potential of Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s recovery of rhetoric by foregrounding the role of questioning at
the heart of reason. By doing so he argues it is possible to achieve an inte­
grated definition of rhetoric which incorporates all three anchoring points,
logos, ethos, and pathos, “without diminishing their respective roles or
putting one of them first”.194 To this end, Meyer defines rhetoric as “the
negotiation of distance between individuals on a given question”.195 For
problematology, then, it is always a question or problem (logos) that moti­
vates the use of rhetoric and argumentation in negotiating the social dis­
tance between arguers (ethos) and audiences (pathos).
While we undoubtedly share Meyer’s ambition of arriving at an inte­
grated approach to rhetoric, we would resist interpreting Perelman’s posi­
tion as “logos-centred”. It is true that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
rediscovery of rhetoric led them to conduct a systematic study of argu­
ments and might therefore be construed as privileging rhetoric’s discursive
side. But it is worth recalling how this field of inquiry was opened up
in the first place: the methodological function of the audience, which is
precisely what our interpretation of the Treatise sought to emphasize. To
recall, it was by only by thematizing the addressive structure of discourse,
that is, an argument’s internal reference to an audience, that their descrip­
tive, quasi-phenomenological study of argumentative techniques became
possible in the first place. For this reason, would it not be more accurate
in Meyer’s terms to interpret Perelman as a “pathos-centred” theorist of
192  Gross and Dearin, Chaim Perelman, x; Angenot, Dialogues de sourds; Michael Leff,
“Perelman, ad Hominen Argument, and Rhetorical Ethos”, Argumentation, Vol. 23
(2009), 305.
193  Meyer, Principia rhetorica, 56, our translation.
194  Meyer, What Is Rhetoric?, 7, italics removed.
195  Meyer, Principia rhetorica, 21, our translation. Similar formulations can also be found
in, among other places, Meyer, “The Brussels School of Rhetoric”, 408; Meyer, What Is
Rhetoric?, 6.


106  The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric
rhetoric?196 Would it not be better to charge the new rhetoric with being
overly audience-centric, as most of its critics allege?
Yet here too we would resist consigning Perelman’s position to any of
Meyer’s three anchoring points. While elegant, Meyer’s triadic schema
misses something essential about the NRP: the very identification of these
three anchoring points already presupposes the methodological work of the
audience (in our sense). In more concrete terms, this means that the intrin­
sic relation between logos, ethos, and pathos, as Meyer understands them,
only becomes philosophically significant if one already appreciates the rhe­
torical dimension (or “rhetoricity”) of discourse; if one already appreciates
that there is more to language than the truth or falsity of propositions inde­
pendent of their speakers and audiences. Put differently, Meyer’s triad—
the moral or ethical character of a speaker (ethos), the affective orientation
of their audience (pathos), and the problem or question to which their
discourse responds (logos)—only matters if one has already called into
question that “age-old assumption in philosophy”, as Austin put it, “that
to say something, at least in all cases worth considering … is always and
simply to state something”.197 There is indeed more to language than the
truth and falsity of statements, and this “more”, we would argue, is the
result of the broader view afforded by a rhetorical concern with audience.
Thus, where Meyer thinks of the audience only in terms of pathos, as the
recipient of discourse with their own unique set of intellectual and affective
dispositions, we would make a distinction between a discourse’s empirical
addressee (its “public”, to take advantage of the additional French term) and
what we have been calling the “audience” (auditoire), following Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca. Where the former refers to those persons who actu­
ally receive the discourse, the latter refers to the arguer’s construction of
those persons, their intended “public”.198 Although some might quarrel
with this terminology, what matters is that some such distinction between
196  While it is true that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca do not develop a theory of the
passions, as Meyer does (Meyer, La Philosophe et les passions. Esquisse d’une histoire
de la nature humaine (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 2007)), they certainly rec­
ognized the importance of the pathetic, or affective aspect of argumentation. In their
1955 article “Les notions et l’argumentation”, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca already
observe that: “A view closer to argumentative reality seems to result from considera­
tions of the functions of language: persuasion is action, the notions one uses are charged
with an affective potential that cannot be neglected; besides their descriptive meaning,
an affective meaning must be taken into account” (Perelman, RH, our translation, our
emphasis, 115). Several decades later, Perelman would reaffirm this point remarking that
his “enlarged conception of reason is not in opposition to the passions” (Perelman, “The
New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, 249).
197  Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 12.
198  We discuss in detail Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s definition of audience as a con­
struction of the arguer in Chapter 4, pp. 157-161.


The Audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s New Rhetoric  107
the empirical addressee and the audience is retained. As we have sought to
emphasize in this chapter, this is because Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
rhetorical conception of audience performs an important methodological
role in the Treatise and is what allows their theory of argumentation to get
off the ground.
3  Conclusion
Following our examination of Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms of rhetoric
in Chapter 1, this chapter has examined a more positive assessment of
rhetoric’s value for philosophy. To recall, we selected Perelman as our
interlocutor for both the explicitness of his defence of rhetoric as well as
his thematization of a distinctively rhetorical concept of audience—the
concept which most troubled Badiou and Cassin. In our interpretation
of the Treatise, we sought to redress certain limitations in the reception
of this work by focusing on the methodological role of the audience. As
we argued, this concept serves as the anchoring point of Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s quasi-phenomenological study of the discursive means
by which arguers seek to act on the adherence of audiences—which we
unpacked in terms of what we called their “philosophical epoché” and
“rhetorical reduction”.
We then surveyed the reception of the NRP across various disciplines
in order to situate both Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work as well as
Badiou and Cassin’s hostility towards rhetoric within a wider historical
context. As we saw, while those working in the human and social sciences
proved rather receptive to the Belgians’s ideas, philosophers remained
more suspicious of their long-standing rival. One outlier to this trend was
Michel Meyer, whose “problematology” seeks to ground Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s vision of rhetoric in a more comprehensive philoso­
phy of questioning. However, we argued that Meyer’s interpretation of
the new rhetoric overlooks the methodological role of the audience. For
Meyer, the audience is reducible to what he calls pathos, one link in the
rhetorical triad alongside logos and ethos. On our reading, by contrast,
what is most important about Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work is
how their concept of audience exposes the rhetorical dimension of all dis­
course—or what we shall later call “rhetoricity”. Simply put, the audience
is what permits us to see discourse in terms of the interplay between logos,
ethos, and pathos in the first place. By presupposing rhetoric’s value for
philosophy, in other words, Meyer neglects to offer an explicit defence of
rhetoric from those who, like Badiou and Cassin, are sceptical about this
very point. By insisting on the importance of our interpretation of audi­
ence, it is to this task that we turn in the following two chapters.


3
If rhetoric is truly a dead end, as Badiou and Cassin allege, their claims
will have to stand up against the more positive assessment offered by
the authors of the NRP. In this and the following chapter, then, we shall
carefully examine Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms of rhetoric by staging a
trial with Perelman. Prosecuting this case fairly requires, on the one hand,
further explicating the details of Badiou and Cassin’s respective argu­
ments, and, on the other, introducing further aspects of Perelman’s (and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s) work than we saw in Chapter 2.
Without hesitating to acknowledge certain problems that Badiou and
Cassin identify with Perelman’s philosophical position, we shall ultimately
argue that these problems are remediable within the original ambit of the
NRP. Specifically, we shall argue that neither Badiou nor Cassin’s critiques
fully appreciate the significance of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhe­
torical conception of the audience—in its own right or as an operative
concept in Badiou and Cassin’s own argumentation. Thus, rather than do
away with rhetoric and discard the concept of audience altogether, we
contend that Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms should instead motivate the
search for an expanded conception of audience.
*
In this chapter, we stage a trial between Badiou and Perelman. This con­
frontation proceeds in three steps. The first step is to establish the terms
of a Badiouian critique of Perelman. As Badiou never explicitly engages
with Perelman or the NRP, we rely on Badiou’s critique of similar targets.
Specifically, we rely on his critique of pluralism in relation to the work
of Hannah Arendt, which allows us to establish the terms of the debate
on equal footing. In brief, Badiou argues that such pluralism is premised
on the “suturing” of philosophy to its political condition—specifically to
a democratic politics. By defining pluralism in relation to a plurality of
Rhetoric on Trial (I)
Badiou v. Perelman
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-4


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  109
opinions rather than truths, Badiou would ultimately find Perelman’s posi­
tion too sophistic.
In a second step we turn to Perelman, this time looking at his broader
philosophical vision for the NRP beyond the Treatise and how he addresses
the charge of relativism. We begin by examining Perelman’s philosophical
pluralism, which informs and motivates the theory of argumentation dis­
cussed in Chapter 2. Drawing support from several key texts, we consider
the origins of Perelman’s pluralism, specifically the influence of the philos­
opher and sociologist Eugène Dupréel. We then turn to Perelman’s notion
of the “reasonable”, which we unpack by showing how a social sense of
what is reasonable emerges from what he calls the “rule of justice” and the
“principle of inertia”. In a final step, we show that beyond the important
but limited status of being reasonable Perelman (and Olbrechts-Tyteca)
endorses a form of argumentative contrastivism in accounting for what
makes arguments stronger or weaker.
In a third step, we evaluate Badiou’s critique of Perelman in light of the
preceding discussion. Although Badiou’s critique allows us to identify the
limits of Perelman’s pluralist position, Badiou’s hostility towards sophistry
prevents him from considering any alternative way of conceiving rhetoric
and, more specifically, the concept of audience as Perelman understands it.
To conclude, we highlight the rhetorical dimension of Badiou’s own philo­
sophical practice as well as his implicit “theory of audience”, which raises
reasonable doubt as to whether rhetoric is truly a dead end for philosophy.
1  Against “Political Philosophy”
In this section we unpack Badiou’s critique of Perelman’s pluralism. As
we saw in Chapter 1, Badiou’s philosophy defines itself over and against
sophistry. Given what we have seen of his critique of contemporary phi­
losophy, it is almost certain that Badiou would consider Perelman a mod­
ern-day sophist. However, since Badiou has never written about Perelman
or the NRP, justifying this step will require turning to what he says about
pluralism elsewhere. Fortunately, Badiou provides a focused critique of
pluralism in the context of the work of Hannah Arendt (more specifically,
Myriam Revault d’Allonnes’s interpretation of Arendt).1
In his essay “Against ‘Political Philosophy’”, the first chapter of
Metapolitics [Abrégé de métapolitique] (1998), Badiou identifies Arendt as
1  The text Badiou is specifically referring to is the French edition of Arendt’s lectures on
Kant’s political philosophy, edited, translated, and introduced by Revault d’Allonnes:
Hannah Arendt, Juger. Sur La Philosophie Politique de Kant, ed., trans. Myriam Revault
d’Allonnes (Paris: Seuil, 1991)/Lectures on Kant’s Political Philosophy, ed. Ronald Beiner
(Chicago, IL: University of Chicago Press, 1982).


110  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
a “characteristic example” of a broader trend that he wants to criticize.2
Indeed, prior to even mentioning Arendt, Badiou identifies his real target
as “political philosophy”, the central operation of which he describes as
the effort “to restore politics … to the exercise of ‘free judgment’ in a
public space where, ultimately, only opinions count”.3 For this reason,
we can reasonably extend Badiou’s criticism to Perelman to the extent
that Perelman’s position shares a number of salient features with that of
Arendt.4 For our purposes, the convergence of two features will suffice: (1)
both Arendt and Perelman are committed to a similar kind of pluralism. By
this, we mean simply that they both start from the premise that questions
of human action (what Arendt calls “politics”) originate from the differ­
ences that exist among any multiplicity of individuals or groups of indi­
viduals. (2) Following from this premise, both Arendt and Perelman are
concerned with the opinions of these individuals, as these opinions serve
as the raw material of debate, dialogue, and decision-making. In what fol­
lows, we argue that these two features are sufficient to charitably extend
Badiou’s critique of sophistry to Perelman.
The Arendtian Spectator of Politics
Before unpacking Badiou’s argument against “political philosophy”, let us
briefly retrace our discussion of Badiou from Chapter 1. To recall, Badiou
argues that philosophy does not itself produce truths. Rather, philosophy
is always conditioned by truths—whether it is aware of it or not. These
truths first appear as “events” within the four domains of science, love, art,
and politics. These events trigger the possibility for the realization of new
truths through a process of “subjectivation”. Through this process individ­
uals become subjects by virtue of their struggle to incorporate the emergent
truth into the world. To these subjects, these “militants of truth”, falls the
task of remaining faithful to the event. For Badiou, the task of the philoso­
pher is to construct a space in which the truths from all of philosophy’s
four conditions can systematically coexist in thought.
2  Badiou, “Against ‘Political Philosophy’”, Metapolitics, trans. Jason Barker (London: Verso,
1998), 11/“Contre la ‘philosophie politique’”, Abrégé de métapolitique (Paris: Éditions du
Seuil, 1998), 20.
3  Badiou, MT, 11/AM, 19–20.
4  Comparisons of the work of Arendt and Perelman include: David A. Frank, “The New
Rhetoric, Judaism, and Post-enlightenment Thought: The Cultural Origins of Perelmanian
Philosophy”, Quarterly Journal of Speech, Vol. 83, No. 3 (1997), 311–331; Frank, “1958
and the Rhetorical Turn in 20th-Century Thought”; Michelle Bolduc, “The New Rhetoric
Project as a Response to Anti-Semitism: Chaïm Perelman’s Reflections on Assimilation”,
Journal of Communication and Religion, Vol. 29, No. 2 (2016), 22–35.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  111
From this picture it follows that the philosopher can no longer claim
the position of impartial spectator, standing at a distance from what she
claims to think or reflect on. To say that philosophy is conditioned by
truths is to say that a philosophy is always committed (or “engaged”, in
the Sartrean sense) in one respect or another to the truths that condition
it, whether positively or negatively. For this reason, Badiou is opposed to
what he derisively calls the “academic division” of philosophy into sup­
posedly objective domains—epistemology, aesthetics, philosophy of mind,
political philosophy, etc. In Metapolitics, Badiou takes issue with political
philosophy in particular and argues for an alternative way of conceiving
the relation between philosophy and politics.
Badiou’s critique of political philosophy does not so much target what
political philosophers believe or where they stand on particular questions as
the very construal of the domain itself—“the political”. As Bruno Bosteels
explains, the tradition of political philosophy Badiou is criticizing “relies
on the idea that it can form a clear and distinct idea—or, better yet, a judg­
ment—about what constitutes the essence of politics, or, at the very least,
about what should constitute the essence of politics, properly speaking”.5
This tradition, which runs through Aristotle and Kant up to more contem­
porary political philosophers such as Claude Lefort seeks to define “the
political” as “a specific domain or invariable instance of the social order,
as distinct from the economical or the moral, for example”.6 From this
definition it then becomes possible for the political philosopher to assess
the relative merits of different kinds of political orders. Yet, regardless of
whether one prefers monarchy, anarchy, or democracy, Bosteels continues,
it remains a tacit presupposition of political philosophy “that the task of
the philosopher always consists in debating the essence of the political act
from the position of an outside observer or belated spectator”.7 Political
philosophers thus set themselves up at a distance from the events and
truths that, for Badiou, are what constitute particular instances of politics.
According to Badiou, Arendt’s work is a forceful representative of
“political philosophy” in this sense.8 In Arendt, politics is neither the
5  Bruno Bosteels, Badiou and Politics (Durham, NC: Duke University Press, 2011), 19.
6  Bosteels, Badiou and Politics, 19.
7  Bosteels, Badiou and Politics, 19.
8  Arendt would certainly object to this label. As Arendt famously puts it in her 1964 inter­
view with Günter Gaus: “I do not belong to the circle of philosophers. My profession, if
one can even speak of it at all, is political theory”. She continues: “The expression “political
philosophy, which I avoid, is extremely burdened by tradition” (Arendt, Essays in Under­
standing 1930–1954: Formation, Exile, Totalitarianism, ed. Jerome Kohn (New York,
NY: Schocken Books, 1994), 1–2). On the significance of these remarks for interpretating
Arendt’s work, see Sophie Loidolt, Phenomenology of Plurality: Hannah Arendt on Politi­
cal Intersubjectivity (New York, NY: Routledge, 2018), 1–2.


112  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
name of a thought nor the name of an action. To Badiou’s consterna­
tion, Arendt’s conception of politics seems concerned only with “public
opinion”.9 Although Arendt would not herself put it this way, Badiou’s
observation is based on Arendt’s identification, following Kant, of the
subject of politics as the “spectator” rather than the “actor”. As Arendt
explains:
what counted in the French Revolution, what made it a world-histor­
ical event, a phenomenon not to be forgotten, were not the deeds and
misdeeds of the actors but the opinions, the enthusiastic approbation,
of spectators, of persons who themselves were not involved. We also
saw that these uninvolved and non-participating spectators—who, as it
were, made the event at home in the history of mankind and thus for all
future action—were involved with one another (in contradistinction to
the Pythagorean spectator at the Olympic games or the spectators in the
Platonic cave, who could not communicate with one another).10
The French Revolution was thus not a “truth-event” in Badiou’s sense; it
was not the becoming-possible of what was previously thought impossible
through the militant engagement of the actors. For Arendt, the significance
of the French Revolution is a retroactive consequence of the opinions of its
spectators. To be fair, Arendt’s point here is that despite their non-involve­
ment in the event, the spectators are nonetheless involved with one another
precisely through their judgment of what happened. It is not as though
each spectator is locked in their own subjectivity and prevented from com­
municating with others like the prisoners in the cave. To the contrary,
Arendt writes, “Judgment … always reflects upon others and their taste,
takes their possible judgments into account. This is necessary because I am
human and cannot live outside the company of men”.11 Yet, for Badiou,
this pluralism in judgment is not enough; the identification of the spectator
as the subject of politics precludes the possibility of thinking politics as a
truth procedure; lost is the connection between militant subjects, the actors
who are actually involved. Put differently, it leaves the spectators confined
to the sphere of opinion, with a sophistic politics.
Metapolitics and Pluralism
By contrast, Badiou wants to defend the singularity of politics [la poli­
tique]—that is, politics as a truth procedure and not something that
  9  Badiou, ME, 13/AM, 22.
10  Arendt, Lectures on Kant’s Political Philosophy, 65, first emphasis ours.
11  Arendt, Lectures on Kant’s Political Philosophy, 67.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  113
happens at a distance. Rather than rely upon the ontic-ontological dif­
ference between politics and the political as Arendt does, Badiou distin­
guishes between politics and “metapolitics”. As he explains:
By “metapolitics” I mean whatever consequences a philosophy is capa­
ble of drawing, both in and for itself, from real instances of politics as
thought. Metapolitics is opposed to political philosophy, which claims
that since no such politics exists, it falls to philosophers to think “the”
political.12
The question posed by metapolitics is thus not, “What is the essence of
politics?” but, “Can politics be thought?”—to borrow the title of Badiou’s
1985 book.13 To ask about the essence of politics (or, more precisely, the
political) is to set politics up as an objective datum, an object of philosoph­
ical speculation. To ask whether politics can be thought, by contrast, is to
ask how philosophy is conditioned by a politics, that is, a specific instance
of politics whose consequences must be taken up into thought.
This difference can be helpfully illustrated with reference to Badiou’s
The Century [Le siècle] (2005). Here, Badiou does not ask the historical
question, “What took place in the 20th century?”, but the philosophi­
cal one, “What was thought in it?”. For Badiou, the thought of a poli­
tics is accessible only from the perspective of what Arendt would call the
“actors”. In his discussion of Nazism, Badiou spells out the implications of
remaining only at the level of the spectator:
There is a way of always leading everything back to what the Nazis did
… that completely precludes any access to what they thought, or imag­
ined they were thinking, in doing what they did. But refusing to think
through what the Nazis themselves thought also prevents us from think­
ing through what they did, and consequently forbids the formulation of
any real politics that would prohibit the return of their actions. As long
as Nazi thinking is not itself thought through it will continue to dwell
among us, unthought and therefore indestructible.14
It is thus not with the aim of judging “the century as an objective datum,
but rather to ask how it has come to be subjectivated” that Badiou
finds Arendt’s preference for the spectator-view problematic—or, better
12  Badiou, MT, xxxix/AM, 7.
13  Badiou, Can Politics Be Thought?, trans. Bruno Bosteels (Durham, NC: Duke University
Press, 2018)/Peut-on penser la politique ? (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1985).
14  Badiou, The Century, trans. Alberto Toscano (Cambridge: Polity, 2007), 3-4/Le siècle
(Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 2005), 13–14.


114  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
perhaps, one-sided.15 Unlike political philosophy, metapolitics tries to
think the consequences of real instances of politics (whether positive or
negative). By limiting one’s purview only to the opinions of the spectators,
one loses any connection to the subject of a politics and the content of its
thought. For Badiou, it will not be the “memory” of what the Nazis did
that will prevent its repetition, but thoroughly thinking through what they
themselves thought.16
Arendt’s preference for the spectator thus transforms politics into an
object of reflection, an invariable element of human experience.17 Once
secured, it conveniently falls to the philosopher to think this object and,
ultimately, submit it to ethical norms. For Badiou, this attitude puts the
philosopher in a very privileged and ultimately self-serving position: the
philosopher becomes the thinker of the confused experience of politics;
the philosopher becomes the one who determines the principles of “good”
politics, namely, a politics that conforms to ethical principles; and, finally,
the philosopher becomes exempt from “any militant involvement in any
genuine political process”.18 In this way, Badiou continues in his Lacanian
jargon, “the Real is kept at arm’s length in the form of judgment”.19
Badiou summarizes his critique of Arendt’s “sophistic” position (and
political philosophy more generally) in five related points:
	(1)	 There is no simple plurality but only a plurality of pluralities (or, in
Badiou’s terms, a “multiplicity of multiplicities”). The apparently neu­
tral gesture of ontologizing politics masks a commitment to an entirely
particular politics, a “democratic” or “parliamentary” politics. This
commitment comes to serve as a model for “the political”, as the space
in which opinions are exchanged and judgments are freely formed.
Ironically, Arendt’s pluralism repeats, at a lower level, the very prob­
lem with Platonism that it tries to overcome: beneath the egalitarian
appearance of plurality lies a hidden monism, a “one” beneath the
15  Badiou, The Century, 5/Le Siècle, 16.
16  This reference to memory is likely an allusion to Paul Ricoeur’s La mémoire l’histoire,
l’oubli [Memory, History, Forgetting] (2000), which Badiou had recently reviewed in
2003. See Badiou, “The Subjected Supposed to Be a Christian”, The Adventure of French
Philosophy, 321-336/“Le sujet supposé chrétien de Paul Ricoeur”, L’aventure de la phi­
losophie française, 81–98. Cf. Paul Ricoeur, La mémoire l’histoire, l’oubli Paris: Éditions
du Seuil, 2000)/Memory, History, Forgetting, trans. Kathleen Blamey and David Pellauer
(Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 2004).
17  Badiou, MT, 10/AM, 19.
18  Badiou, MT, 10/AM, 19.
19  Badiou, MT, 10/AM, 19.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  115
“many”, which invests the space of plurality with a “transcendental
legitimacy”.20
	(2)	 The insistence on the care or maintenance of the political space of
appearances is ultimately too conservative. This way of thinking
reduces politics to the avoidance of evil, the threat of which serves as
the only motivation for political judgment.21 (One can think here of
the expression “the lesser of two evils”, frequently heard around elec­
tion time). Within plurality, as Arendt conceives of it, there is no out­
side from which competing opinions can be measured. It thus falls to
the “one” of common sense to arbitrate competing claims in the form
of an ethics. For Badiou, by contrast, there are “only plural instances
of politics, irreducible to one another, and which do not comprise any
homogenous history”.22
	(3)	 By conceiving the political in ontological terms, as a universal dimen­
sion of human experience (or as something lost and in need of recov­
ery), Arendt’s vision of politics is hostage to consensus. For Badiou,
this vision neglects the “evental” nature of politics. Although the
truths gathered from events are in principle universal, an event’s sta­
tus as an event is never universally shared; recognizing an event as an
event is always a subjective decision. As Badiou explains, “the point
from which a politics can be thought … is that of its actors and not
its spectators”.23 It is thus only through Saint-Just and Robespierre,
he adds, that one can enter the singular truth unleashed by the French
Revolution, and not through Kant or François Furet.
	(4)	 Opinions are never neutral but always conform to a particular poli­
tics. What Badiou calls “real plurality” is characteristic of the plu­
ral instances of different kinds of politics, whereas the plurality of
opinions in Arendt’s sense refers only to one of those instances.24 For
Badiou, there can be no neutral observer of the domain of the political
as such. This implies that Arendt’s position is already conditioned by a
20  Badiou, MT, 16/AM, 16.
21  Badiou explores this point, specifically in relation to Emmanuel Levinas, in more detail
in Ethics: An Understanding of Evil, trans. Peter Hallward (London: Verso, 2012),
8-10/L’éthique. Essai sur la conscience du Mal (Paris: Hatier, 1993), 10–12. Badiou’s
point brings to mind Google’s recently abandoned corporate maxim, “Don’t be evil”.
(In our view, it is unclear what is more alarming: that this phrase was adopted in the
first place or that it was subsequently abandoned.) Cassin examines this maxim herself
in chapter four of Google Me: One-Click Democracy, trans. Michael Syrotinski (New
York, NY: Fordham University Press, 2018), 64-90/Google-moi. La deuxième mission de
l’Amérique (Paris: Éditions Albin Michel, 2007).
22  Badiou, MT, 23/AM, 33.
23  Badiou, MT, 23/AM, 33.
24  Badiou, MT, 24/AM, 34.


116  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
particular politics, a parliamentary politics, concerned more with the
circulation of opinions than with truth.
	(5)	 Politics is not about protecting the plurality of opinions but prescrib­
ing the possibility of something new in relation to what exists. While
Badiou acknowledges that debate and dialogue are necessary to test
and exercise political prescriptions, they are not ends in themselves.25
By remaining within the sphere of what already is, of what is already
known, dialogue and debate are inescapably contaminated by the pre­
suppositions of and a preference for the status quo.
These five points bring us to the question of how Badiou’s critique of
Arendt maps onto Perelman and the NRP. In a certain sense Perelman’s
thinking about politics is considerably less detailed than Arendt’s. It is
thus not necessary here to try and fit Arendt’s and Perelman’s thought into
the same box. Fortunately, however, no such gymnastics are required. For
our argument to hold we simply need to establish that Perelman’s position
shares the same key features as those criticized by Badiou. As we have
already indicated, these two features are (1) Perelman’s commitment to a
similar, if not farther-reaching, pluralism, and (2) his concern with protect­
ing the philosophical importance of opinion. Although his own intellectual
project differs from Arendt in many respects, we shall see the importance
of these two features in Perelman's work in the following three sections.
What Kind of Relativist (if any) Is Perelman?
Philosophical Pluralism
From his earliest writings it is clear that Perelman is motivated by a pro­
found commitment to pluralism. In his 1933 article “On the Arbitrary
in Knowledge”, for example, written when he was only 21, we already
find Perelman arguing for the importance of “tolerance between groups”.26
Although there were many good political reasons for this to be emphasized
in 1930s Europe, Perelman’s plea for pluralistic tolerance is here defended
on strictly philosophical grounds, in the context of a philosophical exami­
nation of knowledge. Indeed, in this text tolerance is for Perelman “the
most immediate practical consequence” of recognizing the necessary role
of the arbitrary in the process of acquiring knowledge.27 In this context, the
25  Badiou, MT, 24/AM, 34.
26  Perelman, “On the Arbitrary in Knowledge”, ICO, 62/“De l’arbitraire dans la connais­
sance”, Archives de la Société Belge de la Philosophie, No. 3 (1933), 44. In their Introduc­
tion to this text, Bolduc and Frank trace the origins and influences of Perelman’s pluralism
(among other topics) back to his largely neglected pre-war writings.
27  Bolduc and Frank, ICO, 33.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  117
“arbitrary” refers to the set of contingent, historically transmitted mean­
ings, values, and notions used by a particular social group. If all knowledge
depends upon the mediating role of these arbitrary elements, there can
be no absolute frame of reference that would encompass all truth claims.
From this it follows that philosophers cannot do away entirely with the
