sphere of the arbitrary: it is not an obstacle to knowledge but a necessary
gateway to it. On this view, there is no contradiction between the pursuit
of knowledge and the recognition that knowledge may take various forms
in relation to the “arbitrary” of a given social group. For Perelman, the
tolerance of value differences is therefore a necessary philosophical virtue.
As Bolduc and Frank argue, this aspiration for tolerance—as political as
it is philosophical—is the “polar star of the [new rhetoric project] and of
the [Treatise]”.28
It is not until his later writings, however, well after his and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s “discovery” of rhetoric and the publication of the Treatise, that
Perelman would begin to write more explicitly about the philosophical
pluralism underlying his work. An exemplary text in this regard is his 1977
essay “The Philosophy of Pluralism and the New Rhetoric”. This essay
opens by introducing the contrasting notions of “pluralism” and “mon­
ism”, both of which Perelman regards as “confused”. Rather than a term
of abuse, a “confused notion” for Perelman is a term whose meaning and
scope fluctuate in relation to different contexts of use.29 Beginning with
monism, Perelman briefly discusses four kinds—axiological, ontologi­
cal, methodological, and sociological—which will later be understood as
expressions of the same philosophical impulse. (1) Axiological monism,
Perelman explains, is the view that conflicts of value can be reconciled by
reducing the conflict to a single value, often the value of perfection, use­
fulness, or truth. (2) Similarly, the related position of ontological monism
regards conflicting claims to reality as mere aspects or appearances of a
more basic, unitary reality. (3) Methodological monism is the view that
there is but one method to follow in pursuit of truth. (4) Sociological mon­
ism, finally, understands the individual’s relation to society as something
like the individual’s relation to God.30
Although this essay was written some 40 years after “On the Arbitrary
in Knowledge”, a clear line of continuity can be detected. Perelman
remains fundamentally opposed to monism because it attempts to bypass
the de facto pluralism of modern societies rather than face the challenge
28  Bolduc and Frank, ICO, 33.
29  Perelman, “The Philosophy of Pluralism and the New Rhetoric”, NRH, 62. For Perel­
man’s most developed treatment of confused notions, see “The Use and Abuse of Con­
fused Notions”, JLA, 95-16/“L’usage et l’abus des notions confuses”, ED, 803–818.
30  Perelman, “The Philosophy of Pluralism and the New Rhetoric”, NRH, 62


118  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
of working through it. In this regard, Perelman draws inspiration from
the work of his maître, Belgian philosopher and sociologist Eugène
Dupréel (1879–1967), whose “sociological pluralism” forms the basis of
Perelman’s discussion in this essay.31 As Perelman explains, Dupréel’s gen­
eral sociology is based on the idea of the “social relationship” [rapport
social], which he defines in Sociologie générale (1948) as existing “between
two individuals when the existence or activity of the one influences the
acts or the psychological condition of the other”.32 In this way, Dupréel
avoids giving primacy to either the individual or the group: since social
relationships are always minimally dyadic, social phenomena can never be
reduced to the individuals that constitute them. Similarly, because social
relationships require the existence of some measurable influence between
individuals, social phenomena cannot simply be attributed to a faceless
and indeterminate group or supra-individual Geist.
Social relationships for Dupréel are essentially forms of interaction that
vary in kind and duration and can be characterized as either “positive” or
“negative”. As Perelman explains, social relationships are said to be “posi­
tive” when based on cooperation, consent, or agreement and “negative”
when based on opposition, conflict, or competition.33 Moreover, social
relationships can interpenetrate one another when they include a common
term. When this is the case social relationships are said to be “compli­
mentary” and serve as the basis for what Dupréel calls a “social group”.
A social group is a collection of individuals who are united together and
distinguished from other individuals through positive and complementary
social relationships.34 Examples of social groups include families, nations,
religions, sports teams, and professions.35
In terms of Dupréel’s general sociology, then, Perelman’s insistence on
the notion of pluralism is a consequence of the sociological fact that indi­
viduals are simultaneously part of many groups, some of which are com­
plementary and some of which are in conflict. As a member of each group,
individuals are beholden to a specific set of values, arranged in a more
or less determinate hierarchy. In the course of modern social life, how­
ever, these distinct sets of values increasingly come into conflict. Consider
Perelman’s example of a “conscientious objector”:
31  For a discussion of Dupréel influence on Perelman’s in this essay, see Guerrini, Les Valeurs
dans l’argumentation, 85–88.
32  Eugène Dupréel, Sociologie Générale (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 1948), 64;
NRH, 64.
33  Perelman, NRH, 64.
34  Dupréel, Sociologie Générale, 20; Perelman, NRH, 64.
35  Perelman, NRH, 64.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  119
The typical case is that of an individual who is part of both a national
group and a religious group which no longer blend in developed socie­
ties. What should he do if the national group drafts him in the army
while the religious group forbids him to kill and even sometimes to
carry arms? Faced with incompatible orders, the individual is forced to
make a choice. If he behaves as a good citizen, he will violate his reli­
gious sect’s prescriptions and vice-versa. It is with such conflict that a
conscientious objector is faced. Rather than conforming to the require­
ments of one or other of the groups in which he participates, he is often
led to take a position toward them. He will have to make comparisons,
to make judgments on the rules of the groups from the vantage point of
a value which transcends the conventions of one group or the other.36
According to Perelman, the greater frequency of such value conflicts in
increasingly diverse societies is the origin of universalist ideals and values
such as autonomy and freedom which presuppose, to some extent, that
individuals no longer wholly identify with one of the groups of which they
are a member.37
For Perelman, Dupréel’s general sociological framework helps to
explain the rise of the modern state. Perelman describes the emergence
of the “politically and legally structured state” as a “remarkable institu­
tion” which, after centuries of disorder and violence, serves to arbitrate
the conflicts that inevitably arise between individuals and groups.38 From
this perspective, an ideal state would be one that fulfils this function with­
out identifying itself with any particular interest. Yet, throughout the
20th century it was precisely this task that many states failed to fulfil. The
“totalitarian” state is thus a state that overidentifies with the interests and
aspirations of a particular social group. Philosophically speaking, a totali­
tarian state is for Perelman a relapse into monism—a monism of power.39
A pluralist state, by contrast, would be one in which there was a genuine
respect for the differences of individuals and groups. Rather than identify
with any group in particular, the function of the pluralist state would be to
moderate the excesses of particular interests by recognizing the existence
of incompatible value sets among social groups, thus striving for reason­
able compromises through permanent dialogue.40
It is for this reason that Perelman places such a premium on the impor­
tance of law. In a democratic society, Perelman argues, it is “up to the
36  Perelman, NRH, 65.
37  Perelman, NRH, 65.
38  Perelman, NRH, 66.
39  Perelman, NRH, 65–66.
40  Perelman, NRH, 67.


120  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
lawmakers, to the courts and to the jurisprudence to establish and main­
tain a balance, always delicate between legitimate claims”.41 In Perelman’s
later writings, law serves as something of a model for philosophy.42 Where
the imitation of the methods of the mathematician led to a philosophi­
cal revolution in logic, Perelman believed that the imitation of the lawyer
would lead to an analogous revolution in argumentation.43 Argumentation
is thus for Perelman the form of reason most appropriate to the resolu­
tion of conflicting values in pursuit of solutions that should aspire to be
reasonable.
Perelman thus sought a philosophical vision capable of responding to
the demands of the de facto pluralism of modern societies. Because the
monist attempts to reduce the plurality of opposing view to one particular
value, typically that of truth, they are incapable of giving due credence to
opposing positions. Does this mean, however, that the pluralist must do
away with truth and reason altogether?
Rather than commit to any kind of relativism, Perelman insists that a
novel conception of truth and reason can be wrested from the hands of the
monist. For the pluralist, Perelman argues that truth should no longer be
conflated with self-evidence, as it is only from within a particular ideologi­
cal vision that a given thesis can appear as self-evidently true. Instead, the
name “truth” should be reserved only for theses that have survived exten­
sive criticism from various perspectives and, for the time being, appear
strongest in relation to competing theses.
As for the notion of reason, pluralism no longer wants to confuse this
notion with an eternal and unchanging faculty shared by all, unaffected
by other faculties and by history. Rather, Perelman argues, the pluralist
should instead understand reason as an ideal of universality peculiar to
Western philosophy. Once this is recognized, reason can be recast as a
philosophical construction, as an ideal audience that constrains the philos­
opher to only select premises which they believe are likely to be accepted
by everyone. This rhetorical conception of reason is what Perelman calls
the “universal audience”.44 The universal audience is the audience to
which every philosopher addresses themselves “even in the absence of an
41  Perelman, NRH, 67.
42  See Perelman, “What the Philosopher May Learn from the Study of Law”, JLA, 163-
174/“Ce que le philosophe peut apprendre par l’étude du droit”, ED, 450–466.
43 “The thesis which I have defended for thirty years is that law plays a role in regard to
argumentation analogous to that of mathematics in regard to formal logic” (Perelman,
“The New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, The New Rhetoric of Chaïm Perelman, ed.
Dearin, 250).
44  We discuss the universal audience at length in Chapter 4, pp. 161–169.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  121
objectivity which imposes itself on everyone”.45 Thus construed, it follows
that reason—if it is to be reasonable and not merely rational—must prove
its universality in the crucible of dialogue and criticism.
On this diagnosis, the monist for Perelman simply jumps the gun; they
proceed from the consent of one to the consent of all, disqualifying those
who do not recognize the same theses as self-evident. The pluralist phi­
losopher, by contrast, is satisfied with presenting a view which they find
reasonable and is likely to win the agreement of a universal audience.
However imperfect in practice, built into this pluralistic vision is the neces­
sity of keeping an open dialogue with broad participation. Moreover, it
also refrains from granting any individual or group the privileged author­
ity to set up any single or fundamental criterion for what is valid or
appropriate—a privilege, Perelman warns, that can only lead to excess and
totalitarianism. In his own words:
Philosophical pluralism demands a search for moderate, and thus well-
balanced solutions to all conflicts, which it considers nevertheless as
unavoidable and recurring. Under the sign of reasonableness, pluralism
does not claim to provide the perfect, unique and final solution, but
simply human solutions—acceptable but capable of being changed and
improved—to the ever-recurring problems created by the coexistence of
men and groups, who prefer a fair compromise to the coercion imposed
in the name of a unique value, irrespective of how important or even
pre-eminent that value may be.46
The Reasonable
The notion of the “reasonable” is central to Perelman’s later thought.
Although often read back into his earlier work, Perelman explains in a
1982 letter to Marcel Côté that he only began to develop “the reason­
able” as a distinct notion from around 1977.47 The key reference here is
Perelman’s 1979 essay “The Rational and the Reasonable”. This essay was
first presented at a symposium entitled “Rationality To-day” organized by
the Department of Philosophy at the University of Ottawa in the fall of
1977. The symposium was dedicated to contemporary understandings of
“rationality” and featured interventions from fifteen speakers, including
45  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, The New Rhetoric of Chaïm Perel­
man, 244.
46  Perelman, NRH, 71.
47  Perelman, Letter to Marcel Côté, 4 January 1982 Fonds Chaïm Perelman, Archives de
l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 21.1.


122  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
Hans-Georg Gadamer, Carl Hempel, Jürgen Habermas, Paul Ricoeur, and
Karl-Otto Apel, among others.48
In “The Rational and the Reasonable” Perelman draws attention to
the difference between the two adjectives, demonstrating his affinity with
the methods of ordinary language philosophy. While both derive from the
same root (ratio), he notes that the two terms cannot be used interchange­
ably and thus preserve an important semantic nuance.49 Whereas one can
speak of a “rational deduction”, for example, one would not speak of a
“reasonable deduction”. Similarly, while it is unproblematic to speak of a
“reasonable compromise”, it would be odd to speak of a “rational com­
promise”. According to Perelman, the use of the term “rational” typically
corresponds to the ideal of mathematical reason, which, conforming to
strict principles, permits the mind to move from precise meaning to precise
meaning in a necessary and self-evident way. “Reasonable”, by contrast, is
typically used in relation to matters of action, that is, to situations where
values come into conflict, where a course of action must be chosen, and
where the outcome of that choice holds consequences for others. Thus,
whereas the rational person would be an “inhuman monster”, as Bertrand
Russell put it, the one who “knows what he wants, and moves toward it
by the straightest line”, the reasonable person is the one “who in his judg­
ments and conduct is influenced by common sense”.50
In broad strokes, then, the terms rational and reasonable map onto
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s opposition between demonstration and
argumentation in the Treatise. Reasonableness therefore draws from a
distinctive semantic network. It is related to notions such as choice, deci­
sion, criticism, and justification. As Perelman explains in “Reflections on
Practical Reason” (1970), arguments are typically used in two kinds of
contexts, contexts of criticism and contexts of justification, which are
essentially two sides of the same coin. When criticized, we argue to justify
48  It is noteworthy that the colloquium consisted of an all-male lineup and no one from
outside of Europe or North America.
49  In response to a question from Kai Nielsen about whether this distinction might only be
an accidental feature of English, Perelman helpfully clarifies his interest in language: “I
am not a linguist, nor a grammarian, and I’m not interested in language as such. For me
language is only a tool to give you insights. In every discipline, especially in the philo­
sophical ones, we adapt language to all needs … For me, meanings are not sacrosanct,
because language is not sacrosanct: language is a tool, I use it as suggestive, and I begin
by that and see how we could use it and adapt it to all philosophical, legal and other
needs. That’s all” (Perelman, “The Rational and the Reasonable”, Rationality To-Day/
La Rationalité aujourd’hui, ed. Theodore F. Geraets (Ottawa: The University of Ottawa
Press, 1979), 222).
50  Perelman, “The Rational and the Reasonable”, NRH, 118. The passage to which Perel­
man is referring here can be found in Brand Blanshard, Reason and Goodness (Milton
Park: Routledge, [1961] 2002), 410–411.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  123
our conduct to others (an audience). When the conduct of others seems to
violate our normative expectations, we offer criticisms that are intended to
bring to light an incompatibility of values. Justification, therefore, presup­
poses the criticism of an audience while criticism is a demand for justifica­
tion from an audience. Thus, unlike demonstration, which strives to be
“rational” in the strict sense, argumentation—whether used in a critical or
justificatory way—strives to earn the adherence of an audience by offering
reasons that support the reasonableness of the conclusion.
Yet, how exactly does Perelman understand this rather vague term?
And how exactly can it be applied to particular cases? Although Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca tend to think that theories of argumentation should
remain neutral on normative questions, as we argued in Chapter 2, their
theory does not at all neglect the normative dimension of argumentative
experience. The notion of the reasonable is a case in point. For Perelman,
the reasonable is first and foremost a “social concept” and serves as a con­
straint that is neither strictly necessary nor completely arbitrary.51 In argu­
mentation, interlocutors are never merely articulating their own point of
view; they are also always (even if tacitly) expressing the reasonable opin­
ion of their social milieu. The reasonable is thus a social concept because
any change in the opinions of the interlocutors may “bring about an exten­
sion or modification of the field of the reasonable”.52
There are two related ideas in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work
that can help us further understand this notion of the reasonable: (1) the
rule of justice and (2) the principle of inertia.
(1) The rule of justice—At first glance, the “rule of justice” appears to
be the only thing Perelman proposes that resembles an evaluative crite­
rion.53 When reading the Treatise, however, one must tread carefully to
avoid misinterpreting the meaning and scope of this notion. In §97, where
the strength of arguments is discussed, the rule of justice is presented as a
“hypothesis” to explain the observation that in argumentative situations
51  In response to a second question from Kai Nielsen about the possible relativism lurking
in his notion of the reasonable, Perelman responds by saying: “what is reasonable is, in
more or less broad lines, accepted by common opinion in a given society. This is a social
concept. It’s not a subjective concept. You cannot just say yourself: I find that unreason­
able, the others don’t find it unreasonable. There’s something in this concept that doesn’t
permit you to use it in a completely arbitrary way” (Perelman, “The Rational and the
Reasonable”, Rationality To-Day/La Rationalité aujourd’hui, 222).
52  Perelman, “The Dialectical Method and the Part Played by the Interlocutor in Dialogue”,
JPA, 167/“La méthode dialectique et le rôle de l’interlocuteur dans le dialogue”, RH, 55.
53  As J. Anthony Blair rightly appreciates, “Perelman holds the surprising view that argu­
ments are to be assessed by using the Rule of Justice” (Blair, “Perelman Today on Justice
and Argumentation”, Groundwork in the Theory of Argumentation: Selected Papers of J.
Anthony Blair (Dordrecht: Springer, 2012), 301).


124  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
“a practical distinction is made between strong arguments and weak
ones”.54 In other words, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are hypothesizing
that the rule of justice is the technique by which persons in situ actually
go about appraising or evaluating the relative strengths and weaknesses of
arguments. When viewed in context, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are
not so much prescribing the rule of justice to us, but rather identifying it as
a technique already at work in the way that we engage with one another
in argument.
This interpretation is further supported by the way in which Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca discuss the rule of justice earlier on. In §52 of the
Treatise it is first introduced as but one argumentative technique among
others under the category of “quasi-logical arguments”.55 Here, the rule of
justice is defined as “giving identical treatment to beings or situations of
the same kind”.56 As an argumentative technique, the rule of justice can be
understood as a kind of appeal to consistency: since we did X in situation
Y, we ought to do X again, given that situation Z is essentially similar to
situation Y. Yet, as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca point out, the short­
coming of this and other quasi-logical techniques is that they assume a
sufficient degree of common adherence between parties. In this case, the
rule of justice requires prior agreement about when it is the case that situ­
ations are essentially similar: “The rule, which is purely formal, requires,
for its application, a foundation in the concrete, anchored to opinions and
agreements which are rarely beyond argument”.57 Here the rule of justice
is discussed as one argumentative technique among others and is given no
special status.
By the time we get to §97, then, the rule of justice is no longer being dis­
cussed at the analytical level of particular argumentative techniques, but at
a higher, more synthetic level. Here, the rule of justice is now understood
to mean: “that which was capable of convincing in a specific situation will
appear to be convincing in a similar or analogous situation”.58 When used
in this sense, the rule of justice becomes a technique by which persons
argue for or against (i.e. assess or evaluate) the quality of an extended
process of argumentation based on what has proved persuasive or con­
vincing in the past. The rule of justice thus becomes an argument about
54  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 464/TA, 616.
55  To recall, quasi-logical arguments are those that most closely resemble demonstrative
reasoning. These argumentative techniques thus presuppose a relatively high degree of
adherence to get off the ground. Unlike argumentation, demonstrative methods of reason­
ing presuppose full adherence to the axioms and rules of a given logical system. Argumen­
tation enjoys no such guarantee.
56  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §52, 218/TA, 294.
57  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §52, 220/TA, 297.
58  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 464/TA, 616.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  125
an argument—or, in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s terms, not an argu­
ment occurring in the discourse, but an argument about the discourse.59
Here, the rule of justice is thus not so much a prescription about how
arguments ought to be evaluated but an observation about the techniques
by which the “practical distinction” between strong and weak arguments
is expressed.
(2) The principle of inertia—Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca provide a
rationale for the above observation about the rule of justice in §52 of the
Treatise. “The reasonableness of this rule and the validity that it is recog­
nized as having”, they explain, “derive from the principle of inertia, from
which originates in particular the importance that is given to precedent”.60
The “principle of inertia” aims to capture what is “precedential” in expe­
rience, that is, the way in which what is normal, habitual, or customary
comes to take on a certain value over and against what is not. As Perelman
puts it in “Reflections on Practical Reason” (1970):
If we hold an opinion, it is reasonable to hold to it and it is not reason­
able to give it up without a reason. This principle of inertia is the basis
of the stability of our spiritual and social life and explains the constant
recourse to precedent when we act. To say that we follow precedent is
the same as saying that we adopt an attitude that needs no justification
because it only applies the rule of justice which treats essentially similar
situations in the same way.61
This principle thus seeks to capture a certain stability in argumentative expe­
rience and provides the experiential basis for what audiences consider rea­
sonable. This idea is further developed in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
1958 essay “On Temporality as a Characteristic of Argumentation”, where
they expand on the essentially temporal character of argumentation. Here,
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca describe how previously successful argu­
ments sediment over time, becoming “types of precedents whose value has
been recognized because of their success”; they become “examples and
59  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 460/TA, 610. In spite of this language, we
would argue that argument criticism or evaluation should be conceived not as a separate
kind of activity but as a re-opening of and a new contribution to the same argumenta­
tive situation. To evaluate an argument is thus to insert oneself into the situation from
which it emerged. The view that argument evaluation should be modelled on the detached
spectator or argument analyst rather than the engaged participant is a hangover from
the demonstrative paradigm, where a clear separation can be made between a logical
language and its metalanguage.
60  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §52, 218–219, our emphasis/TA, 294.
61  Perelman, “Reflections on Practical Reason”, NRH, 131/“Considérations sur la raison
pratique”, ED, 421.


126  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
models that we can consider reliable”.62 It is thus with reference to the rela­
tive stability provided by this inertia that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
explain “the application of the rule of justice to things that succeed one
another in time” and how we attempt to treat “new situations and those
that we have already encountered in the same manner”.63
What Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are describing here is the social
constitution of the reasonable. This becomes more evident in The Realm of
Rhetoric where, discussing the same relationship between the rule of jus­
tice and the principle of inertia, Perelman explains that, “Customs are born
in this way and give a normative value to a habitual course of action”.64
Moreover, in this later reiteration the term “reasonable” now appears
alongside the discussion of argument strength: “In evaluating [arguments]
it is normal to call upon the rule of formal justice which considers as just
and reasonable the treatment of essentially similar situations in the same
manner”.65 In this context, it is clear that the reasonable is understood as a
constraining force that provides a sense of the space in which arguers can
safely move without violating the normative expectations of their audi­
ence. When this threshold is crossed, arguers become vulnerable to criti­
cism, in which case they become obliged, lest they appear unreasonable, to
justify their deviation from what is normal, habitual, or customary.
Although the principle of inertia helps to account for the experiential
basis of the reasonable and its expression in the rule of justice, it is still not
always clear what reasonableness actually means for Perelman in the con­
text of argumentation. Without getting clear on this point, any invocation
of reasonableness is unlikely to be convincing to someone in search of a
more durable standard. Is being reasonable, then, merely a necessary crite­
rion for serious consideration? Or, does it mean something more, namely,
that a particular argument is in fact the strongest? We find that there is
more evidence for the former in Perelman’s essays.
In “Disagreement and Rationality” (1966), for example, Perelman
traces his emphasis on the idea of “reasonable disagreement” and its
importance for philosophy by taking inspiration from Jewish Talmudic
thought. Here, Perelman discusses a controversial disagreement between
the schools of Hillel and Shammai. Regardless of which interpretation was
correct, Perelman observes something that would be rather atypical in the
rationalist tradition of Western philosophy:
62  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, “On Temporality as a Characteristic of Argumentation”,
ICO, 192/RH, 387.
63  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, ICO, 192, translation modified/RH, 387.
64  Perelman, RR, 66/ER, 93.
65  Perelman, RR, 140, our emphasis/ER, 175.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  127
What is remarkable about this disagreement is the fact that the School
of Hillel was not invoked to disregard the School of Shammai’s inter­
pretation, or to show its falsity or its irrationality. Between the two
opposed interpretations, both are seen as equally reasonable; we will
choose, but not on the basis of the falsity or irrationality of the one or
the other.66
As we saw in Chapter 2, this is why Perelman argues that philosophers must
abandon the Cartesian idea that disagreement is always a sure sign of error.
For Perelman, then, the reasonable works something like the bouncer at
a bar. It does not have the power to bestow an argument with any special
status but only to let it in or turn it away. It is merely an argument’s ticket
of entry for serious consideration—even if there is an inevitable degree of
arbitrariness (in Perelman’s sense) to what is reasonable at any given time.
For evidence of this, consider the following passages, one from “Authority,
Ideology, and Violence” (1968) and the second from “The Use and Abuse
of Confused Notions” (1978):
when we are concerned with action, knowing what is just or unjust,
good or bad, what to encourage or forbid, are there objectively control­
lable criteria? Can we speak of objective truth when we are concerned
with decisions, choices and preferable conduct? ... Personally, I think
there is a role for practical reason but that it is purely negative: it allows
us to discard unreasonable solutions.67
even if one does not always agree on the manner in which to act in a
given situation, for several solutions can be equally reasonable, there
normally exists, in a human community at any given moment, general
agreement about what would be unreasonable and, in consequence,
unacceptable or intolerable.68
Perelman’s emphasis here is clearly on the negative. To say that an argu­
ment is reasonable is not to say anything positive about it other than that it
is not unreasonable. Owing to the principle of inertia and social precedent
established by repeated application of the rule of justice, we are typically in
a better position to dismiss arguments and proposals as unreasonable than
to positively identify which is best.
66  Perelman, “Disagreement and Rationality”, NRH, 113/“Désaccord et rationalité des deci­
sions”, ED, 428–429.
67  Perelman, “Authority, Ideology and Violence”, NRH, 142, our emphasis/“Autorité, idéol­
ogie et violence”, ED, 405.
68  Perelman, “The Use and Abuse of Confused Notions”, JLA, 103-104/ED, 815.


128  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
This emphasis on the negative is indicative of the way that Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca understand the task of theory in relation to the oper­
ative argumentative norms of a given society.69 Rather than first asking
the abstract and ideal question of what all reasonable arguments should
look like, they suggest that theorists return to their only reliable guide—
argumentative experience. When arguments draw criticism from an audi­
ence, this means that the normative expectations of that audience, and the
social milieu that they embody, have been transgressed in some way or to
some degree. The fact that an argument appears unreasonable to a certain
audience is the expression of something social.70 For it is only when argu­
ments encounter resistance, criticism, and in extreme cases, violence, that
the operative norms of a social group come to the surface.
What Makes an Argument Strong?
If the reasonable is more of a threshold that qualifies an argument for con­
sideration, the question remains as to how we are to decide in situ which
course of action or which proposal is the most reasonable, that is, the one
we actually ought to choose. What does Perelman offer us in terms of mak­
ing judgments about the strengths and weaknesses of equally reasonable
arguments? Without something more, Perelman might indeed be guilty of
the sophistic relativism that worries Badiou.
We have already seen that for Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca the rule of
justice is a formal notion and therefore cannot tell us in advance whether a
particular argument will appear as strong or weak. Whether this is the case
will ultimately depend on a number of empirical factors related to the audi­
ence. The most exhaustive list of these factors can be found in The Realm
of Rhetoric. They include (1) the audience’s adherence to the argument’s
premises, (2) the pertinence or relevance of the premises, (3) the closeness
or distance the audience may have with the argument’s conclusion, (4)
opposing objections that the audience might have to the argument, and
69  Perelman is similarly reticent to offer a positive definition of “libre examen”, the liberal
principle of “free inquiry” enshrined in the first article of the constitution of the Université
libre de Bruxelles. As Perelman explains in March of 1945: “The principle of free inquiry
[libre examen] does not have to formulate in a positive way the methods it approves in
the search for truth; it oversteps its role and distorts its scope by defining itself with the
help of a specific philosophical doctrine, which, sooner or later, would degenerate into a
dogmatism contrary to its essence” (Perelman, “Libre examen et démocratie”, Moder­
nité du libre examen. Textes de Chaïm Perelman et Jean Stengers (Bruxelles: Éditions de
l’Université de Bruxelles, 2009), 54, our translation).
70  This is why in the Treatise Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca insist upon the close relation­
ship of argumentation to the social sciences, specifically psychology and sociology (Perel­
man and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 20-21/TA 27).


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  129
(5) the manner in which the premises might be refuted.71 Yet, in relation
to each of these factors, the strength of an argument remains a “function
of the audience, of its convictions, of its traditions, and of the methods of
reasoning appropriate to them”.72
However, when directly asked by philosopher Brice Parain about rel­
ativism and his criteria of strong arguments, notice that Perelman does
not point to these empirical factors, throw up his hands, and say “it all
depends on the audience”. Instead, he appeals to the rule of justice:
a strong argument is not a correct or valid argument whose constrain­
ing force would impose itself on everyone. If one could formulate it as a
deductive rule, we would reduce argumentation to demonstration […].
To determine the force of an argument, I believe that it is necessary to
refer to the rule of justice: a strong argument, in a given domain, is an
argument which can prevail over its precedents.73
Perelman’s qualification is instructive. Beyond the rule of justice, he draws
attention to the importance of a given argument’s “domain”. Since the rule
of justice involves applying the same treatment to essentially similar cases,
the problem rests entirely on what makes things “essentially similar”. For
Perelman, beyond the formalism of the rule of justice it is the domain that
supplies the content. As he explains in “Opinion and Truth” (1959):
The various cultures and the various disciplines determine what this
treatment is and work out the categories which, in such or such a field,
give precise meaning to the vague notion of “essentially similar”. What
matters and what does not, the differences that are irrelevant and those
that are decisive—none of this is settled haphazard or by some intuition,
but is defined in conformity with the existing requirements and criteria
in each vocation and in each scientific discipline. The rule of justice
furnishes the common, and purely formal, factor in rational activity.
But the content to which the rule is applied, the manner of making spe­
cific what it leaves indeterminate—these are an affair of human opin­
ions, opinions which bear the stamp of their holder’s personality and,
through it, of all his past, all his education and all the tradition which
he continues and, in case of need, improves and renews.74
71  Perelman, RR, 140/ER, 175.
72  Perelman, RR, 140/ER, 175.
73  Perelman, “L’idéal de rationalité et la règle de justice”, ED, 144, our translation, our
emphasis.
74  Perelman, “Opinion and Truth”, The Idea of Justice and the Problem of Argument, 132,
translation modified and our emphasis/“Opinion et verité”, RH, 370–71.


130  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
In this important passage, Perelman clarifies that the content to which the
rule of justice is applied comes from audiences themselves; the content
is comprised of the opinions that we hold in virtue of the various social
groups, traditions, and disciplines to which we belong and out of which
our individual identities are formed. “Opinion” here should thus not be
understood pejoratively as something epistemically deficient, for instance,
as something opposed to self-evident knowledge. Rather, opinions refer
to the total set of adherences—the facts, presumptions, values, value hier­
archies, and topics—that have been historically curated by a particular
social group, to use Dupréel’s terminology, or within a particular vocation,
domain, or discipline as Perelman puts it here. Understood in this way,
the criteria for determining whether something is “essentially similar” to
something else are to be found among the opinions of those who make up
the domain in question. Opinions are thus not only the particular views
of individuals or social groups; they are also social precedents, that is, the
“existing requirements and criteria” expected by them.
This view brings Perelman very close to the position of Stephen Toulmin
in The Uses of Argument (1958), coincidentally published the same year
as the Treatise.75 Similarly, Toulmin argues against the view that there are
universal rational standards by which to evaluate arguments, observing
that there are both “field-dependent” and “field-independent” aspects to
arguments.76 Thus, for Toulmin, the arguments found in Euclid’s Elements
would be said to belong to one field, while those used in a courtroom
would belong to another.
At first glance, Toulmin’s notion of “field” might appear quite similar
to Perelman’s notion of a “domain”. There is, however, an important dif­
ference. For Perelman, Toulmin’s recognition that standards of argument
evaluation are to a considerable degree field-dependent does not go far
enough; it does not explain who makes up these fields. What exactly are
these fields and how are they constituted? Do these fields not change, and
who decides when an argument belongs to one field rather than another?
From Perelman’s rhetorical perspective, Toulmin’s position stops short of
recognizing the essential role played by the audience. The notion of an
argument field is thus too abstract, a stand-in for what are, in fact, dif­
ferent audiences. It is audiences and the individuals who comprise them
75  Blair also recognizes that there are similarities between Perelman and Toulmin on this
point (Blair, “Perelman Today on Justice and Argumentation”, Groundwork in the theory
of argumentation, 302–303).
76 “Two arguments will be said to belong to the same field when the data and conclusions
in each of the two arguments are, respectively, of the same logical type: they will be said
to come from different fields when the backing or the conclusions in each of the two
arguments are not of the same logical type” (Stephen E. Toulmin, The Uses of Argument,
Updated ed. (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, [1958] 2003), 14).


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  131
that populate, inherit, and transform the various domains, disciplines, and
social groups that enforce a certain standard of reasonableness and fur­
nish more specific criteria for determining the strength of arguments. For
Perelman, in short, the idea of a free-floating argument field results from
an incomplete description. When we dig a little deeper, we discover that
what lies beneath a field are the standards and expectations of an audience.
Is there anything more that Perelman can offer us in relation to the
actual evaluation of arguments? Beyond reasonableness, the rule of justice,
and the domain specific criteria supplied by the audience, there is perhaps
one further clue–although it is something that, regrettably, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca do not develop in great detail. This clue begins from the
idea in §97 of the Treatise that “the strength of an argument shows itself as
much by the difficulty there is in refuting it as by its inherent qualities”.77
Rooted in Perelman’s pluralism, the idea here is that arguments cannot
be said to be strong or weak in isolation, that is, by their “inherent quali­
ties” alone. In addition to these qualities, arguments must also prove their
strength by “resisting objections”.78
But what does it mean to say that strong arguments are those that best
“resist objections”? In our view, what Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are
saying here is that what makes an argument strong is its capacity for resist­
ing objections better than competing arguments. This means that argu­
ments can only be strong (or weak) in relation to other arguments, that
is, in contrast to other arguments on the same question. As Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca are concerned with theorizing arguments as they actu­
ally occur in definite places and times, calling an argument strong requires
looking beyond the argument itself and taking the broader argumentative
situation into consideration.79 Far from an empty appeal to “context”, this
means that one must look to any and all audiences who have supplied,
or could supply, objections or competing arguments. Whether actual or
potential, objections and opposing arguments form the evaluative hori­
zon of any single argument. Phenomenologically, this is why arguments
always appear as “more or less strong, more or less relevant, more or less
convincing”.80
Although the term would not be coined for several decades, these pas­
sages suggest that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s position on argument
77  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 461, our emphasis/TA, 611.
78  Perelman and& Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §97, 461/TA, 611.
79  The richest account of the “total argumentative situation” is that of Tindale. For him,
the six elements that comprise this situation are: (1) the audience, (2) the arguer, (3) the
argument, (4) time, (5) place, and (6) mode. See, Tindale, The Anthropology of Argument:
Cultural Foundations of Rhetoric and Reason (New York, NY: Routledge, 2021), 21.
80  Perelman “The Philosophy of Pluralism and the New Rhetoric”, NRH, 69.


132  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
strength is a form of what some philosophers call “contrastivism”81 (or
sometimes “comparativism”82—a label we saw Cassin herself adopt in
Chapter 1).83 As Scott Aikin neatly summarizes it: “Contrastivism about
reasons is the view that all reasons do the work they do indexed to a
contrast class, so reasons function not just as reasons for something, but
rather as reasons for something as opposed to something else”.84 When it
comes to evaluating arguments, contrastivism maintains that the strength
of an argument can only be determined in relation to the “contrast class”
of competing arguments.85 In other words, before an argument can be
declared the strongest, all the relevant alternatives must first be excluded.86
Perelman’s philosophy thus affirms the ancient sophistic principle of dis­
soi logoi so prized by Dupréel: “The philosopher, like the judge, has an
interest in hearing the opposing points of view before making a decision”
and that he or she “must always be ready to listen to the objections which
could be proposed to him and to take them into account”.87
Yet, even if Perelman’s views find support in contemporary trends in epis­
temology, philosophers like Badiou are unlikely to be persuaded. Indeed,
however admirable Perelman’s unwavering pluralism, Badiou would insist
that his position remains relativistic at its core.88 Many sympathetic to
81  On the use of this term, see Walter Sinnott-Armstrong, “A contrastivist manifesto”, Social
Epistemology, Vol. 22, No. 3 (2008): 257–270; Martijn Blaauw, ed., Contrastivism in phi­
losophy (New York, NY: Routledge, 2013); Justin Snedegar, Contrastive reasons (Oxford:
Oxford University Press, 2017). For a discussion of some similarities between Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s position and contrastivist trends in contemporary argumentation
theory, see Scott, ”What Makes an Argument Strong? Contrastivism in the New Rhetoric,
Informal Logic, Vol. 44, No. 1 (2024), 38-39.
82  On the use of this term, see Ruth Chang, “Contrastivism: The grounds of rational choice”,
in Weighing reasons, ed. Errol Lord and Barry Maguire (Oxford: Oxford University Press,
2016). It is worth noting that Harold Zyskind already used the term “comparative” to
describe Perelman’s position on justification in his 1979 introduction to The New Rheto­
ric and the Humanities (Perelman, NRH, xvi).
83  See Chapter 1, pp. 42–43.
84  Scott Aikin, “Argumentative adversariality, contrastive reasons, and the winners-and-los­
ers problem”, Topoi, Vol. 40, No. 5 (2021), 838.
85  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca account for both synchronic and diachronic dimensions
of the contrast class: synchronically, the contrast class includes relevant objections or
competing arguments within a given domain; diachronically, it includes existing argumen­
tative precedent established through the repeated use of the rule of justice in combination
with the principle of inertia.
86  The difficulty here is of course determining when all of the relevant alternatives have
been considered and who has the authority to say so. Here, Perelman emphasizes the
importance of institutions in making these determinations (Perelman, RR, 139/ER, 174).
87  Perelman, “What the Philosopher May Learn from the Study of Law” (1966), JLA, 173/
ED, 465.
88  Stefan Goltzberg goes no further than observing that Perelman oscillates between recogniz­
ing the existence and non-existence of strong arguments (Goltzberg, Chaïm Perelman, 89).


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  133
Perelman have struggled to avoid this conclusion. Luc Wintgens, for exam­
ple, appeals to a “meta-level” in Perelman, an underlying ethical position
“beyond which one cannot argue” as a way to prevent his pluralism from
collapsing into relativism.89 While there is undoubtedly a strong ethical
motivation behind Perelman’s philosophy and the entire NRP, it seems
unlikely that he would have wanted these ethical commitments to serve as
an underlying normative framework. As we know from the previous chap­
ter, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca wanted to avoid imposing a fixed set
of argumentative norms on all times and places. On their view, argumenta­
tion norms—whether virtuous or vicious—are constituted by communities
of arguers through the practice of arguing. Determining which argumenta­
tive norms are operative within any given social group and which ought
to be normative is not the task of the argumentation theorist alone. It is
a collaborative effort among various disciplines and, importantly, social
actors themselves.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s hesitancy to be overly prescriptive is
thus not so much an oversight as it is a consequence of their appreciation
that, in the final analysis, the criteria of argument strength are to be found
in audiences themselves—the diverse, overlapping, conflicting, and ulti­
mately nebulous collections of social groups that together constitute what
we call, in abstraction, “society”. For Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca,
then, the first question of the argumentation theorist in pursuit of the nor­
mative is not which criteria would or should convince all audiences, but
which audiences find an argument compelling and why. For in the end,
it is only “the quality of the minds which the argument has succeeded in
convincing” which allows us to make a judgment about its value.90 And
thus it is only after an exhaustive comparison of the views of all relevant
audiences that an argument can be said to be stronger than another.
3  Badiou’s Rhetoric
In our preceding discussion, we were able to go deeper into further aspects
of Perelman’s (and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s) work and emphasize two key
aspects of his normative views pertinent to our discussion. We began by
looking at his philosophical pluralism, which extends from the meaning
of individual words all the way up to entire philosophical systems. We
then turned to the essential role he ascribes to opinion. As we saw, the
normative content of argumentation is always supplied by the opinions
of specific audiences. These two features will thus allow us to charitably
89  Luc Wintgens, “Rhetoric, Reasonableness and Ethics: An Essay on Perelman”, Argumen­
tation, Vol. 7 (1993), 459.
90  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 7/TA, 9.


134  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
extend Badiou’s critique of Arendt to Perelman. On this basis, we can now
examine Badiou’s critique of Perelman’s position, which shall be our task
in the remaining two sections. Importantly, our aim here is not to criticize
Badiou in turn but to force these antithetical positions to confront one
another, identify their respective strengths, and reconfigure the very terms
of the debate.
Sophistic Democracy
Although we have presented Perelman’s position as charitably and as
forcefully as possible, from Badiou’s standpoint Perelman’s pluralism and
the importance he places on opinion leave him with little more than a well-
intentioned sophistry. Like Arendt, in Badiou’s eyes Perelman remains
bound, or “sutured”, to philosophy’s political condition. In other words,
Perelman’s philosophical position is motivated by an unacknowledged
political position, a democratic, parliamentary politics—a “democratic
philosophy”, motivated by a fear of absolutism or premature totalization
rather than any militant orientation to truths. According to Badiou, with­
out truth, or some equivalent notion, philosophical pluralism cannot break
free from the endless circulation of opinion. While Perelman acknowledges
that the “truth” or “falsity” of information can and should inform the
basis of our decisions, he qualifies this, adding “only on the condition
that these truths and facts are appreciated as a function of values and
norms which transform them into reasons for pragmatically determined
decisions and action”.91 For Perelman, then, truths must always be subor­
dinated to the values in whose service they are mobilized, and about which
there is always room for argument. And, as we have seen, these values are
always supplied by, and find their material support in, specific audiences.
For Badiou, however, Perelman’s focus on audiences commits him to an
excessive “symmetry of opinion” inherent in the very idea of argumenta­
tion. While Badiou’s own vision of politics does not deny the importance
of testing our views through argument and debate—calling the antinomy
of truth and debate a “bad joke”92—he resists following Perelman in tak­
ing these practices as the model of philosophy.
As with Arendt, Badiou would thus find Perelman equally guilty of
sophistry. To recall, Badiou describes the maxim that governs the sophistic
position as “there are no truths”—a maxim which Perelman would have
to endorse given the strong sense in which Badiou understands “truths”.
91  Perelman, “The Justification of Norms”, JLA, 109/“La justification des normes”, Les sci­
ences humaines et le problème des valeurs/Human Sciences and the Problem of Values, ed.
K. Kuypers (The Hague: Martinus Nijhoff, 1972), 49.
92  Badiou, “Against ‘Political Philosophy’”, MT, 14/AM, 24.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  135
Yet, what exactly does this difference really amount to? In the tenth chap­
ter of Conditions, “Philosophy and Politics”, Badiou further clarifies his
understanding of sophistry by referring to Plato’s “seldom mentioned” but
“crucial” definition of the sophist in Book VI of the Republic. As Badiou
explains, for Plato the sophist is the one who cannot see “the extent to
which the nature of the good and the nature of the necessary differ”.93
Here is the referenced passage to Plato in full:
Not one of those paid private teachers, whom the people call sophists
and consider to be their rivals in craft, teaches anything other than the
convictions that the majority express when they are gathered together.
Indeed, these are precisely what the sophists call wisdom. It’s as if some­
one were learning the moods and appetites of a huge, strong beast that
he’s rearing—how to approach and handle it, when it is most difficult to
deal with or most gentle and what makes it so, what sounds it utters in
either condition, and what sounds soothe or anger it. Having learned all
this through tending the beast over a period of time, he calls this knack
wisdom, gathers his information together as if it were a craft, and starts
to teach it. In truth, he knows nothing about which of these convictions
is fine or shameful, good or bad, just or unjust, but he applies all these
names in accordance with how the beast reacts—calling what it enjoys
good and what angers it bad. He has no other account to give of these
terms. And he calls what he is compelled to do just and fine, for he
hasn’t seen and cannot show anyone else how much compulsion and
goodness really differ.94
Confusing the difference between compulsion and goodness (or necessity
and truth, as Badiou prefers in Plato’s Republic (2012), his “hypertransla­
tion”), the sophist is therefore the one who identifies the meaning of the
good and the bad with the reactions of the majority.95 As far as politics
is concerned, then, it is this subordination of the good to the apparent
necessity of the community that for Badiou truly characterizes sophistry.96
Without the emancipatory potential of truths, politics becomes little more
than an effort in “managing necessity”, or at least what is perceived to
be necessary by the reigning consensus.97 Elsewhere, Badiou identifies the
93  Badiou, “Philosophy and Politics”, CN, 150/“Philosophie et politique”, CS, 219.
94  Plato, Complete Works of Plato, 493a-c, 1115, our emphasis.
95  Badiou, Plato’s Republic: A Dialogue in Sixteen Chapters, with a Prologue and an Epi­
logue, trans. Susan Spitzer (Cambridge: Polity, 2012), 191/La République de Platon. Dia­
logue en un prologue, seize chapitres et un epilogue (Paris: Fayard, 2012), 329.
96  Badiou, “Philosophy and Politics”, CN, 150/CS, 219.
97  Badiou, CN, 151/CS, 219.


136  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
now ubiquitous term “management” as the contemporary nominalist sub­
stitute for genuine politics, that is, politics as a truth procedure.98 Without
truth (or in Plato’s own words here, “goodness”) to interrupt the endless
circulation of opinion, the sophist has no means of gainsaying whatever so
happens to please or anger “the beast”.
Although Perelman introduces the idea of reasonableness and the rule
of justice to mitigate the potential excesses of his argumentative pluralism,
from Badiou’s perspective Perelman remains hostage to a consensualist or
majoritarian view of philosophy. Perelman would undoubtedly object that
he avoids the sophistic position by pointing to the quality of the audience
one is appealing to; that an argument is strong because it is recognized
as being stronger than competing arguments by a reasonable and com­
petent audience in relation to domain-specific criteria. But for Badiou it
remains the case that even among a select audience of the most reasonable
and competent, the decision will ultimately fall on the majority among
them; the view that will carry the day depends on who can most effectively
deploy argumentative techniques to gain the adherence of that specialized
audience.
Indeed, this seems undeniable given Perelman’s explicit attribution of
normative force to common sense. In his view, the burden of proof always
falls on the one who seeks to break with common sense. While Perelman
is of course concerned with accounting for and providing the means by
which that common sense can be challenged or even overruled, this con­
servative element in his thinking clearly distinguishes him from Badiou.99
This preference for common sense or what is precedential is, in Badiou’s
eyes, simply the attempt to “prop up politics with ethics”.100
98  Badiou, Saint Paul, 12/Saint Paul [FR], 13.
99  Perelman addresses the “conservative” element of his thought in his correspondences with
Letizia Gianformaggio Bastida: “You call me a militant conservative. My answer is: what
do you mean by conservative? If you mean that I prefer the argumentation of discourse to
that of violence, you are right. But I realise perfectly well that violence is, in some cases,
inevitable” (Perelman, Letter to Letizia Gianformaggio Bastida, 6 July 1973, Fonds Chaïm
Perelman, Archives de l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 22.5.). In a
subsequent letter he adds, “Nothing new can be introduced without building on the old.
To do otherwise is to be unreasonable and to seek to impose oneself only by violence”
(Perelman, Letter to Letizia Gianformaggio Bastida, 2 August 1973, Fonds Chaïm Perel­
man, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 22.5). The political importance Perelman places on argument
should thus not be confused with any kind of pacifism. As he puts it in 1949: “We no
longer have the illusions of previous centuries and we know that, even if we advocate a
return to argument, argument will not always be sufficient, either in the national commu­
nity or in the international community, and that we will continue to resort to the police­
man and the soldier” (Perelman, “Libre examen, hier et aujourd’hui”, Modernité du libre
examen, 146, our translation).
100  Badiou, “Against ‘Political Philosophy’”, MT, 19/AM, 29.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  137
Even though we have resisted this ethical interpretation ourselves,
Perelman does not provide any clear way out of the problems identified
by Badiou. It would thus seem that for fear of undermining the pluralism
and democratic spirit of his project, Perelman shies away from directly
confronting the problem that Badiou’s account of truth is meant to resolve.
In doing so, whether intentionally or not, Perelman’s position leaves him
with little to lean on but a kind of decorum, that is, the ethical question
of conducting one’s argumentation appropriately, showing due respect to
the rules and traditions of the “parliament”, whether it be a parliament
of a philosophical, political, or any other sort. Thus, for Badiou, the very
aim of Perelman’s project becomes its own downfall; it “makes philosophy
into a general rhetoric, an inventive, modern rhetoric, all that you could
want”.101
Truth’s Address
As we saw in Chapter 1, Badiou characterizes the sophistic position by
(1) its focus on meaning at the expense of a metaphysics of truth and (2)
its privileging of language as the primary site of philosophy. Perelman’s
proximity to sophistry along these lines would thus seem to be the result
of an overcorrection: by pushing back against the demonstrative, logical
paradigm of positivism, he swings too far in the opposite direction, losing
sight of the veritable gains of the sciences, specifically the new standard
of rationality established by modern mathematics. Also critical of positiv­
ism, Badiou thus proposes something of a higher synthesis: only by tak­
ing the ontological import of mathematics seriously can philosophy return
to the “grand style” it has largely abandoned. Unlike the “little style” of
the positivist programme, what Badiou calls the “grand style” conceives
of mathematics “as an absolutely indispensable condition for philosophy
as such; a condition that is at once descriptively external and prescrip­
tively immanent for philosophy”.102 Breaking with the inherent polysemy
of ordinary language, which can mean different things to different audi­
ences, what Badiou prizes most about mathematical demonstration is its
universality, the fact that it is in principle addressed to everyone. It is thus
mathematics, for Badiou as it was for Plato, that “undoes doxa and defeats
the sophist”.103
Yet, as we also saw in Chapter 1, it is curious that Badiou’s critique of
sophistry hinges on the question of addressivity. To recall, his problem with
the linguistic turn and its conception of philosophy as a “general rhetoric”
101  Badiou, HP, 102/MBR, 67.
102  Badiou, “Mathematics and Philosophy”, Theoretical Writings, 14.
103  Badiou, Theoretical Writings, 13.


138  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
is not its interest in language per se, but the limited address that follows
from taking language as the primary site of philosophy. Without recourse
to truth, this general rhetoric has no way of breaking the symmetry of
opinion other than by leaning on the “one” of common sense. Yet, even if
one shares Badiou’s concerns about pluralism—and even if one agrees that
our world is less threatened by the “primacy of rational truths” than the
“unconditioned primacy of opinions”—does one not still find in Badiou a
remarkable and highly effective range of argumentative techniques? And
are these not the very techniques described by Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca in the Treatise? Does Badiou not, for instance, attempt to establish
an association between set theory and ontology? Does he not attempt to
dissociate a new notion of truth from its more conventional, minimalistic
meaning? Indeed, does Badiou’s broader argumentative strategy against
linguistic philosophy not lean heavily on an analogy with Plato’s struggle
against the sophists, an analogy carefully tailored to Badiou’s own audi­
ence? Even without a close reading of Badiou’s argumentation we can
already begin to see that Badiou and Perelman appear to be talking past
one another when it comes to rhetoric.
Just as with Plato, then, Badiou’s critique raises the question of the
status of rhetoric in his own work. Indeed, this is something Badiou seems
aware of—even if he does not understand rhetoric in the same way as
Perelman. In the end, however, for Badiou the philosopher’s trans-his­
torical task of refuting the sophist proves too tempting to permit a more
patient and cautious reconsideration of rhetoric. Yet, for all this, Badiou
often finds himself wandering into what for Perelman, and now for us, is
familiar rhetorical territory. Here is how Badiou himself describes the phi­
losopher’s “return to the cave”:
It is as in Plato: we arrive at the Idea by leaving the cave of appear­
ances, but we must redescend into the cave to illuminate what exists on
the basis of the Idea. And we must do so, even run a certain number of
risks. It is indeed upon returning to the cave that the risk is greatest, at
the moment when you pronounce, from the point of view of what you
consider to be truths, on the world as it appears, and therefore on its
dominant ideologies.104
Even armed with truths, the philosopher must still return to the cave and
confront the dominant opinions one finds there. It is in the second moment
of this double movement that we would locate the place of Perelmanian
rhetoric in Badiou’s philosophy, the place for a rhetoric of truths. For our
104  Badiou, HP, 96/MBR, 61.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  139
argument to stick, however, more is needed than simply identifying a suit­
able place for rhetoric within Badiou’s thinking.
Reading on, Badiou quickly returns to the analogy with Plato, observ­
ing that in the Republic Plato admits that he can only provide an image of
the good. According to Badiou, “Plato starts from a philosophical experi­
ence of the Idea, but the necessity of transmitting this experience remains
for him largely exterior to the content of the experience itself”.105 In Plato,
he continues, it is socially and politically necessary for philosophy to trans­
mit truths such that people do not remain enslaved to dominant opinions.
However, it is on this point that Badiou breaks with Plato. In his own
work, specifically The Immanence of Truths (2018), Badiou will try to
avoid this problem by making the philosophical experience of truths as
transmissible as possible. As he explains in Happiness:
I try to say the maximum that can be said. I am a more affirmative and
less evasive Platonist than Plato. At least, I try! This is my conception of
philosophy: an exercise in the transmission of something we could just
as well declare impossible to transmit. In this sense, then, it is the impos­
sible proper to philosophy, its purpose, its end. I am therefore engaged
in a struggle against contemporary scepticism, cultural relativism, gen­
eralized rhetoric, exactly as Plato was engaged against the sophists. My
position is the affirmation of truth as exception, but not thereby declar­
ing it intransmissible, because that would demonstrate a considerable
weakness in the face of the dominant nihilism.106
For Badiou, then, the transmission of truth is internal to the movement
of philosophy itself. This is all the more important given Badiou’s diag­
nosis of the current state of philosophy and of the contemporary world.
If philosophy is to meet these challenges, it must be willing and able to go
beyond the private satisfaction of merely possessing truths and fulfil its
duty of sharing their universality, and in so doing find a uniquely philo­
sophical kind of happiness [bonheur].107
Without wading into the immense task of assessing Badiou’s own
account of truths, we want to focus on a more pertinent and manage­
able question: What exactly does Badiou mean by “transmission” and
how does he understand philosophy’s specific form of “addressivity”?
To answer these questions, we turn to what Oliver Feltham describes as
one of Badiou’s “most important and most neglected essays”: “What is a
Philosophical Institution? Or Address, Transmission, Inscription” (1992),
105  Badiou, HP, 109/MBR, 74.
106  Badiou, HP, 112/MBR, 76.
107  Badiou, HP, 96/MBR, 61.


140  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
the third chapter of Conditions.108 This essay is particularly important for
our argument as it is Badiou’s most detailed discussion of what could be
called—taking some liberties—his “theory of audience”.
Here Badiou attempts to determine what kind of institution would be
adequate to philosophy as he understands it. His strategy, however, differs
from a more conventional materialist approach to this question. Badiou
does not ask how philosophy is or has been shaped by its immersion in
the natural and social world. Instead, following the same line of argu­
ment as his critique of historicism, he asks what kind of institution would
be adequate to philosophy from the point of view of “philosophy’s own
determination”.109 Or, as he puts it, what he attempts is a “transcenden­
tal deduction of every possible philosophical institution”.110 Badiou thus
explains that he is less concerned with discussing real, empirical institu­
tions like colleges, universities, and academic societies than the way in
which philosophy—in the honorific, normative sense in which he speaks
of philosophy—conducts itself and becomes part of the world. In this
sense, Badiou understands an institution as a way of treating a certain
kind of “knot”, a knot whose purpose is simply to avoid coming undone.
By extension, a “philosophical institution is a procedure of conserving a
knot, a knot in danger of being cut, which would cause its components to
disperse”.111 Focusing on this rather minimal definition, he explains that a
“good” institution is one that cannot be undone, while a bad institution is
one that is fragmentary, dispersive, and parliamentary. He thus contrasts
the density or opacity of the good institution with the mere “management”
of votes, functions, and factions best exemplified by “the particularly
unphilosophical form of the colloquium”.112
What exactly is this “knot”? For Badiou, this knot is composed of
three threads, which he calls philosophy’s (1) “address”, (2) “transmis­
sion”, and (3) “inscription”. It is the knot of these three threads, each of
which holds together the other two in the fashion of Lacan’s Borromean
knot, that make up the institution of philosophy. Let us briefly clarify these
three terms before comparing how they relate to Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s concept of audience.
(1) Address—Thus far we have been using the terms “address” and
“addressivity” to refer to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s conception of
108  Oliver Feltham, “Philosophy”, Alain Badiou: Key Concepts, ed. A.J. Bartlett and Justin
Clemens (Durham: Acumen, 2010), 24.
109  Badiou, “What is a Philosophical Institution? Or Address, Transmission, Inscription”,
CN, 26/“Qu’est-ce qu’une institution philosophique ? Ou: Adresse, transmission,
inscription”, CS, 83.
110  Badiou, CN, 26/CS, 83.
111  Badiou, CN, 27/CS, 84.
112  Badiou, CN, 27/CS, 84–85.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  141
audience which we are continuing to unpack. In Badiou, however, this
term has a very specific and slightly different meaning. Like Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca, “address” for Badiou does not refer to philosophy’s
actual, empirical audience or addressee. For him, and where he differs
from the Belgians, this term refers to the subjective position of those to
whom philosophy is addressed. What characterizes philosophy’s address
in Badiou’s sense, this subjective position, is that “it is purely and simply
empty”.113 Badiou suggests that this might even be one possible definition
of philosophy: that it has no specifiable address; that there is no “real or
virtual community” to which it is directed.114 In other words, philosophy
is not addressed to anyone in particular. Through the mode of questioning,
philosophy’s address is a void, a “poste restante” which may or may not
ever find its addressee. But even if it does arrive, Badiou emphasizes that
“it was not sent to you”.115
(2) Transmission—Not to be confused with address, Badiou under­
stands philosophy’s transmission as the “operation by which [philosophy]
propagates itself through the void of its address”.116 Since it is not sent to
anyone in particular, philosophy is transmitted or perpetuated by the small
number of those who decide that it is addressed to them. Here, Badiou is
careful to point out that this small number “never make up a public or an
audience, because a public is precisely always that which answers or fulfills
an address”.117 Rather than proceed by expanding or extending its message
through an audience, Badiou suggests that philosophy’s transmission is
better understood through the figure of the “disciple”. Unlike a public or
an audience, a disciple is “one who knows that he does not form a public
or constitute an audience but supports a transmission”.118 The disciple of
philosophy is thus not so much the one who is addressed as the one who
is interpellated by a philosophy and decides to become a vehicle of its
transmission.119
113  Badiou, CN, 27/CS, 85.
114  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 85.
115  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 85.
116  Badiou, CS, 28/CS, 85.
117  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 85. It should be noted that Badiou only uses the French word “pub­
lic” and not “audience”. The latter was included by translator Steve Corcoran, probably
with the intention of clarifying the specific sense of the English word “public” in this
context. For a helpful clarification of the relation between these two confused notions (in
Perelman’s sense of “confused”), see Sonia Livingstone, “On the relation between audi­
ences and publics”, Audience and Publics: When Cultural Engagement Matters for the
Public Sphere, ed. Sonia Livingstone (Bristol: Intellect Books, 2005), 17–41.
118  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 85. As above, the term “audience” was added by the translator.
119  Here again, it is noteworthy to observe Althusser’s influence on Badiou. For a general
reading of this influence, see Feltham, “Philosophy”, Alain Badiou: Key Concepts,
13–24. For a more specific reading in relation to their politics, see Bosteels, Badiou and


142  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
(3) Inscription—Finally we come to the third thread, philosophy’s
inscription. If philosophy’s address is empty, and if its form of transmis­
sion is that of an interpellation or discipleship, inscription is that aspect of
philosophy’s institution which “turns the void of the address into a subsist­
ing mark”.120 In other words, to inscribe itself philosophy must acquiesce
to the constraints of time and space through writing—although Badiou
is careful to remind us that philosophy is something “subtracted” from
the written. By writing itself into the world, philosophy attempts to avoid
becoming identical with what is written or simply terminating in its enun­
ciation. Unlike philosophy’s empty address, and unlike its transmission
which is offered to some, it is through inscription, Badiou explains, that
philosophy offers itself to all.121
By speaking of a philosophical institution as the knot of these three
threads, Badiou is offering an account of how philosophy acquires its his­
torical reality. Thus, when these three threads fail to come together, when
tying this knot fails, we can say that there has been philosophy, but not a
philosophy.122 As Badiou explains, “Only the knot confers historicity on
philosophy’s existence. Only it decides that philosophy is there, in the form
of a particular philosophy.”123 To see this for ourselves, we can think of
a case where the thread of inscription failed to bind together address and
transmission. Take, for example, the fire set by Imperial German soldiers
that destroyed the great library at the Catholic University of Louvain (now
KU Leuven) in 1914. By some accounts the fire destroyed over 300,000
volumes and thousands of irreplaceable manuscripts.124 Now imagine that
among the ashes, forever lost to history, there was a unique philosophical
treatise which, had it survived, would have altered the course of the history
of philosophy: while it could be said of this treatise that there has indeed
“been philosophy”—there was an empty address and it could have been
transmitted by being taken up by a (self-)select few—there can no longer
be a living, historical philosophy, for the thread of inscription fell loose
from the institutional knot.
Even without going through all the ways in which the knot can come
loose and rehearsing the imperatives for philosophical institutions which
Politics, 77–109. For Badiou’s own homage to Althusser, see Pocket Pantheon: Figures of
Postwar Philosophy, trans. David Macy (London: Verso, [2008] 2009), 54–89.
120  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 85.
121  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 86.
122  Badiou, CN, 28/CS, 86.
123  Badiou, CN, 28-29/CS, 86.
124  Richard Ovenden, Burning the Books: A History of the Deliberate Destruction of
Knowledge (Cambridge, MA: Belknap Press, 2020), 109–110; see also Alan Kramer,
Dynamic of Destruction: Culture and Mass Killing in the First World War (Oxford:
Oxford University Press, 2007), 6–30.


Rhetoric on Trial (I)  143
Badiou draws from his analysis, we can already draw two conclusions from
our reading. First, this text shows that Perelman is not alone in reflecting on
the addressive structure of discourse. Even though we have not yet exam­
ined the details of Perelman’s conception of audience,125 we can already
see a certain affinity between the concerns of Badiou and Perelman, despite
clearly disagreeing about whether to call these concerns “rhetorical”. Like
the concept of audience in the NRP, Badiou’s triad of terms provides a lens
through which philosophy can be examined qua discourse, as an institu­
tion with a certain historical reality outside the mind of the philosopher.
Second, it also provides a richer vocabulary to articulate a Badiouian cri­
tique of Perelman. For Badiou, Perelman’s rhetorical vision of philosophy,
like all sophistry, would be regarded as suffering from a limited form of
address. Indeed, what should be the empty address of philosophy is seen
as a targeted address; the audience is not so much interpellated by the
idea, but by the arguer who strategically packages the idea for them. For
Badiou, by contrast, philosophy’s empty address is precisely what enables
it to be universal. By understanding the institution of philosophy in terms
of the knot of address, transmission, and inscription, Badiou is thus able
to account for both the spatio-temporal particularity of philosophy qua
discourse as well as its defining universality qua thought.
However, even if one concedes that Badiou’s “theory of audience” is
more nuanced than what we have seen of Perelman’s so far, it would not
change the fact that what interests Perelman about rhetoric—the audi­
ence—is also of great interest to Badiou. Indeed, even if Badiou is correct
to reject pluralism and the tyranny of opinion he associates with sophistry
and “general rhetoric”, it would be a mistake to overlook Badiou’s own
reflections on philosophical addressivity. We should therefore not draw
the same conclusion as he does and regard rhetoric as a dead end. As we
saw in our reading of the Treatise in Chapter 2, Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca have a very different way of conceiving rhetoric than Badiou. For
them, to take an interest in rhetoric is not necessarily to adopt a determi­
nate philosophical position. Rather, to look at philosophy rhetorically is
to look at it from the perspective of a theory of argumentation, a perspec­
tive that can identify audience-relative choices and techniques at work in
its discourse. Understood this way, not only does Badiou himself deploy a
range of argumentative techniques in his works (like all philosophers), but
he also reflects on what we call the rhetorical dimension of philosophy,
even developing a vocabulary for thinking about it.
In the end Badiou thus arrives at the same place as Plato—a highly
ambiguous relation to rhetoric. For, as Perelman never tires of repeating,
125  See Chapter 4, pp. 153–169.


144  Rhetoric on Trial (I)
although Plato indeed denigrates rhetoric as a knack for fooling the igno­
rant crowd, he also dreams of a truly philosophical rhetoric, “a rhetoric
worthy of the gods themselves”.126 This ambiguity, however, should not
lead us to reject Badiou’s position altogether. Rather than offer a critique
of Badiou, therefore, what our confrontation has brought out is the need
to reconfigure the very terms of the debate between philosophy and rheto­
ric. By following Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s more neutral view of
rhetoric, understood as a theory of argumentation, we can now see how
even the Badiouian philosopher could in good faith adopt a rhetorical per­
spective—a rhetoric of truths, but a rhetoric nonetheless.
126  Perelman, RH, 212; Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 7/TA, 9.


4
We now turn our attention back to Cassin and examine her critique of
Perelman, who represents the culmination of what she calls “philosophical
rhetoric”. According to Cassin, Perelman’s work is the best contemporary
illustration of the way that rhetoric, as philosophers understand it, sur­
reptitiously anchors performance (epideixis) and its effects to the implicit
ethics governing ontological discourse. To properly assess Cassin’s criti­
