cism of Perelman, however, we must return once again to the Treatise, this
time addressing the controversial and often misunderstood concept of the
“universal audience”, clarifying its precise meaning and scope.
As before, this chapter also proceeds in three steps. We begin with
Cassin’s critique of Perelman, which firmly places his conception of rhet­
oric within the lineage of Plato. What Cassin specifically objects to is
Perelman’s conception of the universal audience. In her view, the universal
audience imposes a regime that allows us to speak only in the rational
and universal terms of the philosopher. In a second step, we return to
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca and contextualize the universal audience
by carefully examining how they introduce it in the Treatise. This involves
looking at, first, what they call an “effective community of minds”; sec­
ond, how they define audience as a construct of the arguer; and third, the
concept of the universal audience itself. In a final step, we evaluate Cassin’s
critique in light of our reading of Perelman and the NRP more broadly.
While Cassin identifies a certain ambiguity in Perelman’s conception of the
universal audience, her rigid view of philosophy prevents any considera­
tion of an alternative and, we shall argue, more plausible interpretation
that pushes in the same direction as her sophistic logology. To this end, we
examine Cassin’s reading of J.L. Austin’s How to Do Things with Words
to show that Cassin’s own position cannot do without some conception
of audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s sense. For this reason, we
shall argue that their rhetorical concept of audience should be extended
rather than abandoned in favour of a full-fledged sophistics. To conclude,
Rhetoric on Trial (II)
Cassin v. Perelman
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-5


146  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
we end with a general summary of the arguments brought forth in our trial
and restate our verdict in favour of Perelman.
1  From Plato to Perelman
We begin by unpacking Cassin’s critique of Perelman. Unlike Chapter 3,
where we first had to establish the terms of a Badiouian critique of Perelman
through Arendt, we can proceed here directly. As we saw in Chapter 1,
Cassin views philosophical rhetoric as a line of thinking that runs from
Plato right through to Perelman. Cassin herself thus uses Perelman as a
foil to introduce her own vision of sophistics, which seeks to depart from
the Plato-Perelman lineage altogether. We start by returning to several key
points from our discussion of Cassin’s position in Chapter 1, while fleshing
out the details relevant to her critique. For this we rely primarily on “The
Value of Rhetoric: From Plato to Perelman”, a section from the third part
of The Sophistic Effect (1995) which contains the essence of her interpreta­
tion and critique of Perelman.1
Plato or Aristotle?
We will recall that for Cassin what philosophers call “rhetoric” is a philo­
sophical invention, a term coined by Plato to make it appear as though
there was some technique, some technê, that precedes the technician and
not the reverse. By making it appear as though the sophist claimed to have
a certain knowledge or skill, she argues that Plato is trying to equate rheto­
ric with sophistry, the evil double of philosophy. For Plato, rhetoric is thus
to philosophy what phlogiston is to oxygen—close, but “not yet”.2 The
effect of “Plato’s trick”, according to Cassin, has been a deeply ambiguous
understanding of rhetoric within the dominant “ontological” tradition of
philosophy.
In order to shed some light on this ambiguity Cassin draws on the
rhetorical position of Aelius Aristides, the Greek orator of the “second
sophistic” and author of A Reply to Plato.3 Cassin argues that three pos­
sible positions emerge from Aristides’s response to Plato. The first position
is that there are two distinct kinds of rhetoric, best exemplified by the
1  Cassin, ES, 411–435. A nearly identical version of this section was first published as
“Bonnes et mauvaises rhétoriques. de Platon à Perelman” in Meyer, ed., Figures et conflits
rhétoriques (1990), 17–37. All translation of these texts are our own.
2  Cassin, ES, 413.
3  Aelius Aristides, Orations, Volume 1, trans. Michael Trapp (Cambridge, MA: Harvard Uni­
versity Press, 2017). For an overview of Aristides’s works and their relation to the “second
sophistic, see George A. Kennedy, A New History of Classical Rhetoric (Princeton, NJ:
Princeton University Press, 1994), 239–241.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  147
different attitudes taken in the Gorgias and the Phaedrus. According to
this reading, to recall Cassin’s formula, “one plus one equals zero”: by
distinguishing between a sophistical rhetoric and a philosophical rhetoric,
there is effectively nothing specific to rhetoric at all; the entire question
of rhetoric dissolves into an ethical question about the right way to use
language.
The second position distinguishes not between two kinds of rhetoric
but between two uses. Here, rhetoric is understood as an instrument or
technique which can be put to either good or bad ends, ends which fall
beyond the sphere of rhetoric properly speaking. Yet, here again, Cassin
observes that this approach leaves rhetoric subservient to an ethics, an
external finality determined by the philosopher. There is, however, a third
option in Aristides: that of a single rhetoric, a rhetoric which would dis­
rupt the ethical problematic altogether. As Cassin explains, Aristides is
not content to simply read the “good” rhetoric of the Phaedrus against
the “bad” rhetoric of the Gorgias, but instead plays the one off the other.
For Cassin, Aristides thus points in the direction of a non-philosophical
rhetoric, that is, a sophistics.
Slowly building up her argument, Cassin then turns to Aristotle, who,
in reaction to Plato’s negative depiction in the Gorgias, tries to give rheto­
ric a place of its own within his broader philosophy. As Cassin explains,
Aristotle’s Rhetoric has long been seen in continuity with the “good rheto­
ric” of Plato’s Phaedrus.4 Most notably, this is the line of thought taken up
by Perelman, whose work she describes as the attempt “to fulfill the pro­
gramme of the Phaedrus with Aristotle’s Rhetoric”.5 Yet, over and against
Plato, what imposes itself most forcefully on the reader is Aristotle’s effort
to render rhetoric as a distinct, if not autonomous discipline. As she puts it:
Rhetoric is undoubtedly a tekhnê, a dunamis, and even an epistêmê
since it is causal knowledge, which cannot rightly be confused with
either sophistry or philosophy. This (at least attempted) autonomy
of rhetoric beyond ethical normativity is, in my opinion, the same as
the autonomy of politics and in connection with it, to be attributed to
Aristotle's sophistic and anti-Platonic tendency.6
For Cassin, the problem of two rhetorics is thus foreign to Aristotle’s think­
ing. This is clear from the first line of the Rhetoric, where Aristotle estab­
lishes that rhetoric is to be understood as “an antistrophos to dialectic”,
4  Cassin, ES, 423–424.
5  Cassin, ES, 424.
6  Cassin, ES, 424.


148  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
that is, as a “counterpart” or “analogue”.7 With this formulation, Cassin
explains, Aristotle seeks to disrupt the Platonic horizon of concepts by
modifying the terms of the analogy. Whereas in the Gorgias rhetoric stands
in relation to the soul as cooking stands to the body, for Aristotle rhetoric
stands in relation to the persuasive as dialectic stands to the demonstrative.
Thus construed, rhetoric is given its own proper place alongside dialectic
to the extent that each share three features: both have a universal character
(in the sense of being a general competence and dealing with all subject
matters), both deal with premises that are at most probable (eikos), and
finally, both are concerned with reasoning about contraries.
It is in this modified horizon beyond the Platonic analogy that Cassin
turns to Aristotle’s own definition of rhetoric. Citing Aristotle’s first defini­
tion from the first chapter of Book 1, Cassin reminds us that the function
(ergon) of rhetoric is “not to persuade but to see the available means of
persuasion in each case”.8 For Aristotle, the function of rhetoric is thus not
the empirical achievement of persuasion itself, as it was understood in the
Gorgias. Rather, now alluding to Aristotle’s second and more specific defi­
nition in the second chapter of Book 1, rhetoric is defined in terms of the
more speculative, theoretical question of “how” to persuade.9 Rhetoric’s
function is to “see” the available means of persuasion, just as the function
of medicine according to Aristotle is not to create health but to promote it
as much as possible, even in cases where one’s health cannot be recovered.10
Aristotle’s conception of rhetoric thus appears to satisfy Aristides’s wish
for a “third rhetoric”, a rhetoric that avoids the ambiguities of Plato. How
is this so? As Cassin explains, regardless of the uses to which it might be
  7  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, trans. George A. Kennedy, 1354a, 30. While Kennedy prefers to
leave “antistrophos” untranslated, nearly all other English translations opt for “counter­
part” (e.g. Roberts, 1924 [included in The Complete Works of Aristotle]; Freese, 1926;
Cooper, 1932; Lawson-Tancred, 1991; Sachs, 2009; Waterfield, 2018; Reeve, 2018; Bar­
tlett, 2019). Kennedy explains his choice and provides some helpful historical context
in Note 4 of his commentary. In French, Cassin follows Médéric Dufour’s translation,
“analogue”, whereas Pierre Chiron’s more recent translation uses “pendant” [see Bibliog­
raphy entries under Aristotle for complete references]. For more extensive commentary on
this notion see Léon Brunschwig, “Aristotle’s Rhetoric as a “Counterpart” to Dialectic”,
Essays on Aristotle’s Rhetoric, ed. Amélie Oksenberg Rorty (Berkeley, CA: University of
California Press, 1996), 34–55; Brad McAdon, “Rhetoric Is a Counterpart of Dialectic
(Ή ῥητορική ἐστίν ἀντίστροφος τῃ̑ διαλεκτικῃ̑)”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 34, No. 2
(2001), 113–150.
  8  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355b, 36; Cassin, ES, 425.
  9  Cassin, ES, 425; Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355b, 37. We return to this definition in Chapter
6, p. 266.
10  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355b, 36.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  149
put, for Aristotle rhetoric as such remains useful (khrêsimos).11 Although
“usefulness” could easily bring us back to the second conception of rheto­
ric, the one that distinguishes between good and bad uses, Cassin insists
that Aristotle’s definition situates rhetoric prior to or “below the level of
intention”.12 She is not saying, of course, that rhetoric cannot be abused.
But only that abuse would involve going beyond the limits of rhetoric’s
competence. Unlike Plato, then, Cassin argues that Aristotelian rhetoric is
“normed” from within:
[Aristotle’s] rhetoric is normed not by any ethico-philosophical outside
but from within its own competence. The quality of rhetoric, good or
bad, depends neither on its intention nor its finality, but on the good or
bad way in which it exercises its technique. A good orator is one who
practices the tekhnê rhêtorikê elaborated by Aristotle, without going
beyond the limits of its competence.13
Such a conception of rhetoric is thus quite different from the Platonic one
and is closer to her own vision for sophistics.
Yet, despite the sophistic threads in Aristotle’s Rhetoric, she argues that
it is not this Aristotle that the tradition of “philosophical rhetoric” has fol­
lowed. In Perelman, who according to Cassin is the most eminent example
of this, it is not this line of thought in Aristotle’s Rhetoric that is followed
but a mix of the Rhetoric and the Topics, the latter being Aristotle’s trea­
tise on dialectic.14 In this way, despite Perelman’s frequent references to
Aristotle, Cassin views the framing of his conception of rhetoric as more
Platonic than it might appear. For Aristotle, rhetoric is defined as a distinct
discipline with its own specific function. For Plato, by contrast, rhetoric
is simply philosophy (or dialectic) when understood properly. As Cassin
argues, it is this Platonic framing that Perelman adopts in his work.15 There
is a twist, however. Where for Plato “good” rhetoric is simply philosophy,
Cassin notes that it is the other way around for Perelman: “good” philoso­
phy is simply rhetoric.
Indeed, Cassin is correct to say that what Perelman calls the “realm of
rhetoric” in the 1970s does seem to swallow up the entire field of human
discourse, philosophy included. Consider the following passage from the
11  Cassin, ES, 426. In Book 1, Chapter 1 of the Rhetoric Aristotle provides four reasons why
rhetoric is useful (1355a-b). We discuss one of these reasons in more detail in Chapter 6,
pp. 270–271.
12  Cassin, ES, 426.
13  Cassin, ES, 428.
14  Cassin, ES, 429.
15  Cassin, ES, 430.


150  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
conclusion of The Realm of Rhetoric (1977), where Perelman provides
what is perhaps the clearest expression of how he understands rhetoric’s
relation to philosophy:
we affirm that every discourse which does not claim an impersonal
validity belongs to rhetoric. As soon as communication tries to influ­
ence one or more persons, to orient their thinking, to excite or calm
their emotions, to guide their actions, it belongs to the realm of rhetoric.
Dialectic, the technique of controversy, is included as one part of this
larger realm.16
Perelman does therefore seem to depart from the Aristotelian approach of
defining rhetoric as a distinct discipline, and in so doing makes rhetoric
synonymous with philosophy itself. For Cassin it is this move which brings
Perelman back into the Platonic horizon of rhetoric—albeit with a clear
preference for the Phaedrus over the Gorgias. As we have already seen,
rather than rely on a rhetoric designed to fool the crowd, Perelman is after
a rhetoric that can please the “gods themselves”.
Audience of the Gods
By emphasizing that it can do more than speak to the ignorant crowd,
Perelman is looking to remedy philosophy’s long-standing hostility towards
rhetoric. Cassin is thus correct to point out that for Perelman the value
of one’s rhetoric has nothing to do with the “object of persuasion”, but
rather the “subjects persuaded”.17 Indeed, for Perelman it is the quality of
the audience to which one’s rhetoric is addressed that determines whether
it is being put to “good” philosophical use. But how exactly is the qual­
ity of an audience determined? Apart from the more general issue of how
rhetoric is framed, Cassin’s criticism of Perelman largely focuses on his
answer to this question. At quite a distance from her own position, Cassin
explains that on this point Perelman clearly responds in favour of the uni­
versal, specifically what he calls the “universal audience”.18 According to
Cassin, this choice ultimately betrays his interest in rhetoric, signalling his
true allegiance to philosophy.
How does Cassin interpret Perelman’s notion of the universal audience?
Although we will examine how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca under­
stand it below, we first need to see how Cassin understands it. Here is her
characterization:
16  Perelman, RR, 162/ER, 198.
17  Cassin, ES, 432.
18  Cassin, ES, 432–433.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  151
The “universal audience” is the Kantian avatar of the “audience of the
gods” from the Phaedrus: it is not an empirical audience, actually given,
but an ideal audience, a regulating principle. Such an audience is not
only, like real audiences, a “construction of the speaker”, but its incar­
nation as well as its idea varies “with the centuries, the times, the pro­
gress of science”. It is therefore without naivety, but in full compliance
with ethics, if not full moral conformity.19
On Cassin’s reading, the universal audience is thus an ideal, a model audi­
ence constructed by the speaker in accordance with their subjective projec­
tion of what is most reasonable. At the same time, the speaker attempts to
“incarnate” this ideal audience in the real, empirical audience onto which
it is projected. The entire problem, however, rests on how one understands
the “ideal” status of the universal audience and its role as a “regulating
principle”.
What does Cassin take this to mean? On her reading, the universal audi­
ence brings Perelman back into the company of Aristotle. However, this
is no longer the potentially “sophistic” Aristotle of the Rhetoric, but the
Aristotle of Book IV of the Metaphysics as we discussed in Chapter 1. To
recall, Cassin argued that the dominant “ontological” tradition of philoso­
phy is premised on an exclusion of those who do not speak in accordance
with the principle of non-contradiction—those who speak like “plants”
rather than human beings. For Cassin, something similar is going on here
with Perelman. Instead of the principle of non-contradiction, however, it is
Perelman’s idea of the “universal audience” doing the work.
We can see what worries Cassin by looking at a question Perelman him­
self raises in “Proof in Philosophy” (1952). Here Perelman asks: “What do
we do when, advancing a proposition which appears objectively valid, to
which all reasonable being should adhere, we encounter one or many stub­
born minds that persist in rejecting it?”.20 The first option Perelman con­
siders is modifying one’s conception of the universal audience. In our view,
this seems to be Perelman’s actual answer. Yet, it is the second response
he considers that worries Cassin. The second option would be simply to
“exclude the obstinate ones from the set of reasonable beings”.21 While
Cassin recognizes that Perelman explicitly rejects this move as philosophi­
cally illegitimate, she fears that “philosophy cannot do without such an
exclusion”, alluding back to her sophistic critique of Aristotle.22 For her,
19  Cassin, ES, 432–433.
20  Perelman, “Proof in philosophy”, Hibbert Journal, Vol. 52, No. 4 (1954), 358, translation
modified/“De la prevue en philosophie”, RH, 276.
21  Perelman, “Proof in philosophy”, 358, translation modified/RH, 276; Cassin, ES, 432.
22  Cassin, ES, 433.


152  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
philosophy as such is constituted by the exclusion of those who do not
speak in the controlled ontological terms of the philosopher. Although
Perelman thinks his and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s recovery of rhetoric marks a
profound break within the philosophical tradition in a more pluralistic and
anti-dogmatic direction, Cassin thinks he has simply repeated this exclu­
sion at another level—this time not in terms of a “decision on meaning”
but in terms of a “decision on the audience”, we might say.
Cassin’s interpretation is therefore that Perelman’s philosophy is under­
written by an ethical imperative to address the universal audience. For her,
the universal audience, which Perelman himself often compares to Kant’s
categorical imperative,23 leaves rhetoric right where it began in Plato’s
Gorgias: without any specificity and entirely subservient to philosophy.
As she puts it:
The rhetorical imperative of addressing the universal audience, together
with the petitio principii that it is the fiction of an honest mind, in both
senses of the genitive, is thus empowered to distinguish between good
and bad efficacy. The universal audience is the operator of the confu­
sion between rhetoric, philosophy and ethics.24
Indeed, for Cassin the very notion of a universal audience begs the ques­
tion. If we want to know what counts as a “good” use of rhetoric, Perelman
will tell us that it involves the use of discursive means that would convince
a universal audience. Yet, if we want to know who counts among this
privileged group, all we are told is that they are the most honest, and criti­
cal minds in relation to the subject in question. Cassin’s fear is that it will
always fall to the whims of the philosopher to decide, when encountering
a “stubborn mind”, whether to humbly modify their conception of the
universal audience or exclude their interlocutor from the sphere of reason­
able beings altogether.
From Cassin’s perspective, any apparent proximity between herself and
Perelman is misleading. Although both share a common concern with the
effects of discourse, Cassin explains that for Perelman “the effect is paved
by good intentions”.25 This sophistic criticism is thus quite different from a
philosophical one. Take the notable philosophical critique of Paul Ricoeur,
for example, who takes issue with Perelman’s attempt to subsume every
kind of discourse under rhetoric.26 Against any such annexation, Ricoeur
23  See, for example, Perelman, “Justice and Reason”, JLA, 72–73.
24  Cassin, ES, 434.
25  Cassin, ES, 433.
26  We return to the relation between Ricoeur and Perelman, as well as Ricoeur’s critique of
Perelman, in Chapter 5.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  153
argues that rhetoric is bound by its defining telos, persuasion, a finality
that prevents it from subsuming the disinterestedness of “authentic philo­
sophical discussion”.27 Yet, for Cassin, Ricoeur’s concern is entirely mis­
placed. Even if Perelman’s new rhetoric could subsume philosophy, Cassin
argues, it nonetheless continues to distinguish itself from sophistry by its
“good proairesis”, and thus receives its values from “an ethics precon­
ceived by philosophy”.28 There is thus no real difference between philoso­
phy and rhetoric. To escape finally from the rhetorical tricks of philosophy,
a sophistics must maintain “a strict problematic of the effect, not with,
but against every problematic of intention”.29 It is only in this way, she
concludes, that a non-philosophical response to the question of rhetoric’s
value can be found; a rhetoric that is no longer philosophy in yet another
disguise, but, finally, a sophistics.
2  Perelman and the Universal Audience
Cassin’s main charge against Perelman is that his conception of rhetoric
is ultimately too philosophical, culminating in the notion of the universal
audience which repeats the same exclusionary gesture as Aristotle that we
saw in Chapter 1. Where Aristotle writes this “transcendental exclusion”
into the very conditions of meaningful language use, Perelman has written
it into the addressive structure of discourse. In order to assess Cassin’s crit­
icism, we now turn our attention to the way that Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca define audience, specifically the universal audience. As this notion
has been subject to more controversy than any other in the reception of
the NRP, we will proceed gradually, paying careful attention to the way
that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca themselves introduce the concept of
audience.
We begin in this section by returning to the Treatise’s Introduction,
looking at what Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca call the “effective com­
munity of minds” presupposed by any genuinely argumentative encounter.
Next, we look at how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca define audience as a
construction of the arguer. Finally, we come to the notion of the “univer­
sal audience” itself. These discussions prepare our assessment of Cassin’s
27  Paul Ricoeur, “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”, From Metaphysics to Rhetoric, ed.
Michel Meyer, trans. Robert Harvey (Dordrecht: Kluwer, 1989), 141/“Rhétorique—Poé­
tique—Herméneutique”, De la métaphysique, ed. Michel Meyer (Bruxelles: L’Éditions de
l’Université de Bruxelles), 147/“Rhétorique, poétique, herméneutique”, Lectures 2. La
contrée des philosophes (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1999), 485–486.
28  Cassin, ES, 434.
29  Cassin, ES, 434–435.


154  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
critique of Perelman, which, as we shall see, hinges on how we interpret
these related notions.
An Effective Community of Minds
After drawing their initial distinction between argumentation and dem­
onstration in §1 of the Treatise, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca observe
that the basic assumption of argumentation, as they understand it, is “the
existence of an intellectual contact”. By this they mean that argumentation
requires, at a given moment, the realization of an “effective community of
minds” [une communauté des esprits effective].30 This fecund idea is one
of the primary features that distinguishes argumentation from demonstra­
tion; it is this fleeting yet intensely vivid actuality that lends an argumenta­
tive situation its singularity. This contact is not just the coming together of
bodies in a particular time and place, although it is certainly also that; it
is more specifically a cognitive or intellectual contact whereby individuals
have, for a certain duration, the opportunity to meet the minds of others.
Somewhat different from our emphasis on the centrality of “audience”,
commentators like John Arthos have attempted to place this idea at the
centre of the Treatise, suggesting that it evokes an unexplored “dialogical
ontology” hidden by Perelman’s more conventional communicative model
of speaker-message-audience.31 In our own reading of the Treatise, how­
ever, we have cautioned against too quickly reading an ontology into the
project, as this seems to flatly contradict the intentions of the authors.
Keeping closely to the text, §2 takes up this idea by identifying a num­
ber of minimal conditions for the formation of an effective community
of minds and, by extension, for argumentation. Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca identify three main conditions. The first, and “indispensable mini­
mum for argumentation”, is the existence of a common language, that is,
a technique or medium which allows communication to take place.32 Yet,
even a common language proves insufficient where there is no prior agree­
ment as to how this language is to be used. A second condition is therefore
some preliminary agreement, however implicit, “arising from the norms
set by social life”.33 Without these norms, as Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s example of nonsensical language from Alice in Wonderland clearly
shows, even the willingness to converse proves insufficient to facilitate any
30  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §1, 14/TA, 18.
31  John Arthos, “Almost speaking a new rhetoric: The strangeness of the text of La Nouvelle
Rhétorique”, Southern Journal of Communication, Vol. 70, No. 1 (2009), 32.
32  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §2, 15/TA, 19.
33  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §2, 15/TA, 20.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  155
meaningful contact between minds. The third and final condition they
introduce is thus the most important:
To engage in argument, a person must attach some importance to gain­
ing the adherence of his interlocutor, to securing his assent, his mental
cooperation … It is also to be observed that wanting to convince some­
one always implies a certain modesty on the part of the initiator of the
argument; what he says is not “Gospel truth,” he does not possess that
authority which would place his words beyond question so that they
would carry immediate conviction. He acknowledges that he must use
persuasion, think of arguments capable of acting on his interlocutor,
show some concern for him, and be interested in his state of mind.34
This third condition can be summarized simply as having concern for one’s
addressee. Importantly, this need not be taken in a moral sense, but simply
in the sense of taking the other into consideration: to enter into argument
with someone is, to some degree, to take into consideration who they are
and what they believe.
For Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, this third condition of realizing
an “effective community of minds” sharply distinguishes argumentation
from violence. As they discuss later in Part One, the very use of argument
“implies that one has renounced resorting to force alone, that value is
attached to gaining the adherence of one’s interlocutor by means of rea­
soned persuasion, and that one is not regarding him as an object”.35 Thus,
so long as this community of minds remains “actual” [effective], the use
of violence is excluded as a means of resolving the motivating exigency. In
this sense, violence is the least rhetorical form of social action. As anthro­
pologist David Graeber observes, “Violence may well be the only way in
which it is possible for one human being to have relatively predictable
effects on the actions of another without understanding anything about
them”.36 With the exception of violence, he continues, to have any influ­
ence on others “one at least has to have some idea who they think they are,
who they think you are, what they might want out of the situation, and a
host of similar considerations”.37
Having established that some concern for one’s audience is a basic con­
dition of argumentation, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca exemplify this in
34  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §2, 16/TA, 20–21.
35  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §13, 55/TA, 73.
36  David Graeber, Revolutions in Reverse: Essays on Politics, Violence, Art, and Imagination
(London: Minor Compositions, 2011), 48.
37  Graeber, Revolutions in Reverse, 48.


156  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
§3 with an enduringly prescient observation about the limits of “factual”
discourse:
The authors of scientific reports and similar papers often think that if
they merely report certain experiments, mention certain facts, or enun­
ciate a certain number of truths, this is enough of itself to automatically
arouse the interest of their hearers or readers. This attitude rests on
the illusion, widespread in certain rationalistic and scientistic circles,
that facts speak for themselves and make such an indelible imprint on
the human mind that anyone would be forced to give their adherence
regardless of their disposition.38
Rather than downplay the importance of facts and evidence, the point here
is to emphasize that even the most seemingly neutral, indisputable obser­
vations take on a rhetorical dimension when used argumentatively. The
contact of minds is thus not something guaranteed by the truth or objec­
tivity of certain statements on their own, but something that first must be
established through the use of argumentative techniques as well as various
institutions (in a broad sense).
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are thus not attempting to downplay
the importance of marshalling evidence for one’s claims. Rather, they are
simply pointing out that the intended audience of a scientific statement
is a highly specialized one and addressing that audience relies on a com­
plex network of institutions to bring certain minds into contact. It is these
institutions which make it possible that the simple reporting of a fact can
constitute an argument:
It is true that these authors when addressing a learned society or publish­
ing an article in a specialized journal, can afford to neglect the means of
entering into contact with their audience [public], for the indispensable
link between speaker and audience is provided by a scientific institu­
tion, the society, or the journal. In such a case, the author has merely
to maintain, between himself and the public, the contact already estab­
lished by the scientific institution.39
Arguers are thus never confronted with an audience in a vacuum; they
always find themselves within a certain institutional context which can
either facilitate or impede the necessary contact of minds. In the case of
scientific experts, it is the role of universities, scientific journals, and so on
to provide a competent audience capable of carefully evaluating the results
38  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §3, 17, translation modified/TA, 22–23.
39  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §3, 18/TA, 23.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  157
of, say, a vaccine trial, long before (if ever) those results reach the general
public. To stick with the same example: an important conclusion here is
that we should not confuse the institutionally established audience of sci­
entific research with the heterogeneous and non-expert audience we often
refer to as “public opinion”. The rationalistic and scientistic “illusion”
denounced by Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca thus does not refer to the
truth of scientific statements but to their address: the illusion results from
the premature universalization of what is but a particular audience.
Constructing the Audience
Our discussion of the “effective community of minds” has brought us to
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s key insight that all argumentation is
audience-relative, in the sense that it aims at securing the adherence of
those to whom it is addressed. What remains to be seen is precisely how
“audience” is defined. In Chapter 2, we discussed the concept of audi­
ence in the general terms of the Treatise’s Introduction. There we saw that
audience was defined as the addressive structure of discourse, in terms
of their observation that “all argumentation develops with a view to an
audience”.40 Yet, it is only in §3-9 of Part One that the details of this con­
cept come into full view. It is to these sections that we now turn.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca raise the question of how “audience”
is to be defined in the final paragraph of §3. They first consider defin­
ing it as those explicitly addressed by a speaker. This definition, however,
proves too limited. In many cases, such as a parliamentary address, to
take their example, the audience is not so much the person named (e.g. the
speaker of the assembly) but other members of parliament or the broader
citizenry. After running through a few more similar examples, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca conclude that there can be no reliable way to deter­
mine a speaker’s audience in terms of “material criteria” alone.41 Indeed,
this problem is even more acute in the case of writing. Thus, rather than
attempt to provide such a criterion, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca opt
instead for a definition based on the intentions of the speaker. For the pur­
poses of rhetoric, then, they define “audience” as “the ensemble of those
whom the speaker wishes to influence by his argumentation”.42 Whether
implicit or explicit, every speaker must in some way think of those they
wish to persuade. And for Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca these people
form the “audience” to which the speaker’s discourse is addressed.
40  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 5, translation modified/TA, 6.
41  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §3, 19/TA, 25.
42  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §3, 19/TA, 25.


158  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
As the title of §4 emphasizes, the audience is thus defined as a “construc­
tion” of the arguer (speaker or writer). This imagined audience, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca explain, “is always a more or less systematized
construction”.43 This means that arguers operate on the basis of a more
or less conscious representation of their intended audience. While some
commentators have objected to the need for “any audience constructs”,44
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca disagree. In their view, a specific concept
is needed to designate the measure arguers use when making argumenta­
tive choices. This measure, so to speak, is the “audience” they construct
of their addressee(s). Indeed, for Perelman, the audience is precisely what
makes rhetoric rhetoric, which, as he says elsewhere, is at work “wherever
there is choice”.45
The audience is thus a practical concept. If one’s argumentation is to
be effective (whatever one’s particular goal may be), “care must be taken
to conceive the imagined audience [auditoire présumé] as close as possi­
ble to reality”.46 When an arguer fails to do so sufficiently, that is, when
their construction of the audience diverges too strongly from their actual
interlocutor, they risk “begging the question”—which can arise either
from a faulty construction or from being unconcerned with one’s audi­
ence entirely. Not to be confused with the “demonstrative” conception
of begging the question (petitio principii), Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
reinterpret this fallacy in rhetorical terms, that is, in terms of the relation
between arguer and audience. In formal logics, begging the question occurs
when an argument’s conclusion is already implied by one of its premises.
For the Belgians, however, an arguer is said to beg the question rhetori­
cally when—whether out of ignorance or by neglect—they choose points
of departure that their interlocutor does not adhere to or may even contest.
This is a serious error as it forecloses the possibility of achieving the con­
tact of minds needed for argumentation to develop.47
If the audience is a construction of the arguer, however, how many
kinds of audiences are there? How many types of audience can arguers
43  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 19/TA, 25.
44  J.E. Sigler, “The New Rhetoric’s Concept of Universal Audience, Misconceived”, Argu­
mentation, Vol. 29 (2015), 346.
45  Perelman, “Introduction: Chaim Perelman’s Address as the Ohio State University”, Prac­
tical Reasoning in Human Affairs: Studies in Honor of Chaim Perelman, ed. James L.
Golden and Joseph J. Pilotta (Dordrecht: D. Reidel Publishing, 1986), 12. In Chapter 6
(pp. 267–270) we shall develop this point further, as thinking about rhetoric in this way
suggests that the “audience” might be at work in human action generally and not only
in discourse.
46  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 20, translation modified/TA, 25.
47  It is here that we find what is perhaps the only practical maxim in the Treatise: adapt to
one’s audience, whatever its nature (Perelman & Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §5, 25/TA, 33).


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  159
construct? We need to tread carefully when answering these questions,
as the treatment of audience in the Treatise is a source of great contro­
versy. To begin, we turn to the closing lines of §5 where Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca explain that their study will be most fruitful if it is “based
on the consideration of audiences in their concrete, particular multiform
aspect”.48 Consequently, as they add in §6, this means that “audiences are
almost infinite in their variety”.49 To bring the discussion into workable
limits, however, they note that the subsequent four paragraphs (§6-9) will
focus only on the characteristics of certain audiences, audiences that will
be of particular importance for the philosopher.50
As we have seen them do elsewhere, rather than impose a pre-estab­
lished typology onto experience, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca take
their point of departure from ordinary language and the distinctions that it
preserves. Here, they focus on the distinction between “persuading” [per­
suader] and “convincing” [convaincre], which they observe seems to arise
out of “that age-old debate between the partisans of truth and those of
opinion, between philosophers seeking the absolute and rhetors engaged
in action”.51 To the person concerned with action, persuading surpasses
convincing, as conviction is but one intellectual step on the road to some
future conduct. To the philosopher, by contrast, persuading is cheaper
than convincing, as the former is often negatively associated with emotions
and the passions, while the latter pertains to the intellect alone.
Yet, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca refuse to adopt any static hierar­
chy between these terms. Indeed, they argue that both positions are equally
guilty of isolating and hypostasizing certain elements from a larger totality.
Thus, rather than adopt any “objective” stance towards the distinction,
their analysis need only “recognize that our language makes use of two
notions, convincing and persuading, and that there is a slight and percepti­
ble difference in the meaning of the two terms”.52 Here again we discover
the authors’s ambitions of ontological neutrality when it comes to the tech­
nical notions of their study. As they stipulate:
We are going to apply the term persuasive to argumentation that only
claims validity for a particular audience, and the term convincing to
argumentation that is supposed to gain the adherence of every rational
48  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §5, 26, translation modified/TA, 34.
49  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §6, 26/TA, 34.
50  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §5, 26/TA, 34.
51  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §6, 26–27, translation modified/TA, 35.
52  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §6, 28/TA, 36.


160  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
being. The nuance involved is a delicate one and depends essentially, on
the idea the speaker has formed on the incarnation of reason.53
The use of the terms “persuasive” and “convincing” is thus relative to
an arguer’s construction of their audience, or, what amounts to the same
thing, to their idea of the “incarnation of reason”. In this way, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca avoid drawing this distinction along the lines of the
subjective and the objective (as Kant does, for example).54 Where “persua­
sive” refers to arguments that are constructed to earn the adherence of a
determinate set of auditors with a certain set of common beliefs, “convinc­
ing” refers to arguments constructed with an eye to anyone and everyone.
“Our viewpoint”, they explain, “has the advantage of showing that the
difference between the terms convincing and persuading is always unpre­
cise and in practice must remain so”.55
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are thus not claiming to invent anything
new. They are simply pointing out that the semantic nuances of ordinary
language permit us to distinguish between arguments which, on their own
terms, purport to be valid for some and which purport to be valid for all.
For example, does an argument rely on highly specific premises that could
not plausibly earn the assent of anyone who is not already a member of an
equally specific social group? Or does an argument contain premises of a
more general nature that could in principle earn the assent of any reason­
able being, regardless of any group affiliation? Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca would say that where the former aims to be persuasive, the latter
aims to be convincing.
It is on the basis of such distinctions that they introduce the “three kinds
of audiences” which they explain enjoy a certain privilege in current prac­
tice and in the view of philosophers. It bears repeating that these audiences
are not introduced as ideal types. They are introduced simply to provide
the theory of argumentation with a technical vocabulary for describing
the way that arguers, as a matter of fact, construct their arguments, for
describing the different “measures” by which arguers make argumentative
choices. The three audiences that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca intro­
duce here are: (§7) the universal audience, (§8) the single interlocutor, and
(§9) the self-deliberating audience, i.e. oneself. Although the latter two
audiences merit their own dedicated treatment, we can only discuss them
here insofar as they shed light on the universal audience.56 As we have seen,
53  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §6, 28, translation modified/TA, 36.
54  Kant, Critique of Pure Reason, trans. Guyer, A820/B848, 685.
55  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §6, 29/TA, 38.
56  For an account of the self-deliberating audience in the NRP and its implications for con­
temporary argumentation research, see Tindale, “Self-deliberation and the Strategy of the
Pseudo-Dialogue”, Co-herencia, Vol. 17, No. 32 (2020), 159–178.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  161
it is this distinctively philosophical audience that Cassin’s critique focuses
on.
The Universal Audience
The universal audience is undoubtedly the notion most associated with
Perelman’s name. It is also the most controversial. Perelman must have
been aware that the term’s oxymoronic construction would run the risk
of upsetting everyone and pleasing no one—which is precisely what hap­
pened: rhetoricians found the term too philosophical, and philosophers
found it too rhetorical. For their part, the rhetoricians (particularly those
in the United States) could not see past Perelman’s universalist language
and saw the notion as a relapse from rhetoric back into traditional phi­
losophy. These readings, however, were largely based on a misunder­
standing of §7, which admittedly fails to draw sufficient attention to the
dissociation at work in the exposition. Perelman himself tried to correct
this misunderstanding in the 1980s.57 The philosophers, on the other hand,
remained suspicious of audiences in general, but tried to accommodate
the universal audience in terms of past philosophical precedent. Perelman
himself would often compare the universal audience to Kant’s categorial
imperative, while many of his contemporaries noticed parallels to the work
of Jürgen Habermas and Karl-Otto Apel, however much Perelman would
resist these comparisons.58
While more recent scholarship has resolved many of the initial interpre­
tative difficulties surrounding this notion, there remains considerable disa­
greement about its intent, scope, and enduring value.59 For the purposes
57  Perelman’s response can be found in “The New Rhetoric and The Rhetoricians”, The New
Rhetoric of Chaim Perelman, ed. Dearin, 242–248. Perelman’s comments are directed
towards Lisa S. Ede and John W. Ray, whose essays can be found in the same volume.
58  See, for example, Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, 249.
59  Competing interpretations include those of James L. Golden, “The Universal Audience
