22, No. 3 (1989): 157–173; George C. Christie, The Notion of an Ideal Audience in Legal
Argument (Dordrecht: Springer, 2000); Schmetz, L’argumentation selon Perelman (2000),
47–88; Gross and Dearin, Chaim Perelman (2003), 31–42; Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhé­
torique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”, Perelman. Le renouveau de
la rhétorique (2004), 21–37; Tindale, Rhetorical Argumentation: Principles of Theory
and Practice (Thousand Oaks, CA: Sage Publications, 2004), 133–154; Antonio Raul
De Velasco, “Rethinking Perelman’s Universal Audience: Political Dimensions of a Con­
troversial Concept”, Rhetoric Society Quarterly, Vol. 35, No. 2 (2005), 47–64; Scott F.
Aikin, “Perelmanian Universal Audience and the Epistemic Aspirations of Argument”,
Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 41, No. 3 (2008), 238–259; Charlotte Jørgensen, “Interpret­
ing Perelman’s Universal Audience: Gross Versus Crosswhite”, Argumentation, Vol. 23,


162  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
of our discussion, however, we will not wade into debates surrounding
the reception of the universal audience and its many interpretations and
(mis)appropriations. Our goal is not to assess the merits of the universal
audience for rhetorical criticism or argumentation theory, but to provide a
faithful interpretation of its role in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s the­
ory of argumentation for the purposes of evaluating Cassin’s criticism of
Perelman. We thus refer to other interpretations only where it sheds light
on our own position either by reinforcing it or introducing a helpful point
of contrast.
As with many of the concepts in the Treatise, the universal audience is
introduced (in §7) by means of an argumentative dissociation. To recall,
a dissociation attempts to instigate a rupture within a single term, idea,
or concept; it identifies an internal incompatibility that requires a distinc­
tion between what the arguer contends should be regarded as two dis­
tinct notions. In this case, the idea that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
are attempting to dissociate is that of reason itself. Using the diagram for
philosophical pairs, this dissociation can be represented as follows:
reason
universal audience
What philosophers have traditionally called “reason” (in the position of
term I) will no longer be reified as a part of the soul, mind, or brain; it
will no longer be conceived as any kind of discrete faculty or metaphysical
order to which the philosopher has privileged access. Instead, reason will
be recast as an argumentative activity with a specific kind of address—a
universal address that invites reflection and criticism from its addressees at
the level of what the arguers takes to be generally accepted, that is, what is
reasonable within a given milieu.60
In fairness, it is not hard to see why this dissociation in §7 was lost on
many readers. As we saw in Chapter 2, the idea of dissociation will not
be introduced for several hundred pages, in the section on argumentative
No. 1 (2009), 11–19; Crosswhite, “Universalities”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 43, No.
4 (2010), 430–448; Loïc Nicolas, “The Function of the ‘Universal Audience’ in Perelman’s
Rhetoric: Looking Back on a Theoretical Issue”, The Promise of Reason (2011), 48–54;
Sigler, “The New Rhetoric’s Concept of Universal Audience, Misconceived” (2015); Tin­
dale, The Philosophy of Argument and Audience Reception (Cambridge: Cambridge Uni­
versity Press, 2015), 58–74; Alan G. Gross, “Misunderstanding the Universal Audience”,
Advances in the History of Rhetoric, Vol. 22, No. 3 (2019), 290–302.
60  As Golden observes, Perelman owes this idea to Aristotle, for whom the “reputable” or
“generally accepted opinions” (endoxa) serve as a kind of universal audience in the prac­
tice of dialectic (Golden, “The Universal Audience Revisited”, 288; Aristotle, Topics [in
The Complete Works of Aristotle], 100a20, 167).


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  163
techniques. Without this technique in mind, one could easily get the
impression reading §7 that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are attempting,
rather clumsily, to combine a classical conception of reason with a rhetori­
cal theory of audience. As Tindale shows, however, “there are two notions
of the universal audience in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work: the
traditional one they are resisting and the one they are advocating”.61
What, then, is the view they advocate? Keeping in mind that we are still
in Part One of the Treatise, and thereby still reflecting on argumentation
in a relatively static manner, the following passage is the closest thing to
a definition of the universal audience that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
provide:
Instead of believing in the existence of a universal audience, analogous
to the divine mind which can assent only to the “truth” [term I], we
might more rightly characterize each speaker by the image he himself
forms of the universal audience he seeks to win over to his views [term
II].62
Noting the dissociation at work, the universal audience is thus much less
substantive than it may first sound to the philosopher. The universal audi­
ence does not “exist” in any strong sense; it is rather an image formed by
the speaker. Indeed, the universal audience, they continue, “is constituted
by each person on the basis of what he knows about his fellow human
beings, in such a way as to transcend the few oppositions of which he is
aware”.63 Indeed, there is no one universal audience, as “each culture, each
individual, has his own conception of the universal audience”.64
Even if relieved that the universal audience is not to be understood sub­
stantively, the philosopher may still worry that it goes too far in the other
direction. Is the universal audience then part of a reductive enterprise, a
rhetorical effort to unmask philosophy as “just another discourse”? On
this question, too, the philosopher can rest easy. For Perelman, the univer­
sal audience is not an attempt to do away with reason but an attempt to
provide a better description of what philosophers are actually doing when
they argue philosophically. As Perelman will put this retrospectively, “Our
aim was to show that philosophers cannot do without rhetoric; whenever
they make any appeal to reason, they actually address their discourse to
61  Tindale, Rhetorical Argumentation, 138–139.
62  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §7, 33, translation modified, our emphasis/TA, 43.
Note that Wilkinson and Weaver’s English translation does not italicize “in the existence
of”, as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca do in the original.
63  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §7, 33, translation modified/TA, 43.
64  Perelman and& Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §7, 33, translation modified/TA, 43.


164  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
a universal audience”.65 Thus, when Descartes declares that “reason” has
persuaded him to discard all but the most certain of his opinions, or when
Kant writes that he will institute a “court of justice” to judge the rightful
claims of reason, it is not some distant, imperceptible order (“Reason”)
these philosophers are communing with. Rather, they are simply address­
ing their argumentation to a universal audience—they are constructing
their discourse in such a way that any reasonable person (as they conceive
of them) could follow their reasoning and willingly assent to the same con­
clusions.66 Of course, the philosopher may be perfectly mistaken in their
conception of this person—for which the history of philosophy provides
more than a paucity of evidence. But this does not change the fact that
philosophers attempt to argue on the basis of reasons that any reasonable
person can follow. The universal audience is thus not an ideal or pre-given
objectivity against which one’s argumentation can be measured, but the
arguer’s construction of their own intended audience. As Perelman puts
it, “every philosopher addresses himself to the universal audience as he
conceives it, even in the absence of an objectivity which imposes itself on
everyone”.67
To further clarify things, let us turn to Perelman’s own account of the
origins of the universal audience. In the question period following an
address delivered at Ohio State University in November of 1982, Perelman
was asked to comment further on the meaning of his curiously formulated
concept. In his reply, he tells the story of how he first came to the idea:
I was teaching a course in the history of philosophy, and when I came
to the Medieval period and was treating Thomas Aquinas, it became
clear to me that he was dealing with essentially the same idea in his
two major works—Summa Theologica and Summa Contra Gentiles.
Yet there was a major difference regarding the audience. He wrote one
book for the theologians and then he wrote another book against those
who were not the believers of the church. To whom did he address
then? That was a book in philosophy. And there was only one thing
that you could say and that was that it was an appeal to reason. People
who are not committed to special beliefs—to the special religion, to the
special dogma—were the target of his remarks. He made the distinction
between things you had to believe and things you could prove. There
he alluded only to things you could [prove]; that means to the rational
and to the reasonable. And I asked myself, what does the appeal to
65  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, 242.
66  Descartes, Philosophical Essays and Correspondence, 104; Kant, Critique of Pure Rea­
son, Axii, 101.
67  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and the Rhetoricians”, 244.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  165
reason mean? Whom is he addressing? The difference was mainly a
difference of audience. And I decided he was addressing anybody who
could be considered as a reasonable being—without special beliefs and
commitments. There was no appeal to some national pride; there was
no appeal to some particular belief. There was an appeal to any rational
being who would read his book. So I called it the universal audience not
because everybody will read his books. You know well that books in
philosophy, especially those written in Latin, are not read by everybody.
But there are no particular beliefs and values to which he may appeal.
He only appealed to what he believed could be or would be admitted
by everybody. Surely he was mistaken on many points because for him
reason and philosophy was that of Aristotle; and he quoted Aristotle
as being the guarantor of what everybody would admit. But we always
make mistakes thinking that this would appeal to everybody.68
As Perelman recounts here, the universal audience was intended to describe
a difference in address. It describes the difference between arguing to a
particular audience (in the case of Summa Theologica, those who already
affirm the faith) and arguing to a universal audience (in the case of Summa
Contra Gentiles, those who do not affirm the faith but whom Aquinas
nonetheless seeks to convince of its truth). When addressing a universal
audience, then, the arguer cannot rely upon the specific beliefs and commit­
ments of any particular social group. They must construct their audience
differently, elevating their discourse beyond the commitments of particu­
lar audiences in search of a higher, or deeper sense of what is reasonable.
As we discuss below, this sense of what is reasonable is not something
wholly pre-given, but something that the arguer is attempting to bring
about through their argumentation, which contains their construction of
the universal audience within it.
Despite Perelman’s best efforts, many would continue to interpret
the universal audience as an external norm or standard of validity out­
side of the argumentative situation. In an article which was otherwise
met with Perelman’s full approval, “Philosophie du langage et philoso­
phie de l’argumentation” [Philosophy of Language and Philosophy of
Argumentation] (1977), Belgian philosopher Gilbert Hottois detects cer­
tain “monological connotations” in the universal audience.69 On Hottois’s
reading, the universal audience is the asymptotic ideal of argumenta­
tive discourse, the ideal of the perfect argumentative technique whose
68  Perelman, “Introduction: Chaim Perelman’s Address as the Ohio State University”, 14.
69  Gilbert Hottois, “Philosophie du langage et philosophie de l’argumentation”, Revue de
Métaphysique et de Morale, Vol. 82, No. 4 (1977), 504, our translation.


166  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
“universal validity” would overcome endless dialogue and bring about
final agreement.70
In a letter from March of 1978 Perelman responds to Hottois’s “inter­
esting” and “well-informed” article, disagreeing with his characterization
of the universal audience. Here, Perelman corrects Hottois (in a way not
unlike the correction he will later make to the American rhetoricians) not­
ing that the point is not to construct universal argumentative techniques
but to observe the way that each philosopher attempts to do so different­
ly.71 “As far as I am concerned”, Perelman clarifies, “the universal audi­
ence is only a construction, a hypothesis which the speaker presents to his
audience and puts to the test”.72 He continues by rejecting any kind of final
or absolute discourse which would bring an end to dialogue and criticism,
adding that “any interpretation that would give such an interpretation of
my thought can only be based on a misunderstanding”.73 Perelman is thus
unequivocal that the universal audience is to be understood as a construc­
tion of the arguer and not a pre-existing norm or external standard of
validity.
Here we get to the crux of the misunderstanding. For Perelman, the
universal audience is a norm (as a term II in a philosophical pair), but it
is a norm of the arguer’s own construction, a norm to which the arguer
submits their own discourse, depending on how they want to address their
audience. Just as with Perelman’s example of Aquinas, the same empirical
addressee can be addressed in multiple ways. Depending on the arguer’s
goals, they can be addressed as members of the Catholic faith (particular
audience) or, more generally, as reasonable human beings who may or
may not be Catholics (universal audience). Like all matters rhetorical, it is
a question of choice, and a difficult choice at that, as this more expansive
form of address comes with greater obligations. As Perelman remarks later
in the Ohio State address, “the universal audience has the obligation to
answer anyone who would object”.74 Addressing the universal audience
thus involves the greatest risk, as the arguer adopts a considerable burden
of proof, opening themselves up to a wide range of possible, and largely
unforeseeable objections and criticisms.
This brings us to the temporal dimension of the universal audience.
Although one would not get this impression from reading §7 alone, the
universal audience, like the rest of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
70  Hottois, “Philosophie du langage et philosophie de l’argumentation”, 504.
71  Perelman, Letter to Gilbert Hottois, 7 March 1978. Fonds Chaïm Perelman, Archives de
l’Université libre de Bruxelles, BE.ULB-ARCH/89PP 23.1, our translation.
72  Perelman, Letter to Gilbert Hottois, 7 March 1978, our translation and emphasis.
73  Perelman, Letter to Gilbert Hottois, 7 March 1978, our translation.
74  Perelman, “Introduction”, 15.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  167
technical vocabulary, is essentially temporal. When addressing a universal
audience, an arguer attempts three difficult tasks simultaneously: (1) they
anticipate the standards of what is reasonable for all of humanity; (2) they
construct their audience as the historical embodiment of those standards;
and (3) they attempt to institute those standards through the performance
of their argumentation. Although statically rendered, each of these three
tasks can be observed in a concrete instance of argumentation.
To illustrate this, let us consider a non-philosophical example, Leon
Trotsky’s History of the Russian Revolution. As a key figure in the events
in question, Trotsky feels obliged in the Preface to justify to his readers
that what follows will not be a partisan defence of his own political views
but a scientific history of the Russian Revolution of 1917:
There remains the question of the political position of the author, who
stands as a historian upon the same viewpoint upon which he stood
as a participant in the events. The reader, of course, is not obliged to
share the political views of the author, which the latter on his side has
no reason to conceal. But the reader does have the right to demand that
a historical work should not be the defence of a political position, but
an internally well-founded portrayal of the actual process of the revolu­
tion. A historical work only then completely fulfils the mission when
events unfold upon its pages in their full natural necessity.75
While few writers will be this explicit, Trotsky’s exceptional lucidity dem­
onstrates what addressing the universal audience looks like. As we can see
here, in doing so (1) he anticipates the expectations of his reader, whom he
appreciates has “the right to demand that a historical work should not be
the defence of a political position”. He also anticipates the standards that
his reader will deploy in determining when a historical work truly “ful­
fils [its] mission”. But this anticipation is equally (2) a construction. This
construction not only presents us with an image of how he conceives the
universal audience, the “incarnation of reason” for Perelman, but it also
establishes the standards to which he will hold his own argumentation:
The serious and critical reader will not want a treacherous impartial­
ity, which offers him a cup of conciliation with a well-settled poison of
reactionary hate at the bottom, but a scientific conscientiousness, which
for its sympathies and antipathies—open and undisguised—seeks sup­
port in an honest study of the facts, a determination of their real con­
nections, an exposure of the causal laws of their movement. That is the
75  Leon Trotsky, History of the Russian Revolution, trans. Max Eastman (London: Penguin
Books, [1932] 2017), xvii-xviii.


168  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
only possible historic objectivism, and moreover it is amply sufficient,
for it is verified and attested not by the good intentions of the historian,
for which only he himself can vouch, but the natural laws revealed by
him of the historic process itself.76
The universal audience is here constructed as a “serious and critical
reader”, as someone who will not settle for the false impartiality of the
reactionary historian, but who will insist on being presented with the natu­
ral laws of the historic process itself.77 But this construction is equally (3)
an institution. Through the performance of his argumentation, Trotsky
is attempting to institute his conception of the universal audience as the
standard against which his own history as well as those of past and future
historians ought to be measured. In so doing, he thus hopes—should it
achieve the adherence it intends to secure—to contribute to the develop­
ment of the reasonable itself.78
To summarize: (1) anticipating that his reader may be sceptical about
his ability to provide an objective historical account, (2) constructing his
reader as a universal audience which expects nothing less than an “honest
study of the facts”, and (3) instituting this conception of the universal audi­
ence as the standard to which he will hold his argumentation, Trotsky thus
constrains his own range of possible argumentative choices. Regarding his
selection of “data” (les donnés), he will thus no longer be at liberty to “rely
in any degree upon personal recollections”, for example. Instead, he will be
obliged to base his exposition only on “historically verified documents”.79
With regard to presentation or style, he will thus opt to speak of himself
only in the third person, judging that the subjective tone is “not permis­
sible in a work of history”.80 Just as Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca theo­
rize, Trotsky, as “someone concerned with the adherence of the universal
audience”, “will renounce … arguments that this universal audience, as
he conceives it, would find inadmissible; he will deem it almost immoral
to resort to an argument which is not, in his own eyes, a rational one”.81
76  Trotsky, History of the Russian Revolution, xviii.
77  To be clear, this is only Trotsky’s vision of historical objectivity, of what history should
look like if it is written to earn the adherence of the universal audience as he sees it. The
reader is of course free to dispute this—as there would be no need for Trotsky to include
it at all if the point was not arguable in the first place.
78  This triadic schema of the universal audience, or indeed any audience, can be read along
the lines of Ricoeur’s threefold mimesis, which we discuss throughout Chapter 6.
79  Trotsky, History of the Russian Revolution, xvii.
80  Trotsky, History of the Russian Revolution, xvii.
81  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §101, 483, translation modified/TA, 640.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  169
3  Who’s Afraid of Rhetoric?
In our preceding discussion, we examined the most controversial kind of
audience in the NRP, the universal audience. To introduce this notion, and
further clarify how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca approach audiences
more generally, we began with their idea of an “effective community of
minds”, the actualization of which presupposes some concern for the audi­
ence on the part of the arguer. Second, we then looked at Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s general conception of audience, which they define as
“the ensemble of those whom the speaker wishes to influence”, regardless
of the degree to which this construction is performed consciously. Finally,
we came to the universal audience itself, which has often been interpreted
as an unshakeable remnant of philosophical rationalism. Drawing on our
interpretation of the Treatise in Chapter 2 and paying close attention
to Perelman’s characterization of this idea in other texts and letters, we
argued that the universal audience is best understood in terms of an argu­
er’s anticipation, construction, and institution of the standards to which
their own argumentation ought to be held.
At this point two questions remain to be asked. The first is whether
Cassin’s critique of the universal audience as a regulating idea, as a
“Kantian avatar of the audience of the gods”, is an accurate depiction of
Perelman’s position. The second, addressed in the final section, is whether
Cassin’s critique is sufficient to justify the abandonment of rhetoric in
favour of sophistics. We begin with the first.
Audience of the Gods?
To recall, Cassin’s main charge against Perelman is that his conception of
rhetoric is too philosophical. More specifically, it is too universal and too
bound-up with the ontological model of logos. She alleges that by invoking
the universal audience Perelman effectively repeats Aristotle’s “transcendental
exclusion”, only this time at the level of address rather than meaning. What
this means for Cassin is that the ethical intention at the heart of Perelman’s
understanding of rhetoric constrains the arguer to speak to audiences in the
“right” way, and thereby occludes serious reflection on what she calls the
“third dimension of speech”, namely, epideixis or speech as performance.
How accurate is this characterization of Perelman? To what extent
is his vision of rhetoric truly centred around a “problematic of inten­
tion”? To respond to this question, it may be helpful to take a small
detour through what Emmanuelle Danblon calls the “thorny question of
the universal audience”.82 Much like our own, Danblon’s analysis of the
82  Emmanuelle Danblon, Rhétorique et rationalité. Essai sur l’émergence de la critique et de
la persuasion (Bruxelles: Éditions de l’Université de Bruxelles, 2002), 192, our translation.


170  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
universal audience in “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question
de l’auditoire universel” (2004) takes its point of departure from Cassin’s
emphasis on the ambiguity of Perelman’s position. According to Danblon,
the difficulty arises from his attempt to strike a balance between an ideal
conception of the universal audience and a more operational or descriptive
conception. She explains that, on the one hand, the universal audience can
be understood in a strong normative sense, that is, as a regulating prin­
ciple or, on the other, as a straightforward psychological description of
how arguers conceive or represent their audiences. Like Cassin, Danblon
emphasizes how Perelman’s introduction of the universal audience effec­
tively subsumes dialectic under rhetoric. “This step is important”, she
contends:
This conception of things reveals the [Treatise’s] concern to construct
a theory of argumentation that articulates a certain regulating ideal of
reason with the contingent reality of the adherence of audiences, as real
as they are passionate. Aware of the fact that such an approach runs
the risk of an unsurpassable paradox, Perelman hesitates, here again,
between [1] a minimalist conception of the universal audience—each
speaker constructs his or her speech according to the image he or she
forms of the universal audience—and [2] a hypertrophic vision in which
“one counts on the adherence of those who subject themselves to the
givenness of experience or to the light of reason”.83
Whether ambiguous or paradoxical, Danblon’s analysis points to a clear
problem. Does the universal audience not commit a rather elementary peti­
tio principi?84 How can the universal audience be both a speaker’s con­
struction and a “hypertrophic” ideal of reason? While many readers have
struggled with this apparent tension, how sure are we that this is really
Perelman’s position? In constructing this opposition, Danblon cites two
passages to emphasize the tension.85 The first poses no problem; it is a
faithful paraphrase of how the universal audience is defined. The second,
however, is more curious.
The second passage Danblon cites is taken from the third paragraph
of §7. Yet, as we have already seen, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca have
at this point not yet introduced their own conception of the universal
83  Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”,
Perelman. Le renouveau de la rhétorique (2004), 24, our translation.
84  This is the hypothesis examined by Guy Bouchard in “Le recours à l’universel auditoire
implique-t-il une pétition de principe ?”, Philosophiques, Vol. 7, No. 2 (1980), 161–188.
85  Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”,
24.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  171
audience. They are still establishing “term I” of the philosophical pair
from which they will dissociate their own view (term II). Here, Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca are describing—albeit somewhat confusingly by
already using the rhetorical terminology of audiences—how philosophers
have traditionally conceived of reason. Specifically, they are describing,
in broad strokes, the empiricist conception of reason, “those who sub­
ject themselves to the givenness of experience”, and the rationalist con­
ception, those who subject themselves to “the light of reason”.86 Indeed,
as Perelman emphasizes to the American rhetoricians in 1984, it is this
conception of reason to which he is opposed. Referring to the very passage
cited by Danblon, Perelman explains:
This traditional conception, which links importance to previously guar­
anteed objectivity and not to the adherence of an audience, rejects all
rhetoric not based upon knowledge of the truth. This point of view,
to which I am opposed, was described in the [Treatise] in such a suffi­
ciently convincing manner as to lead certain rhetorician readers to con­
sider it as expressing my own ideas.87
Thus, upon closer examination, the apparent ambiguity or paradoxical
nature of Perelman’s position begins to fade. Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca do have two conceptions of the universal audience. But they are
not at all in tension. In the order they appear in the Treatise, the first
corresponds to the traditional philosophical conception of reason, which
attempts to bootstrap the entire rhetorical problematic by appealing to
some “previously guaranteed objectivity”. The second conception is
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s own.
Our own interpretation would thus be much closer to what Danblon
calls the “minimalist conception of the universal audience”. What this way
of reading the universal audience emphasizes is not an ideal agreement or
consensus, but the specific form of address that it describes.88 While there is
undoubtedly a strong ethical dimension to Perelman’s philosophizing, it is
86  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §7, 31/TA, 41. As the passage has been slightly
modified in translating Danblon, here is the unmodified passage from the 1969 English
translation: “The basis for relying on the adherence of those who submit to the data
of experience or to the light shed by reason is the speaker’s affirmation of that which
corresponds to an objective fact, of that which constitutes a true and even necessary
assertion”(Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §7, 31–32).
87  Perelman, “The New Rhetoric and The Rhetoricians”, 242–243.
88  This way of interpreting the universal audience brings Perelman much closer to someone
like Chantal Mouffe than either Rawls or Habermas, as Mouffe herself acknowledges
in an interview (Lynn Worsham and Gary A. Olson, “Rethinking Political Community:
Chantal Mouffe’s Liberal Socialism”, JAC, Vol. 19, No. 2 (1999), 169.


172  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
important to recall the distinction we have drawn between his own philo­
sophical pluralism and the theory of argumentation set out in the Treatise.
To recall, on our reading the goal of the Treatise is to offer a general
theory capable of describing any kind of argumentation whatsoever, even
philosophical argumentation, without presupposing what should count as
rational in advance. While Danblon is correct to say that this approach
may run the risk of a certain kind of relativism, it certainly does not run
the risk of sliding into “an authoritarian position in which one would have
to define reason a priori”.89
To bring our discussion back to Cassin, we can now pose the ques­
tion directly: to what extent is Perelman’s rhetoric a paradigm case of an
intention-based rhetoric, that is, a rhetoric which presupposes the “good”
intentions of the speaker? Our response is this: Perelman’s way of think­
ing about rhetoric does indeed concern itself with the intentions of argu­
ers, but it is not intention-based in the sense Cassin alleges. For Cassin,
the intention, specifically the intention behind the universal audience, is a
hidden ethical norm serving as a bulwark against the dangerous excesses
of sophistry. For Perelman, however, this “intention”—whether in refer­
ence to the universal or any other kind of audience—refers simply to “the
ensemble of those whom the speaker wishes to influence by his argumenta­
tion”, i.e. Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s technical definition of audi­
ence. This definition applies no more to the universal audience than it does
to particular audiences. In other words, the “intention” of the speaker, this
time in Cassin’s sense of the term, is left open.
Indeed, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca even pre-empt Cassin’s sophis­
tic criticism when anticipating how philosophers might react to their pro­
ject. Where Cassin worries that the universal audience constrains speakers
to conform to a pre-established ethics, the philosopher usually has the
opposite concern. For the philosopher, rhetoric is usually understood to
invite, or at least leave the door open for, immoral or manipulative speech.
In both cases, however, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s answer is the
same. As they explain in §5:
the orator is nearly always at liberty to give up persuading an audience
when he cannot persuade it effectively except by the use of methods
that are repugnant to him. It should not be thought, where argument is
concerned, that it is always honorable to succeed in persuasion, or even
to have such an intention.90
89  Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”,
23, our translation.
90  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §5, 25/TA, 32.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  173
If Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca sought to provide a discourse with
a clear ethical banister, why would they leave so much freedom to the
speaker? On our reading, however, this openness to the speaker’s inten­
tion makes good sense. To emphasize the constitutive role of the audience
in discourse and to advise arguers to adapt themselves accordingly is not
to impose (im)morality. Rather, it is simply to provide a better and more
nuanced description of the situation in which we all find ourselves as argu­
ers. In fact, it is the only sound advice one can give if one’s argumentation
has any chance of being effective.
Our reading thus seeks to push Danblon’s response to Cassin one step
further. As Danblon rightly observes when discussing the particular audi­
ence, the “effect [the speaker] hopes to achieve is the essential criterion of
a discourse’s value”.91 For us, however, this point should also be extended
to the universal audience. In this way, we can agree with Danblon that in
certain places the Treatise “sounds surprisingly sophistic”.92
If a Sophist Speaks in a Forest
In this final section we come to the question of whether Cassin’s critique
is sufficient to justify abandoning Perelman’s conception of rhetoric in
favour of her sophistics. If we are to defend Perelman here, we need to
provide further evidence that his conception of rhetoric is capable, at least
in principle, of accommodating the sophistic “problematic of the effect”.
More concretely, this means showing to what extent the NRP can incorpo­
rate Cassin’s concern with performance (epideixis).
To begin, let us briefly retrace Cassin’s argument. In The Sophistic
Effect, Cassin distinguishes between the “ontological” and “logological”
paradigms of logos. She contends that the ontological paradigm, specifi­
cally the “Parmenidean-Aristotelian regime” of ontology, takes it that lan­
guage “commemorates” being; its task, under the norm of adequation, is
to say what is.93 Logology, by contrast, takes it that discourse creates being
in the first place, that being is an effect of saying. What Cassin is empha­
sizing is the power of language to create “worlds”, that is, the very being
disclosed by ontological discourse. It is in this sense that “discourse creates
being, and it is why its meaning can only be apprehended afterwards, from
the point of view of the world it produced”.94
91  Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”,
23, our translation.
92  Danblon, “La Nouvelle Rhétorique de Perelman et la question de l’auditoire universel”,
23, our translation.
93  Cassin, ES, 73.
94  Cassin, ES, 73.


174  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
But to what extent does this inversion really differ from Perelman? In
other words, what makes Cassin’s concept of performance truly sophistic
rather than “merely rhetorical”? And how meaningful is this distinction
in the first place? As Cassin sees it, what is at stake here is the status of
what she calls “world effects” (effets-monde), the outcome of sophistic
discourse overlooked by the philosopher’s ontological model of language.
As she explains, “one of these world effects may be the rhetorical effect on
the behaviour of the audience, but this is only one of its possible effects”.95
Here, Cassin is attempting to distinguish between rhetoric’s narrow con­
ception of effect, i.e. persuasive effects on an audience, and the broader
world-building effects of sophistic performance. Sophistic discourse is thus
“not only a performance in the epideictic sense of the term”—that is, in
the narrow Aristotelian sense of epideictic rhetoric, which he defines as
but one of three rhetorical genres.96 Rather, sophistic discourse “is through
and through a performative in the Austinian sense of the term … It is demi­
urgic, it fabricates the world, it makes it become”.97
As we noted in Chapter 1, this Austinian connection is not fully elabo­
rated until Quand dire, c’est vraiment faire [How to Really Do Things
with Words] (2018), the sequel to The Sophistic Effect.98 Through a close
reading of Austin’s How to Do Things with Words (1962), here Cassin
directly poses the question of how her sophistic conception of discourse
differs from what philosophers like Perelman call “rhetoric”. Her argu­
ment runs something like this: philosophy has traditionally understood
discourse along two principal axes, the axis of ontology (“speaking of”)
and the axis of rhetoric (“speaking to”). The dominant philosophical
tradition has always privileged the former, the traditional locus of truth.
Once this step is made, any way of speaking that strays too far from the
ontological axis can be accused of neglecting truth. From its very incep­
tion, then, what philosophers have traditionally called “rhetoric” was, by
default, subordinated to philosophy—whether an individual philosopher
is sympathetic to rhetoric or not. Cassin’s point, then, is that this rigid
“philosophical” opposition must be abandoned altogether, and discursive
performance must be thought on its own terms, as a “third dimension of
language”.99
95  Cassin, ES, 73.
96  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, trans. Kennedy, 1358b, 48.
97  Cassin, ES, 73.
98  As we indicated in Chapter 1, parts of Quand dire, c’est vraiment faire have been trans­
lated as a chapter in Sophistical Practice (2014). When possible, we will cite both texts
for reference.
99  Cassin, QD, 85/“How to Really Do Things with Words”, SP, 194.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  175
To this end, Cassin rereads these three axes of language (philosophy,
rhetoric, sophistics) against Austin’s distinction between locutionary, illo­
cutionary, and perlocutionary acts. By specifying the relation between
sophistic performance and Austin’s conception of the illocutionary, Cassin
looks to clarify how sophistics exceeds the opposition between philosophy
and rhetoric, or, at least, “rhetoric thought philosophically”.100
Without detailing each step of her close reading, Cassin concludes that,
for Austin, truth is but a particular case of the happiness or felicity of a
speech act. To emphasize the stakes of her interpretation, she contrasts it
to that of Stanley Cavell in “Passionate and Performative Utterance”.101 As
Cassin reads him, Cavell offers a “philosophical” interpretation of Austin
in that he tries to preserve the connection between performatives and the
ontological axis of language, the condition of truth. This means, as Austin
himself puts it in Lecture IV, “that for a certain performative utterance to
be happy, certain statements have to be true”.102 Yet, as Cassin points out,
the evidence that Cavell’s interpretation leans on comes before Austin’s
“sea change” in Lecture VIII, that is, where he abandons the performative/
constative distinction and introduces the distinction between locutionary,
illocutionary, and perlocutionary acts. The important difference is that
the performative/constative distinction is a distinction among statements
whereas the locutionary/illocutionary/perlocutionary distinction is a dis­
tinction within one and the same speech act. For Cassin, Cavell’s argument
thus sidesteps the radicality of Austin’s analysis after the sea-change. In
Austin’s own words:
the doctrine of the performative/constative distinction stands to the
doctrine of locutionary and illocutionary acts in the total speech act
as the special theory to the general theory. And the need for a general
theory arises simply because the traditional ‘statement’ is an abstrac­
tion, an ideal, and so is its traditional truth or falsity.103
With this the terms are reversed. No longer is the constative contrasted
with the performative but the newly baptized “locutionary” is subsumed
under the illocutionary, it too is now understood as an act. What is impor­
tant for Cassin about this reversal is not that some truths are required for
100  Cassin, QD, 90/SP, 197.
101  Stanley Cavell, “Passionate and Performative Utterance”, Contending with Stanley
Cavell, ed. Russell B. Goodman (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2005), 177–198.
Cavell briefly reflects on Cassin’s work in “Beginning to Read Barbara Cassin”, Hypatia,
Vol. 15, No. 4 (2000), 99–100.
102  Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 45.
103  Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 148.


176  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
a performative to be happy, which is Cavell’s point, but that truth itself
becomes a performative effect; “that the session finds itself open when the
performative is given under conditions of felicity. In other words, when the
performative is happy, the constative that it becomes is true”,104 For Cassin,
it is thus here that “we pass beyond the performative, stricto sensu unde­
finable, to touch on a performativity broadened out to performance”.105
On Cassin’s reading, then, Austin leaves Aristotle for Gorgias after
the sea-change, trades ontology for logology. Performativity is no longer
opposed to truth, but truth becomes a particular case of felicity, the onto­
logical discourse of philosophy is integrated as but one modality of sophis­
tic performance. Yet, even after the sea-change, what are we to make of
the difference between the illocutionary and the perlocutionary? This dif­
ference revolves around the question of “effects”, which, as Cassin points
out, “is where the difference between the perlocutionary and the illocu­
tionary is constantly stressed and where it constantly gets away”.106 The
problem is thus where to draw the line between illocutionary force and
perlocutionary effects. According to Cassin, the difficulty here arises from
Austin’s remark that the illocutionary is itself “bound up with effects”.107
While Austin wants to avoid reducing illocutionary acts to the perlocu­
tionary, to the achievement of a certain effect, and thereby collapsing the
distinction, he recognizes that an “effect must be achieved on the audience
if the illocutionary act is to be carried out”.108 The role of the audience is
therefore crucial. As Cassin herself admits, “If no one hears that the session
was open or that I excused myself, then it is as if I hadn’t said anything”.109
Yet, Cassin wants to be careful here as this discussion runs the risk of
blurring the distinction between the more or less intentional production of
effects on audiences (rhetoric) and the world effects characteristic of sophis­
tic logology. What matters for Cassin is that the narrow “rhetorical” con­
cern with effects on audiences does not draw our attention away from the
fact that sophistic discourse quite literally “performs another world”.110
On the one hand, we have “the perlocutionary, with its rhetorical effect
on the other by saying—subjective, one might say”. And, on the other, we
have “the illocutionary, the most active of speech acts, capable of directly
changing the state of the world in saying and exceeding the perlocution­
ary with something like an immediate and objective world effect”.111 Once

106  Cassin, QD, 120/SP, 210.
107  Cassin, QD, 120/SP, 211; Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 118.
108  Austin, How to Do Things with Words, 116.

Rhetoric on Trial (II)  177
we are clear on this distinction, the question now is whether the NRP can
accommodate Cassin’s sophistic conception of performance, or whether it
succumbs to her critique of “philosophical rhetoric”.
We have already shown that Cassin’s critique of Perelman rests on a
strong normative reading of the universal audience. But through our dis­
cussion of Danblon and the “thorny question” of the universal audience,
we have also seen that Cassin’s strong interpretation relies on a misreading
of §7 and, moreover, contravenes the explicit aims of the Treatise. This
casts doubt on Cassin’s claim that there is a hidden ethical intention to
Perelmanian rhetoric, an intention that constrains speakers to construct
their arguments in a universal way, excluding all those who disagree as
irrational or unreasonable. Yet, a further step is required to show that
what Perelman calls rhetoric is not the same as the “philosophical rheto­
ric” that worries Cassin. Our final step, then, is to show that Perelman is
not narrowly concerned with persuasive effects, but capable of accommo­
dating, at least in principle, what Cassin calls “performance”.
To begin let us return to the NRP’s rhetorical conception of argu­
mentation. For Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, argumentation is first
and foremost a way of acting. As they explain in “On Temporality as a
Characteristic of Argumentation”, for instance, unlike demonstration:
Argumentation seeking adherence … is essentially an action: the action
of an individual that we may call, in a very general sense, the orator,
upon an individual that we may also call, in a very general sense, the
listener [l’auditeur], and this action is done in order to trigger another
action.112
Breaking with the traditional demonstrative paradigm, this actional
approach means that argumentation is no longer conceived first as an
object of the mind, an abstract premise-conclusion structure, but an other-
directed action; to argue is “to act upon an audience, to modify an audi­
ence’s convictions or dispositions through discourse”.113 In a return to
Aristotle, the purpose or telos of argumentation is thus to be found in
the audience itself.114 Indeed, this point is built into the very definition
of argumentation found throughout the NRP. “The aim of argumenta­
tion”, as Perelman puts it in The Realm of Rhetoric, is “to elicit or increase
the adherence of the members of an audience to theses that are presented
112  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, ICO, 182/RH, 374.
113  Perelman, RR, 9/ER, 28.
114  On the Aristotelian origins of this point, see Tindale, The Philosophy of Argument and
Audience Reception, 52.


178  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
for their consent”.115 By engaging in the social practice of argumentation,
arguers tacitly seek to act on the adherence of their intended audience,
whether consciously aware of this or not.
To respond to Cassin’s critique that Perelmanian rhetoric harbours a
hidden ethical intention we should ask ourselves a simple but crucial ques­
tion. Why, in their frequently repeated definition of argumentation, do
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca consistently opt for the term “adherence”
rather than “persuasion” or “conviction”? After all, these are also terms
they discuss in the Treatise (§6). In other words, if Cassin is right that their
rhetorical conception of argumentation is solely and narrowly concerned
with producing (perlocutionary) effects, why do they bother introducing
“adherence” at all?
Our hypothesis is this: Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s view is that
arguers can only bring about effects in the world—even “world effects”
in Cassin’s sense—by acting on the pre-given adherence of an audience.
While the terms “persuade” and “convince” refer to the scope of an argu­
ment’s intended validity (i.e. its validity for a particular or universal audi­
ence), “adherence” refers to the total set of an audience’s intellectual and
affective dispositions.116 If a speaker has any intention of being effective, if
they are going to do things with words—whether persuading in a narrow
sense or performing new worlds—they must in some way act on the adher­
ence of an audience. Though lacking the vocabulary of performance and
performativity, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca prove strikingly prescient
when they describe argumentation as “an action which always tends to
modify a pre-existing state of affairs. This is true even of epideictic speech:
this is why it is argumentative”.117
It is to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s further credit that they leave
open the question of ends. Indeed, the very need for a general theory of argu­
mentation presupposes that arguments can be put to different ends—or, to
use more contemporary terminology, different “argumentative goals”.118
But, regardless of which goal an arguer has in mind, argumentation must
in every case act on the adherence of some audience. Thus, whether one
wants to simply open a session or create a praiseworthy Helen, a certain
modification of someone’s adherence is required. It is this modification of
115  Perelman, RR, 11/ER, 29.
116  A further distinction could be made here between adherence as that which pre-exists the
argumentative situation, and adherence as that which results from it.
117  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §13, 54, our emphasis/TA, 72.
118  For a critical review of different argumentative goals in argumentation theory, see Dima
Mohammed, “Goals in Argumentation: A Proposal for the Analysis and Evaluation of
Public Political Arguments”, Argumentation, Vol. 30, No. 3 (2016), 221–245.


Rhetoric on Trial (II)  179
adherence, and not the production of persuasion or conviction, that we
find in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s definition of argumentation.
While we are quickly reaching the limits of Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s expressed thoughts on this question, we need not go any further
to reach our verdict: even though they do not develop the point them­
selves, their thinking is sufficiently broad to incorporate the kinds of
effects associated with sophistic performance, Cassin’s “third dimension”
of language. In our view, the NRP can in principle accommodate the dis­
cursive production of any discursive effect. This would even include the
kinds of world effects that, for Cassin, are decisively non-rhetorical (in her
sense)—such as creating a new Helen, or redefining “sophistry” in a world
where the term’s pejorative meaning has reached a nearly Habermasian
consensus. Indeed, within the framework of the Treatise arguers are free
to challenge, contest, or even agitate their interlocutor; Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s theory of argumentation does not prescribe that argu­
ers must persuade their audience at any cost—the great concern of philo­
sophical and sophistic critics alike. Yes, sometimes we do argue in order to
persuade our interlocutor, sometimes to further develop our own thinking.
But we can also argue to create distance between our position and another,
to dissociate new ideas from the commonplace, the original from the cli­
ché. To do so, however, we need not pander to our interlocutor—just as
Cassin does not pander to her philosophical reader. Instead, one carefully
shifts one’s audience from their immediate opponent to a different audi­
ence, an onlooker, a spectator, or some broader (even future) public. It is
this new, more universal audience that will be used to judge the former and
which can be assessed and criticized with just the same right as the content
of arguments themselves.
This flexible rhetorical conception of argumentation bears little resem­
blance to the straightjacketed “philosophical rhetoric” criticized by Cassin.
Arguers are by no means forced to address themselves to, and in the man­
ner appropriate for, a universal audience in the strong, normative sense.
They are free to choose their own argumentative goals and they will find
an instructive and systematic arrangement of argumentative techniques in
the Treatise to help them do so. Yet whatever their goals and whichever
techniques they choose, they will have to construct some image of their
audience, as the adherence of some audience must be modified if their
discourse is to have any effect. For this reason, the concept of audience
is equally indispensable for the sophist. Instead of abandoning the notion
altogether, it should therefore be extended, extended to accommodate the
sorts of concerns raised by Cassin that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
admittedly do not address.


180  Rhetoric on Trial (II)
4  Conclusion
This brings us to the end of our trial. In this and the preceding chapter
we sought to assess the truth of Badiou and Cassin’s respective critiques
of rhetoric against the more positive assessment found in Perelman and
the NRP. In Chapter 3, we saw that Badiou would find Perelman’s rheto­
ric too sophistical, as it presupposes a pluralism that sutures philosophy
to its political condition. From Badiou’s perspective, Perelman’s rhetori­
cal philosophy would be regarded as parasitic on democratic politics,
which, lacking a strong conception of truth, cannot help but fall prey to
the infinite circulation of opinions. In this chapter, we saw that Cassin
finds Perelman’s rhetoric too philosophical. From Cassin’s perspective,
Perelman’s rhetorical concern with audiences prevents him from thinking
performance for its own sake, that is, beyond its immediate perlocution­
ary effect on the audience. Despite coming at rhetoric from very different
places, Badiou and Cassin ultimately agree that Perelman’s attempt to rec­
oncile philosophy and rhetoric is a dead end.
Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms are not without their merits. Indeed, both
authors identify important limitations to Perelman’s philosophy as well
as gaps and unresolved problems within the NRP more generally. What
Badiou’s criticism brings to light is Perelman’s failure to distinguish his own
philosophical position more clearly from the general theory of argumenta­
tion he developed with Olbrechts-Tyteca in the Treatise. Similarly, what
Cassin’s criticism brings to light is Perelman’s failure to develop more fully
(and in some cases simply clarify) his conception of the universal audience.
While we have not hesitated to acknowledge these issues, we maintain that
they do not undermine the value of the NRP’s central idea—its rhetorical
concept of audience. Indeed, in the case of both Badiou and Cassin we
have been able to identify the place for such a concept in their respective
projects—and even its role in their own argumentation. Thus, rather than
do away with rhetoric and discard the concept of audience altogether, the
verdict of these two chapters is that Badiou and Cassin’s criticisms should
instead motivate the search for an expanded conception of audience. It is
to this expanded conception of audience that we now turn.


5
We begin our search for an expanded conception of audience by turn­
ing to one of Perelman’s contemporaries, Paul Ricoeur (1913–2005). Best
known for his contributions to hermeneutics, Ricoeur is perhaps an unex­
pected source for rhetorical insight. Indeed, rhetoric is, at most, a fleet­
ing reference in his works. Yet, a careful examination reveals a far more
expansive engagement with rhetoric than even Ricoeur himself is aware.
Not only does his argumentation display a remarkable generosity to his
interlocutors and readers, but, more importantly, he also innovates many
of the fundamental concepts and categories long prized by rhetoricians. In
both senses Ricoeur’s proximity to rhetoric thus greatly exceeds his own
appreciation. A similar point is made by Andreea Deciu Ritivoi in Paul
Ricoeur: Tradition and Innovation in Rhetorical Theory (2006)—the only
monograph on the subject. By focusing on four topoi characteristic of the
rhetorical tradition operative in Ricoeur’s work—doxa, phronesis, epideic­
tic, and polis—she seeks to show “how rhetorical theory changes when we
look at it through Ricoeur’s lens”.1
While this line of thought has hardly been exhausted—evidenced by the
work of Ritivoi and Steven Mailloux and John Arthos among others2—
we want to approach Ricoeur’s relation to rhetoric in a different way.
Inversely, our question is not what Ricoeur’s philosophy brings to rhe­
torical theory, but what rhetoric brings to Ricoeur’s philosophy. In other
words, what stands out in Ricoeur’s philosophy when approached with an
eye to Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s problematic of the audience? This
1  Andreea Deciu Ritivoi, Paul Ricoeur. Tradition and Innovation in Rhetorical Theory
(Albany, NY: State University of New York Press, 2006), 47.
2  Ritivoi, “Ricoeur and Rhetorical Theory. Paul Ricoeur on Recognition in the Public
Sphere”, Ricoeur Across the Disciplines, ed., Scott Davidson (New York, NY: Continuum,
2010), 122–141; Steven Mailloux, Rhetoric’s Pragmatism. Essays in Rhetorical Hermeneu­
tics (University Park, PA: Penn State University Press, 2017); John Arthos, Hermeneutics
After Ricoeur (London: Bloomsbury Academic, 2018).
Extending the Audience
Ricoeur’s Missed Encounter with Perelman
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-6


182  Extending the Audience
question will occupy us for the remainder of our study. Our wager here
is that Ricoeur’s hermeneutic philosophy can be read against the grain to
extend the concept of audience found in the NRP. Should this wager pan
out, we will have further evidence that rhetoric is not the dead end Badiou
and Cassin take it to be.
In this chapter, however, we shall focus on the preliminary question
of whether it is legitimate to speak of a rhetorical conception of audi­
ence in Ricoeur’s work. For instance, it might be objected that we are
simply imposing our question where it does not belong. To address this
concern, we shall show that a distinctively rhetorical conception of audi­
ence, in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s sense, can be incorporated into
Ricoeur’s hermeneutic philosophy of action. As we shall see, however,
doing so involves proposing an alternative understanding of the relation­
ship between rhetoric and hermeneutics.
Before we begin, a few words are needed on what exactly connects
Perelman and Ricoeur.3 While the shortest line between these two con­
temporaries would undoubtedly be through Ricoeur’s critique of Perelman
in his essay “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics” (1986), we shall follow a
different and more circuitous route—through Ricoeur’s The Rule of
Metaphor (1975), where it is precisely the lack of any reference to Perelman
that proves most instructive. This point was not lost on Perelman. In the
Foreword to The Realm of Rhetoric (1977), Perelman expresses a certain
amount of frustration at his colleague (and a number of other French writ­
ers) for unduly neglecting his and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work. What is strik­
ing about The Rule of Metaphor—and what must certainly have struck
Perelman when reading it—is that in the text where Ricoeur deals most
explicitly with rhetoric there is but a single (and rather trivial) footnote to
the Treatise. Like Perelman, we cannot help but view Ricoeur’s omission
as something of a lost opportunity. As we shall argue, what is lost in this
missed encounter is their shared emphasis on the importance of audiences.
Consequently, both Perelman and Ricoeur miss out on the resources of the
other’s perspective—rhetoric and hermeneutics, respectively.
This chapter proceeds in two main steps. Before examining Perelman
and Ricoeur directly, we first need to acquaint ourselves with Ricoeur’s
3  Born just one year apart, the two philosophers seem to have already been acquainted in
the early 1950s. To our knowledge, their earliest recorded encounter was in Brussels at “Le
Colloque International de Phénoménologie” in April of 1951, organized by Father Herman
Leo Van Breda, founder and director of the Husserl archives at the Catholic University
of Louvain’s Institute of Philosophy. While only Ricoeur gave a paper, presenting along­
side Maurice Merleau-Ponty and others, Perelman’s name appears on the list of attendees
(Herman Leo Van Breda, ed., Problèmes actuels de la phénoménologie. Actes du colloque
international de phénoménologie, 1951 (Paris: Desclée de Brouwer, 1952), 162). We discuss
Ricoeur’s intervention at this colloquium in Chapter 6.


Extending the Audience  183
curious relation to rhetoric in two exemplary texts. This brings us to The
Rule of Metaphor where we then examine Ricoeur’s selective engagement
with rhetoric against the background of the broader “rhetorical turn” tak­
ing place in francophone philosophy and literary theory in the 1970s. In
a second step, we turn to Ricoeur’s missed encounter with Perelman and
identify their common concern with audiences. By bracketing Ricoeur’s
own (often borrowed) conception of rhetoric, we show that questions of
audience are not at all foreign to Ricoeur’s thinking, but something he
confronts on his own terms—albeit under the banner of hermeneutics.
We then illustrate this point by looking at Ricoeur’s explicit critique of
Perelman in “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”. Finally, this shared con­
cern with audiences brings us to consider how Ricoeur’s hermeneutics
can conceive of an expanded conception of audience, one that overcomes
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s more psychological notion. By analogy
with Ricoeur’s critique of romantic hermeneutics, we argue that it is possi­
ble to move away from Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s “romantic” con­
ception of audience in the direction of what we call the “audience of the
text”. Surprisingly, however, this does not require subordinating rhetoric
to hermeneutics. Resisting Ricoeur’s way of coordinating these two disci­
plines, we argue that the object of rhetoric would be better understood as
a specific dimension of discourse or “rhetoricity”.
1  Rhetoric in Ricoeur (1950s–70s)
In part one, we survey Ricoeur’s relation to rhetoric from the 1950s to the
1970s by looking at several exemplary texts. We begin in the 1950s with
“Work and the Word” (1953), an important early essay with many sub­
terranean resonances to Ricoeur’s work in the following decades. Moving
on to the 1960s, we then turn to Freud and Philosophy: An Essay on
Interpretation (1965) where rhetoric comes up in two places of interest.
As we will see, Ricoeur rarely thematizes rhetoric and when he does it is
exclusively in the context of its use by other writers. Finally, we come to the
1970s with The Rule of Metaphor where it becomes clear that Ricoeur’s
narrow disciplinary conception of rhetoric is belied by the broader rhetori­
cal themes in his work.4
4  This narrow view is why rhetoricians like James Crosswhite see Ricoeur as an obstacle to
what he calls “deep rhetoric”, which he introduces by challenging Ricoeur’s “Rhetoric-
Poetics-Hermeneutics” (Crosswhite, Deep Rhetoric, 17–28).


184  Extending the Audience
Work and the Word (1953)
Although he does not yet use the term, we would locate Ricoeur’s first
substantive engagement with rhetoric in his 1953 essay “Work and the
Word”, first published in the journal Esprit and later included in History
and Truth [Histoire et verité] (1955). Here, Ricoeur explores the nexus
between saying and doing, speech and action, and the various modalities
of the “power of speech”. Given that he does not use the term “rhetoric”,
this essay is all the more striking for the breadth of rhetorical themes that
it touches upon.5
As François Dosse recounts in his biography, Paul Ricœur. Les sens
d’une vie, Ricoeur’s essay is an intervention in a debate at Esprit con­
cerning the idea of a “civilization of work” [civilisation du travail], then
popular among militant Christian socialist intellectuals—in particular, the
economist Henri Bartoli who coined the phrase.6 As Dosse notes, Bartoli’s
position in “Les chrétiens vers une civilisation du travail” [Christians
towards a civilization of work] (1952) is inspired by Marx’s critique of
alienation as well as the theology of Father Marie-Dominique Chenu.7
Drawing from his experiences with various unions and worker’s organi­
zations in Grenoble, Bartoli articulates a vision of a civilization freed from
the dehumanizing conditions of work imposed by the capitalist mode of
production. For Bartoli, a genuine civilization of work would no longer
reduce the human being to the value of their labour, to their role in produc­
tion dictated by capitalist social relations. Rather, it would be one where
the economy is organized around a more complete picture of the human
person.8 Bartoli’s contribution is thus to analyse the aims and obstacles
to a civilization that would truly be “the result of the action of workers
themselves”.9
Responding to Bartoli, Ricoeur begins his intervention by clarifying that
his own aspirations are “in complete accord with the presuppositions of
this philosophy of work”, and that he fully adheres “to these philosophical
5  The term “rhetoric” is used pejoratively in Kelby’s English translation, but it does not
appear in the original. See Ricoeur, “Work and the Word”, History and Truth, trans.
Charles A. Kelby (Evanston, IL: Northwestern University Press, [1965] 2007), 218/“Travail
et parole”, Histoire et verité (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, [1955] 1967), 238.
6  François Dosse, Paul Ricœur. Les sens d’une vie (Paris: Éditions La Découverte, 2001), 176.
7  Dosse, Paul Ricœur, 177. Father Chenu is considered by some to be the grandfather of the
liberation theology movement owing to his influence on Peruvian Priest Gustavo Gutiérrez,
who studied with Chenu in Paris and cites him with some frequency in the founding book
on liberation theology (see Gustavo Gutiérrez, A Theology of Liberation: History, Politics,
and Salvation, Revised ed., trans. Sister Caridad Inda and John Eagleson (Maryknoll, NY:
Orbis Books, [1971] 1988)).
8  Bartoli, “Les chrétiens vers une civilisation du travail”, 15.
9  Bartoli, “Les chrétiens vers une civilisation du travail”, 23, our translation.


Extending the Audience  185
presuppositions and to this socio-economic aspiration”.10 Ricoeur’s aim is
thus not to refute Bartoli but to point out the limitations of his position.
At issue here is the question of whether the notion of “work” [le travail]
truly exhausts the human condition in the way Bartoli seems to suggest.
Does work, labour, or praxis truly encompass all forms of human activity?
Does it capture, for example, all acts of love, play, and contemplation? As
a “notion which signifies everything no longer signifies anything”, Ricoeur
is hesitant to overextend the meaning of “work” to cover the full expanse
of human activities.11
Instead, Ricoeur attempts to combine this interpretation of the human
condition with another term, one which “penetrates [work] through and
through”—that of speech, the spoken word [la parole].12 Pre-figuring
much of what he will later develop in terms of symbolic mediation,13 here
Ricoeur observes that philosophies of work such as Bartoli’s (whether they
be of a Marxist, existentialist, or Christian variety) understand “work”
in a void; they fail to appreciate it against the backdrop of any contrary
notion and are thus unable to determine its scope. To avoid this limitation,
Ricoeur will therefore take speech as work’s “other”—“this other among
others which justifies and contests the glory of work”.14
Ricoeur’s first contribution to the debate, then, is to set the static dis­
tinction between work and word, doing and saying, or labour and speech,
into dialectical motion. Ricoeur begins by assuming “the most favora­
ble interpretation to the pragmatist conception of language”, specifically
Pierre Janet’s idea that the simplest and most primitive form of speech was
the “imperative cry”.15 Take for instance the imperative “go!”. On the one
hand, this imperative utterance stands out from the plane of action—it
does not really “take place” in the same sense as the going itself. On the
other hand, the imperative remains bound to the plane of action: it is moti­
vated by a pre-existing course of action—one in which it seeks to intervene
or to disrupt; it anticipates a new course of action and incites someone
to bring it about. In this sense, “the spoken word outruns every gesture
by signifying it. It is the understood meaning of what is to be done”.16
Although operating at a certain distance from the plane of action, it is in
action that the imperative word finds its motivating exigency as well as its
practical terminus.

11  Ricoeur, HT, 198–199/HV, 239–240.
12  Ricoeur, HT, 199/HV, 240.
13  We discuss Ricoeur’s theory of the symbolic mediation of action in Chapter 6, p. 245.
14  Ricoeur, HT, 200/HV, 241.
15  Ricoeur, HT, 200/HV, 241.
16  Ricoeur, HT, 201/HV, 242.


186  Extending the Audience
The imperative use of language thus plays an important role in the pro­
cess by which agents take a step back from a course of action and submit
it to reflection. The imperative, Ricoeur explains,
allows for the fulfillment of a first distanciation, a first reflective with­
drawal, which, thanks to the interval, the gap hollowed into the plenum
of the gesture in the act of being performed, allows for the projective
design of the total gesture.17
By way of example, a tool left to itself is resistant to change; it simply does
what it does. However, when problems arise in practice, it is the word and
its power of signification that enable the user to reflect on and question
the tool’s use.18 The word holds the tool in suspense, inviting the possibil­
ity of asking how it might be used differently. As Ricoeur observes, even
those words which seem to have the least intention of acting on and trans­
forming the world—the mathematical language of Euclid and Galileo, for
example—are responsible for the qualitative leap from tool to machine, to
those technologies that we too often forget cannot work without the word.
Ricoeur thus concludes that it is no longer tenable to subscribe to any
behaviourist or epiphenomenalist interpretation of language. Ricoeur’s
target here is the (vulgar) Marxist view that language is merely a compo­
nent of a society’s superstructure, distinct from and ultimately determined
by its economic base (or infrastructure). To the contrary, Ricoeur contends
that language is, in fact, “just as much infrastructure as superstructure”—a
thesis which he will develop further in the coming decades.19
It is within this context that we come to the distinctive “power of
speech” [la puissance de la parole]. Having seen the interpenetration of
work and word, Ricoeur now asks what “new operation” the word brings
into play within the confines of work.20 In other words, how can we under­
stand the peculiar “work” of speech without confusing it with “work” in
the sense of production? Ricoeur’s response consists in identifying three
ways in which language acts or operates: (1) the influence it has on others,
(2) its reflexive action on the speaker, and (3) the operation of the sign on
meaning itself.
(1) Action on others—The first aspect of the power of speech is the
way that it acts on others. This aspect is quite clear with respect to the
17  Ricoeur, HT, 201, translation modified/HV, 243.
18  It is unclear in this essay whether Ricoeur is attributing the power of what he will later
call “distanciation” to language alone, or whether it is already inherent to practice. In any
case, the latter is more accurate from a descriptive point of view.
19  Ricoeur, HT, 202/HV, 244.
20  Ricoeur, HT, 203/HV, 245.


Extending the Audience  187
imperative word, which, as Ricoeur notes, is the use of speech that most
resembles work. To eliminate the looming ambiguity, however, Ricoeur is
quick to distinguish between the different senses of action in the context
of work and speech. Whereas the action of work pertains to production,
the action of speech pertains to influence. Production acts on its object in
an impersonal way—as an impersonal “it”—and without any significant
reciprocity between the relata. Influence, by contrast, operating in the sec­
ond person, is inescapably personal and implies a reciprocal transforma­
tion of speaker and hearer (or audience). While the hammer is no more
than superficially affected by the nail it strikes, the authority of the captain
who commands the soldier to “go!” depends on whether they accept the
command and carry out the action or not. To accept the command is to
reaffirm the captain’s authority, to refuse is to undermine it. The influ­
ence of speech is thus not to be confused with the effects of work: unlike
work which operates in the register of cause and effect, speech intends to
bring about a “sequel” [une suite]—in the narrative sense of a subsequent
action. The most appropriate verb phrase for imperative speech is thus
not so much faire (to do) as the more mediated “faire faire” (in this con­
text, to get someone to do something). By distinguishing between these
two planes, Ricoeur can help overcome the philosophical prejudice that
rhetoric is merely concerned with “working” on audiences in the sense of
production when it is in fact attempting to influence them. Although not
without its own risks, it is at the level of influence that the word operates,
playing, for example, an important role in ordering the social relations that
organize production.
(2) Action on oneself—The second aspect of the power of speech is its
reflexive action on the speaker. Much like the way that the imperative
word orders social relations, the very subjectivity of the speaker is ordered
by the interior word that accompanies speech. Indeed, it is through the
imperative word, Ricoeur writes, that the human being becomes a “sig­
nifying being” in the first place.21 To speak is not just to act on the other,
it is also to act on oneself, to decide oneself; the imperative involves a
decision, an elucidation of oneself that “breaks up the previous affective
confusion”.22 Without the imperative word, that is, “if I say nothing to
myself, I do not emerge from the inhuman confusion of the beast. Without
the word I am no more ordered than my work”.23
(3) Action on meaning—The third aspect of the power of speech is the
way that it acts on meaning itself. This aspect, Ricoeur explains, is the most
21  Ricoeur, HT, 203/HV, 245.
22  Ricoeur, HT, 203/HV, 246.
23  Ricoeur, HT, 204/HV, 246.


188  Extending the Audience
hidden of the three: it is the “operation of the sign itself on meaning”.24
If speech is able to incite others to action, it is not because it “does” or
“makes” anything in the strict sense. In the case of the imperative, if speech
incites another to action it is because “it signifies what is to be done”.25
The word thus “phrases” action by identifying and arranging various ele­
ments. The command “go!”, for example, identifies the addressee as a
subject capable of undertaking the action; it also identifies the speaker as
someone capable of issuing commands. But most importantly, it also signi­
fies the “going” as the action “to be done”—an intention that, in this case,
can only be fulfilled by the “sequel” or subsequent action of the addressee.
The word thus lends grammar to action, phrasing it, articulating it such
that it can emerge from its initial state of confusion.
By focusing on the specific activity of language in contradistinction to
that of work, Ricoeur finds himself among the first philosophers of the
20th century to investigate the pragmatic or performative dimension
of language.26 Here, Ricoeur joins the likes of Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca, Austin, and, more recently, Cassin, who—in different ways—each
seek to overturn the age-old assumption that to say something, at least in
all cases worth considering, “is always and simply to state something”.27
Although we have remained with the example of the imperative while
introducing these three aspects of the power of speech, Ricoeur’s analysis
applies to other modes of speech as well, which he discusses at varying lev­
els of detail—e.g. interrogative speech, optative speech, poetic speech, etc.
Yet, as Ernst Wolff observes, what matters here is not the exhaustiveness
of Ricoeur’s list or the terminology he uses, but his conclusions: specifi­
cally, the recognition that there are many ways to use language and they
do different things.28

as the inspiration for his reflections comes from elsewhere. Specifically, Ricoeur refers to
Walter Porzig’s Das Wunder der Sprache: Probleme, Methoden und Ergebnisse der mod­

Extending the Audience  189
Despite the fact that Ricoeur makes no mention of rhetoric in this text,
it is striking that the three aspects of the power of language he identifies—
(1) its influence on the other, (2) its reflexive action on the speaker, and (3)
the operation of the sign on meaning itself—reveal a concern with three
features of language captured by the classical rhetorical triad of pathos,
ethos, and logos—which, as Meyer argues, is what makes of rhetoric a
“well-defined and unified discipline”.29 What makes this parallel more
than a simple point of interest is that these three aspects will remain cen­
tral themes throughout Ricoeur’s writings in the guise of (1) otherness, (2)
selfhood, and (3) the symbolic mediation of action. Looking back from the
perspective of Ricoeur’s later writings, “Work and the Word” thus marks
the beginning of a long-term, and largely unexplored, engagement with
rhetoric.
Freud and Philosophy (1965)
The first noteworthy appearance of “rhetoric” in Ricoeur’s published
works is in De l’interprétation. Essai sur Freud [Freud and Philosophy: An
Essay on Interpretation] (1965). Yet, as it is hermeneutics and not rhetoric
that is at issue in this text, close attention must be paid to the context of
the two places in which rhetoric is discussed in order to get a sense of how
Ricoeur understands it.
The three books that make up Freud and Philosophy constitute Ricoeur’s
first major study in hermeneutics. While the term “hermeneutics” makes
its first appearance already in The Symbolism of Evil (1960)—and is argu­
ably already in practice as early as 1953 in “Work and the Word”—Freud
and Philosophy is Ricoeur’s first book to thematize the “hermeneutic
field” and attempt to coordinate its relation to “reflective philosophy”.30
Although motivated by this larger problematic, this text is first and fore­
most a reading of Freud and the place of psychoanalysis amongst compet­
ing interpretations of the human being and human culture.
