neutique et problématologie de notre condition langagie﻿̀re (Paris: Presses Universitaires
de France, 2000).
30 “A reflective philosophy is the contrary of a philosophy of the immediate. The first truth—
I am, I think—remains as abstract and empty as it is invincible; it has to be ‘mediated’
by the ideas, actions, works, institutions, and monuments that objectify it. It is in these
objects, in the widest sense of the word, that the Ego must lose and find itself” (Ricoeur,
Freud and Philosophy: An Essay on Interpretation, trans. Dan Savage (New Haven, CT:
Yale University Press, 1970), 43/De l’interprétation. Essais sur Freud (Paris: Éditions du
Seuil, 1965), 53–54).


190  Extending the Audience
This conflict of interpretations brings Ricoeur into confrontation with
problems of language. It is important to recall that it is 1965, and Ricoeur
is writing in the wake of structuralism, particularly the structural anthro­
pology of Claude Levi-Strauss. New advancements coming from linguistics
posed a radical challenge to existing methodologies in the human and social
sciences as well as traditional forms of philosophical reflection. Indeed,
structuralism—alongside a number of other intellectual currents coming
from various disciplines and philosophical traditions—had unleashed a
powerful centrifugal force in the house of language, a force that called into
question the systematic unity of discourse long sought by philosophy.
Responding to the problem of the “unity of human language”, Ricoeur
echoes one of the conclusions of “Work and the Word”. “How can lan­
guage be put to such diverse uses as mathematics and myth, physics and
art?”, he asks.31 This time, however, what is at stake is not the question
of how the various uses of language inform and limit the pretensions of
work, but what these uses tell us about the human being. In Freud and
Philosophy Ricoeur’s contribution to this broader question is the study
of one particular use of language—symbolic language, that is, linguistic
expressions which mean something other than what is said.
Without adhering strictly to Freud, Ricoeur reserves the term “sym­
bolic” for a particular region of human discourse. Symbolic language
refers to that use of language where an initial meaning opens up to a sec­
ond, less immediate meaning via the first. Psychoanalysis thus finds itself
as one competing approach within the broader “hermeneutic field”, which
is itself part of a still broader theory of language that would encompass
the totality of human discourse. Although it is in the context of this wider
problematic that his investigation is situated, Ricoeur’s aim here is charac­
teristically modest; there is no pretence to provide a comprehensive philos­
ophy of language. Freud, and psychoanalysis more generally, represent but
one perspective on language that must be worked through if the full range
of signs and symbols through which human beings express themselves is
to be understood. All Ricoeur wants to show is that the confrontation
with hermeneutics is necessary for philosophy and that issues pertaining to
symbolic discourse can be addressed in an epistemologically sound manner
despite their resistance to univocity.32
31  Ricoeur, FP, 3, our emphasis/DI, 13.
32  In “Philosophy and Language”, a 1965 interview with Alain Badiou for the French televi­
sion series Le Temps des philosophes (recorded the same year as the publication of Freud
and Philosophy), Ricoeur explains his approach to the problem posed by the diversity of
“language games”, see Tho and Bianco, eds., Badiou and the Philosophers, 68-69/Paul
Ricoeur and Alain Badiou, “Philosophie et langage. Emissions de philosophie produites
par la Radio-Télévision scolaire en 1965–1966”, Cahiers philosophiques (1993), 63). The
video recording of the interview, along with the entire series, is freely available via Gallica


Extending the Audience  191
In the first chapter of Book I, Ricoeur establishes the scope of his study
and defines his preferred notion of “symbol”. His definition charts a middle
course between an overly broad and an overly narrow definition. On the
one hand, there is the broad meaning of symbol found in Ernst Cassirer’s
Philosophy of Symbolic Forms (1923–29), which, according to Ricoeur,
“designates the common denominator of all the ways of objectivizing,
of giving meaning to reality”.33 On the other hand, there is the narrow
meaning, originating in Platonism and Neoplatonism, which understands
the relation of first meaning to second meaning as an “analogy”.34 For
Ricoeur, the former is too all-encompassing to strictly designate multiple-
meaning expressions, and the latter is too restrictive in the sense that anal­
ogy is merely one of the ways in which manifest and latent meaning can
be related.
To justify his preference for a middle course, in the second chapter
Ricoeur attempts to delimit the field of application of the concept “sym­
bol” in relation to the act of interpretation. Ricoeur insists that the relation
between these two terms is not external but internal. “A symbol exists”,
he explains, “where linguistic expression lends itself by its double or mul­
tiple meaning to a work of interpretation”.35 Mutually implicated, the two
terms must therefore be defined together.
As with the concept of symbol, Ricoeur’s preferred definition of inter­
pretation is also located between two others: one short, that of biblical
exegesis, and one long, that of Aristotle in On Interpretation. Beginning
with Aristotle, Ricoeur traces the origins of philosophy’s resistance to her­
meneutics and all things equivocal. In Aristotle, the term hermêneia does
not refer to anything like a science of signification but to signification itself.
In this sense, to interpret is simply to signify something, to “say something
about something”. Although Aristotle’s notion of interpretation should, in
principle, encompass the signification of any complete sentence (e.g. com­
mands, wishes, questions, declarative sentences, etc.), the “logical aim” of
the philosopher, which defines signification in terms of univocity, prevents
the development of “semantics for its own sake”.36
It is here that “rhetoric” makes its first appearance. By systematically
preferring univocity over equivocity, Aristotle attempts to insulate logic
and the particular use of language that concerns it from all others. “The
logician”, Ricoeur explains, “leaves the other types of discourse to rhetoric
(https://gallica​.bnf​.fr/), the online digital library of the Bibliothéque nationale de France.
Note that the pages cited above were cut from the video and appear only in the transcript.
33  Ricoeur, FP, 10/DI, 20.
34  Ricoeur, FP, 16–17/DI, 26–27.
35  Ricoeur, FP, 18/DI, 28.
36  Ricoeur, FP, 22–23/DI, 32–33.


192  Extending the Audience
and poetics and retains only declarative discourse”.37 Here is where this
exclusion occurs in Aristotle’s On Interpretation:
Every sentence is significant … but not every sentence is a statement-
making sentence [i.e. a proposition], but only those in which there is
truth or falsity. There is not truth or falsity in all sentences: a prayer is a
sentence but is neither true or false. The present investigation deals with
the statement-making sentence; others we can dismiss since considera­
tion of them belongs to the study of rhetoric or poetry.38
We are already familiar with the consequences of this gesture from our dis­
cussion of Cassin and what she calls Aristotle’s “decision on meaning”.39
Now, in the context of Ricoeur’s discussion, this decision leaves rheto­
ric, poetics, psychoanalysis, and hermeneutics—as well as every other dis­
cipline not primarily concerned with declarative discourse—beyond the
reach of philosophical rationality.
For Ricoeur, however, if something like a comprehensive philosophy
of language is to be found, the struggle against sophistic equivocity needs
to be counterbalanced by a struggle against Eleatic univocity. For even in
Aristotle the notion of being cannot be univocally defined. After all, there
are “many senses in which a thing may be said to ‘be’”.40 Indeed, “The
famous distinction of the many meanings of being”, Ricoeur explains, “is
not an anomaly in discourse, an exception in the theory of signification”.41
To the contrary, he continues, it “cuts across the whole of discourse”
and cannot be definitively overcome.42 Aristotle’s restrictive definition of
interpretation thus leads to a semantics detached from the rational aspira­
tions of logic. Yet, even within logic’s insulated sphere, the recognition of
the multiple meanings of being “opens a breach in the purely logical and
ontological theory of univocity”.43 Thus, even in Aristotle the attempt to
delimit signification to univocity cannot fully sever its connection with the
polysemy of ordinary language.

problématique aristotélicienne (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 1962), 201).

Extending the Audience  193
This point is even clearer in relation to symbolic logic, which—more
directly than Aristotle—struggles to make a clean break with “ordinary
language and its incurable ambiguity”. As Ricoeur explains:
This struggle begins with the expulsion of all that does not give factual
information from the properly cognitive sphere of language. The rest of
discourse is then classified under the heading of the emotive and horta­
tory functions [function directive] of language; that which does not give
factual information expresses emotions, feelings or attitudes, or urges to
others to behave in some particular way.44
By focusing exclusively on this particular and more manageable region of
signification, symbolic logic thus excludes the entire hermeneutic field as
well as the obligation to provide a unified and systematic theory of human
discourse.
Although Ricoeur’s aim in Freud and Philosophy is to make room for
hermeneutics in philosophy by locating its place within the movement of
reflection, his argument equally makes room for rhetorical and poetical
discourse. For the time being, however, it is sufficient that we recognize the
peripheral place of rhetoric in the context of Ricoeur’s project.
Having situated his reading of Freud within the larger problematic of
language in Book I and having carried out that reading over the course
of Book II, it is finally in Book III that Ricoeur offers his philosophical
interpretation by confronting Freud’s discourse with others (specifically,
scientific psychology and phenomenology).45 It is here that the second and
more revealing reference to rhetoric can be found.
Rhetoric’s second appearance takes place in the context of Ricoeur’s
critique of the “linguistic” interpretation of the unconscious, specifically
that of Jacques Lacan.46 Here Ricoeur pushes back against Lacan’s dic­
tum that the unconscious is “structured like a language”.47 For Ricoeur,
this interpretation is not capable of wholly replacing Freud’s economic
interpretation. At most, he argues, the linguistic interpretation can be

45  Ricoeur, FP, 344/DI, 363.

itself”—thereby avoiding discussions of “dissidents turn adversaries”, “students turned
dissidents”, as well as “disciples turned creators”, the category where he places Lacan
(Ricoeur, FP, xi/DI, 8). For a full-length study of Ricoeur’s relation to Lacan, see Karl
Simms, Ricoeur and Lacan (London: Continuum, 2007).

Alain Miller, trans. Russell Grigg (New York, NY: W.W. Norton & Company, [1981]
1993), 166–167.


194  Extending the Audience
seen to run parallel to unconscious mechanisms, which only reinforces
Ricoeur’s point that these mechanisms “are accessible only in their rela­
tion to hermeneutics”.48 What Ricoeur concludes from this is that Lacan’s
comparison with the unconscious should be made not with linguistics but
with “rhetoric”.49
It is important to note that Ricoeur’s critique of Lacan here relies on
Émile Benveniste’s “Remarks on the Function of Language in Freudian
Theory” (1956).50 This reference is significant because it is from Benveniste
that Ricoeur borrows his operative conception of rhetoric. Referring to
Benveniste Ricoeur explains that rhetoric, “with its metaphors, its meton­
ymies, its synecdochies, its euphemisms, its allusions, its antiphrasis, its
litotes, is concerned not with phenomena of language but with procedures
of subjectivity that are manifested in discourse”.51 Despite Benveniste’s
interesting phrase “procedures of subjectivity manifested in discourse”,
this long list of tropes indicates that rhetoric is being taken in the nar­
row sense of “stylistics”—as Benveniste himself reveals in the passage cited
by Ricoeur, remarking that the properties Freud attributes to dreams (as
recounted) are more comparable to “style” than “language” in a fuller
sense.52
While authors such as Perelman will reject the reduction of rhetoric to
stylistics, it is this sense of rhetoric that both Benveniste and Ricoeur have
in mind. Indeed, as Ricoeur’s subsequent discussion reveals, the rhetorical
analogy of metaphor and metonymy to the unconscious process of dis­
placement and condensation is based on a narrow, stylistic conception of
rhetoric that takes as its base unit the single word rather than larger units
of discourse such as the sentence or the text. As we will see, it is this nar­
row conception of rhetoric that Ricoeur carries into The Rule of Metaphor.
The Rule of Metaphor (1975)
Thus far we have discussed two of Ricoeur’s texts, one from the 1950s and
one from the 1960s. In “Work and the Word” there is no explicit mention
of rhetoric, but it is not hard to see that Ricoeur’s discussion touches on
rhetorical themes if the term is taken in a sufficiently broad sense. In Freud
and Philosophy, by contrast, Ricoeur does openly discuss rhetoric in his
48  Ricoeur, FP, 396/DI, 416.
49  Ricoeur, FP, 400/DI, 420.
50  Émile Benveniste, Problems in General Linguistics, trans. Mary Elizabeth Meek (Miami,
FL: University of Miami Press, 1971), 65–75/Problémes de linguistique générale (Paris:
Gallimard, 1966), 75–87.
51  Ricoeur, FP, 400/DI, 420.
52  Ricoeur, FP, 400, n. 70/DI, 420, n. 70.


Extending the Audience  195
attempt to bring symbolic discourse within the purview of philosophical
reflection. Here, however, the term is taken in the narrow sense of stylis­
tics, which refers primarily to the form of discourse and takes the word as
its basic unit of meaning.
From the mid to late 1970s the term “rhetoric” begins to appear with
more frequency in Ricoeur’s writings. His investigations into the symbolic
structure of action, the social imaginary, the creative power of metaphors
and plots, and other related themes, inevitably brings him into contact
with authors and works associated with the “rhetorical turn” taking place
within the broader linguistic turn in 20th century thought. It is in these
years that the names of I.A. Richards, Gérard Genette, Roland Barthes,
Wayne Booth, Chaïm Perelman, Kenneth Burke, Clifford Geertz, Hayden
White, and Tzvetan Todorov, among others, begin to appear with greater
frequency in Ricoeur’s writings. Yet, among Ricoeur’s texts from the 70s it
is most notably in The Rule of Metaphor [La métaphore vive] (1975) that
we find a substantive and extended engagement with rhetoric.
The Rule of Metaphor is a patient, but ambitious work dedicated to
the question of how meaning is innovated through the use of language.
In essence, Ricoeur’s thesis is that meaning is created metaphorically.
Rather than understand metaphor as but one figure of speech—tradition­
ally understood as the substitution of one word (literal) for another (fig­
urative)—he understands metaphor as “the rhetorical process by which
discourse unleashes the power that certain fictions have to redescribe
reality”.53 Leaving aside Ricoeur’s peculiar use of the term “rhetoric” for
the time being—peculiar because from here on he will instead describe
metaphor as a “poetic” process—this dynamic conception of metaphor
is gradually developed over the course of eight studies that cover a wide
range of authors and disciplinary perspectives dealing with metaphor.
As Figure 5.1 depicts, the book is structured according to various levels
of analysis each of which contributes something to Ricoeur’s notion of
living metaphor. The first two studies begin with rhetoric, which, accord­
ing to Ricoeur, is concerned with metaphor at the level of the single word.
Rhetorical theories of metaphor are thus considered “substitution theo­
ries” in the sense that a metaphor is understood as the substitution of a lit­
eral word for a figurative one. Studies 3, 4, and 5 turn to semantics, which
treats metaphor at the level of the sentence. At this level, the locus of meta­
phor is no longer just the word but the statement as a whole. What the
statement contributes is the operation of predication necessary to account
for the metaphorical innovation of meaning. Finally, in studies 6, 7, and 8
53  Ricoeur, The Rule of Metaphor: The Creation of Meaning in Language, trans. Robert
Czerny et al. (Milton Park: Routledge, 2003), 5/La métaphore vive (Paris: Seuil, 1975),
11.


196  Extending the Audience
a hermeneutic perspective is taken, and metaphor is considered at the level
of texts, that is, units of meaning composed of, but larger than, words and
sentences. It is this perspective which allows Ricoeur to consider not only
the sense of metaphorical discourse but also its reference—that is, how
metaphor relates to reality. Only at this level can metaphor be seen as
more than a stylistic figure but the way that meaning is created through the
use of language. This discursive conception of metaphor, living metaphor,
serves Ricoeur as a criterion for assessing the comprehensiveness of a phi-
losophy of language. Yet, he is careful to point out that the development of
perspectives in his study does not seek to “replace rhetoric with semantics
and the latter with hermeneutics”. Instead, the point is “to justify each
approach within the limits of the corresponding discipline and to demon-
strate the systematic continuity of viewpoints by following the progression
from word to sentence and from sentence to discourse”.54
If the primary aim of the book is to account for the metaphorical process
by which meaning is created, Ricoeur must equally address the question of
“what philosophy is implied in the movement that carries the investigation
from rhetoric to semantics and from sense toward reference”.55 This aspect
of the book reconnects us with a thread running through the two texts we
have already examined. Ricoeur now provides a more substantive response
54 Ricoeur, RM, 6/MV, 12.
55 Ricoeur, RM, 304/MV, 323.




Hermeneutics
(text)

Extending the Audience  197
to the question he posed at the beginning of Freud and Philosophy con­
cerning the problematic unity of the many forms of human discourse.
Somewhat lost in translation, but well captured in in the original French
title, is the idea that metaphor is where language is most visibly alive.
This is meant in two senses. The first concerns language itself and the
second concerns the hearer, reader, or, more generally, the audience who
must interpret the metaphor. First, metaphor is living in the sense that it
breathes new meaning into an already constituted language. But, in a sec­
ond sense, metaphor is also living “by virtue of the fact that it introduces
the spark of imagination into a ‘thinking more’ at the conceptual level”.56
Sticking with the first for now, Ricoeur understands the metaphorical
use of language essentially as an innovation in meaning—in terms of both
sense and reference. Just as metaphor creates new lexical meaning (sense)
out of the ruins of an impossible literal interpretation, it equally creates
a new reference, a “second-order reference”, that redescribes reality by
means of the tension between two previously unrelated semantic fields. For
Ricoeur, metaphor “invents” in both senses of the word: “what it creates,
it discovers and what it finds, it invents”—a conception of discourse that
blurs the sharp line Cassin draws between ontological and performative
discourse.57
Over ten years after Freud and Philosophy, Ricoeur comes back to the
problem of the unity of human discourse, arguing in favour of a “relative
pluralism of forms and levels of discourse”.58 Here again Ricoeur seeks
a middle ground between two equally untenable positions: on the one
hand, a strong Wittgensteinian position that sees nothing in the diversity
of human discourse but “a radical heterogeneity of language games”, and,
on the other, a totalizing position that would reduce them all to one foun­
dational discourse. Ricoeur attempts to strike a delicate balance between
these two positions by thematizing the role of speculative discourse, that
is, philosophical discourse itself. To the first position, Ricoeur argues that
speculative discourse marks a break, or stands in “discontinuity” with, the
plurality of language games—to stick with Wittgenstein’s terminology. As
for the second, he argues that language games cannot simply be reduced to
or grounded in one overarching meta-discourse. To the contrary, one of the
most interesting insights in The Rule of Metaphor is that the metaphoric
process is itself responsible for the constitution of the diverse “semantic
fields” in which language is used.59

57  Ricoeur, RM, 283/MV, 301.
58  Ricoeur, RM, 304/MV, 323–324.

198  Extending the Audience
To clarify Ricoeur’s position on speculative discourse—and, by exten­
sion, his own stance on philosophy and the method he adopts in The Rule
of Metaphor—it is instructive to examine his critique of Jacques Derrida’s
argument in “White Mythology: Metaphor in the Text of Philosophy”
(1971).60 In this essay Derrida argues, in a strongly Nietzschean vein, that
philosophical discourse is nothing more than metaphorical discourse that
has forgotten its own metaphorical status. For Derrida, Ricoeur explains,
“discourse on metaphor is itself infected by the universal metaphoricity
of philosophical discourse”, which leads to the following paradox: “there
is no discourse on metaphor that is not stated within a metaphorically
engendered conceptual network. There is no non-metaphorical standpoint
from which to perceive the order and the demarcation of the metaphorical
field”.61 For Derrida, then, philosophical concepts are something like Bruce
Willis’s character Malcolm Crowe in M. Night Shyamalan’s The Sixth
Sense—they do not realize they are already dead. Thus deconstructed, phi­
losophy is but a macabre procession of metaphysical ghosts through the
cemetery of their own forgotten metaphoricity.
We can summarize Ricoeur’s response to this position in two steps.
First, Ricoeur argues that Derrida overemphasizes the opposition between
the literal and the figurative. Derrida considers metaphor only from the
perspective of semiotics and not semantics (a broader trend that Ricoeur
criticizes throughout). Where semiotics is concerned with language as a
system of signs, semantics is concerned with language as discourse, that is,
as it is actually used. The basic unit of semantics is thus not the sign, but the
sentence. From the vantage point of Ricoeur’s analysis, Derrida’s semiotic
analysis fails to broach the more important issue of “the play of semantic
pertinence and impertinence” that characterizes the living power of meta­
phor.62 By integrating the semantic level into his conception of metaphor,
Ricoeur requires no hard and fast distinction between the literal and the
figurative. The literal, for Ricoeur, is simply what is lexicalized—what is

of Philosophy, trans. Alan Bass (Sussex: The Harvester Press, [1971] 1982), 207–271.
For a close reading of the debate, see Jean-Luc Amalric, Ricoeur, Derrida. L’enjeu de la
métaphore (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 2006). For a reading of the debate
that sides more with Derrida, see Leonard Lawlor, Imagination and Chance: The Dif­
ference between the Thought of Ricoeur and Derrida (Albany, NY: State University of
New York Press, 1992). For more general studies of Ricoeur and Derrida, see the sec­
ond chapter of Johann Michel, Ricoeur and the Post-Structuralists: Bourdieu, Derrida,
Deleuze, Foucault, Castoriadis, trans. Scott Davidson (London: Rowman & Littlefield,
[2013] 2015), 31–71; Eftichis Pirovolakis, Reading Derrida and Ricoeur: Improbable
Encounters between Deconstruction and Hermeneutics (Albany, NY: State University of
New York Press, 2010).
61  Ricoeur, RM, 338–339/MV, 364.
62  Ricoeur, RM, 343/MV, 368–369.


Extending the Audience  199
current or usual. It is thus discourse (language use rather than language
as a system) and not a metaphysics that serves as the criterion for what is
literal and what is figurative.
Second, Ricoeur argues that by focusing on how metaphors die rather
than on how they are born, Derrida only tells us half the story. In other
words, Derrida deploys only a “hermeneutics of suspicion”; his aim is
to unmask the unthought connection between a hidden metaphysics and
worn-out metaphor.63 Beyond this one-sided emphasis—which Ricoeur
describes as “more seductive than earth-shattering”—what interests
Ricoeur are cases “where philosophical discourse deliberately has recourse
to living metaphor in order to draw out new meanings from some seman­
tic impertinence and to bring to light new aspects of reality by means of
semantic innovation”.64 Here, then, is the other half of the story: the birth
of new living metaphors or, most relevant for philosophy, the rejuvena­
tion of dead metaphors, “allow a new conceptual production to be grafted
onto the metaphorical production itself”.65 Thus, to take one of Ricoeur’s
(French) examples, it is only because we no longer hear “prendre” [take,
to take hold of] in “comprendre” [comprehend, understand] that the latter
can have any properly philosophical meaning.66
Ricoeur’s critique of Derrida prepares the way for his own understand­
ing of philosophical discourse. For Ricoeur, the speculative discourse
proper to philosophy is both continuous and discontinuous with the plu­
rality of other forms of discourse: continuous in that speculative discourse
“has its condition of possibility in the semantic dynamism of metaphorical
utterance”; discontinuous in that it “has its necessity in itself, in putting
the resources of conceptual articulation to work”67. While it is true that
speculative discourse appears second in the order of discovery, it is first in
the order of grounding. Ricoeur’s argument is thus not that philosophi­
cal discourse can get off the ground despite the metaphorical process but
because of it: philosophical concepts are sustained by the movement of
living metaphor even as they draw away from it and move into the level of
conceptual thinking.68
This outline of the broader argument in The Rule of Metaphor brings us
to the issue of rhetoric and its place within the work as a whole. Rhetoric
makes its first appearance in the context of Ricoeur’s attempt to situate
the problem of metaphor historically. Does the return to the problem of

64  Ricoeur, RM, 344, translation modified/MV, 370.
65  Ricoeur, RM, 347/MV, 373.
66  Ricoeur, RM, 346/MV, 372.

200  Extending the Audience
metaphor, he asks at the outset of the first study, commit us to the hopeless
project of “resurrecting rhetoric from its ashes?”69 To answer this ques­
tion Ricoeur begins with Aristotle, who in the Rhetoric provides the first
systematic treatment of the field, establishing its scope and its relation to
philosophical dialectic. According to Ricoeur, rhetoric for Aristotle covers
three areas: (1) a theory of argumentation concerned with the invention
of arguments and proofs, (2) a theory of style concerned with the manner
in which one’s arguments are presented, and (3) a theory of composition
concerned with the various parts of a speech and their arrangement in view
of the orator’s situation.70
To show how far things have come (for the worse), Ricoeur compares
Aristotle’s picture of rhetoric with more recent work from the 1970s.
Borrowing Gérard Genette’s phrase “restricted rhetoric” [la rhétorique
restreinte] from his 1970 article of the same name,71 Ricoeur explains
how rhetoric has been progressively reduced to style—to but one of the
three parts identified by Aristotle. Momentarily bracketing the causes, this
reduction to style has had the effect of severing rhetoric’s connection to
argumentation, that is, to dialectic, which in Aristotle is what preserved its
connection to philosophy and, more specifically, logic.72 Without this con­
nection, Ricoeur explains—still paraphrasing Genette—rhetoric became
an “erratic and futile discipline”.73
We emphasize Ricoeur’s use of Genette here because Genette is
undoubtedly the source of the claim that this “restricted rhetoric”, with
its “penchant for classifying figures of speech”, is all that can be found in
“the latest treatises on rhetoric”.74 Or, more precisely, as Ricoeur says a
few pages later, “rhetoric as we know it from the last modern treatises is
amputated from its major part, the treatise on argumentation”.75 Had we
not already examined Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s “new rhetoric”
in Chapter 2, we might be inclined to believe Genette and Ricoeur. After
all, this claim has carried considerable weight since at least Peter Ramus

72  For a recent discussion of this connection, see Christof Rapp, “Aristotle and the Dia­
lectical Turn of Rhetoric”, Aristotle—Contemporary Perspectives on His Thought: On
the 2400th Anniversary of Aristotle’s Birth, ed. Demetra Sfendoni-Mentzou (Berlin: De
Gruyter, 2018), 223–236.
73  Ricoeur, RM, 9/MV, 14.
74  Ricoeur, RM, 9, our emphasis/MV, 13–14.
75  Ricoeur, RM, 12–13/MV, 18.


Extending the Audience  201
(which is the figure Perelman identifies with the decline of rhetoric).76 Yet,
things are not so straightforward.
Which “modern treatises” are being referred to here? Genette’s article
is a review of three works all published in the academic year 1969–70:
Groupe µ’s Rhétorique générale (1970), Michel Deguy’s “Pour une théo­
rie de la figure généralisée” (1969), and Jacques Sojcher’s “La métaphore
généralisée” (1969).77 The most important of the three for our purposes is
Groupe µ’s Rhétorique Générale [A General Rhetoric], as it is this text that
Ricoeur takes as his immediate reference point for so-called “new rheto­
ric”. For, as Ricoeur explains in the Introduction, his selection of material
is limited to “only those theories that at one and the same time carry a
viewpoint to its highest degree of expression and contribute to the progress
of the overall argument”.78
How then does Ricoeur understand Groupe µ’s project and why does
he take it to be the highest expression of “new rhetoric”? As he explains in
the opening lines of Study 5:
The common aim of the works of the new rhetoric … is to renovate the
essentially taxonomic enterprise of classical rhetoric by founding the
species of classification on the forms of the operations that take place
at all levels of articulation in language. In this respect the new rhetoric
is dependent on a semantics [of the word] taken to its highest degree of
structural radicalism.79
Ricoeur thus identifies the “common aim” of the works associated with
“new rhetoric” as an effort, however radical from a structuralist per­
spective, to provide a systematic taxonomy of figures. Unlike Ricoeur’s
analysis, which moves from smaller to larger units of meaning, Groupe µ
maintains that “all rhetorical operations rest on a fundamental property
of linear discourse—that discourse can be decomposed into smaller and
smaller units”.80 In other words, to account for metaphor they turn inward
towards the micro or infralinguistic level of language (a semantics of the
76  Perelman, RR, 3/ER, 19.
77  Genette, “La rhétorique restreinte”, 158/Figures of Literary Discourse, 103; Groupe μ,
Rhétorique générale (Paris: Seuil, [1970] 1992)/A General Rhetoric, trans. Paul B. Burrell
and Edgar M. Slotkin (Baltimore, MD: Johns Hopkins University Press, 1981); Michel
Deguy, “Vers une théorie de la figure généralisée”, Critique, Vol. 25/269 (1969), 841–861
[incorrectly titled “Pour une théorie de la figure généralisée” in Genette’s article]; Jacques
Sojcher, “La métaphore généralisée”, Revue internationale de philosophie, Vol. 23/87
(1969), 58–68.
78  Ricoeur, RM, 6–7/MV, 12.
79  Ricoeur, RM, 157/MV, 173.
80  Groupe µ, A General Theory of Rhetoric, 25/Rhétorique générale, 30.


202  Extending the Audience
word) rather than outward towards the level of discourse (a semantics
concerned with units of meaning larger than single sentences). Ricoeur
does not deny the merits of this kind of analysis—indeed, he attempts to
incorporate it into his own approach. Yet to the extent that it wants to
account for metaphor, new rhetoric cheats: the concept of “deviation”
[écart] on which A General Rhetoric relies is parasitic on the semantic level
of discourse which its methodology simultaneously disavows.
Here Ricoeur’s preference for Benveniste over Saussure comes to the
fore. As he summarizes his critique of new rhetoric towards the end of
the fifth study: “a rhetoric aspiring to generality cannot operate merely
in the ‘interior’ space … between sign and meaning”; it must also con­
sider “the ‘exterior’ space between sign and referent”, as it is only here,
at the level of discourse, that we can understand how figures have the
power to disturb the lexicon as well as have a referential function.81 For
Ricoeur, a truly “general rhetoric” must not remain at the level of semiot­
ics (i.e. a semantics of the word) but must also break through to the level
of semantics in the full sense, where, as we know from the third study, the
analysis bottoms out not in the most microscopic lexical entities but in
what Benveniste calls the “instance of discourse” [l’instance de discours].82
According to Ricoeur, this error stems from “the tyranny of the word in
the theory of meaning”, the result of which has been the “reduction of
metaphor to a mere ornament”.83
This brings us to Ricoeur’s divergence from the diagnosis offered
by Genette regarding the causes of rhetoric’s historical decline. Where
Genette sees the decline as resulting from the progressive reduction of
rhetoric’s once vast empire to the small enclave of tropes, Ricoeur locates
it in this “tyranny of the word”, “an error that affects the theory of tropes
directly”.84 The problem for Ricoeur is thus not how to restore the origi­
nal domain of rhetoric—which he adds may even be impossible for cul­
tural reasons. The problem is rather “to understand in a new way the very
workings of tropes, and, based on this, eventually to restate in new terms
the question of the aim and purpose of rhetoric”.85 Yet, even if Ricoeur’s
explanation for rhetoric’s decline is more fundamental than Genette’s, his
analysis relies upon the same assumption: that there is nothing at all new
about new rhetoric; that all new rhetorics share the “common aim” of
renovating “the essentially taxonomic enterprise of classical rhetoric”.86
