82  Benveniste, Problems in General Linguistics, 217/Proble﻿̀mes de linguistique générale, 251.
83  Ricoeur, RM, 51/MV, 64.
84  Ricoeur, RM, 51/MV, 64.
85  Ricoeur, RM, 50/MV, 64.
86  Ricoeur, RM, 157/MV, 173.


Extending the Audience  203
In the second part of this chapter, we shall challenge this assumption
by bringing Perelman into the discussion, as his philosophical engagement
with rhetoric not only problematizes Genette and Ricoeur’s story but also
provides the necessary conceptual resources to meet Ricoeur’s challenge—
“to restate in new terms the question of the aim and purpose of rhetoric”.
2  The Missed Encounter
Our search for an expanded conception of audience now brings us to
the missed encounter between Perelman and Ricoeur. We begin with
Perelman’s 1977 Foreword to The Realm of Rhetoric [L’empire rhéto­
rique]. In this short text Perelman takes issue with Ricoeur’s neglect of
the NRP in The Rule of Metaphor and attempts to differentiate his and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s efforts from other so-called “new rhetorics”. After
reconstructing their missed encounter, we then come to Ricoeur’s explicit
critique of Perelman in “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”. Here we locate
Perelman’s problematic of the audience in The Rule of Metaphor, draw­
ing attention to Ricoeur’s emphasis on the audience’s active role in the
metaphorical process. In a final step, we show how Ricoeur’s hermeneu­
tic philosophy contributes to the extended conception of audience we are
looking for. As we shall see, however, this requires reframing Ricoeur’s
understanding of rhetoric in favour of what we call “rhetoricity”, the rhe­
torical dimension of discourse.
Foreword to L’empire rhétorique
In the Foreword to The Realm of Rhetoric (regrettably excluded from the
English translation), Perelman complains that certain French writers had
neglected his work in their “rediscovery” of rhetoric.87 Here, Perelman
singles out Genette’s article, Roland Barthes’s “Ancient Rhetoric”
[“L’ancienne rhétorique”]88—both published in the same 1970 volume of
Communications—and Ricoeur’s The Rule of Metaphor. Yet, of the three
writers it seems that it is Ricoeur’s neglect that most bothers Perelman.89
87  For a more detailed account of Perelman’s reaction, see Michelle Bolduc, Translation and
the Rediscovery of Rhetoric, 279–83.
88  Roland Barthes, “L’ancienne rhétorique”, Communications, Vol. 16 (1970), 172–223/The
Semiotic Challenge, trans. Richard Howard (Oxford: Basil Blackwell, 1988), 11–93.
89  One hypothesis for Ricoeur’s neglect of Perelman may have to do with the audience of
the lectures that would eventually become The Rule of Metaphor. As Ricoeur explains in
the Introduction, the eight studies that make up the book grew out of a seminar course
he first gave at the University of Toronto in the Department of Comparative Literature
in 1971. Although this material was further presented in Paris, Leuven, and Chicago, it is
likely that the original, primarily English-speaking audience played some role in the selec­


204  Extending the Audience
Whatever the reason, it is clear that Perelman lamented the loss of a poten­
tial philosophical ally. Indeed, given their personal acquaintance and rel­
ative familiarity with each other’s work, it is all the more peculiar that
nowhere in The Rule of Metaphor but a single and rather trivial footnote
does Ricoeur cite Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca.90
Citing the same passages of Genette and Ricoeur we discussed above,
Perelman asks how his French colleagues could neglect the “new rheto­
ric” for which he and Olbrechts-Tyteca had been advocating for over two
decades:
Given that great writers such as Aristotle, Cicero, and Quintilian have
devoted remarkable works to rhetoric, the art of persuading, how is it
that classical rhetoric has been limited to the study of figures of style?
How is it that the best-known works of rhetoric in France in the 18th
and 19th centuries were those of Dumarsais … and Fontanier … who
saw in rhetoric only ornament and artifice? This perspective has earned
classical rhetoric the hatred of the romantics (“war on rhetoric, peace
to grammar”) and the disdain of our contemporaries, lovers of the sim­
ple and the natural. And how can we believe that the rehabilitation of
rhetoric, that a new rhetoric could be limited to updating the rhetoric
of figures, to “renovate the essentially taxonomic enterprise of classical
rhetoric”?91
The reference in the last line, we will recall, is to Ricoeur’s characteriza­
tion of the “common aim” of the works associated with “new rhetoric”.
Whether his work is being included in this enterprise or neglected entirely,92
Perelman points out that his and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s new rhetoric not only
tion of materials. Given that the ambitions of the Treatise are explicitly philosophical,
Groupe µ’s exclusive concern with literature and the structuralist orientation of A General
Rhetoric would have made these authors the more attractive choice for a seminar course
in a literature department.
90  In the third study, Ricoeur notes that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s terms “theme” and
“phoros” might be good translations of I.A. Richards’s “tenor” and “vehicle”. Ricoeur
hesitates, however, adding that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca “limit the application of
their pair of terms to analogy, that is, the relationship of proportionality” (RM, 93, n. 27/
MV, 105–106, n. 2).
91  Perelman, ER, 11, our translation.
92  While Ricoeur neglects Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, the 1970 issue of Communica­
tions which forms the basis of Ricoeur’s discussion does not do so entirely. In Michèle
Lacoste’s annotated bibliography, she remarks that while Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyte­
ca’s work is “original”, their concern with argumentation rather than figures leaves it “on
the margins of most modern revivals of rhetoric” (“Choix bibliographique”, Communica­
tions, Vol. 16 (1970), 235, our translation). Perelman also takes issue with this passage,
which seemingly misses the whole point of his work.


Extending the Audience  205
pre-empts any possible association with taxonomy, but further that it had
also laid the foundation for rhetoric’s rapprochement with philosophy by
restoring its lost connection to argumentation.
Perelman’s clear frustration stems from his belief that his and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s work has already made considerable headway in the direction
indicated by both Genette and Ricoeur. To recall, the problem for Genette
was that so-called “general rhetorics” were not general at all; they merely
generalize one part of rhetoric—stylistics—and attempt to pass it off for
the whole. For Ricoeur, the problem was not so much to restore rhetoric’s
original domain, but rather “to understand in a new way the very work­
ings of tropes, and, based on this, eventually to restate in new terms the
question of the aim and purpose of rhetoric”.93 Either way: not only does
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s new rhetoric redress Genette’s concern
about “restricted rhetoric”, but it also addresses Ricoeur’s point by sub­
ordinating stylistics to what Perelman takes to be rhetoric’s properly argu­
mentative function:
When figures are examined out of context, like dried flowers in an her­
barium, we lose sight of their dynamic role: they all become figures of
style.
If they are not integrated into a rhetoric conceived as the art of per­
suading and convincing, they cease to be rhetorical figures and become
ornaments, concerned only with the form of discourse: it is therefore
not serious to envisage a modern recovery of rhetoric, even a rhetoric of
figures, outside of an argumentative context.94
Reiterating a point that he and Olbrechts-Tyteca make in the Treatise,
Perelman notes that any criteria used to distinguish rhetorical figures from
poetic figures of style already presupposes rhetoric’s more basic argumen­
tative function.95 It is thus only by bracketing any reference to this argu­
mentative context, Perelman argues, that a purely taxonomic rhetoric can
get itself off the ground in the first place.
What is most striking about Perelman’s response is how similar it is
to Ricoeur’s argument throughout The Rule of Metaphor that theories
which neglect the level of discourse (in Benveniste’s sense of referring to
93  Ricoeur, RM, 50/MV, 64.
94  Perelman, ER, 14–15, our translation.
95 “We consider a figure to be argumentative if, in bringing about a change of perspective,
its use appears normal in relation to the newly proposed situation. If, by contrast, the
discourse does not bring about the adherence of the audience to this argumentative form,
the figure will be perceived as ornamentation, as a figure of style. It can inspire admira­
tion, but on the aesthetic level or as a testament to the orator’s originality” (Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §41, 169/TA, 229).


206  Extending the Audience
the “instance of discourse”) are incapable of accounting for metaphor’s
innovative function. As we saw in Ricoeur’s critique of Derrida, to avoid
any metaphysical distinction between the literal and the figurative, Ricoeur
argues that it is “use in discourse that specifies the difference between the
literal and metaphorical, and not some sort of prestige attributed to the
primitive or the original”.96
Although Perelman’s work precedes that of Benveniste, Perelman’s
conception of argumentation serves an analogous task as discourse. As
Benveniste defines it, “Discourse must be understood in its widest sense:
every utterance assuming a speaker and an audience, and in the speaker, the
intention of influencing the other in some way”.97 Similarly, for Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca, the aim of argumentation is “to elicit or increase the
adherence of the members of an audience to theses that are presented for
their consent”.98 Triangulated in this way through Benveniste, what there­
fore unites Perelman and Ricoeur in their critique of structuralist rheto­
rics is their common recourse to the properly semantic level of discourse.
Although in pursuit of different ends, it is this point which allows Perelman
to reconnect rhetoric with argumentation and what allows Ricoeur to pre­
serve the creative power of metaphor at the level of both meaning and
reference.
Rhetoric’s Empire
Having drawn attention to the missed encounter between Perelman and
Ricoeur in The Rule of Metaphor and identifying their shared emphasis
on the level of discourse (or “argumentation” in Perelman’s case), we are
now in a better position to look at Ricoeur’s explicit critique of Perelman
in “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”. This debate will bring out the differ­
ences and shortcomings of Perelman and Ricoeur’s respective conceptions
of rhetoric, opening the space for us to introduce our own view.
Within Ricoeur’s corpus, this essay first appears in the collection
Lectures 2. La contrée des philosophes (1992). However, it was first pub­
lished several years earlier among a collection of essays, edited by Michel
Meyer, entitled De la métaphysique à la rhétorique [From Metaphysics to
Rhetoric] (1986), in honour of Perelman two years after his death. The
two texts are identical with one small but instructive exception.99 The
96  Ricoeur, RM, 343, our emphasis/MV, 369.
97  Benveniste, Problems of General Linguistics, 208–9, translation modified/Proble﻿̀mes de
linguistique générale, 241–2.
98  Perelman, RR, 9/ER, 28.
99  Incidentally, there is also a sentence omitted in the English translation (presumably in
error): the last sentence of the fifth paragraph of the first section, on rhetoric.


Extending the Audience  207
1986 version contains an introductory paragraph that was omitted from
the Lectures 2 version:
The following study arises from a lecture given in 1970 at the Institut
des Hautes Etudes in Belgium in the presence and under the presidency
of Professor Perelman. This lecture having never been published, it is
an honor to be invited by his friends and disciples to join in the hom­
age to the man who for several decades was the master philosopher of
Brussels.100
What is the significance of this seemingly trivial exclusion? Although it did
not appear until 1986, Ricoeur explains that the published text is based on
a lecture originally delivered in Brussels in 1970 (with Perelman in attend­
ance). Regrettably, the original 1970 version was never published. Even
without the original, however, this detail is instructive: although seemingly
written over a decade apart, parts of the text were written much closer to
The Rule of Metaphor than one might think from looking at the dates of
either published version. This would explain why The Rule of Metaphor
is the only text of his own that Ricoeur mentions and why the final para­
graphs read almost like a summary of his findings in those eight studies.
Moreover, this would also explain why Ricoeur’s analysis and critique of
rhetoric are strikingly similar in the two texts. This permits us to examine
the two texts together within the context of our discussion of Ricoeur’s
work in the 1970s.
This is not to say, however, that Ricoeur did not revise his critique since
1970. In fact, beyond references to The Rule of Metaphor from 1975,
the published version makes explicit reference to Perelman’s The Realm
of Rhetoric, which, as we know, was not itself published until 1977. Of
course, without the original text to compare it to, it is difficult to say pre­
cisely what was written when. Regardless, what we can say is that the pub­
lished version of 1986 contains Ricoeur’s response to Perelman’s criticism
from his 1977 “Foreword” to The Realm of Rhetoric that could not have
appeared in Ricoeur’s original essay.
Turning to the text itself, “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics” begins by
setting out the difficulties involved in analysing three disciplines each of
which attempt to “totalize”, as Ricoeur puts it, the entire field of discourse.
(Note Ricoeur’s characterization of the three as “disciplines”, which will be
important for our argument below.) As in The Rule of Metaphor, Ricoeur
is continuing to use the term “discourse” in the sense of “configurations
100  Ricoeur, “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”, FMR, 137/“Rhétorique—Poétique—Hermé­
neutique”, DMR, 143.


208  Extending the Audience
of meaning more extensive than the sentence”.101 This concern with
larger units of meaning—namely, texts—is what distinguishes Ricoeur’s
approach from that of Austin or Searle, for example. Speech-act theory,
in the broadest sense, establishes itself at the level of the sentence. Even if
Austin’s reference to the “total speech situation” gestures towards larger
configurations of meaning, the distinction between locutionary, illocution­
ary, and perlocutionary is derived from an analysis of individual sentences.
Ricoeur is thus interested in how sentences come together to form these
larger units whose meaning can neither be reduced to the meaning of sen­
tences, nor understood without them. Paraphrasing Benveniste, Ricoeur
is thus not interested in language [le langage] as a system but as the phe­
nomenon of “somebody saying something to someone about something”
[quelqu’un dit quelque chose à quelqu’un sur quelque chose].
The three disciplines in the title each attempt to understand sentences
in terms of the larger discursive units they compose. Rather than side with
any one perspective, Ricoeur will argue—taking up a similar line of argu­
ment as in The Rule of Metaphor—that it falls to “a philosophy of lan­
guage” to make sense of their respective competencies and limitations.
More specifically, it will be the task of this philosophy of language “to
distinguish within these coordinated functions the three major mediations
which make it so that language is not unto itself its own end”.102 Echoing
“Work and the Word”, Ricoeur explains that these three mediations are:
“the mediation between man and world, the mediation between man and
another man, the mediation between man and himself”.103
Here we should pause and ask ourselves a simple question: is Ricoeur
still talking about rhetoric in the same way as in The Rule of Metaphor?
Without announcing the shift, Ricoeur no longer appears to be talking
about rhetoric in the ancient or classical sense, or even the “new rhetoric”
of Groupe µ which follows suit by taking the word as its basic unit of
meaning. No, in this essay rhetoric is placed at the same level as poetics
and hermeneutics—at the level of discourse rather than at the level of the
word as it was in the first two studies of The Rule of Metaphor. Perhaps
motivated by Perelman’s response in the “Foreword” to The Realm of
Rhetoric, here Ricoeur adopts Perelman’s conception of rhetoric which
preserves—or restores (depending on one’s point of view)—its connection
to argumentation.
In spite of this difference between The Rule of Metaphor and “Rhetoric-
Poetics-Hermeneutics”, Ricoeur’s critique of rhetoric remains relatively
unchanged. Although he may now recognize that new rhetorics do not
101  Ricoeur, FMR, 137/DMR, 143/L2, 481.
102  Ricoeur, FMR, 137/DMR, 144/L2, 481.
103  Ricoeur, FMR, 137/DMR, 144/L2, 481–482.


Extending the Audience  209
all share with Groupe µ the “common aim” of renovating the taxonomic
enterprise of classical rhetoric, he has not changed his mind about rheto­
ric’s disciplinary limits—even a rhetoric installed at the level of discourse.
According to Ricoeur, rhetoric is defined by four essential characteristics:
	(1)	 Typical situations—Taking his cue from Aristotle, Ricoeur claims that
rhetoric is defined in terms of the “typical situations” that require it.
In the Rhetoric, Aristotle famously derives three species of rhetoric—
deliberative, judicial, and epideictic—from the three kinds of audi­
ences typical of those contexts. These typical contexts guarantee a
certain consistency in the composition and interests of the addressees
proper to each genre. As Ricoeur explains, it is the exigencies of these
situations and not rhetoric per se that introduce the need for compet­
ing discourses, the requirement that a judgment be made, and that a
decision be taken.104
	(2)	 Probable argumentation—The second characteristic concerns the role
played by argumentation in rhetoric. Argumentation is the mode of
reasoning located halfway between “the constraint of necessity and
the arbitrariness of contingency. Between the proof and the sophism
there reigns the probable argument whose theory Aristotle inscribed in
dialectic, thus making of rhetoric the ‘antistrophe’, that is, the rejoin­
der of dialectic”.105 In the typical situations mentioned above, con­
cerned as they are with matters of human action, the most rhetoric can
aspire to is probable knowledge—a knowledge born through the test
of criticism and refutation but must ultimately remain open to future
contingencies.
	(3)	 Orientation towards the audience—Even if one admits (though many
philosophers would not) that the argumentation characteristic of rhe­
torical discourse has its own distinct epistemic status, its aim nonethe­
less remains persuasion. For Ricoeur, the fact that argumentation has
its telos in the audience—that it is “action through discourse” [dis­
cours agissant] directed towards others—limits its claim to cover the
entire field of discourse. Indeed, as he will argue in relation to poetics
and hermeneutics, not all discourse aims to persuade its audience.
	(4)	 Use of conventional ideas—The fourth and final characteristic follows
from the previous one: if persuasion is the goal of argumentation, the
orator is obliged to rely upon “conventional ideas” [idées admises]
accepted by their audience. For this reason, Ricoeur adds, “argumen­
tation can hardly have any creative function”, as it is only concerned
104  Ricoeur, FMR, 138/DMR, 144–145/L2, 482–483.
105  Ricoeur, FMR, 138/DMR, 145/L2, 483.


210  Extending the Audience
with transferring the audience’s existing adherence from premises to
the conclusion106—a view Perelman completely rejects.107 Thus, how­
ever complex, all the argumentative techniques at an orator’s disposal
remain hostage to what their audience already believes.
These four (highly contestable) points are followed by a short paragraph
on style (which could not have appeared in the 1970 version). Here,
Ricoeur responds, albeit rather obliquely, to Perelman’s remarks in the
1977 “Foreword”, doubling down on his position about the limits of rhet­
oric. This short and unassuming paragraph reads as follows:
Finally, we must say a word about elocution and style to which the mod­
erns have had far too great a tendency to reduce rhetoric. We cannot
disregard them, however, precisely because of the orientation toward
the audience. The figures of style, turns of phrase and tropes, turn the
art of persuasion into an art of pleasing even when they are in the ser­
vice of argumentation and do not sink into simple ornamentation.108
Thus, even when rhetoric establishes itself at the level of discourse by pre­
serving its connection to argumentation, its connection to (1) typical situ­
ations, its claim only to (2) probable knowledge, its (3) persuasive telos,
and its reliance on (4) conventional ideas, all prevent it from imposing its
empire on the still vaster territory of discourse.
Without placing this passage against the background of Perelman’s
“Foreword”, it would be easy to miss the point. To recall, Perelman’s frus­
tration with Ricoeur and the other French writers is that by neglecting the
argumentative anchor of rhetoric, they allow the discipline to drift away
into tropology and taxonomy. Here, however, Ricoeur wants to insist that
even argumentation is subordinated by rhetoric’s persuasive function: the
stylistic dimension of rhetoric cannot be disregarded “because of the ori­
entation toward the audience”.109 For Ricoeur, figures of style thus always
threaten to turn rhetoric from the art of persuasion into mere flattery or
the art of pleasing—“even if they are in the service of argumentation”.
This is because, as Ricoeur goes on to argue, the very “seat” (foyer) of
rhetoric is fundamentally ambiguous.110 (Here we benefit from having
106  Ricoeur, FMR, 139/DMR, 145/L2, 483.
107  We have seen Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s account of argumentative innovation in
our discussion of dissociation and their theory of philosophical pairs inChapter 2, p. 81.
108  Ricoeur, FMR, 139, translation modified, our emphasis/DMR, 145–146/L2, 484.
109  Ricoeur and Badiou thus seem to be of one mind in thinking that the audience poses a
permanent threat to argumentation. See Chapter 1, p. 45.
110  Ricoeur, FMR, 139/DMR, 145/L2, 483.


Extending the Audience  211
already examined Cassin’s analysis of the “ambiguity” of rhetoric, which
also targets Ricoeur.)111
On Ricoeur’s reading, the constitutive ambiguity of rhetoric results from
where it rests: not so much on a “seat”—a metaphor which promises a cer­
tain degree of stability and comfort—as a knife-edge. This is to say that
rhetoric has the tendency to either devolve into pathology (e.g. ideology
and propaganda in political discourse) or aggrandize itself by attempting
to totalize the entire field of discourse—philosophical discourse included.
Although the two tendencies are certainly related, Ricoeur’s concern here
is not so much the former as the latter; what is at issue here is the legiti­
macy of rhetoric’s claim, vis-à-vis poetics and hermeneutics, to totalize the
entire field of discourse.
Ricoeur offers two reasons why rhetoric’s claim to totalize the field of
discourse is illegitimate. Both points have to do with rhetoric’s unthought
presuppositions: “[1] rhetoric can free itself entirely neither from typi­
cal situations which isolate its generative seat, nor from [2] the intention
which defines its finality”, i.e. persuasion.112 On both points Ricoeur’s
criticism is fundamentally the same: while rhetoric’s empire may be far
reaching, there is one enclave that it can never completely overrun—phi­
losophy. Unlike philosophy, he claims, rhetoric will always be bound to
the typical contexts and ends which define it. Although Ricoeur admits
that he cannot, strictly speaking, refute what he takes to be Perelman’s
identification of rhetoric with first philosophy, he nonetheless insists (with
a nod to the “universal audience”) that philosophy, in its most honest
forms, transcends the art of persuasion:
I am certainly not naïve enough to believe that philosophers free them­
selves not only from the constraints but also from the pathology that
infects our debates. The fact remains that the aim of philosophical dis­
cussion, in its most honest forms—if it is up to the standard of what has
been called the universal audience—transcends the art of persuasion and
of pleasing which prevail in in the abovementioned typical situations.113
While few could fault Ricoeur for his honesty, we might pause here and ask
how fair this is to Perelman. Having already noted the slippage between the
more restrictive, ancient conception of rhetoric in The Rule of Metaphor
and the broader conception in “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”, it seems
that Ricoeur’s criticism is more applicable to Aristotle’s conception of
rhetoric than Perelman’s. As we have seen, the rhetorical conception of
111  See Chapter 1, pp. 45-49.
112  Ricoeur, FMR, 140/DMR, 147/L2, 485.
113  Ricoeur, FMR, 140–1/DMR, 147/L2, 485–6.


212  Extending the Audience
audience in the NRP is less a function of the situation than it is a con­
struction of the speaker. In fact, it was precisely to avoid the problem of
“typical situations” that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca chose to define
audience as “the ensemble of those whom the speaker wishes to influence
by his argumentation”—to avoid the patent difficulty in determining an
arguer’s audience “by purely material criteria”.114 Even in the most rigor­
ously prescribed contexts, arguers are free to construct their audience as
they wish—even in ways that might push back against the pre-given con­
straints of typical situations.
By defining audience in this way, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
are looking to assuage the philosopher’s longstanding fear of rhetorical
manipulation which, they argue, is not a problem with audiences per se,
but rather the philosopher’s image of audiences as inherently incompetent.
Where the rhetor performs to the credulous masses, as the story goes, the
philosopher communes with reason itself—or at least an honest interlocu­
tor. But Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca ask, “why not allow that argu­
mentation can be addressed to every kind of audience?”115 By raising the
quality of one’s audience, they suggest, do we not also raise the quality
of rhetoric? However honest they may or may not be, philosophers also
address themselves to determinate audiences. For Perelman, the point is
thus not to reduce philosophy to “mere rhetoric”, to one genre of discourse
among others, but to elevate rhetoric to the purposes of philosophy—a dis­
course which strives, however imperfectly, to transcend what is persuasive
only to particular audiences in pursuit of a more universal audience.
If we accept Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s way of dissociating rhet­
oric from this long-standing philosophical prejudice, can we not find a
comparable appreciation of audiences in Ricoeur’s work? Keeping only to
The Rule of Metaphor for the moment, specifically the third study where
Ricoeur first introduces the idea of metaphor as a process of “semantic
innovation”: surprisingly, here it is not the speaker who plays the most sig­
nificant role in this process, but the reader; for it is the reader who “works
out the connotations of the modifier that are likely to be meaningful” and
thus pushes “the frontier of non-sense further back”. In other words, it is
the reader who accomplishes the metaphor’s “work of meaning” [travail
du sens].116
Indeed, the activity of the audience or reader is so central to Ricoeur’s
argument that he describes it as the only perspective capable of overcom­
ing the substitution theory of metaphor and the “rhetoric of the word”
upon which it is based:
114  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 19/TA, 25.
115  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 7, our emphasis/TA, 9.
116  Ricoeur, RM, 111, translation modified/MV, 123.


Extending the Audience  213
One must adopt the point of view of the hearer or reader and treat the
novelty of an emerging meaning as his work within the very act of hear­
ing or reading. If we do not take this route, we do not really get rid of
the theory of substitution.117
The importance of this point must not be underestimated. In fact, we know
from Ricoeur’s interviews with Francois Azouvi and Marc de Launay in
Critique and Conviction [La critique et la conviction] (1995), that it was
precisely “the role of the reader” or audience that Ricoeur felt was under­
developed in The Rule of Metaphor and would thus receive further treat­
ment in terms of “refiguration” in the three volumes of Time and Narrative
[Temps et récit] (1983–1985).118
The Audience of the Text
If language always involves “somebody saying something to someone
about something”, then Ricoeur’s question, “from where do you speak?”,
which he is reported to have posed to his students at the beginning of
his seminars, implies a corollary question—“to whom do you speak?”119
By following the trajectory of language from the one who initiates action
to the one who receives it (or “suffers it” as Ricoeur sometimes puts is),
Ricoeur’s question places him squarely within Perelman’s “realm of rhet­
oric”. Indeed, the question of the audience, the question of who one is
speaking to, as well as their active role in semantic innovation, are cen­
tral concerns in Ricoeur’s work. If this is the case, however, why does
Ricoeur himself not connect these themes to rhetoric? Why does Ricoeur
not feel the need to go beyond hermeneutics to think through questions of
audience?120
To answer these questions it is helpful to return to Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s argument for why their rhetorical conception of
117  Ricoeur, RM, 114/MV, 127.
118  Ricoeur, Critique and Conviction. Conversations with François Azouvi and Marc de
Launay, trans. Kathleen Blamey (New York, NY: Columbia University Press, 1998), 83/
La critique et la conviction. Entretien avec François Azouvi et Marc de Launay (Paris:
Calmann-Lévy, 1995), 129. We discuss Ricoeur’s notion of refiguration in Chapter 6,
pp. 241-244.
119  As Richard Kearney recounts: “When I arrived in Paris in 1977 to study with the phi­
losopher, Paul Ricoeur, the first question he asked everyone is his seminar was: d’où
parlez-vous? Where do you speak from?” (Richard Kearney, Anatheism. Returning to
God After God (New York, NY: Columbia University Press, 2011), xi.)
120  It is beyond the scope of our discussion to address the reasons why Ricoeur turns to
hermeneutics in the first place. For an account of the “plurality of originary questions”
to which Ricoeur’s turn towards hermeneutics responds, see Chapter V of Wolff, Lire
Ricoeur depuis la périphérie, 137–177, our translation.


214  Extending the Audience
audience is defined as a construction of the speaker. After explaining the
difficulties involved in determining a speaker’s audience in terms of “purely
material criteria”, they further explain that the “difficulty is even greater in
the case of a writer’s audience, as in most cases it is impossible to identify
his readers with certainty”.121 Even if a writer has a clear idea of who they
seek to influence, it is clearly impossible for a writer to fully imagine every
reader who might stumble upon their text. For this reason, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s choice of defining audience as a construction serves
their goal of providing a theory that is as general and widely applicable as
possible—a theory that applies equally to argumentation in speech and in
writing.
Indeed, this is one of the main differences they identify between ancient
rhetoric and their new rhetoric. In the Introduction to the Treatise Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca point out that the ancients understood rhetoric as
an exclusively oral art, as the art of persuasive speech in a public setting.
What makes their rhetoric new, then, is precisely the attempt to extend
the framework of spoken rhetoric to argumentation as such, observing
that “the aim of oratory, the adherence of minds, is the same as that of all
argumentation”.122 As they explain, the aim of their study is not the way
that arguments are actually communicated but the “structure of argumen­
tation”—reminding us that the addressive structure of discourse remains
even when “the physical absence of his readers can lead a writer to believe
that he is alone in the world”.123 They thus wanted their framework to
be general enough to account for the structures common to both speech
and writing, especially in view of the predominance of printed texts at the
time.124 Yet from a hermeneutic perspective it is nonetheless regrettable
that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca provide no account of the differences
between oral and written argumentation.125
121  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 19, our emphasis/TA, 25.
122  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 6/TA, 7.
123  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 6/TA, 8.
124  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 6/TA, 8. The theory of argumentation in the Trea­
tise aspires to be general in at least two senses: in the sense of being applicable to various
media through which argumentation takes place but also to different disciplinary fields
and cultural contexts. At the end of her 1979 essay “Les couples philosophiques”, Olbre­
chts-Tyteca suggests the promise of using the theory of philosophical pairs presented in
the Treatise outside of the Western tradition and Indo-European cultures (Olbrechts-
Tyteca, “Les couples philosophiques. Une nouvelle approche”, Revue Internationale de
Philosophie, Vol. 33, No. 127/8 (1979), 98). Perhaps even more than Perelman, Olbre­
chts-Tyteca emphasizes the open-ended methodology of the theory of argumentation
and its need to be applied and revised in light of further application.
125  Ricoeur was thus perhaps not entirely mistaken then for continuing to see Perelman’s
conception of rhetoric in terms of the four characteristics he attributed to Aristotle and
the oral paradigm of ancient rhetoric. In our view, this remains one of the fundamental


Extending the Audience  215
It is here that we touch on the point where Perelman and Ricoeur begin
to talk past one another. Although Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca con­
sider written argumentation to fall within the purview of their new rheto­
ric, Ricoeur continues to think of rhetoric primarily in terms of spoken
discourse. As far as Ricoeur is concerned, our hypothesis is that this stems
from his use of Aristotle as his model for understanding rhetoric and its
“generative seat”.
In fact, this is precisely why hermeneutics, the art of interpreting texts,
is needed in the first place. As Ricoeur explains in “Rhetoric-Poetics-
Hermeneutics”, hermeneutics is needed as soon as “the geographical, his­
torical, cultural distance which separates the text from the reader gives rise
to a situation of misunderstanding which can only be overcome in a plural
reading, that is, a multivocal interpretation”.126 While misunderstandings
can of course also arise in contexts of speech, the more immediate relation
between speaker and listener admits, at least in principle, of clarification
through dialogue. A hermeneutics of conversation would thus only be a
“pre-hermeneutics”, Ricoeur explains. What characterizes hermeneutics
proper is a unique difficulty introduced by writing. Unlike speech, writing
severs the meaning of the text from the writer’s intended meaning.127 The
reader cannot simply converse with the speaker when misunderstandings
arise—there is only the reader and the text.128
But what exactly is a “text” and why does it introduce the need for
a special discipline, that is, a discipline other than rhetoric (as Ricoeur
understands it)? We have already seen in The Rule of Metaphor as well
as “Rhetoric-Poetics-hermeneutics” that texts are units of discourse more
extensive than words and sentences. But what does this really mean? To
answer these questions, we turn to some of the essays in From Text to Action:
Essays in Hermeneutics, II [Du texte à l’action. Essais d’herméneutique,
II] (1986). Although Ricoeur does touch upon these issues in The Rule of
Metaphor, they are more directly addressed in these essays from the 1970s.
In the first part of “The Model of the Text: Meaningful Action
Considered as a Text” (1971) Ricoeur explains the difference between
spoken and written language in terms of the way that they respectively
tensions in the Treatise: Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca attempt to provide a general
theory of argumentation within a framework that still bears the traces of ancient rheto­
ric’s exclusive concern with speech.
126  Ricoeur, FMR, 144/DRM, 150/L2, 489.
127  Ricoeur, FMR, 144/DMR, 150/L2, 490.
128  It should also be emphasized that, for Ricoeur, “every reading of a text always takes
place within a community, a tradition, or a living current of thought” (Ricoeur, The
Conflict of Interpretations: Essays in Hermeneutics, ed. Don Ihde (London: Continuum,
2004), 3/Le conflit des intérprétations. Essais d’herméneutique (Paris: Éditions du Seuil),
23).


216  Extending the Audience
actualize discourse. Not to be confused with language as a system (la
langue), discourse refers to linguistic usage or the “event” of language,
whose basic unit is the sentence. Ricoeur identifies four traits common to
all forms of discourse that allow him to subsequently distinguish between
speaking and writing. These four traits are: (1) temporality—discourse is
realized temporally, in the present, whereas language as a system is vir­
tual and outside of time; (2) autoreferentiality—discourse refers back to
its speaker by means of indicators such as personal pronouns; (3) refer­
entiality—discourse is about something; it refers to a world that it claims
to describe, express, or represent, whereas signs in language refer only
to other signs in the same system; and (4) addressivity—unlike language,
which is only the condition for communication, discourse has an other, an
interlocutor to whom it is addressed.
Ricoeur thus describes the different ways that these four traits of dis­
course are actualized [effectuée] in spoken and written language:
	(1)	 Temporality—In speech, discourse is actualized as a fleeting event; it
appears and disappears. In writing, on the other hand, discourse is
fixed to an external support. What writing inscribes, however, is not
the “saying” itself, but the “said” (dit) of speaking.129 In phenomeno­
logical terms, what writing inscribes is the “noema” of the speaking
and not the event itself. To flesh this out further Ricoeur draws on
speech-act theory to identify those aspects of meaning exteriorized
through the event of speaking, which lend themselves to material fixa­
tion. The “said” is not only the sentence in the narrow sense of the
locutionary act but also the illocutionary force and the perlocutionary
action to the extent that these aspects of the speech act are “codified,
gathered into paradigms, and where, consequently, they can be identi­
fied and reidentified as having the same meaning”.130 The term “mean­
ing” (signification) thus covers all such aspects and levels of discourse
that make its inscription possible.
	(2)	 Autoreferentiality—Speech designates its speaker by a number of
diverse indicators that are immediately present in the discourse event.
Ricoeur explains this by pointing to the overlap between the subjective
intention of the speaker and the meaning of the discourse: to under­
stand the one is to understand the other. This is why, for example,
there is hardly any difference between the questions “What do you
129  Ricoeur, “The Model of the Text”, From Text to Action: Essays in Hermeneutics, II,
trans. Kathleen Blamey and John B. Thompson (Evanston, IL: Northwestern University
Press, 1991), 146/“Le modèle du texte: l’action sensée considérée comme un texte”, Du
texte à l’action. Essais d’herméneutique, II (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1986), 208.
130  Ricoeur, FTA, 147/DTA, 209.


Extending the Audience  217
mean?” and “What does that mean?”. In writing, however, this dif­
ference becomes more acute: “the author’s intention and the mean­
ing of the text cease to coincide”.131 What is at stake here, Ricoeur
explains, is the connection between the verbal meaning of the text and
the mental intention of its author. While this link is “distended and
complicated” rather than abolished, the point is that what the text
says becomes more important than what the author meant to say.
	(3)	 Referentiality—If discourse is always referential, then what is spoken
about in speech is the “situation common to the interlocutors”.132 This
environing situation is responsible for the fact that ostensive gestures,
demonstratives, verbal tenses, and the adverbs of time and place can
all be given a common reference. In written discourse, by contrast,
things are not so clear. Although we have touched on this issue briefly,
Ricoeur argues in The Rule of Metaphor that Frege’s distinction
between “sense” (Sinn) and “reference” (Bedeutung) applies not only
to descriptive discourse but to poetic discourse as well. Although this
requires a dedicated discussion of its own, at the moment it is sufficient
to emphasize that, for Ricoeur, written language, like all discourse,
“cannot fail to be about something”.133 But the aboutness of writing is
not the “situation” of speech but what Ricoeur calls a “world”. Just
as writing frees the meaning of a text from the mental intentions of
its author, it also frees its reference from the limits of ostensive refer­
ence. The “world” is thus “the ensemble of references opened up by
texts”.134
	(4)	 Addressivity—Spoken language is directed towards an interlocu­
tor that is equally present in a shared situation. In writing, however,
discourse can no longer simply be characterized in terms of its sec­
ond person address. Writing “explodes” the dialogical situation to
the extent that it can be read by anyone capable of reading; “what is
written is addressed to the audience that it creates itself”.135 By escap­
ing the momentary character of the event, the immediate reference
back to its author, and the narrowness of ostensive reference, written
discourse equally escapes the constraints of any proximate audience.
With the fixation of discourse by writing, the conversational dialogue
thus losses its privileged status as the model for every form of “under­
standing”. It is for this reason that Ricoeur describes this fourth trait
as the “most exemplary” accomplishment of discourse in writing.
131  Ricoeur, FTA, 148/DTA, 210.
132  Ricoeur, FTA, 148/DTA, 210.
133  Ricoeur, FTA, 148/DTA, 211.
134  Ricoeur, FTA, 149, our emphasis, translation modified/DTA, 188.
135  Ricoeur, FTA, 150/DTA, 212.


218  Extending the Audience
When actualized in writing, these four traits of discourse introduce new
considerations that are often too quickly re-interpreted back into the more
familiar terms of speech. This is especially true of addressivity. As with the
other three traits, writing equally severs the arguer’s construction of their
audience from the “audience that the [text] creates itself”. This clearly
raises questions about Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical defini­
tion of audience. Indeed, as Tindale points out, “Perelman’s definition of
audience as those who an arguer intends to influence seems to distance him
from any clear solution to the problem of how arguments delivered in one
historical period find an audience at a later time”.136 While Ricoeur does
not take the same route as Tindale, who attempts to provide a Perelmanian
response to this question, it is precisely this kind of problem to which
Ricoeur’s hermeneutics is suited.
The problem of the historical argument is hermeneutic in Ricoeur’s
sense because it emerges out of “the geographical, historical, [and] cultural
distance which separates the text from the reader”.137 While Plato (to bor­
row Tindale’s example) obviously could not have anticipated the concerns
of his modern readers, that does not in any way seem to have diminished
his contemporary relevance (take Badiou, for example). However, if from
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical perspective the effectiveness of
an argument is to be determined in terms of the imagined audience of its
author, one would have to conclude that Plato’s arguments fall short in a
number of important respects. Yet here we should follow our intuition that
something must be wrong with the analysis if this is the only conclusion we
can draw. Following Ricoeur, we would thus want to make a distinction
between the situational addressee proper to spoken language and the “sec­
ond-order” audience of written language. Going beyond Ricoeur, then,
the problem with Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s definition of audience
is not so much to do with rhetoric itself but their romantic conception of
it—in the same sense that Ricoeur understands and criticizes the tendency
of “romantic” hermeneutics.138
In terms of Ricoeur’s broader argument, “romantic hermeneutics” can
be understood as the other side of the coin to structuralism—Ricoeur’s other
theoretical adversary in these essays. Where structuralism takes linguistic
signs to refer only to other signs within the same system, hermeneutics of a
136  Tindale, The Philosophy of Argument and Audience Reception, 213.
137  Ricoeur, “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”, FMR, 144/DMR, 147/L2, 485–86.
138  While Perelman, as Frydman observes, does not advocate a model of interpretation cen­
tred on the author’s intention (Frydman, Le sens des lois, 642), it is difficult to explain
how he can avoid this charge without presupposing some account of what Ricoeur calls
hermeneutic distanciation. However, adjudicating this matter fairly would require a
dedicated discussion of Perelman’s account of juridical reasoning where this problem is
treated in more detail (cf. Perelman, Logique juridique, §24, 35).


Extending the Audience  219
romantic tendency (Dilthey and Schleiermacher, for example) take the ref­
erence of written language to be the psychological intentions of its author.
As Ricoeur explains in “The Hermeneutic Function of Distanciation”
(1973), a romantic hermeneutics is thus one that tries to decipher the
author’s intended meaning lying in wait behind the text.139 As he goes on
to point out, however, this romantic route is closed “once we take distan­
ciation by writing and objectification by structure seriously”.140
Ricoeur’s own approach thus tries to avoid the pitfalls of both struc­
turalism and romantic hermeneutics: to structuralism, Ricoeur argues that
even the most sophisticated forms of literary fiction (Joyce’s Finnegan’s
Wake, for example) have a reference, what Ricoeur calls a “world”; to
romantic hermeneutics, Ricoeur agrees that writing has a reference, but
argues that it is not the author’s intended meaning behind the text, but
what he calls the “world of the text”—a world which unfolds in front of
the text through the interpretative work of the reader.141
Yet, in his broader critique of romantic hermeneutics (which cuts across
The Rule of Metaphor and the related texts from the 1970s that we have
been considering), Ricoeur tends to distinguish his own approach to her­
meneutics in terms of the different way it conceives of reference. As a result,
he pays less attention to the trait of addressivity. In light of our own dis­
cussion, however, it is precisely this trait that interests us most. Although
he does not develop this aspect at length in any of the texts we have been
considering, Ricoeur has fortunately done much of the groundwork for a
non-romantic conception of the rhetorical audience—not the audience in
the mind of the author, but what we shall call the “audience of the text”.142
Without yet settling on any specific terminology, a non-romantic rheto­
ric would need to be able to distinguish between the way that a speaker
constructs their audience (à la Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca) and the
way that a text constructs it. Note that this would not be a distinction
between the way that speakers and writers construct their audience—this
would remain a question of the arguer’s psychological construction of their
audience. Instead, what is needed is an account of the way that texts them­
selves construct their audiences. Such an account, however, would require
going beyond the disciplinary boundaries Ricoeur establishes between her­
meneutics and rhetoric.
139  Ricoeur, “The Hermeneutic Function of Distanciation”, FTA, 86/“La fonction hermé­
neutique de la distanciation”, DTA, 128.
140  Ricoeur, FTA, 84/DTA, 126.
141  Ricoeur, FTA, 84/DTA, 126.
142  In one instance, Ricoeur himself actually uses the phrase “audience of the text” (Ricoeur,
Interpretation Theory: Discourse and the Surplus of Meaning (Fort Worth, TX: Texas
Christian University Press, 1976), 31). However, as we explain below, Ricoeur means it
in a hermeneutic rather than a rhetorical way.


220  Extending the Audience
While Ricoeur’s hermeneutics accounts for the discontinuity between
speaking and writing,143 it stops short of fully incorporating Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical conception of audience. For Ricoeur, the dis­
tanciation introduced by writing means that a text is no longer addressed
to an immediate interlocutor but to anyone capable of reading. Although
he recognizes that “what is written is addressed to the audience that [the
text] creates itself”, from a hermeneutic perspective this means only that
the “vis-à-vis of the written is just whoever knows how to read”.144 Yet
from a rhetorical perspective the audience of the text is never simply
indeterminate—or “empty”, as Badiou defined the subjective position of
address proper to philosophy.145 The audience of the text is as determinate
as the audience of the speaker.
Thus, while hermeneutics accounts for the fact that a book is addressed
to anyone who picks it up off the shelf and reads it, rhetoric accounts for
that feeling of fate when the same book seems to have fallen into our hands
at just the right moment; or that strange sense of misrecognition when
a political advertisement seems addressed to someone with a value set
entirely different from our own. In other words, if hermeneutics accounts
for the fact that texts are open to being read by anyone capable of read­
ing, rhetoric accounts for the specific way that texts construct their reader.
Understood this way, Ricoeur’s hermeneutics does not simply swallow up
Perelman’s rhetorical problematic. Even if we accept that there is a miss­
ing hermeneutic component to the NRP (which we do), rhetoric retains its
own specificity, even when dealing with texts.146
Rhetoricity
This conclusion brings us back to Ricoeur’s analysis of the three disci­
plines named in the title of “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”. Although
our own discussion has brought out the irreducibility of rhetoric to herme­
neutics and vice versa, it has not been for the same reason as Ricoeur.147
143  Of course, one should not overemphasize the discontinuity. It is also possible to show the
continuity between speaking and writing in terms of the four traits of discourse Ricoeur
identifies. For a discussion of this, see Ernst Wolff, Between Daily Routine and Violent
Protest: Interpreting the Technicity of Action (Berlin: De Gruyter, 2021), 45–50.
144  Ricoeur, “The Model of the Text”, FTA, 150/DTA, 212.
145  See Chapter 3, p. 141.
146  Less concerned with policing disciplinary borders than Ricoeur, Perelman is happy to
concede that what interests him is “not always called rhetoric” and sometimes “may be
called hermeneutics” (Perelman, “Introduction: Chaim Perelman’s Address as the Ohio
State University”, in Golden and Pilotta, eds., Practical Reasoning in Human Affairs,
11).
147  We bracket Ricoeur’s discussion of “poetics” for now but return to it in Chapter 6.


Extending the Audience  221
We agree with him that rhetoric and hermeneutics are concerned with dif­
ferent things, but we disagree with his way of coordinating them.148 While
it is uncharacteristic of Ricoeur to theorize using typologies, this is the
approach he adopts here. In a step above speech-act theory, installed at the
level of the sentence (or the phrase), Ricoeur explains that he is in search
of “a typology of the properly discursive, that is, hyperphrastic, usage of
discourse”.149 Although perhaps closer to Searle than to Austin, Ricoeur
is unambiguous about pursuing a typology, a typology of disciplines con­
cerned with discourse.
By introducing the language of typology Ricoeur suggests that rhetoric
and hermeneutics are related vertically, so to speak, standing in two rival
disciplinary silos, each attempting to overshadow the other and “total­
ize” the field of discourse. As we saw above, this view is confirmed as
Ricoeur goes on to differentiate each discipline in terms of its generative
seat and the finality or end to which it is directed. Carved up this way,
Ricoeur can say little more by way of conclusion than that each discipline
must be left “undisturbed” in its respective place, advising only an obscure
“complementarity”.150
Yet is this disciplinary typology really the best way to coordinate the
relationship between rhetoric and hermeneutics? This is the point we want
to push Ricoeur on. What sort of “complementarity” does he have in
mind? Is he suggesting that rhetoric and hermeneutics should not overstep
their disciplinary boundaries because they are concerned with different
kinds of discourse? Or is his point rather that rhetoric and hermeneutics
are just concerned with different aspects of discourse? Given Ricoeur’s
phenomenological sensitivity, it is unlikely that he would want to endorse
the typology of action implied by the former. But judging by the criteria
he uses to distinguishes the disciplines, it is hard to see how he could avoid
doing so when pressed.
While it is difficult to determine exactly where he falls on the issue,
Ricoeur would not be the first philosopher to view rhetoric as a disci­
pline concerned with a certain kind of discourse. Indeed, our reading of
Cassin in Chapter 1 showed the origins of this view in Plato. To recall,
in the Gorgias Plato contrasted the rhetoric of the sophists with the dia­
lectic of the (Platonic) philosopher, the former being the cheap imitation
of the latter. This way of thinking has become so commonplace that, at
148  For a range of responses to the question of the relationship between rhetoric and her­
meneutics (including Ricoeur’s “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”), see Walter Jost and
Michael J. Hyde, eds., Rhetoric and Hermeneutics in Our Time: A Reader (New Haven,
CT: Yale University Press, 1997).
149  Ricoeur, FMR, 138, translation modified/DMR, 144/L2, 482.
150  Ricoeur, FMR, 148–49/DMR, 154–155/L2, 495.


222  Extending the Audience
least in everyday English, the term “rhetoric” is largely synonymous with
deception or manipulation, introducing a dissociation between rhetorical
speech and speech that is transparent and truthful. Aristotle, too, as we
have just seen from Ricoeur’s reading, thinks of rhetoric as a certain kind
of speech. While viewed more positively than Plato, rhetoric for Aristotle
is that kind of speech required by and crafted for three typical social con­
texts. Despite their differences, in both cases rhetoric remains a particular
kind of discourse.
Perhaps the most well-known analogue of this view in contemporary
philosophy is that of Jürgen Habermas. In The Theory of Communicative
Action (1981) Habermas famously makes a typological distinction between
strategic and communicative action. Though he does not explicitly invoke
the conflict between philosophy and rhetoric, its trace is easily detected.
Defining strategic action as “a reciprocal influencing of one another by
opponents acting in a purposive-rational manner”, Habermas could just as
well be talking about rhetoric. By contrast, the nobler pursuit of “reaching
understanding among members of a life-world” is associated with philoso­
phy.151 Yet, the point we want to draw attention to is the following: when
defining strategic and communicative action as “action types”, Habermas
explains that he is not looking “to designate two analytic aspects under
which the same action could be described”. To the contrary, Habermas
insists that there is a difference in kind between strategic and commu­
nicative action; they are different types of action or ways of conducting
oneself. Any given instance of action must therefore be classified as one or
the other.
Habermas’s antipathy to rhetoric is no secret.152 But notice the simi­
larity even with rhetoric’s defenders, for whom it is a distinct set of
“weapons” to those of the philosopher, as Quintilian puts it citing Lucius
Crassus.153 One prominent example is Ernesto Grassi, who argues that
rhetorical speech is “the basis of rational thought” and thus a precondi­
tion of philosophy itself.154 While this way of construing things certainly
elevates rhetoric, it continues to define it as a distinct way of speaking.
151  Jürgen Habermas, The Theory of Communicative Action, Volume 1: Reason and the
Rationalization of Society, trans. Thomas McCarthy (Boston, MA: Beacon Press, [1981]
1984), 286.
152  Famously, Gadamer defends rhetoric against Habermas (and, less famously, endorses
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca) in his “Afterword” to Truth and Method (Hans-Georg
Gadamer, Truth and Method, revised second edition, trans. Joel Weinsheimer and Don­
ald G. Marshall (London: Bloomsbury, 2004), 593).
153  Quintilian, The Orator’s Education, Volume V: Books 11–12, ed. and trans. Donald A.
Russell (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2002), 223.
154  Ernesto Grassi, Rhetoric as Philosophy: The Humanist Tradition (University Park, PA:
The Pennsylvania State University Press, 1980), 20.


Extending the Audience  223
Donald Phillip Verene argues something similar in The Rhetorical Sense of
Philosophy (2021). As he explains, his title aims to capture “how rhetori­
cal speech both brings philosophical speech into existence and allows it to
endure and be understood”.155 Much like Grassi, here again the rhetorical
and the philosophical are presented as different ways of speaking, different
kinds of action.
It is precisely this typologization of discourse (and of action more gener­
ally) that we want to avoid.156 Rather than continue to think of rhetoric as
being concerned with a particular kind of discourse, would it not be better
to take the opposite approach? Would it not be better to define the object
of rhetoric as a particular aspect of discourse? This would be our preferred
way of characterizing what Ricoeur called the “complementarity” between
hermeneutics and rhetoric. Thus, where Ricoeur offers a disciplinary
typology of the uses of discourse—using the different social contexts and
competing aims of the disciplines as criteria—we shall instead view them
horizontally, so to speak, as different perspectives on any given instance of
discourse, of discourse as such. In other words, instead of taking rhetoric
and hermeneutics as disciplines concerned with different discourse types,
we argue that they are concerned with different dimensions of discourse.157
Here we follow the “anti-typological” strategy of Hans Joas and, more
recently, Ernst Wolff in thinking about action.158 Where Joas empha­
sizes the “creative dimension to all human action”, Wolff emphasizes
that “technicity” is “not a type of action, but an attribute of action in
155  Donald Phillip Verene, The Rhetorical Sense of Philosophy (Ithaca, NY: Cornell Univer­
sity Press, 2021), x.
156  In this regard, our argument comes close to that of Gerald Posselt. Using Foucault’s idea
of a “double reading” Posselt argues for a “rhetorized” philosophy that avoids repro­
ducing “the traditional dispute between philosophy and rhetoric” (Posselt, “Rhetorizing
Philosophy: Toward a ‘Double Reading’ of Philosophical Texts”, Philosophy & Rheto­
ric, Vol. 52, No. 1 (2019), 28). Posselt’s Foucauldian approach would thus serve as a
fruitful point of comparison to the Perelmanian-Ricoeurian view we offer here.
157  Despite the ambiguity of “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”, we think Ricoeur would
endorse this non-typological view. For instance, at the Ottawa conference on rationality
in 1977, Ricoeur revealingly asks the following question to Habermas after the latter
presents his typology of action: “I have a first general question concerning the strategy of
the paper, if I may use the word ‘strategy’ with regard to a question of agreement and not
of strategic action …. The question is: why proceed by means of a typology?” (Geraets,
ed., Rationality To-Day/La Rationalité aujourd’hui, 207). The inability to separate the
strategic from the communicative is precisely the sort of problem that Ricoeur wants
to avoid, but—as we shall see below—is inevitable if one proceeds by way of typology.
158  Comparison could also be made here to the way that Austin understands the distinc­
tion between locutionary, illocutionary, and perlocutionary acts as “different senses or
dimensions” of one and the same speech act (Austin, How to Do Things with Words,
109).


224  Extending the Audience
general”.159 Importantly, neither author seeks to reduce human action to
its creative or technical dimension. Rather, in their respective studies on
these two different dimensions of action, they approach human action as
a multifaceted phenomenon that can and must be examined from various
perspectives and in terms of its various dimensions (on their own and in
their interrelations).160
The advantage of this approach is that it avoids the problem encountered
by Ricoeur’s disciplinary typology. To recall, Ricoeur’s fear is that rhetoric
might seek to “totalize” the field of discourse for itself. By this he means
something like exclusively viewing all instances of discourse through the
disciplinary lens of rhetoric (as he understands it). While Ricoeur rightly
sees that this gesture would involve the usurpation of philosophy, subsum­
ing it under the norm of persuasion, his fear can be dispelled by redefining
the object of rhetoric in a non-typological way. Rather than continue to
think of rhetoric as one type of language use among others, we argue that
it is more fruitful to define the object of rhetoric as a general dimension of
discourse—a dimension of discourse we call “rhetoricity”.161
To define the object of rhetoric as rhetoricity is not to reduce every use
of language to “mere rhetoric” or to submit all discourse to the norm of
persuasion and rhetoric’s disciplinary presuppositions—as Ricoeur under­
stands them and rightly cautions against. Rather, it is to recognize that
all discourse has an addressive character: that all discourse constructs a
determinate audience by means of specific techniques. Moreover, it is to
159  Hans Joas, The Creativity of Action, trans. Jeremy Gaines and Paul Keast (Cambridge:
Polity, 1992), 4; Wolff, Between Daily Routine and Violent Protest, 86. See also Wolff,
“‘Technology’ as the Critical Social Theory of Human Technicity”, Journal of Philo­
sophical Research, Vol. 41 (2016): 333–369.
160  In the case of Joas, his concern is to avoid taking “rational action” as a theoretical start­
ing point, which has the unfortunate consequence (among others) of creating a residual
“non-rational” category of action. As he explains, all such theories “create an evaluative
framework into which they force the multiplicity of action phenomena. This is true irre­
spective of the definition each theory of action gives to the concept of rationality” (Joas,
The Creativity of Action, 145–147). Joas’s point bears a certain resemblance to Perelman
and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s methodological problem of how argumentation, as the expres­
sion of reason, can be studied without already presupposing a particular conception of
rationality (see Chapter 2, p. 67).
161  In 1990 Bender and Wellbery coined a similar sounding term, “rhetoricality”, to capture
the historical transformation of rhetoric concomitant with modernism. Where the clas­
sical rhetorical tradition “rarified speech and fixed it within a gridwork of limitations”,
modernism, they argue, is the age of rhetoricality, “a generalized rhetoric that penetrates
to the deepest levels of human experience” (John Bender and David E. Wellbery, “Rhe­
toricality: On the Modernist Return of Rhetoric”, in The Ends of Rhetoric: History,
Theory, Practice, eds. John Bender and David E. Wellbery (Stanford, CA: Stanford Uni­
versity Press, 1990), 25). Our conception of “rhetoricity” is more cautious in that it is
not anchored to postmodernity or any other historical period.


Extending the Audience  225
recognize that these techniques were chosen, consciously or not, by some­
one in definite historical circumstances in view of modifying the adher­
ence of their intended audience in some particular way. Even in the case
of writing and other forms of symbolic expression—where the “text” is
distanciated from its author—the “audience of the text” remains; this
externalization of the author’s audience construction is embedded in the
text, lying in wait for a reader. The fact that certain discursive choices are
inscribed in the text and not others, is what gives the rhetorical perspective
its specificity and where it derives its importance for philosophy and all
fields concerned with human action.
As we are not the first to use “rhetoricity”, it is important to spec­
ify where our definition departs from those of others. While the mean­
ing of the term enjoys no consensus, it is perhaps best known from the
work of Diane Davis. Treated most extensively in Inessential Solidarity:
Rhetoric and Foreigner Relations (2014), Davis introduces “rhetoricity”
as the “always prior … condition for what is called the ‘art’ of rhetoric”.162
Drawing heavily on Levinas, her aim is “to expose an originary (or preo­
riginary) rhetoricity—an affectability or persuadability—that is the condi­
tion for symbolic action”. In other words, before I utter a single word,
before I put pen to paper—indeed, before it is even meaningful to speak
of the “I” at all—rhetoricity in her sense is already operative; it is “a con­
stitutive persuadability and responsivity that testifies … to a fundamental
structure of exposure”.163
At the risk of seeming to quibble over definitions, our approach differs
from Davis on the question of whether we really want to call this “rheto­
ricity”. To be clear: we do not for a moment dispute the phenomenon she
identifies, this “always prior relation to the foreign(er) without which no
meaning-making or determinate (symbolic) relation would be possible”.164
Our concern is rather that calling this phenomenon rhetoricity runs the
risk of diluting what, following Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, we take
to be the essence of all things rhetorical: the concern, both practical and
theoretical, with the audience. Our own view, then, is more cautious. For
us, rhetoricity refers plainly to a particular dimension of discourse (and
action more generally, as we shall argue in Chapter 6). This clear referent
162  Diane Davis, Inessential Solidarity: Rhetoric and Foreigner Relations (Pittsburgh, PA:
University of Pittsburgh Press, 2010), 2.
163  Davis, Inessential Solidarity, 2, 3.
164  Davis, Inessential Solidarity, 2. We do have some reservations about taking such a Levi­
nasian view too far, which we address via Ricoeur in Chapter 6, pp. 264-265.


226  Extending the Audience
leaves no ambiguity as to what exactly rhetoricity is the rhetoricity of—
human action.165
3  Conclusion
We began our search for an expanded conception of audience by turning
to Ricoeur’s hermeneutic philosophy, examining his implicit and explicit
discussions of rhetorical themes in three exemplary texts—“Work and the
Word” (1953), Freud and Philosophy (1965), and The Rule of Metaphor
(1975). By bracketing Ricoeur’s narrow conception of rhetoric in these
texts, we then reconstructed the missed encounter between Perelman and
Ricoeur in The Rule of Metaphor, showing that Ricoeur shares Perelman’s
concern with audiences, albeit from a hermeneutic perspective. The evi­
dence for this was particularly clear in his account of living metaphor.
Unlike theorists who look to understand metaphor by dissecting language
into smaller and smaller units, Ricoeur shows that the innovative power of
metaphor can only be accounted for at the higher level of discourse, where
the metaphorical process is finally accomplished (or not) by an audience.
In the case of the written word, it is the reader who actualizes the semantic
potential of a metaphorical statement, thus bringing a new meaning into
the language and simultaneously redescribing reality.
This overlapping concern with audiences finally brought us to consider
how Ricoeur could contribute to the extended conception of audience we
were looking for. By analogy with Ricoeur’s critique of romantic herme­
neutics, we showed how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s “romantic”
conception of audience can be developed into the “audience of the text”—
the externalized audience construction embedded in the text. Surprisingly,
however, this hermeneutic extension of the audience did not erase the spec­
ificity of Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical problematic. If the
hermeneutic question in this context is, “What kind of addressivity does
writing introduce?”, the rhetorical question (bracketing the usual meaning
of this expression) remains, “How does the text construct its reader?”.
To extend the concept of audience with hermeneutics it was therefore not
necessary to abandon Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical perspec­
tive altogether. In fact, what it did require was resisting Ricoeur’s way of
framing the relationship between the two. Where Ricoeur seems to under­
stand rhetoric and hermeneutics as rival disciplines concerned with dif­
ferent types of discourse, we argued that rhetoric’s object should instead
165  Given the limited scope of our argument here, which requires further unpacking in
Chapter 6, we leave it to a future study to explore the consequences of our approach for
the debate between Davis and Mailloux over rhetorical hermeneutics (cf. Davis, Ines­
sential Solidarity, 66–85; Mailloux, Rhetoric’s Pragmatism, 71–85).


Extending the Audience  227
be understood as a particular dimension of discourse. Unlike theories of
style, to which rhetoric is so often reduced and which focus exclusively on
linguistic form, we have not found rhetoricity to be a feature of language
alone. Rather, as a dimension of discourse—“somebody saying something
to someone about something”—rhetoricity emerges from the interplay of
what Meyer would call logos, ethos, and pathos—or, in the case of a writ­
ten text, language, author, and reader.
In the next and final chapter, we shall use our expanded concept of
audience to provide an answer to the question we introduced at the begin­
ning of this chapter: namely, what stands out in Ricoeur’s philosophy
when approached from a rhetorical perspective? By continuing to read his
relation to rhetoric against the grain, we shall see that Ricoeur’s work pro­
vides conceptual resources for further developing Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s NRP while at the same time allowing us to flesh out our responses
to Badiou and Cassin’s concerns about rhetoric.


6
In his 1939 essay “Attention”, Ricoeur invites us to reflect on the fol­
lowing paradox: while the act of paying attention makes something new
appear, what appears was in another sense already there.1 This paradox
approximates our approach to Ricoeur’s philosophy here: to make some­
thing appear that is neither entirely there nor entirely absent—rhetoric.
If the previous chapter justified the possibility and fruitfulness of using
Ricoeur to extend Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s conception of audi­
ence, we now advance the argument by pursuing three threads in Ricoeur’s
work that come into focus when viewed through a rhetorical lens. Pace
Cassin and Badiou, each thread illustrates a different aspect of what we are
calling the rhetoricity of philosophy.
The first thread responds to Cassin’s charge that rhetoric is too philo­
sophical. Here we argue that Ricoeur’s notion of “refiguration” reconciles
the apparent tension between her sophistic conception of performance and
Perelman’s rhetorical conception of audience. As we shall see, both the
philosopher and the sophist must address themselves to someone if their
ideas are to have any effect; and this is true whether one’s aim is to disclose
a fact about the world or to perform something new. The second thread
responds to Badiou’s charge that rhetoric is too sophistical. Here we use
Ricoeur’s conception of “phronetic judgment” to expose the constitutive
rhetoricity of philosophical practice. What Ricoeur helps us emphasize is
that there is an audience both upstream and downstream from the philoso­
pher—even a philosopher like Badiou, who rejects the sort of pluralism
Perelman advocates.
The third thread takes us beyond the terms of the Badiou-Cassin debate.
Having defined the object of rhetoric as “rhetoricity”, we then turn to
1  Ricoeur, “Attention: A Phenomenological Study of Attention and Its Philosophical Con­
nections”, Philosophical Anthropology, ed. Johann Michel and Jérôme Porée, trans. David
Pellauer (Cambridge: Polity, 2016), 31/Écrits et Conférences 3—Anthropologie philos­
ophique, ed. Johann Michel and Jérôme Porée (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 2013), 62.
Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of
Philosophy
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-7


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  229
“rhetoric” itself. Taking our cue from Aristotle, we argue that rhetoric is
best understood as a basic human capacity. As we shall see, it is by exercis­
ing this capacity that discourse and action take on their rhetoricity. Using
Ricoeur’s philosophical anthropology as a starting point, we show that
the work of our rhetorical capacity is visible in everyday social interaction
as well as in highly specialized contexts like philosophy. We conclude by
showing why those who stand to gain most from this view are those who
understand philosophy not only as a way of interpreting the world but also
as a way of changing it.
1  Rhetorical Refiguration
This section argues that Ricoeur’s conception of “refiguration” undercuts
Cassin’s sharp distinction between a sophistic conception of performance
and a rhetorical conception of audience. We begin by tracing the develop­
ment of Ricoeur’s philosophy of action from his first major work, Freedom
and Nature (1950) to “Phenomenology and Hermeneutics” (1975), where
he takes stock of the evolution of his method. Doing so brings out the
increasing importance he places on the receptive capacity of human beings.
We then turn to Time and Narrative (1983–1985) where receptivity takes
on a newfound importance in his threefold conception of mimesis. As
Ricoeur helps us show, Cassin’s opposition is untenable because discourse
never acts on the world directly; any world effect must be brought about
by an audience, the hearer or reader of discourse, who incorporates it into
the world through the work of refiguration.
The Voluntary and the Involuntary
Well-known for introducing Husserl to several generations of philoso­
phers in postwar France, Ricoeur’s extensive philosophical itinerary is
inseparable from the story of phenomenology. Although first introduced to
Husserl’s writings in 1935 while studying at the Sorbonne, Ricoeur’s part
in our story begins in a prison camp in Pomerania, where he would begin
his translation of Husserl’s Ideas I (1913) as well as the sketch of his first
major project, a “philosophy of the will”.2 After five long years in captiv­
ity, Ricoeur returned to France and began to prepare the two works that he
would submit for his doctorate: his translation of Ideas I and Freedom and
2  Ricoeur, “Intellectual Biography”, The Philosophy of Paul Ricoeur. The Library of Living
Philosophers: Volume XXII, ed. Lewis Edwin Hahn (Chicago, IL: Open Court, 1995), 6–7,
10/Réflexion faite. Autobiographie intellectuelle (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, [1995] 2001), 35,
41. For a detailed account of Ricoeur’s time as a prisoner of war from 1940 to 1945, see
Part II of Dosse, Paul Ricœur. Les sens d’une vie, 71–95.


230  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
Nature: The Voluntary and the Involuntary [Philosophie de la volonté.
1. Le volontaire et l’involontaire] (1950) [hereafter The Voluntary and
the Involuntary], part one of his projected three-volume philosophy of the
will.3
As Ricoeur recounts in his “Intellectual Biography” (1995), The
Voluntary and the Involuntary was conceived as “a counterpart in the prac­
tical sphere” to Maurice Merleau-Ponty’s Phenomenology of Perception
(1945).4 Unlike Jean-Paul Sartre in Being and Nothingness (1943), who
takes more of a distance to Husserl, Ricoeur is closer to Merleau-Ponty
in viewing his work as an extension of Husserl’s project. Specifically,
Ricoeur wants to extend Husserl’s method into new domains of conscious
(i.e. intentional) life.5 Ricoeur recalls that, at the time, it seemed to him
that Merleau-Ponty’s analysis of perceptual consciousness had “carried
to perfection only the description of representative acts”.6 As Dermot
Moran explains, what Ricoeur rejects is “Husserl’s classification of will­
ing (along with memory, imagination, etc.) as a form of ‘representation’
(Vergegenwärtigung)”.7 This left the practical sphere in need of its own
dedicated description—as Husserl had already suggested in §95 of Ideas I.8
It is to this task that Ricoeur would devote himself in his thesis.
Defended and published in 1950, The Voluntary and the Involuntary
is an eidetic description of the voluntary and involuntary structures of
human action. Simply put, it is a description of what makes wilful action

Herméneutique, ed. Daniel Frey and Nicola Stricker (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 2010), 18.

6  Ricoeur, “Intellectual Biography”, 11/RF, 44. For a dedicated treatment of Ricoeur’s rela­
tion to Merleau-Ponty, see Marc-Antoine Vallée, “Ricoeur and Merleau-Ponty: From Per­
ception to Action”, A Companion to Ricoeur’s Freedom and Nature, ed. Scott Davidson
(Lanham, MA: Lexington, 2018), 3–15.

186.
