Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  231
really action and not some other mode of consciousness such as perception,
memory, or fantasy. Unlike other kinds of description, an eidetic descrip­
tion of willing engages in a specific form of abstraction which brackets
what Ricoeur calls the “fault” [la faute]—the pathological or compromised
dimension of human existence, in both moral and non-moral senses.9 This
form of abstraction is an adaptation of Husserl’s “eidetic reduction”, the
method of bracketing the empirical fact that something is the case in order
to work out its essence (or eidos).10 Provided that “we remain constantly
on guard against any Platonizing interpretation of essences and consider
them simply as meanings or principles of intelligibility”, Ricoeur explains,
eidetic description enables the phenomenologist to identify the key vol­
untary and involuntary structures of human life which “precede any
empirical, inductive study undertaken according to experimental methods
borrowed from the natural sciences”.11
By means of this method, Ricoeur identifies three primary structures of
the will: (1) deciding, (2) acting, and (3) consenting. To will is thus (1) to
decide upon a projected end based on motives, (2) to activate one’s powers
or capacities for the purposes of realizing that end, and (3) to acquiesce to
various forms of necessity that impede the realization of one’s project as
intended. What is distinctive of Ricoeur’s approach to the will, and where
it proves more nuanced than that of Sartre, for instance, is his emphasis
on the ineliminable tension between voluntary and involuntary aspects
of each structure. Thus, (1) the reasons that motivate our decisions are
always entangled with the needs and desires of the body, just as (2) our
ability to realize ends is always dependent on our preformed skills, emo­
tions, and habits. Similarly, (3) our consent to the situation in which we
find ourselves is always constrained by the experienced necessity of our
  9  Ricoeur, Freedom and Nature: The Voluntary and the Involuntary, trans. Erazim V. Kohák
(Evanston, IL: Northwestern University Press, 2007), 3/Philosophie de la volonté. 1. Le
volontaire et l’involontaire (Paris: Éditions Points, [1950] 2009), 19.
10  Ricoeur, FN, 4/VI, 20. For Husserl there is an essence correlated to every experience of
a fact such that things appear to us in one way rather than another. In his commentary
accompanying his translation of Ideas I (translated separately into English as A Key to
Edmund Husserl’s Ideas I (1996)), Ricoeur is careful to emphasize that this does not
commit the phenomenologist to any kind of (bad) Platonism: “One cannot insist too
much on the non-metaphysical character of the notion of essence; it is introduced here
[§2, 11] dialectically as a correlate of fact, as that which provides a fact with the neces­
sary determination so that it has this particular meaning rather than another” (Ricoeur,
A Key to Edmund Husserl’s Ideas I, trans. Bond Harris, Jacqueline Bouchard Spurlock,
Pol Vandevelde, ed. Pol Vandevelde (Milwaukee, WI: Marquette University Press, 1996),
65–66/Husserl, Idées directrices pour une phénoménologie et une philosophie phénomé­
nologique pures. Tome premier. Introduction générale à la phénoménologie, trans. Paul
Ricoeur (Paris: Gallimard, 1950), 18–19).
11  Ricoeur, FN, 4/VI, 20.


232  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
character, the unconscious, and various other biological realities. Despite
the schematic presentation, Ricoeur is careful to remind us that it is always
one and the same will under consideration, his analysis only highlight­
ing different points of view.12 “In reality”, as he puts it in the conclusion,
“each moment of freedom—deciding, moving, consenting—unites action
and passion, initiative and receptivity, according to a different intentional
mode”.13
As The Voluntary and the Involuntary is concerned more with doing
phenomenology than explicating its methodology, it is helpful to turn to
Ricoeur’s essays from the same period for further clarification. Of par­
ticular help here is “Method and Tasks of a Phenomenology of the Will”
(1952), first presented in Brussels in 1951 at Father Herman Van Breda’s
international colloquium on phenomenology.14 As a change in audience
involves a change in focus, here Ricoeur is more explicit in the way that he
situates his approach in relation to Husserl and the broader phenomeno­
logical movement.15
Two aspects of this essay are of relevance to our discussion. The first
is Ricoeur’s defence of Husserl’s patient method of intentional analysis
“when applied to the practical functions of consciousness”.16 This allows
Ricoeur to distinguish his approach from other forms of “existential” phe­
nomenology. The second is Ricoeur’s argument that a faithful extension of
the phenomenological method to the practical functions of consciousness
involves a repudiation of Husserl’s doctrine of transcendental idealism
wherein perceptual consciousness becomes the prototype for every other
mode of consciousness. Let us examine these two points in more detail.
First, Ricoeur’s application of Husserl’s method to practical conscious­
ness leads him to conclude that intentional analysis need not go through “a
12  Ricoeur, FN, 341/VI, 427.
13  Ricoeur, FN, 483/VI, 602.

Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  233
prior phenomenology of perception and objectivating acts in general” but
should instead proceed directly to a reflection on the correlation of willing
and what is willed.17 Despite the apparent departure from Husserl, Ricoeur
suggests that, unlike some of his contemporaries (primarily Merleau-
Ponty, although he does not mention him by name), this approach remains
faithful to Husserl’s actual practice in the period of the Ideas.18 Above all,
Ricoeur seeks to distinguish the “existential direction” of his own phe­
nomenology of the will from what he regards as other, less rigorous “exis­
tential” appropriations of Husserl’s method.19
Second, despite this criticism of a certain kind of existential phenom­
enology, Ricoeur also seeks to distance himself from Husserl’s transcen­
dental idealist interpretation of the phenomenological method. Indeed,
“the more I read Husserl”, Ricoeur will write a few years later, “the more
I become convinced that the method as practiced draws the philosopher in
a direction that is less and less compatible with the method as philosophi­
cally interpreted”.20 Ricoeur’s phenomenology of the will, which began as
an extension of “a method tested elsewhere to a new segment of reality”,
comes to turn “its results against the doctrine, which has gradually become
indistinguishable from the method”.21
The doctrine of transcendental idealism, as Ricoeur explains, is any
attempt, broadly speaking, “at relating the conditions of the appearance of
things to the structure of human subjectivity”, a view which places Husserl
in the lineage of Kant and Hegel.22 Not to be confused with metaphysi­
cal idealism, according to which the existence of objects depends on con­
sciousness, Husserl’s transcendental idealism is more specifically the view
that consciousness is responsible for the way that objects appear, or are
first given to consciousness as objects of experience; or, in other words, for
constituting the meaning that objects take on in their appearing.23 What is
ultimately at issue for Ricoeur, then, is the question of how to understand
the “constitution” of the voluntary and involuntary aspects of willing, or
17  Ricoeur, HP, 214/EP, 67.
18  Ricoeur, HP, 214–215/EP, 67–68.
19  Ricoeur, HP, 228/EP, 85.
20  Ricoeur, “Phenomenology”, The Southwestern Journal of Philosophy, Vol. 5, No. 3
([1953] 1974), 155/EP, 168.
21  Ricoeur, HP, 221/EP, 76.
22  Ricoeur, “Existential Phenomenology”, HP, 203/“Phénoménologie existentielle”, Ency­
clopédie Française. Tome XIX. Philosophie Religion, ed. Gaston Berger (Paris: Société
Nouvelle de l’Encyclopédie Française, 1957), 19.10–9.
23  Husserl clarifies the meaning of his “transcendental-phenomenological idealism” in his
1930 “Nachwort” to Ideas I, see Husserl, Ideas: General Introduction to Phenomenology,
trans. W.R. Boyce Gibson (London: Routledge, 2002), xlii–xliii).


234  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
to what extent consciousness is responsible for the intentional achievement
of practical “objects”.
Ricoeur’s objection to Husserl’s transcendental idealist interpretation
of phenomenology follows the same logic as his objection to certain exis­
tential interpretations: both see every mode of consciousness through the
lens of one dominant mode rather than taking each mode on its own terms.
Where the existential interpretation leads to an “indistinct existential mon­
ism” modelled on a vague sense of praxis, Husserl’s transcendental ide­
alism is extrapolated from the analysis of those modes of consciousness
founded on representation.
Against both interpretations, Ricoeur insists that noematic reflection
on “the project” (the intentional correlate of deciding), “the pragma” (the
intentional correlate of moving or acting), and “the situation” (the inten­
tional correlate of consenting) disconfirms what Husserl expected future
analysis would bear out—namely, the assumption that the affective and
practical strata of consciousness are also founded on the same “objectivat­
ing acts” as theoretical consciousness.24 Against this “logistic prejudice”
[préjugé logiciste], Ricoeur suggests that the “practical life of conscious­
ness presents original problems which in principle are not resolved by an
interpretation of ‘representation’”; specifically, that voluntary life provides
“privileged and irreducible access to the problems of constitution”.25
What is ultimately at issue in this critique is, paradoxically, transcenden­
tal phenomenology’s exaggeration of the creative role of consciousness. By
tacitly privileging “representational” consciousness, transcendental phe­
nomenology fails to register the properly dialectical relationship between
activity and passivity that emerges from a dedicated description of the
practical life of consciousness.26 For Husserl (later reproduced by Sartre
in terms of the “for-itself” and the “in-itself”), there is an irresolvable
dualism between the pure activity of consciousness and the pure passiv­
ity of what is other. By contrast, what Ricoeur’s study shows is that all
“creation” harbours a hidden receptivity and, inversely, that experiences
of passivity also presuppose some degree of activity.27 Indeed, it is on this
point that Ricoeur seeks to “revise” Husserl’s transcendental interpreta­
tion in a more “existential direction”, without falling back into the more
24  Ricoeur, HP, 221/EP, 76.
25  Ricoeur, HP, 221, 222, our emphasis/EP, 76, 78.
26  Ricoeur, HP, 222/EP, 78.
27  Ricoeur rejects the idea that human action can create something ex nihilo. This is why
he generally prefers the term “innovation” to “creation”, which we have already seen in
his discussion of “semantic innovation” in The Rule of Metaphor. Ricoeur discusses his
views about creation further in his debate with Cornelius Castoriadis, see Suzi Adams,
ed., Ricoeur and Castoriadis: On Human Creation, Historical Novelty, and the Social
Imaginary (London: Rowman & Littlefield, 2017), 5).


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  235
voluntaristic position of someone like Sartre. The major achievement of
Ricoeur’s thesis, then, is its illustration of “the specifically human modality
of freedom. Human freedom is a dependant independence, a receptive ini­
tiative. The notion of creation is directly opposed to the mode of existence
which characterizes human willing”.28
The Hermeneutic Condition
The tension between freedom and constraint captured by the phrase
“receptive initiative” is one of the most prominent themes in Ricoeur’s
work. At every level of analysis, he will emphasize the productive tension
between the active and the passive. Let us look briefly at how Ricoeur
maintains this tension as he moves beyond the level of pure phenomeno­
logical description.
In Finitude and Guilt [Philosophie de la volonté. 2. Finitude et culpa­
bilité] (1960), the second volume of his philosophy of the will, Ricoeur
begins to reinsert the human being into its socio-cultural and historical
context. With the eidetic reduction no longer in place, it is now possible to
thematize the symbolic language through which the previously bracketed
“fault” of human experience is expressed. Thus, if The Voluntary and the
Involuntary sketched the fundamental possibilities of action as an “undif­
ferentiated keyboard upon which the guilty as well as the innocent man
might play”, then Finitude and Guilt turns to hermeneutics to decipher
the myths through which the meaning of guilt and innocence are culturally
transmitted. At this point in Ricoeur’s work, a hermeneutics is methodo­
logically necessary to introduce mythic discourse into philosophy without
violating the specificities of either genre.29
This is a point worth pausing over, as Ricoeur’s interest in symbols is
often incorrectly assumed to be motivated by personal religious conviction
rather than something imposed on his philosophy by objective historical
circumstances. As Ricoeur is careful to explain, the task of interpreting
religious symbolism only “has a precise meaning now, at a certain stage
in philosophical discussion, and, more broadly, in connection with cer­
tain traits of our ‘modernity’”.30 If there is any meaning to the idea of
doing philosophy in or after modernity for Ricoeur, it is that philosophy
can neither assume the pre-given meaning of a particular cultural tradition
nor pretend to be self-inaugurating.31 This double bind is well-captured
28  Ricoeur, HP, 228, our emphasis/EP, 85.
29  Ricoeur, FM, xlii; FC, 25.
30  Ricoeur, SE, 349/FC, 568.
31  Ricoeur, FM, 4-5/FC, 41.


236  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
by Ricoeur’s expression “the symbol gives rise to thought”.32 On the one
hand, philosophy cannot simply reflect on itself—it must have something
to reflect on. But, on the other, reflecting on available symbols introduces
cultural contingency into its discourse owing to “the historical, geographi­
cal, cultural origin of the philosophical question itself”.33
This recourse to hermeneutics in Ricoeur’s thinking thus brings recep­
tion to light as a philosophical problem. Restaging the dialectic of the
voluntary and the involuntary at the level of thought, the freedom of phil­
osophical reflection thus requires the inert immediacy of the symbol. The
symbol, however, is not simply what is given but must itself be “won”
through interpretation.34 Between the two impasses, then, Ricoeur pursues
a third way, “a creative interpretation of meaning, faithful to the impul­
sion, to the gift of meaning from the symbol, and faithful also to the phi­
losopher’s oath to seek understanding”.35
Though Ricoeur has travelled some distance from his 1950 doctoral
thesis, it is not until “Phenomenology and Hermeneutics” (1975) that
Ricoeur will stop and take stock of the extent to which his methodology
has parted ways with Husserl.36 Ricoeur’s thesis in this essay is twofold:
on the one hand, he argues that hermeneutics definitively negates Husserl’s
transcendental interpretation of phenomenology, but that, on the other,
there nonetheless remains a “mutual belonging” between hermeneutics
and phenomenology. In other words, that “phenomenology remains the
unsurpassable presupposition of hermeneutics” and “phenomenology can­
not constitute itself without a hermeneutical presupposition”.37 His her­
meneutic critique of Husserlian idealism here offers a useful summary of
his position on the productive tension between reception and innovation
in the mid-70s.
To expose the gap between his own hermeneutic phenomenology
and the idealistic interpretation of phenomenology, Ricoeur summarizes
Husserl’s position in five theses:
32  Ricoeur first used this expression as the title of a 1959 essay (Ricoeur, “The Symbol Gives
Rise to Thought, PA, 107–123/“Le symbole donne à penser”, EC3, 175–199). It was later
used as the title to the conclusion of The Symbolism of Evil, 347–257/FC, 566–577.
33  Ricoeur, SE, 20/FC, 222–223.
34  Ricoeur, SE, 348/FC, 567.
35  Ricoeur, SE, 348/FC, 567.
36  Ricoeur, “Phenomenology and Hermeneutics”, From Text to Action: Essays in Herme­
neutics II, trans. Kathleen Blamey and John B. Thompson (Evanston, IL: Northwestern
University Press, 1991), 25, n. 1/“Phénoménologie et hérméneutique: en venant de Hus­
serl”, Du texte à l’action. Essais d’herméneutique, II (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1986), 43.
37  Ricoeur, FTA, 26/DTA, 44.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  237
	(1)	 Transcendental phenomenology claims the status of a self-grounding
science.
	(2)	 The foundation of transcendental phenomenology lies in the intuition
of experience [Erfahrung].
	(3)	 Only that which is immanent to subjectivity is indubitable.
	(4)	 Transcendental consciousness and empirical consciousness are distinct
perspectives on experience distinguishable only by means of the tran­
scendental reduction.
	(5)	 Phenomenological reflection has an ethical character insofar as it is an
autonomous, self-responsible act.
Ricoeur introduces hermeneutics by responding to Husserl’s position point
for point:
	(1)	 Belonging as the ontological condition of understanding—The sci­
entific ideal of transcendental idealism encounters its limit in the
ontological condition of understanding. While this condition is often
expressed negatively as “finitude”, Ricoeur prefers the more positive
concept of “belonging” [appartenance], which emphasizes the histori­
cal character of human life.38 For Ricoeur, the question of ultimate
foundations still pertains to the sphere of representation, of objecti­
fying thought, which presupposes a certain gap between subject and
object. Belonging, by contrast, emphasizes that the questioner first
“shares in the very thing about which he questions”.39
	(2)	 Understanding is mediated by interpretation—Husserl’s emphasis on
intuition as the final court of epistemic appeal neglects the fact that all
experiences, even our most basic perceptions, are mediated by prior
interpretation. In the following section we discuss this point in terms
of what Ricoeur calls the “symbolic mediation of action”. For now,
we can simply appreciate that the process of interpretation is never
resolved in a final intuition but can always be taken up again. The
historical situatedness of interpretation thus “places the interpreter in
medias res and never at the beginning or the end”.40
	(3)	 Critique of the subject—While phenomenology can be credited for its
incessant critique of the immediacy or givenness of objects, it fails to
extend this critique to the subject itself. Unlike objects, the “ruses of
self-consciousness” are far more subtle and harder to detect.41 The vari­
ous distortions to which consciousness is subject require incorporating
38  Ricoeur, FTA, 29/DTA, 49.
39  Ricoeur, FTA, 30/DTA, 50.
40  Ricoeur, FTA, 33, translation modified/DTA, 54.
41  Ricoeur, FTA, 33/DTA, 54.


238  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
something like a critique of ideology into hermeneutics. As a dialec­
tical counterpart to “belonging”, Ricoeur thus introduces the con­
cept of “distanciation” [distanciation]. The mediation of experience
by “texts” (in the broader sense of this term discussed in Chapter 5)
means that we only “belong to a historical tradition through a relation
of distance which oscillates between remoteness and proximity”.42
	(4)	 Shift from subjectivity to the world—The great irony of transcendental
phenomenology is that its mobilization of intentionality—the thesis
that consciousness is fundamentally directed away from itself—failed
to lead it beyond the sphere of immanence. To avoid hypostatizing
subjectivity by giving it the sole power of constituting meaning, phe­
nomenology must shift its axis from subjectivity to the world—that is,
as Ricoeur puts it elsewhere, to the “works, institutions, and cultural
monuments in which the life of the spirit is objectified”.43 As we have
already observed, hermeneutics subordinates the author’s intention
to the “matter of the text”, thus allowing phenomenology to remain
faithful to its original insight: that the meaning of consciousness lies
outside of itself.
	(5)	 Subjectivity as the terminus of understanding—The idealist thesis of
the self-founding and therefore self-responsible act of the phenomenol­
ogist situates subjectivity at the beginning of the process of understand­
ing. Hermeneutics, by contrast, makes subjectivity the destination of
the “hermeneutic arc”. Unlike romantic hermeneutics, the task of the
subject is not to reconnect with the original intention of the author,
but to “respond to the matter of the text, and hence to the proposals
of meaning the text unfolds”.44 To understand oneself thus involves
a double movement: to appropriate the text is to make it one’s own,
but to make it one’s own involves disappropriating oneself, letting the
matter of the text be what it is. In other words, as Ricoeur puts it, “I
exchange the me, master of itself, for the self, disciple of the text”.45
The common core of these five theses is that subjectivity is first receptive
before it is active. To be a subject is thus first to be subject to—to the
actions of others, to a symbolic universe of already constituted meanings,
to existing institutions, to history, and so on. Yet, we should be careful
here not to conflate reception with simple passivity. For Ricoeur, reception
involves activity; to receive always involves a minimal degree of work, the
42  Ricoeur, FTA, 35/DTA, 57.
43  Ricoeur, “Existence and Hermeneutics”, The Conflict of Interpretations, 21/Le conflit des
interpretations, 47.
44  Ricoeur, FTA, 37/DTA, 60.
45  Ricoeur, FTA, 37/DTA, 60.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  239
work of appropriation. This is even the case in his early phenomenology of
the will, where each moment of freedom “unites action and passion, initia­
tive and receptivity, according to a different intentional mode”.46
Thus, if it was through phenomenology that Ricoeur first accounts for
the way that meaning is made, it is not until he begins to incorporate her­
meneutics into his philosophy that he can more fully account for the fact
that it is never made under circumstances of one’s own choosing but, as
Marx puts it, “under circumstances directly found, given and transmitted
from the past”.47 In the next section, we examine Ricoeur’s reinterpreta­
tion of the hermeneutic circle and its emphasis on the active role of the
audience to further substantiate our response to Cassin.
Quand lire, c’est faire
In Chapter 5 we discussed the importance Ricoeur assigns to the role of the
reader in The Rule of Metaphor. Whether in speech or in writing, it is the
audience who accomplishes the “work of meaning” that brings the meta­
phorical process to a close in the form of a semantic innovation. Though
we were successful in using Ricoeur’s hermeneutics to expand Perelman’s
conception of audience to include forms of writing, this only partly
advanced our argument from where we left off in Chapter 4. There, in our
Perelmanian response to Cassin, we argued that a speaker or author must
first modify the pre-existing adherence of an audience if their discourse is to
have any effect in the world. What remains to be seen, however, is precisely
why this is the case: the audience must do something. But what exactly
does the audience do? For this we turn to Ricoeur’s theory of “threefold
mimesis”, developed primarily in the three volumes of Time and Narrative
[Temps et récit] (1983–1985). It is here that we see the dialectic of freedom
and constraint at work, where the two terms are put into productive ten­
sion by the individual reader, resulting in what Ricoeur calls “refiguration”.
Alongside The Rule of Metaphor, Time and Narrative is the closest
thing to Ricoeur’s projected “poetics of the will”, the third volume of his
unfinished philosophy of the will. In these two works which “form a pair”,
Ricoeur is occupied with the “same basic phenomenon of semantic innova­
tion” but in different ways.48 Like metaphors, narratives are also produced
46  Ricoeur, FN, 483/VI, 602.
47  Karl Marx, “The Eighteenth Brumaire of Louis Bonaparte”, in Karl Marx and Friedrich
Engels, The Marx-Engels Reader, ed. Robert C. Tucker (New York, NY: W.W. Norton &
Company, 1978), 595.
48  Ricoeur, Time and Narrative: Volume 1, trans. Kathleen McLaughlin and David Pellauer
(Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 1984), ix/Temps et récit. Tome 1. L’intrigue
et le récit historique (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1983), 9.


240  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
“entirely on the level of discourse”, which is to say at a level above that of
the sentence.49 Discourse, we will recall, involves “someone saying some­
thing about something to someone”. What we want to focus on is precisely
the active contribution of the audience, this “someone” to whom discourse
is addressed.
The central claim of Time and Narrative is that narrative configura­
tions are the “privileged means by which we re-configure our confused,
unformed, and at the limit mute temporal experience”.50 This thesis is as
strong as it sounds. Over three volumes, Ricoeur will argue that there is a
“transcultural form of necessity” to the correlation between “the activity
of narrating a story and the temporal character of human experience”; or,
in other words, that “time becomes human to the extent that it is artic­
ulated through a narrative mode, and narrative attains its full meaning
when it becomes a condition of temporal existence”.51
Where The Rule of Metaphor argued that metaphors introduce a
new semantic pertinence by means of an impertinent attribution, Time
and Narrative argues that narratives introduce a new congruence in the
organization of a series of events by means of “emplotment” [mise en
intrigue]—whereby “goals, causes, and chance are brought together within
the temporal unity of a whole and complete action”.52 Analogous to his
argument about metaphorical reference, Ricoeur claims that narratives
should not be understood as merely fictitious or imaginary playgrounds
of meaning. For Ricoeur, narratives too have a reference, which is why
historians can also construct narratives without succumbing to the charge
of mere fabrication. However indirect it may be, all narratives refer back
to “the field of human action and its temporal values”.53 It is this traceable
reference to human action that will allow Ricoeur, on the one hand, to
respect the epistemological break of historical explanation from narrative
understanding, without, on the other, severing this connection entirely—
Ricoeur accounts for this indirect connection in terms of what he calls
“historical intentionality”.54
This insistence on action thus places Ricoeur’s hermeneutic theory
of narrative in the lineage of Aristotle’s Poetics. Like Aristotle, Ricoeur
understands the plot—that which is produced by the act of narrating—as
the mimēsis or “creative imitation” of action.55 Here, mimesis should not
49  Ricoeur, TN1, ix/TR1, 9.
50  Ricoeur, TN1, xi/TR1, 12.
51  Ricoeur, TN1, 52/TR1, 105.
52  Ricoeur, TN1, ix/TR1, 9.
53  Ricoeur, TN1, xi/TR1, 12.
54  Ricoeur, TN1, 228–229/TR1, 400–402.
55  Ricoeur, TN1, 45/TR1, 93.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  241
be confused with a copy of some pre-existing reality (as the translation
“imitation” might suggest), or the redoubling of presence (as the alterna­
tive translation “representation” might suggest). Rather, Ricoeur wants to
preserve the “two sides” of poetic configuration he detects in the meaning
of Aristotle’s expression “mimēsis praxeōs”, the imitation of action.56 As
Ricoeur explains, “we have to conceive of mimetic activity as a connection
and not just as a break”. On the one hand, mimesis breaks with action to
the extent that it is depicted in the mode of “as if”. But, on the other hand,
mimesis retains a connection to action to the extent that it is an imitation
of action and borrows its articulations in the poetic work of configura­
tion.57 This emphasis on both the continuity and discontinuity of mimetic
activity leads Ricoeur to his threefold conception of mimesis.
Ricoeur describes the three moments of mimesis in terms of pre-figu­
ration (mimesis I), configuration (mimesis II), and refiguration (mimesis
III). The best place to begin is with mimesis II, the pivot point of Ricoeur’s
analysis and the most tangible of the three.58 Mimesis II refers to what we
have just discussed as emplotment, the creation of a plot. In other words,
it is the poetic configuration itself, or the “text” as we discussed in the
previous chapter. Mimesis I and III, on the other hand, refer respectively to
what is “upstream” [en amont] and “downstream” [en aval] from mimesis
II.59 Upstream, as it were, we have mimesis I, which designates the mimetic
operation’s reference to the “pre-figured” world of action. Downstream
from mimesis II, we have what Ricoeur calls the act of “refiguration”,
mimesis III, whereby the spectator or reader completes the mimetic process
by reintegrating the work back into the field of action.
Here we see the significance of Ricoeur installing his theory of narra­
tive at the level of discourse rather than that of the sign or the sentence.
Concerned only with the internal laws of a work, a purely semiotic the­
ory abstracts mimesis II from I and III. By contrast, the task of Ricoeur’s

in Cassin, ed., The Dictionary of Untranslatables, 659–675. See also Nidesh Lawtoo,

9–40.
57  Ricoeur, TN1, 46/TR1, 93.
58  Here we follow Ricoeur’s use of Roman numerals to designate the three moments of
mimesis rather than the subscript numerals used in McLaughlin and Pellauer’s English
translation.
59  Translators McLaughlin and Pellauer do not retain the metaphor of upstream and down­
stream, opting instead for the more neutral language of the “two sides” of configuration
(Ricoeur, TN1, 46/TR1, 94). For an argument that unpacks the power of this metaphor—
drawing on Castoriadis rather than Ricoeur, but which we think dovetails with what fol­
lows—see Mats Rosengren, “On Being Downstream”, The Past’s Present: Essays on the
Historicity of Philosophical Thinking, ed. Marcia Sá Cavalcante Schuback and Hans Ruin
(Huddinge: Södertörns högskola, 2005): 203–218.


242  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
hermeneutic theory is to “reconstruct the set of operations by which a
work lifts itself above the opaque depths of living, acting, and suffering, to
be given by an author to readers who receive it and thereby change their
acting”.60 In other words, hermeneutics is concerned with “the entire arc
of operations by which practical experience provides itself with works,
authors and readers”.61 While it is tempting to focus solely on the activity
of the author (the movement from mimesis I to II), Ricoeur insists that it is
really the reader who brings unity to the mimetic circle: “the traversal from
mimesis I to mimesis III, by way of mimesis II”; it is the reader “who takes
up through doing something—the act of reading”.62
Refiguration thus brings the mimetic circle to the end of its cycle.
Through the act of reading, narrative attains its full meaning by being rein­
corporated into the field of action. Far from a simple aesthetics of recep­
tion, mimesis III, or the act of refiguration, “marks the intersection of the
world of the text and the world of the hearer or reader; the intersection,
therefore, of the world configured by the text and the world wherein real
action occurs and unfolds its specific temporality”.63 The difference here is
the emphasis on reference. As we discussed in Chapter 5, Ricoeur under­
stands the reference of a text to be what he calls the “world of the text”
rather than the intentions of the author. What is novel about Time and
Narrative is its introduction of the “world of the hearer or reader”, which,
through the act of reading, is brought to bear by audiences who “receive
[the world of the text] according to their own receptive capacity, which
itself is defined by a situation that is both limited and open to the world’s
horizon”.64 Analogous to the world of the text, the world of the reader
does not refer to the simple environment or Umwelt of the reader, but “the
whole set of references opened up by every sort of descriptive or poetic
text I have read, interpreted and loved”.65 As Ricoeur explains, focusing on
the collision of these two worlds sidesteps the false choice between an aes­
thetics of reception and an ontology of the poetic work—thus conceived,

61  Ricoeur, TN1, 53/TR1, 107.
62  Ricoeur, TN1, 53, our emphasis/TR1, 107.

tion against those collected in, for example, Philip Goldstein and James L. Machor, eds.,
New Directions in American Reception Study (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2008).
In addition to providing a helpful overview of the main currents in American rhetorical
studies, Steven Mailloux traces the development of reader-response criticism in his chap­
ter “Rhetoric” in The Bloomsbury Handbook of Literary and Cultural Theory, ed. Jeffrey
R. Di Leo (London: Bloomsbury, 2019), 102–111.
64  Ricoeur, TN1, 77, our emphasis/TR1, 146. We shall return to this idea of a “receptive
capacity” below. .
65  Ricoeur, TN1, 80/TR1, 151.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  243
reference begins to appear more like “co-reference”, a dialogical phenom­
enon, rather than the isolated work of the author or the text alone.66
This sketch is sufficient to clarify our disagreement with Cassin.67
Though our trial between Cassin and Perelman in Chapter 4 ended with a
decision in Perelman’s favour, we did not hesitate to recognize the limits to
his thinking. At the time, however, our conclusion could only be phrased
negatively: Cassin’s theory of sophistic performance cannot do without
some conception of audience, even though Perelman could do little more
than point us in the right direction. In other words, with Perelman all we
could say is that doing things with words presupposes an audience—to
perform a “new Helen” some crowd must be moved by the performance.
With Ricoeur, however, we are now able to explain why exactly this is
the case. We can now see that Cassin’s opposition between a sophistic con­
ception of performance and a rhetorical conception of audience is unten­
able because we never act on the social world as such. An audience always
mediates the author’s configuration and the world-effect. Performance is
thus not so much a “third dimension” of language as a felicitous conjunc­
tion of the other two: “speaking of” and “speaking to”. For example, if
one intends to breathe new meaning into an outdated phrase, improve the
reputation of an unpopular politician, or dispute the value of a treasured
national symbol, we are free to choose from a range of available means.
But, rhetorically speaking, our aim in each case is to invite our audience to
effect these changes alongside us—or, to perform the dissociation, to use
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s vocabulary. This is because, in the final
analysis, it is the audience who does the work of refiguration by incorpo­
rating these changes into their practices.
Moreover, the “involuntary” or “receptive” dimension of this pro­
cess prevents reading from ever being simply arbitrary and voluntaristic.
Ricoeur’s threefold conception of mimesis allows us to appreciate the vari­
ous constraints imposed on the actors involved by the very nature of the
practice. The reader may not refigure the world in any way they choose;
their response is bound by the actual configuration of the text as much as
66  Ricoeur, TN1, 78/TR1, 148.
67  Ricoeur returns to the intersection of the world of the text and the world of the reader
in Chapter Seven of Time and Narrative: Volume 3, trans. Kathleen Blamey and David
Pellauer (Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 1988), 157–179/Temps et récit. 3.
Le temps raconté (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1985), 284–328. Here, in a discussion devoted
largely to fictional writing, Ricoeur revises his earlier vocabulary, arguing that the prob­
lematic of refiguration must ultimately free itself from the language of reference if it is to
accommodate narrative in both fiction and history. For a detailed study of Ricoeur’s the­
ory of reading (as well as its relation to Gadamer), see Daniel Frey, L’interprétation et la
lecture chez Ricoeur et Gadamer (Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 2008), 214–255.


244  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
the virtual “community” of other readers they bring to bear on it.68 Even
on the first point, we can see that the text resists us.
We have already examined one such aspect of this configuration in
Chapter 5: the “audience of the text”. The constitutive rhetoricity of a
text means readers must negotiate the extent to which they coincide with
the audience of the text—the audience construction that is paradoxically
only in the text through the act of reading. In Time and Narrative and
elsewhere, Ricoeur describes the dialectic of freedom and constraint at
work in this process as a “clash of two strategies”. Where the text contains
the author’s “strategy of persuasion”, the reader adopts the “strategy of
a game, even of a battle, involving suspicion and rejection, that allows
the reader to practice the distance of appropriation”.69 Thus even without
broaching epistemological and ethical questions, we can see, at a purely
descriptive level, that the refigurative work of the audience is characterized
by the same “receptive initiative” as any other form of human action.
2  Rhetorical Phronesis
This section argues that Ricoeur’s concept of “phronetic judgment” brings
out the constitutive rhetoricity of philosophical practice, even that of a
philosopher like Badiou. To do so we trace Ricoeur’s reflections on ideol­
ogy and utopia from his Lectures on Ideology and Utopia (1986), first
delivered in 1975, to his later writings on selfhood and the just from the
1990s. As most commentators who discuss Ricoeur’s contribution to ide­
ology critique focus on his writings from the 1970s alone,70 the longer
view we adopt here brings out an important continuity in Ricoeur’s socio-
political thought.71 What Ricoeur’s account of phronetic judgment brings
68 “[E]very reading of a text always takes place within a community, a tradition, or a living
current of thought, all of which display presuppositions and exigencies—regardless of
how closely a reading may be tied to the quid, to “that in view of which” the text was
written” (Ricoeur, “Existence and Hermeneutics”, The Conflict of Interpretations, 3/Le
conflit des interprétations, 23).
69  Ricoeur, “The Problem of Hermeneutics”, Hermeneutics, 18/Écrits et conférences 2, 46.
70  Notable examples include, Bernard P. Dauenhauer, The Promise and Risk of Politics (Lan­
ham, MA: Rowman & Littlefield, 1998); David M. Kaplan, Ricoeur’s Critical Theory
(Albany, NY: State University of New York Press, 2003); Pierre-Olivier Monteil, Ricoeur
politique (Rennes: Presses Universitaires de Rennes, 2013); John Arthos, Hermeneutics
After Ricoeur; Johann Michel, Homo Interpretans: Towards a Transformation of Herme­
neutics, trans. David Pellauer (Lanham, MA: Rowman & Littlefield, [2017] 2019).
71  John Arthos, for example, has recently argued that Ricoeur’s political thought underwent
a “significant shift of emphasis” in his later work (Arthos, Hermeneutics After Ricoeur,
202), while Marc Crépon emphasizes its greater consistency (Marc Crépon, “‘Du paradox
politique’ à la question des appartenances”, L’Herne. Ricoeur (Paris: Éditions de l’Herne,
2004), 307).


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  245
out is that the philosopher’s discourse, like any other, has an audience both
upstream and downstream from it. Following the same logic as Ricoeur’s
threefold mimesis, philosophy is both responsive to a pre-figured world
and addresses itself to an audience in view of motivating some refiguration.
Ideology and Utopia
One of the central theses of Ricoeur’s work is that action is symbolically
mediated. As early as “Work and the Word”, Ricoeur is arguing against
the view that symbolism is a superficial layer of “representation” or illu­
sory “superstructure” erected upon a more basic layer of pure (i.e. non-
symbolic) action. Ricoeur maintains that such a view is self-defeating, as
there would be nothing for symbols to represent if action was not already
symbolically mediated from the beginning.
In “The Symbolic Structure of Action” (1977), for instance, Ricoeur
formulates this position as follows: “there is no human action unless it
has already been articulated, mediated, and interpreted by symbols”.72
We have already seen this thesis at work in Ricoeur’s theory of narrative.
Rather than view narrative as an external symbolic configuration, tacked
on to action after the fact, narration borrows symbolic resources from the
constitutive symbolism of action. The “representative gap” between the
two levels is thus the same gap exploited by both historical and fictional
narrative—albeit for different ends and by different means.73
To distinguish between these two layers of symbolism, Ricoeur intro­
duces the terms “constitutive” (or “constituting”) symbolism and “rep­
resentative symbolism”.74 Where the former refers to the largely implicit
symbolic mediation which constitutes the “unconscious structure of a
community”, the latter refers to the “aesthetic, moral, political, and reli­
gious symbols of a community which constitute a distinct layer of that
community’s practice, discourse, and institutions”, whether reflective or
not.75 Yet, the service symbolism renders to action here is double-edged:
though symbolism plays a necessary role in constituting our words and
deeds as meaningful, it is precisely for this reason that it also harbours the
potential for various kinds of distortion at the level of how we represent
this symbolism to ourselves.
The prime example of such symbolic distortion is the phenomenon of
ideology. One of the main threads of Ricoeur’s work in the 1970s is the
72  Ricoeur, “The Symbolic Structure of Action”, PA, 176/“La structure symbolique de
l’action”, EC3, 277.
73  Ricoeur, PA, 191/EC3, 299–300.
74  Ricoeur, PA, 177–178/EC3, 279.
75  Ricoeur, PA, 177–178/EC3, 279.


246  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
attempt to show that his hermeneutic philosophy can respond to the theo­
retical and practical challenges posed by this phenomenon.76 As ideology
is a notoriously slippery concept, let us approach the problem by means
of an example.
In 2005, then United States Senator from Illinois Barack Obama made
the following remark to the members of the American Library Association:
at a time when truth and science are constantly being challenged
by political agendas and ideologies; a time where so many refuse to
teach evolution in our schools, where fake science is used to beat back
attempts to curb global warming or fund life-saving research; libraries
remind us that truth isn’t about who yells the loudest, but who has the
right information.77
In this otherwise agreeable remark about libraries, the sharp opposition
drawn by Obama between truth and science, on the one hand, and politi­
cal agendas and ideologies, on the other, is indicative of the way that ideol­
ogy is often used and understood in public discourse. Here, terms such as
truth, knowledge, science, and objectivity are opposed to all that expresses
the interests, desires, and ends of particular political actors. This dissocia­
tive technique casts Obama as a credible and fair-minded figure, able to
soberly distinguish the ideological from the non-ideological without in any
way succumbing to the former.
Yet, this strategy raises a philosophical problem: how can he actually
assume this position, when, then as now, many would strongly contest
his claim to ideological neutrality? Though we are right to be suspicious
of any claim to total impartiality, we would also not want to reject the
possibility out of hand; for doing so would seem to resign us to the end­
less perpetuation of ideological interests. In a first approach, both options
appear unacceptable.
This is precisely the dilemma Ricoeur seeks to address in Lectures on
Ideology and Utopia (1986) [hereafter Lectures], first delivered at the
University of Chicago in 1975.78 Here, Ricoeur develops two main lines
of argument. First, taking inspiration from Karl Mannheim’s Ideology and
76  Perhaps the most well-known example of this is his attempt to mediate between the philo­
sophical hermeneutics of Hans-Georg Gadamer and the critical theory of Jürgen Habermas,
see Ricoeur, “Hermeneutics and the Critique of Ideology”, FTA, 270–307/“Herméneu­
tique et critique des ideologies”, DTA, 367–416.
77  Barack Obama, “Literacy and Education in a 21st-Century Economy”, 25 June 2005.
Transcript freely available at: http://obamaspeeches​.com​/024​-Literacy​-and​-Education​-in​
-a​-21st​-Century​-Economy​-Obama​-Speech​.htm.
78  Ricoeur, Lectures on Ideology and Utopia, ed. George H. Taylor (New York, NY: Colum­
bia University Press, 1986), ix. Note that these lectures were first published in English


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  247
Utopia: An Introduction to the Sociology of Knowledge (1929), Ricoeur
situates ideology and utopia within a common framework—as two poles
of the “social and cultural imagination”.79 Second, he shows how think­
ing ideology and utopia together allows us to work through the above
problem posed by ideology, sometimes called “Mannheim’s paradox”.
Although Ricoeur offers a compelling case for the first, in our view he has
not yet worked out a satisfying answer to the second. For this, however,
we must look to Ricoeur’s later work. For now, we begin with Ricoeur’s
first argument.
The methodology Ricoeur employs in the Lectures is that of a “genetic
phenomenology” or “regressive analysis of meaning”.80 Unlike an “ideal
typical analysis”, this approach permits Ricoeur “to reach a level of
description without being outside the interconnections between ideology
and utopia”.81 In other words, Ricoeur begins by examining the way that
ideology and utopia first appear in social reality, typically as pathological
phenomena, and tracing back their more neutral, even positive functions.
What is novel about Ricoeur’s strategy is that it allows him to avoid
two alternative approaches to ideology which he regards as problem­
atic. The first is the approach taken by Marx and Engels in The German
Ideology. Here, ideology is not understood as the passage from one sym­
bolism to another—as it is in Ricoeur—but from a pre-symbolic stage
of “real praxis”, i.e. meaningless human action, directly to a representa­
tive symbolism.82 The second is the approach taken by figures as differ­
ent as Mannheim and Louis Althusser (and perhaps Obama). Rather than
oppose ideology to real praxis, this approach opposes the ideological to
the scientific.
For Ricoeur, however, both strategies fail to appreciate that symbolism
is constitutive of social reality. Instead, these approaches each posit an
uncompromised place from which ideological distortion can be identified
before being translated into French in 1997 as L’idéologie et l’utopie, trad. Myriam
Revault D’Allonnes et Joël Roman (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1997).
79  For clarification on Ricoeur’s use of this compound expression, see George Taylor’s
explanatory footnote, Ricoeur, LIU, n. 1, 323. For a helpful overview of this growing
research area, see Suzi Adams, “What are Social Imaginaries?: A Pathway Through the
Labyrinth”, International Journal of Social Imaginaries, Vol. 2 (2023): 183–212.
80  Ricoeur, LIU, 311/IU, 408. Ricoeur also practices genetic phenomenology in other works,
most notably in Time and Narrative. As he explains in Volume 1, his use of genetic phe­
nomenology there aims “to discover in the phenomenon of the we-relation [l’être-en-
commun] the origin of the connection between individuals and particular societies” (TN1,
198/TR1, 348). See also Paul Ricoeur, “Le ‘questionnement à rebours’ (Die Rückfrage) et
la réduction des idéalités dans la Krisis de Husserl et L’ide﻿́ologie allemande de Marx”,
Alter: Revue de phe﻿́nome﻿́nologie, Vol. 5 (1997): 315–330.
81  Ricoeur, LIU, 311/IU, 408.
82  Ricoeur, “The Symbolic Structure of Action”, PA, 188/EC3, 294–295.


248  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
and dismissed—a pre-symbolic “praxis”, on the one hand, and “science”
on the other. The problem with this is that it is no longer possible to explain
precisely what gets distorted by ideology. By focusing on the constitutive
symbolism of social life, Ricoeur argues that we preserve the critical value
of Marx’s notion of ideology, showing how it is possible for social reality
to appear distorted in the first place.
Of the various levels Ricoeur identifies, ideology first appears as “distor­
tion”, a pathological modification of the social imagination that Marx and
Engels call “false consciousness”.83 At this surface level, ideology appears
as something like an interested misrepresentation of social relations that
reinforces some form of domination. Beneath this level, however, Ricoeur
identifies the more neutral, even positive ideological function of “integra­
tion”, which he adapts from anthropologist Clifford Geertz.84 Elsewhere,
Ricoeur describes ideology’s more basic integrative function as “an unsur­
passable phenomenon of social existence, insofar as social reality always
has a symbolic constitution and incorporates an interpretation, in images
and representations, of the social bond itself”.85 This deeper layer of ideol­
ogy accounts for ideology’s distortive function in the sense that it answers
the question of what becomes distorted under certain conditions.
Yet how is it possible “that ideology plays these two roles, the very
primitive role of integration of a community and the role of distortion of
thought by interests?”86 Ricoeur’s answer is premised on the idea that any
system of authority must present itself as legitimate—legitimate to some­
one. Here he relies on Max Weber, who argues that—apart from the direct
use of force or violence—subjects are never merely passive recipients of
power.87 Indeed, legitimation is a meaningful process in which subjects
are, to some extent, active participants. Ricoeur thus defines “legitima­
tion” as the “meaningful procedures” through which a system of authority
establishes this cooperation beyond the naked use of force.88 Ideology, as
the imaginative representation of the social bond, thus steps in to fill the
83  Ricoeur, LIU, 8/IU, 25. Marx and Engels do not actually use the term “false conscious­
ness” in The German Ideology. Ricoeur suggests that Mannheim probably borrowed it
from Lukács (Ricoeur, LIU, 164/IU, 220). Engels does however use the term “falschen
Bewußtsein” (translated into English as “a consciousness that is spurious”) in a 1983 let­
ter to Franz Mehring (Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels, Marx & Engels: Collected Works,
Volume 50: Letters 1892–1895 (London: Lawrence & Wishart, 2010), 164).
84  Ricoeur, LIU, 12/IU, 31.
85  Ricoeur, “Science and Ideology”, FTA, 255/“Science et idéologie”, DTA, 347.
86  Ricoeur, LIU, 12/IU, 31.
87  For a more detailed analysis of the ways in which Ricoeur draws from Weber, see Ernst
Wolff, “The Place of Max Weber in Ricoeur’s Philosophy: Power, Ideology, Explanation”,
Journal of French and Francophone Philosophy—Revue de la philosophie française et de
langue française, Vol. 28, No. 2 (2020): 70–93.
88  Ricoeur, LIU, 154/IU, 209.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  249
“credibility gap” that always exists “between a system of authority’s claim
to legitimacy and our response in terms of belief”.89
The second line of thought in the Lectures concerns “Mannheim’s para­
dox”. Ricoeur summarizes the paradox in these terms:
What is the epistemological status of discourse about ideology if all dis­
course is ideological? How can this discourse escape its own exposition,
its own description? If sociopolitical thought itself is entwined with the
life situation of the thinker, does not the concept of ideology have to be
absorbed into its own referent?90
Though Ricoeur respects the “intellectual integrity” with which Mannheim
confronts the problem, he argues that Mannheim’s “relationism” ulti­
mately fails to resolve the paradox. The reason for this, he explains, is that
Mannheim’s analysis is “founded upon the contrast between ideology and
science”.91 Mannheim thus attempts to carve out a “scientific” place from
which the philosopher or social scientist can overlook the whole field of
ideology—“scientific” here meaning a place from which social reality can
be accessed in a pre-symbolic way such that the observer is not subject to
any ideological motivation.
Instead of opposing ideology to science, Ricoeur argues that the only
way out of Mannheim’s paradox is to ground the basis of socio-political
thought elsewhere.92 Ricoeur’s own approach is thus much closer to the
early Marx, provided that we no longer oppose ideology to mute praxis,
but understand it in terms of the transition from one form of symbolism
to another. For Ricoeur, there can be no question of stepping outside of
ideology and relating directly to the conditions of social existence in a pre-
symbolic way.93 To resolve the paradox we can only work from within the
“circle of ideology”, that is, from within the social imagination itself.
This brings us to the other side of the social imagination, utopia—the
concept Ricoeur relies on in his first attempt to solve Mannheim’s para­
dox. As with ideology, Ricoeur’s genetic phenomenology reveals that uto­
pia also admits of both positive and negative functions.94 The pathological
function of utopia is already known to anyone familiar with the everyday
89  Ricoeur, LIU, 183/IU, 244.
90  Ricoeur, LIU, 9/IU, 27.
91  Ricoeur, LIU, 9/IU, 27.
92  Ricoeur, LIU, 9/IU, 27.
93  Ricoeur, LIU, 145/IU, 199.
94  For a more extensive treatment of Ricoeur’s conception of utopia than can be offered
here, see Vicky Iakovou, “To Think Utopia with and beyond Paul Ricoeur”, From Ricoeur
to Action: The Socio- Political Significance of Ricoeur’s Thinking, ed. Todd S. Mei and
David Lewin (New York, NY: Continuum, 2012), 113–35.


250  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
meaning of the term. Especially in political contexts, “utopia” often means
something like a naïve fiction that allows us to imaginatively escape from
the constraints of our actual circumstances.
More positively, however, Ricoeur reminds us that utopia is also that
remarkable power of the imagination to put oneself beyond what is actual,
to explore new possibilities that may not seem possible at first glance. For
Ricoeur, this imaginative power is essential for any critical endeavour as
it can help the critic discern differences between the conceivable and the
actual. Borrowing a metaphor from Mannheim, Ricoeur speaks about the
way that utopia can “shatter” the obviousness of a given social order.95
Understood in this way utopia becomes an essential resource for breaking
free of the closed circle of ideology.
It is with this understanding of the utopian imagination that we reach
Ricoeur’s first solution to Mannheim’s paradox. “There is no answer to
Mannheim’s paradox”, Ricoeur explains, “except to say that we must try
to cure the illness of utopia by what is wholesome in ideology … and try to
cure the rigidity, the petrification, of ideologies by the utopian element.96
Ricoeur’s suggestion here is that the positive functions of ideology and
utopia can correct the pathological function of the other. When ideology
becomes pathological, utopia can draw our attention to the difference
between what is conceivable and what is actual, modifying the limits of
our closed ideological horizon. When utopias become pathological, on the
other hand, that is, when the conceivable forgets the actual, the gravita­
tional pull of ideology can be used to bring people together around matters
of common concern.
Despite the attractive symmetry of Ricoeur’s solution, we find it more
promissory than definitive. Although that would be typical of Ricoeur, we
mean this here in a stronger sense. Ricoeur’s solution strikes us as espe­
cially tentative for textual reasons: rather than end on this note, Ricoeur
continues, concluding the Lectures with a series of remarks that point
in the direction of future work. In the final paragraph he mentions “a
judgment of appropriateness”, a “concrete judgment of taste”, and the
“capacity to appreciate what is fitting in a given situation”—each of which
revolves around the notion of “practical wisdom” or phronesis. By tracing
these threads into Ricoeur’s later work, the profile of a more comprehen­
sive solution to the problem of ideology begins to emerge.
95  For a discussion of this metaphor in the context of Ricoeur’s The Rule of Metaphor and
his Lectures on Imagination, see George H. Taylor, “Delineating Ricoeur’s Concept of
Utopia”Social Imaginaries, Vol. 3, No. 1 (2017), 41–60.
96  Ricoeur, LIU, 312/IU, 409.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  251
Practical Reason
By invoking “practical wisdom” at the end of the Lectures Ricoeur signals
his broadly Aristotelian approach to practical philosophy. Indeed, after
clarifying in “Science and Ideology” (1974) that he sees the problem of
ideology as being of a “practical nature”, Ricoeur draws attention to the
different levels of exactness that can be expected in practical matters like
politics as opposed to theoretical matters like the exact sciences.97
For Aristotle, this difference is the basis of the distinction between theo­
retical and practical reason. Where theoretical reason is concerned with
the pursuit of certain knowledge based upon necessary and self-evident
first principles, practical reason, by contrast, is concerned with the sphere
of human action in all its novelty, contingency, and uncertainty.98 This
is why the methods appropriate to practical matters are deliberation and
argumentation—for “no one deliberates about things that cannot be oth­
erwise”—whereas theoretical matters can be worked out by logical dem­
onstration alone.99
Without adopting Aristotle’s metaphysical views, Ricoeur retains
Aristotle’s emphasis on keeping practical reason connected to notions like
desire, interest, and preference (prohairesis).100 Recall the above quote
from Obama, where truth and science were opposed to “political agendas
and ideologies”. The assumption there is that some political assertions are
motivated by desires and interests while others are neutrally guided by the
right information, one’s own personal motivations being irrelevant. For
Ricoeur, however, such a distinction is impossible to maintain given the
essential role of interests and desires in practical reason.101 This is because
97  Ricoeur, “Science and Ideology”, FTA, 247, 255/DTA, 336, 347–348. Ricoeur often
quotes Book I of the Nichomachean Ethics, where Aristotle explains that we should only
“look for the degree of exactness in each kind of investigation that the nature of the sub­
ject matter allows” (Aristotle, Nichomachean Ethics, trans. C.D.C Reeve (Indianapolis,
IN: Hackett, 2014), 1094b23-24, 3). For example, Ricoeur, LIU, 178-179/IU, 238; FTA,
246/DTA, 335.
98  For Ricoeur’s discussion of Aristotle’s conception of the﻿́ôria and the break between
the practical and the theoretical, see Ricoeur, Being, Essence, and Substance in Plato
and Aristotle, trans. David Pellauer and John Starkey (Cambridge: Polity, 2013), 241,
162–63/Être, essence, et substance chez Platon et Aristote. Cours professé à l’université
de Strasbourg en 1953–1954, ed. Jean-Louis Schlegel (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 2011),
166–167.
99  See Aristotle, The Complete Works of Aristotle, 1139a11–14; On Rhetoric, 2nd ed., trans.
George A. Kennedy (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2007), 1357a1–6, 41.
100  For a discussion of the development of Ricoeur’s conception of practical reason from
the essay “Practical Reason” to Oneself as Another, see Laurent Jaffro, “La conception
Ricoeurienne de la raison pratique”, Études Ricoeuriennes/ Ricoeur Studies, Vol. 3, No.
1 (2012): 156–171.
101  Ricoeur, “Practical Reason”, 191, FTA/“La raison pratique”, DTA, 267.


252  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
the kind of truth that practical reason is concerned with involves an agree­
ment with “correct” or “right” desire.102 Ricoeur’s conception of practical
reason thus remains Aristotelian in the sense that it unites thought and
action towards “practical truth”.103
When it comes to practical matters, then, our desires, interests, and pref­
erences—in short, all that is symbolically mediated—must not be excluded
from the analysis if the problem of ideology is to avoid aporia. Without
recourse to knowledge of a pre-symbolic reality beyond all dispute, prac­
tical questions can only be judged by offering reasons for acting. When
mobilized as a reason for acting (vis-à-vis an actual or possible audience),
even the most precise knowledge becomes subject to what Ricoeur above
called the “rough and schematic character of argumentation”. Indeed, to
act under the impression that what ought to be done can be determined by
purely theoretical or scientific means would be to conflate the two kinds of
reason in a particularly dangerous way, even calling their conflation “the
most dangerous of all ideas”.104
In spite of this strong warning, however, Ricoeur insists we should find
something “liberating” in the idea that practice cannot be wholly guided
by theoretical knowledge, reminding us that there nonetheless remains a
certain rationality to practice.105 And we should find this idea liberating, he
continues, because it allows us to deal rationally with the domain of action
in a way that is both ontologically and epistemologically appropriate: for
the domain of action is, ontologically, that of “changing things” and, epis­
temologically, that of the likely or the probable (eikos).106 Once it sheds
its “theoretical claim to knowledge”, then, practical reason can assume its
properly “critical function” in the political sphere: “to unmask the hidden
mechanism of distortion through which the legitimate objectification of
the communal bond becomes an intolerable alienation”.107
This sketch of practical reason brings us to where we left off at the end
of the Lectures. Reading beyond Ricoeur’s initial response to Mannheim’s
paradox, he continues: “My more ultimate answer [to Mannheim’s
