103  Paul Ricoeur, The Course of Recognition, trans. David Pellauer (Cambridge, MA: Har­
vard University Press, 2005), 87/Parcourse de la reconnaissance. Trois études (Paris: Gal­
limard, 2004), 146. For a more recent interpretation of practical truth in Aristotle that
dovetails with that of Ricoeur, see C. M. M. Olfert, Aristotle on Practical Truth (Oxford:
Oxford University Press, 2017). Against the “objects view” of the difference between
theoretical and practical reason, Olfert argues that the distinguishing feature of practical
reason is the fact that it aims at practical truth.
104  Ricoeur, “Practical Reason”, FTA, 199/DTA, 277.
105  Ricoeur, “Practical Reason”, FTA, 199/DTA, 278.
106  Ricoeur, “Practical Reason”, FTA, 199/DTA, 278.
107  Ricoeur, “Practical Reason”, FTA, 206/DTA, 286.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  253
paradox] is that we must let ourselves be drawn into the circle and then
must try to make the circle a spiral”.108 We read this emphasis on the per­
sonal as an important clue for why Ricoeur takes the problem of ideology
to be a matter of practical reason. As a phenomenon pertaining to the
symbolic mediation of human action, ideology is ultimately a problem for
selves in determinate social relations. It is selves who act, and it is therefore
selves who will either remain trapped in the circle of ideology or succeed
in making it a spiral.
Yet, what is it about Ricoeur’s conception of the self that can help bring
practical resolution to the problem of ideology? As we shall see, it is the
self’s capacity for judgment. While there are hints throughout the Lectures
that suggest a turn towards judgment, the most telling clue occurs in the
closing sentences:
[Mannheim] talks of a criterion of appropriateness. This criterion is
rather difficult to apply but it may be our only alternative … The judg­
ment of appropriateness is the way to solve [Mannheim’s] noncongru­
ence problem. It is a concrete judgment of taste, an ability to appreciate
what is fitting in a given situation. Instead of a pseudo-Hegelian claim
to have a total view, the question is one of practical wisdom, we have
the security of judgment because we appreciate what can be done in a
situation. We cannot get out of the circle of ideology and utopia, but the
judgment of appropriateness may help us to understand how the circle
can become a spiral.109
As we will see, these terms—“practical wisdom” or phronesis and the
related ideas of a “judgment of appropriateness”, a “concrete judgment of
taste”, or “what is fitting in a given situation”—will reappear in Ricoeur’s
later works, shedding new light on the problem of ideology with which we
began.
Although judgment is a recurring theme throughout Ricoeur’s writings,
it is not until Oneself as Another (1990), The Just (1995), and Reflections
on The Just (2001) that a more complete picture of the concept begins to
emerge.110 In these writings, Ricoeur understands judgment not simply as
an individual act of cognition but as a capacity of selfhood, made possible
by its intersubjective and institutional constitution.111
108  Ricoeur, LIU, 312, our emphasis/IU, 409.
109  Ricoeur, LIU, 314, our emphasis/IU, 411.
110  Ricoeur’s earliest in-depth study of judgment is a 1958–59 course. See Ricoeur, Le Juge­
ment. Cours de M. Ricoeur (Paris: Groupe de Philosophie, 1959).
111  Commentators such as Gregory D. Hoskins have gone so far as to argue that it is this
“capacity to judge … that subtends the various capacities that constitute the subject of


254  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
In Oneself as Another, the concept of phronesis is understood as the
third and final moment of practical decision making.112 In the three studies
that make up Ricoeur’s “little ethics” (studies 7, 8, and 9), Ricoeur defends
three essentially related theses: distinguishing between ethics and moral­
ity, he argues that (1) there is a primacy of ethical teleology over moral
deontology; yet, (2) despite its primacy, it is necessary for the ethical aim
to “pass through the sieve” of moral norms; and that (3) when the norms
of morality, by virtue of their universality, lead to a practical impasse,
recourse must be had back to ethics in the form of a particular kind of judg­
ment which Ricoeur wants to qualify “with the adjective phronetic”.113 It
is thus within this idea of “phronetic judgment” that Ricoeur consolidates
his earlier “utopian” solution. In phronetic judgment, utopia corresponds
to the self’s capacity to attest to a teleological vision, to an ethical aim.
Ethics is not without checks and balances, however. In Oneself as
Another Ricoeur identifies three features of phronesis that can assist us
in managing human finitude when forming phronetic judgments. These
features are: (1) “respect for adverse positions”, (2) the search for the “just
mean”, and (3) the counsel of the wise, or the phronimos.114 These three
features can be clarified by means of an analogy with law. In a legal con­
text, each case brought to trial revolves around a conflict that has occurred
between the parties involved. One of the principal tasks of a judge is to
hear out the opposing sides so that all the relevant points of view can be
taken into consideration and put into productive conflict. In forming her
judgment, the judge aims to determine the “just mean” between the parties
with respect to legal precedent in essentially similar cases. In this example,
the phronimos can be likened to relevant legal precedent, the testimony of
expert witnesses, and other features of a trial which, in principle, improve
the chances of coming to a just decision.
Ricoeur’s philosophical anthropology” (Gregory D. Hoskins, “The Capacity to Judge
and the Contours of a Theory of Political Judgment”, in Paul Ricoeur and the Task of
Political Philosophy, ed. Greg S. Johnson and Dan R. Stiver (Lanham, MD: Lexing­
ton, 2013), 98.) In a similar vein, Philippe Lacour argues that the logic of judgment in
Ricoeur’s work is “the key” to his thinking on practical reasoning as a whole (Philippe
Lacour, “Le jugement et sa logique dans la philosophie de Ricoeur (Deuxième partie)”,
Études Ricoeuriennes/ Ricoeur Studies, Vol. 8, No. 1 (2017), 140).
112  Summarizing his little ethics, Ricoeur states in Critique and Conviction that the problem
which phronesis sought to answer was that of making “new decisions in the face of
difficult cases: the hard cases of law, medicine or everyday life” (Ricoeur, Critique and
Conviction, 92/La critique et la conviction, 141).
113  Ricoeur, Oneself as Another, trans. Kathleen Blamey (Chicago, IL: The University of
Chicago Press, 1992), 170/Soi-même comme un autre (Paris: Éditions du Seuil, 1990),
200–201; Ricoeur, The Just, trans. David Pellauer (Chicago, IL: The University of Chi­
cago Press, 2000), xxii/Le Juste (Paris: Éditions Esprit, 1995), 25.
114  Ricoeur, OA, 273/SA, 317–318.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  255
Yet, this is but an analogy. There are important differences between a
court of law and other social contexts. As Ricoeur points out: “The whole
question is … whether a moral system, which does not have the support
of the judicial institution, is capable of establishing its own coherence”.115
What marks the essential difference here, Ricoeur continues, is that on
the moral plane we are most often dealing with unexpressed “specifica­
tory premises” that mark the intermingling of institutionalized relations of
domination and violence.116 This difference is crucial as phronetic judgment
must avoid succumbing to the pathological effects of ideology and utopia,
each of which have the potential to obscure various forms of domination.
Returning to the remainder of the clue identified at the end of the
Lectures—the “judgment of appropriateness”, the “concrete judgment of
taste”, or that which is “fitting in a given situation”—it now seems that
these ideas are serving as a placeholder for what in The Just Ricoeur will
call “the equitable”—phronesis actualized in judgment.117 Again we can see
Ricoeur make use of Aristotle, specifically Book V of the Nichomachean
Ethics, where he distinguishes between equity and (legal) justice. The prob­
lem for Aristotle is this: since all law is universal and conduct is particular,
a difficulty arises in attempting to subsume conduct under a law without
in some sense violating the law’s universality.118 Importantly, this is not a
theoretical problem, but a practical one; it is constitutive of interpreting
and applying the law. For Aristotle, the equitable is thus “a correction of
the law where it is defective owing to its universality”.119
Phronetic judgement is thus faced with a similar difficulty (a difficulty
analogous to that of semantic innovation in the metaphorical process): it
has to rewrite the rules, as it were, in a way that is more faithful to those
rules than another. In cases of civil disobedience, for example, individuals
deliberately violate the law in order to live up to some higher sense of jus­
tice than the legal. This inescapably involves risking oneself on something
new, an innovation that can never be fully accounted for in terms of what
is traditional, precedential, or conventional. In other words, phronetic
judgment attempts to institute what is equitable in situations where injus­
tice will have to be done to one set of values for the sake of another. The
two vices to be avoided in this process are, on the one hand, the rigid uni­
versality of deontological norms, which can often function ideologically
(in the negative sense) by masking and reinforcing domination and, on the
115  Ricoeur, OA, 278/SA, 323.
116  Ricoeur, OA, 279–280/SA, 325.
117  Ricoeur, TJ, xxiv/J1, 27.
118  Aristotle, Nichomachean Ethics, 1137b10–14.
119  Aristotle, Nichomachean Ethics, 1137b26–27.


256  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
other, the capricious particularity of ethical aims or unchallenged utopias,
which have yet to pass the test of deontology.
Spiralling the Circle
We are now ready to see how Ricoeur’s notion of “phronetic judgment”
can help to “spiral” the practical circle of ideology and utopia.
In the Preface to The Just, Ricoeur revisits his discussion of phronesis
from Oneself as Another. This time, however, he introduces the three dis­
ciplines named in “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics” into the equation:
The whole problem, which I will risk qualifying with the adjective
phronetic, lies in exploring the middle zone where the judgment is formed,
halfway between proof, defined by the constraints of logic, and sophism,
motivated by the desire to seduce or the temptation to intimidate. This
middle zone can be designated by many names, depending on the strat­
egy used: rhetoric, to the extent that rhetoric, following Aristotle’s defini­
tion, consists in giving a “rejoinder” to dialectic, itself understood as a
doctrine of probable reasoning; hermeneutic to the extent that this joins
application to understanding or explanation; poetic, to the extent that
the invention of an appropriate solution to the unique situation stems
from what, since Kant, we have called the productive imagination, in
order to distinguish it from the merely reproductive imagination.
Today I would say that the reflective judgment of Kant’s third
Critique brings together the three aspects distinguished by these three
disciplines: probability, subsumption (or application), innovation.120
In attempting to navigate between an indifferent (ideological) universalism
and a capricious (utopian) particularism, Ricoeur explains that phronetic
judgments draw on the key aspect of each discipline: the probable argu­
mentation of rhetoric, subsumption or application characteristic of her­
meneutics, and the innovation of poetics. Each discipline thus contributes
something to the effort of phronetic judgment.
However elegant, we would advise approaching this synthesis with cau­
tion. As we saw in the previous chapter, Ricoeur’s way of distinguishing
these three disciplines is, at best, deeply ambiguous and, at least where
rhetoric is concerned, counterproductive. For instance, why does Ricoeur
ascribe such a limited role to hermeneutics given its much broader meaning
in his own work? And why is poetics the only discipline that can lay claim
to innovation? Do interpretation and argumentation not also innovate?
120  Ricoeur, TJ, xxii/J1, 25.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  257
We would thus propose a different approach. Rather than view the
three aspects of phronetic judgment in terms of the disciplinary traits of
hermeneutics, poetics, and rhetoric, as Ricoeur suggests, our suggestion
would be to interpret them along the lines of threefold mimesis, where a
similar logic can be observed.
(1) Mimesis I (pre-figuration)—Phronetic judgment must always start
from within ideology. This is why Ricoeur insists on the importance of
critique in his hermeneutics, emphasizing the essential, albeit insufficient,
role of theoretical knowledge and scientific explanation. Ideology critique
is thus not “merely interpretative” but involves “the necessity of a detour
through the explanation of causes”.121 Indeed, “rather than constituting
mutually exclusive poles”, explanation and understanding are for Ricoeur
“relative moments” in the process of interpretation.122 By pursuing the
dialectic of explanation and understanding it is possible to break open
the closed circle of ideology. Critique can thus introduce some distance
from our original position of ideological belonging, neutralizing ideology’s
motivational pull by thematizing it and submitting it to reflection.123
(2) Mimesis II (configuration)—Critical distance is only one moment;
a broken circle does not yet make a spiral. Phronetic judgment must then
attempt to innovate a more equitable outcome. As a practical problem, it
falls on individuals, whether acting alone or in concert, to innovate equi­
table solutions to conflicts arising from socio-political life, especially when
institutions fail. As Ricoeur puts it in the final paragraphs of the Lectures:
It is too simple a response … to say that we must keep the dialectic [of
ideology and utopia] running. My more ultimate answer [to Mannheim’s
paradox] is that we must let ourselves be drawn into the circle and then
must try to make the circle a spiral. We cannot eliminate from a social
121  Ricoeur, “Hermeneutics and the Critique of Ideology”, FTA, 292/DTA, 397.
122  Ricoeur, “Explanation and Understanding”, FTA, 126/“Expliquer et comprendre”,
DTA, 180.
123  Viewing phronetic judgment as Ricoeur’s later solution to the problem of ideology helps
to address Johann Michel’s criticism that Ricoeur’s earlier “utopian” solution does not
do justice to the role of the social sciences in helping to unveil and demystify ideo­
logical distortion (Michel, Homo Interpretans, 100). This also helps to address a related
point raised by Rahel Jaeggi, who criticizes Ricoeur’s “hermeneutics of suspicion” as a
method of ideology critique for “still [being] hermeneutics”. The problem, she argues,
is that hermeneutics involves having to “reconstruct the perspective of those concerned
… not in an external and objective way but as the agents experience them” (Rahel Jae­
ggi, “Rethinking Ideology”, New Waves in Political Philosophy, ed. Paul Boudewijn de
Bruin and Christopher F. Zurn (New York, NY: Palgrave Macmillan, 2009), 80). This is
only half right: it is correct to say that hermeneutics is, broadly speaking, interested in
understanding the way agents experience things; but it overlooks the important role of
scientific explanation in Ricoeur’s critical hermeneutics.


258  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
ethics the element of risk. We wager on a certain set of values and then
try to be consistent with them; verification is therefore a question of our
whole life. No one can escape this. Anyone who claims to proceed in a
value-free way will find nothing.124
The subject(s) of phronetic judgment will ultimately have to risk them­
selves on a more or less determinate vision of the good life. This is the uto­
pian moment of phronetic judgment, which carries inherent risk because
the referent of the innovation is only a desired future. Yet, it is important
to recall that innovation for Ricoeur does not mean creation ex nihilo.
Rather, it “is a way of responding in a creative fashion to a question pre­
sented by things”.125 By responding to competing proposals and the wider
phronimos of the community, the judgment aims at a “conversion of the
imaginary”, attempting to stir up “the sedimented universe of conven­
tional ideas”.126 It is here that a new meaning is produced, and the circle
first broken by critique is set into motion.
(3) Mimesis III (refiguration)—If the innovation of phronetic judgment
is to become actual, it must be refigured back into the sphere of action by
an audience. In other words, the innovation must be found persuasive by
those who will, directly or indirectly, suffer the resulting course of action.
This raises the question of the sort of “truth” phronetic judgment can
aspire to. In Reflections on The Just, Ricoeur dedicates a section of his
essay “Justice and Truth” to this question, using the same language of “fit”
that we encountered at the end of the Lectures:
What kind of truth is at issue here? It is a truth that fits … Can we speak
then of objectivity? No, not in the constative sense of this term. It is a
question rather of the certitude that in this situation this is the best deci­
sion, what has to be done. It is not a matter of constraint; the force of
this conviction has nothing to do with a factual determination. It is the
sense hic et nunc of what obviously fits, of what ought to be done.127
Since phronetic judgment aims at the realization of what is equitable in
singular situations, its truth cannot be merely “constative”—it does not
aim to describe things as they are; it aims to set them right. In other words,
because phronesis is not constrained by factual determinations alone, it
must be recognized as phronetic by those involved. The audience (as well
124  Ricoeur, LIU, 312/IU, 409.
125  Ricoeur, RM, 146/MV, 161.
126  Ricoeur, FMR, 143/DMR, 150/L2, 489.
127  Ricoeur, Reflections on the Just, trans. David Pellauer (Chicago, IL: The University of
Chicago Press, 2007), 70/Le Juste 2 (Paris: Éditions Esprit, 2001), 82.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  259
as the phronimos more broadly) thus plays an essential constraining role
in deciding what is “truly” equitable.
Here, Ricoeur takes inspiration from Kant’s notion of reflective judg­
ment—more precisely, Hannah Arendt’s attempt to extract a theory of
political judgment from Kant’s work.128 As with judgments of taste, the
kind of universality sought by phronetic judgment is “communicabil­
ity”. Paradoxically, communicability attempts to institute a universality
that is not pre-given. The truth of phronetic judgment is thus bound to
its ability to be persuasively communicated to others.129 The argumenta­
tion this requires, however, as a practical matter, is governed by a “logic
of the probable”, where conclusions are never final. Even when success­
ful, the outcome of phronetic judgment may always be challenged and
those responsible may be asked to defend their decision before others. And
in cases where persuasion fails altogether, it will always fall to someone
to start again, to look for new ways of arguing that will (re)activate the
social imagination and disrupt ideological distortion. As Aristotle recog­
nized long ago: it is not enough to merely know the true and the just; these
values must also be actualized in discourse, lest they be defeated by their
opposites.130
By taking a longer view on Ricoeur’s thinking about ideology, we can
see how the concept of phronetic judgment in his later writings consoli­
dates certain aspects of his earlier reflections on ideology, utopia, and the
social imagination. Above all, Ricoeur exposes the idleness of trying to
detach politics from our desires and preferences. This serves as a much-
needed corrective to the idea that politics can be conducted on the basis of
facts and data alone. The problem with this complacent line of thinking is
that it surrenders a central site of political struggle—the social imaginary.
To overcome ideological distortion new visions for the future are needed.
The task of ideological criticism, then, is not to escape the social imagina­
tion, but to find a foothold within it and risk oneself on something new.
Phronetic judgment is Ricoeur’s model for doing so. While inescapably
128  Ricoeur, “Aesthetic Judgment and Political Judgment According to Hannah Arendt”, TJ,
97–98/“Jugement esthétique et jugement politique selon Hannah Arendt”, J1, 147–148.
Notably, Ricoeur makes a similar criticism of Arendt as Badiou regarding her emphasis
on the “retrospective” dimension of judgment, see TJ, 107/J1, 159.
129  For an account of Ricoeur’s theory of truth that moves in the same direction as our
argument, see Todd S. Mei, “Constructing Ricoeur’s Hermeneutical Theory of Truth”,
in Hermeneutics and Phenomenology in Paul Ricoeur: Between Text and Phenomenon,
ed. Scott Davidson and Marc-Antoine Vallée (Cham: Springer, 2016), 197–215. Mei
discusses in more detail the role of communicability and agreement in Ricoeur’s herme­
neutical theory of truth, which he describes as a “holistic fallibilism”.
130  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355a21–24, 35.


260  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
fallible, this process finds support in the very selves who initiate it and
accept responsibility for whatever consequences it may bring.
Phronetic judgment also highlights the limits of Ricoeur’s narrow disci­
plinary conception of rhetoric (to say nothing of hermeneutics and poetics).
Other than the above passage in The Just, Ricoeur does not explain any
further how or in what way these “three aspects” are related—other than
directing the reader to “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics”.131 Fortunately,
however, we are not obliged to follow Ricoeur here, as our own discussion
of “rhetoricity” allows us to think of the object of rhetoric in a different
way. As we shall now see, this allows us to further develop our response
to Badiou.
At the end of our trial between Badiou and Perelman in Chapter 3, we
ultimately sided with Perelman—though not without having to concede
that there are limits to (or at least open questions about) the viability of his
radical pluralism. To recall, we showed that Badiou would find Perelman’s
rhetorical vision of philosophy too sophistical; that his philosophical plu­
ralism is sutured to philosophy’s political condition. According to Badiou,
such a “parliamentary” view leaves the philosopher without any clear rela­
tion to truth, and thus hostage to whatever happens to be the opinion of
the majority. From our reading of the Treatise in Chapter 2, however,
we saw no reason to conflate Perelman’s own philosophical position with
the concepts he and Olbrechts-Tyteca developed for their theory of argu­
mentation. Specifically, we argued that their most important concept, the
audience, can be seen at work in Badiou’s own philosophy, albeit clothed
in different terminology.
As with Cassin, this preliminary argument against Badiou was largely
negative. With Ricoeur—though somewhat against his will—we are now
able say why even Badiou should recognize the importance of rhetoricity:
there is an audience both upstream and downstream from what Badiou
understands as the philosophical act.
On the one hand, philosophy is always responsive to the pre-figured
world from which it emerges—even if only to break with it, as Badiou
rightly insists that it should. Ricoeur’s conception of phronetic judgment
forefronts this responsive movement not only on social and political mat­
ters but also within philosophical debates. If a philosopher wishes to effect
any sort of break, she must first be attuned to the social reality she hopes
131  As we pointed out in Chapter 5, “Rhetoric-Poetics-Hermeneutics” was first conceived in
1970. However, it would not appear in print until 1986 in De la Métaphysique à la Rhé­
torique and again in 1990 in Lectures 2. The published (and presumably revised) version
is thus more contemporaneous with the essays that make up The Just than the original,
and there is no evidence in the latter that Ricoeur had changed his mind about the mean­
ing of these terms. In fact, Ricoeur makes no other mention of rhetoric in The Just at all.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  261
to improve—including society’s representations of that reality, the social
imagination. Rhetoric is thus not only concerned with “probable argumen­
tation” as Ricoeur seems to believe; it is also the capacity to attune oneself
to others, to the views that have come before me; to what is traditional,
customary, or precedential in a given context. (We shall explore the sense
in which rhetoric should be understood as a capacity below.)
Ricoeur’s own sensitivity to this rhetorical consideration is especially
pronounced in one of his interventions in États Généraux de la Philosophie
(1979):
[W]e must always bear in mind what the indices, the signs of [the] cur­
rent need for philosophy are, wherever this need may be, namely, first
of all in the current state of the mathematical and physical sciences, in
the juridical sciences, in culture, in its literary and other expressions,
and also in the street … Let us always take as our starting point what is
required [démandé] by others. If we claim that there is a need [demande]
for philosophy, we must not only perceive the signs of it, but hear what
is being said before we have a critical relationship with what we have
heard.132
This remark encapsulates much of what we have sought to emphasize
about Ricoeur’s sensitivity for the rhetorical dimension of discourse. Not
unlike the way Badiou defines philosophy in relation to its conditions,
Ricoeur insists that philosophy is first and foremost responsive to what is,
strictly speaking, non-philosophical: the sciences, both natural and human,
the world of culture, and politics (“the street”). These areas of human life
make certain demands on the philosopher; they introduce certain exigen­
cies to which she must in some way respond. Yet, before she can respond
to these demands, before she can conduct her critical work, she must first
“perceive the signs” of a need for philosophy, she must first “hear what is
being said”.
On the other hand, the movement of philosophy never terminates in the
mind of the thinker (a moment of philosophy missing from Rodin’s famous
sculpture). The philosophical act shares the same logic as phronetic judg­
ment: breaking with prevailing opinion and taking a new position on a
given question, the philosopher too invites their audience to join them in
the shared work of refiguration. Even the most contemplative philosophers
address themselves to one audience or another, in one way or another. For
example, the relatively recent distinction between “academic” and “pub­
lic” philosophy is an entirely audience-based distinction.
132  États généraux de la philosophie (16 et 17 juin 1979), (Paris: Flammarion, 1979), 55–56,
our translation, emphasis added.


262  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
But even if critical of such distinctions, Badiou’s own practice attests to
a concern with philosophy’s rhetoricity. Consider his Socratic injunction
to “corrupt the youth” or his extremely diverse forms of output (e.g. trea­
tises, essays, interviews, theatre pieces, short stories for children, documen­
tary films, etc.) The point is not that rhetoric is only involved in some of
these genres; it is involved in each case: in the different ways he responds to
and interprets pre-figured social reality with an eye to some audience who
will refigure and change it. By refusing to adopt Ricoeur’s narrow disci­
plinary conception of rhetoric and opting instead to think of all discourse
as having a certain rhetoricity, we can see that rhetoric is involved both
upstream and downstream from Badiou’s own philosophical practice.
3  Rhetorical Capacity
But what exactly does it mean to say that rhetoric is involved both upstream
and downstream in this way? How do we define “rhetoric” and how does
it relate to rhetoricity? In this final section we argue that rhetoric should
be defined along Aristotelian lines as the basic human capacity to reflect
on audiences and imagine what is potentially persuasive. It is by exercis­
ing this capacity that actors bestow action with rhetoricity, its rhetori­
cal dimension. We begin by discussing Ricoeur’s conception of selfhood
and its “capacity for reception” in Oneself as Another (1990), which we
suggest is the place where rhetoric can be incorporated into—or grafted
onto—his philosophical anthropology. We then show that our rhetorical
capacity can be seen across the full spectrum of human action, from every­
day forms of social interaction to highly specialized contexts like philoso­
phy. However omnipresent, we conclude by showing why our rhetorical
capacity is of particular importance to critical theories.
Oneself as an Audience
The itinerary followed by the ten studies of Oneself as Another [Soi-même
comme un autre] (1990), Ricoeur explains, leads “from a highly abstract
to a more concrete characterization of the sort of beings we assert our­
selves to be in opposition to things and animals”.133 Though he will add
to this list later,134 the self is characterized here as having four main capa­
bilities—(1) saying, (2) doing, (3) narrating, and (4) imputing—which, as
133  Ricoeur, “Human Beings as the Subject of Philosophy”, PA, 196/“L’Homme comme
sujet de la philosophie”, EC3, 306.
134  Ricoeur, The Course of Recognition, 109–134/Parcours de la reconnaissance, 179–214.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  263
Wolff puts it, are “telescoped into each other”.135 Each capability responds
not to the question of what but who: “Who is speaking?”, “Who is nar­
rating?”, etc. When posed this way, the response can only take the form
of a self who “attests”, in the first person, to being capable of the action.
What becomes progressively clearer throughout the studies, however, is
that each capability presupposes interaction with both the intersubjective
other (“you” in the second person) and the institutional other (“one” in
the third person).136
What we want to focus on from Ricoeur’s broader argument is his dis­
cussion of one of the three forms of otherness he identifies as being con­
stitutive of selfhood—namely, the otherness of other people.137 Though
only thematized in the tenth study, Ricoeur explains that the “other than
self” has been present at every level of analysis in Oneself as Another. This
form of otherness effectively corresponds to what we have been calling, in
rhetorical terms, the audience. In his remarks on the occasion of his accept­
ance of the Kluge Prize in 1994, Ricoeur summarizes how an “audience” is
implied by each of our basic capacities:
each of them requires a vis-à-vis. Discourse is addressed to someone
capable of responding, questioning, of entering into conversation and
dialogue. Action occurs in conjunction with other agents, who can help
or hinder. Narrative assembles multiple protagonists within a single
135  Wolff, Between Daily Routine and Violent Protest, 83.
136  This is the meaning of Ricoeur’s clunkier but altogether more accurate description of
the project as a hermeneutics of the “acting and suffering human” [l’homme agissant
et souffrant]—a description which emphasizes the tension between the active and the
passive that we have discussed as far back as The Voluntary and the Involuntary.
However far back this theme can be traced, this way of formulating things is heav­
ily indebted to Arendt, cf. Hannah Arendt, The Human Condition, 2nd ed. (Chicago,
IL: The University of Chicago Press, [1958] 1998), 190. For a helpful overview of
Ricoeur’s personal and scholarly relation to Arendt, see Morny Joy, “Paul Ricoeur,
Hannah Arendt, and the Journey to Justice”, The Ambiguity of Justice: New Perspec­
tives on Paul Ricoeur’s Approach to Justice, ed. Geoffrey Dierckxsens (Leiden: Brill,
2020), 227–254.
137  The metacategory of “otherness” in Oneself as Another is no less polysemic than
“being”, “acting” or “identity”. Though it is the form of otherness that we will focus on,
Ricoeur insists that “the Other” is not reducible “to the otherness of another Person”
(OA, 317/SA, 368). Also discussed as forms of otherness are what Ricoeur calls, follow­
ing Merleau-Ponty, “the flesh”, the corporal mediation of self and world, and, following
Heidegger, “conscience”, the most hidden form of otherness experienced in the relation
of the self to itself. Strictly speaking, Ricoeur argues that these three forms of otherness
should be called “experiences of passivity”, whereas the term “otherness” ought to be
reserved for speculative discourse alone. As he puts it, these experiences of passivity
become “the attestation of otherness”. In this way, Ricoeur avoids reducing otherness
to sameness and thereby restoring the self to a place of foundation (OA, 318/SA, 368).


264  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
plot. A life story includes a multitude of other life stories. As for imput­
ability, frequently raised by accusation, it makes me responsible before
others … Finally, promising calls for a witness who receives it and
records it.138
In other words, the audience is present at every level of the constitution of
the self, all the way down to its very ontology.
This point is made most emphatically in Ricoeur’s tenth and final study
on the ontology presupposed by each of his preceding analyses. Here he
positions himself against both Husserl and Levinas, each of whom attempts
to construct the dialectic between self and other in a “unilateral” manner.
On the one hand, there is Husserl’s attempt in Cartesian Meditations “to
derive the alter ego from the ego” and, on the other, there is Levinas, who
“reserves for the Other the exclusive initiative for assigning responsibility
to the self”.139 Husserl and Levinas thus propose inverse solutions to the
problem of the constitution of the other: Husserl proceeds outwards from
the ego, while Levinas proceeds from the other towards the ego.
Of the two, however, it is Levinas who poses the greater challenge to
Ricoeur’s argument. Levinas not only challenges the notion of identity but
does so in a way that would undercut Ricoeur’s main distinction between
ipse- and idem-identity. According to Levinas, the otherness of the other
cannot be expressed in the language of relation without tacitly assimilat­
ing it to oneself and thus denying its genuine otherness. His solution is to
posit a radical dissymmetry between other and same whereby all initiative
is placed on the side of the other, with the ego emerging only in response
to the other’s accusation. In other words, as Levinas himself puts it, “[a]
subject is a hostage”.140
Ricoeur’s response to Levinas is that this argument makes the same
mistake as Husserl: it only poses the dialectic of self and other one way. To
overcome this, “the preeminently ethical movement of the other toward
the self” must be made to intersect with Husserl’s strategy, what Ricoeur
calls the “gnoseological” movement from self to other.141 Only by follow­
ing such a two-pronged approach, he continues, can we preserve what
Levinas’s position renders “unthinkable”: “the distinction between self
138  Ricoeur, “Personal Capacities and Mutual Recognition”, PA, 292/“Capacités person­
nelles et reconnaissance mutuelle”, EC3, 448.
139  Ricoeur, OA, 331/SA, 382.
140  Emmanuel Levinas, Otherwise than Being: Or Beyond Essence, trans. Alphonso Lingis
(Pittsburgh, PA: Duquesne University Press, [1974] 1998), 112. Cited by Ricoeur in OA,
338/SA, 390.
141  Ricoeur, OA, 339/SA, 391.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  265
and I, and the formation of a concept of selfhood defined by its openness
and its capacity for discovery”.142
What is missing in Levinas, then, is an account of how the other’s call
can actually awaken a sense of responsibility in me in the first place. Indeed,
Ricoeur asks, how could I be solicited without “presupposing a capacity
of reception” on the part of the self? If the self was as closed as Levinas
claims, how could it even be addressed? How could it hear the voice of
the other at all? Ricoeur’s answer, and the point we want to emphasize,
is what he calls our “capacity of reception” [capacité d’accueil], without
which the self would be incapable of being addressed at all. Pace Levinas,
Ricoeur argues that: “One has to grant a capacity of reception to the self
that is the result of a reflexive structure, better defined by its power of
reconsidering preexisting objectifications than by an initial separation”.143
For Ricoeur, then, selfhood cannot simply come from without. To this
extent, Levinas undermines his own position by insisting on an absolute
separation between self and other. If one is to distinguish between different
figures of the other, he or she must in some sense be capable of recognizing
them as such and such an other (e.g. teacher vs. executioner). Responsible
for this power of “discernment and recognition” is what Ricoeur calls the
“receptive capacity” of the self.
For Ricoeur there is thus no contradiction in maintaining both move­
ments—from same to other and from other to same—as dialectically com­
plementary. The positions of Husserl and Levinas do not annihilate one
another but point to an intersection observable in the analysis of some­
thing as simple as a promise. What the other expects of me is met by my
capacity to meet their expectation. “If another were not counting on me”,
as he puts it, “would I be capable of keeping my word, of maintaining
myself?”144
To summarize, then, what emerges from Ricoeur’s emphasis on the
receptive capacity of human beings is the idea that the self is both a listener
and a speaker, a sufferer and an actor, narrated and a narrator, and so on.
As Ricoeur’s philosophical anthropology brings out, the basic receptivity
of selfhood means that being “in audience” to others is never a purely pas­
sive affair. Rather, it involves a similar “clash of two strategies” inherent to
the practice of reading as we saw above. What we now want to explore is
how this general picture of selfhood can serve as the basis for a broader—
or “deeper”, as James Crosswhite puts it—conception of rhetoric.
142  Ricoeur, OA, 339/SA, 391.
143  Ricoeur, OA, 339, SA, 391.
144  Ricoeur, OA, 341, SA, 391.


266  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
Our Rhetorical Capacity
To account for the way action acquires its rhetoricity, we argue that rheto­
ric is best understood as a basic capacity of human beings. Just as Ricoeur
shows that the historian’s craft draws upon the human being’s everyday
capacity for narration, we want to show that the quintessential philosophi­
cal skill of producing and receiving arguments draws upon our everyday
capacity for rhetoric.
To identify rhetoric with what Ricoeur calls the self’s “capacity of
reception” is to side with Aristotle over Plato. Where Plato (at least in the
Gorgias) maligned rhetoric as a pseudo-technique, Aristotle provides it
with a place of its own, identifying rhetoric as a “counterpart” or “ana­
logue” [antistrophos] to dialectic:
Rhetoric is an antistrophos to dialectic; for both are concerned with
such things as are, to a certain extent, within the knowledge of all peo­
ple and belong to no separately defined science. A result is that all peo­
ple, in some way, share in both; for all, up to a point, try both to test
and uphold an argument [as in dialectic] and to defend themselves and
attack [others, as in rhetoric].145
Rhetoric is thus analogous to dialectic in that neither belong to a separately
defined subject but are concerned with something in which all people par­
ticipate—whether by natural skill or through methodical training.146
If rhetoric is something that everyone engages in to some extent, then it
cannot simply be defined as a kind of knowledge (epistēmē) or technique
(technē). This is the great contribution of Aristotle, who instead defines
rhetoric as a theoretical capacity—“a capacity [dunamis], in each particu­
lar case, to see [theōrēsai] the available means of persuasion”.147 By this
definition, the function of rhetoric (its ergon) is thus not persuasion itself,
but the theorization or observation of what is potentially persuasive in a
particular situation. For Aristotle, in other words, rhetoric is more about
innovating “that which one can advance for a cause”, as Nietzsche puts it
in his lectures on rhetoric. In this way, “the rhetor can defend a difficult
cause, just as a physician who cares for an incurable patient”.148
145  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1354a, 30, insertions belong to Kennedy.
146  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1354a, 30.
147  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355b, 37, translation modified.
148  Friedrich Nietzsche, Friedrich Nietzsche on Rhetoric and Language, ed., trans., Sander
L. Gilman et al. (New York, NY: Oxford University Press, 1989), 9.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  267
But what exactly does it mean to theorize the available or potential
“means of persuasion” [pisteis]?149 Since the objective (or telos) of a
speech always relates to a hearer, what is potentially persuasive can only
be determined in relation to actual audiences.150 However, where Aristotle
thinks that there are only three kinds of audiences—corresponding to three
typical social contexts, as we have seen—Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
offer a more flexible and nuanced view. For them, the “audience” is not
defined as the empirical addressee(s) of a discourse but a construction of
the speaker or author—a construction which provides a basis for making
various rhetorical choices. By defining audience this way, Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca retain the basic Aristotelian function of rhetoric without
limiting its applicability to only three social contexts.
To rewrite Aristotle’s definition in the terms of the NRP, we could say
that the capacity to theorize the available means of persuasion is the capac­
ity to reflect on what would be reasonable to an audience. This involves
imagining what they might take to be true, what values they might hold,
what their interests might be, what emotional state they might be in, and
so on. And it is this capacity that speakers and writers draw upon when
they construct their audiences.
While the quality of this reflection will vary (according to one’s natu­
ral skill, training, access to information, experience of other audiences,
etc.) this is a capacity that everyone can in principle exercise. Indeed,
this is precisely why Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca insist on studying
argumentation across the widest possible range of social contexts, from
“discussion around the family table” to “debates in a highly specialized
environment”.151 The human capacity for this distinctively rhetorical form
of reflection is not only to be found in specific institutionalized contexts
like courtrooms, political assemblies, and academic journals; the capacity
to reflect on audiences, and to construct them in this or that way, can be
observed across the full spectrum of human action.
149  Where John Henry Freese (1926) and Robin Waterfield (2018) translate “pisteis” as
“proofs”, W. Rhys Roberts (1984) and Robert C. Bartlett (2019) translate “modes of
persuasion”; Joe Sachs (2009) and C.D.C. Reeve (2018) opt for “means of persuasion”,
while George A. Kennedy (2007) sometimes leaves the term untranslated. For a detailed
discussion of the term, see Christopher Carey, “Rhetorical Means of Persuasion”, Essays
on Aristotle’s Rhetoric, 399–415.
150  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1358b, 47. Given their importance, it is surprising how little
empirical attention is paid to actual audiences in rhetorical studies. For a helpful cor­
rective, see Jens E. Kjeldsen, “Studying Rhetorical Audiences—A Call for Qualitative
Reception Studies in Argumentation and Rhetoric”, Informal Logic, Vol. 36, No. 2
(2016): 136–158; Kjeldsen, ed., Rhetorical Audience Studies and Reception of Rhetoric:
Exploring Audiences Empirically (Cham: Palgrave Macmillan, 2018).
151  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, 8/TA, 10.


268  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
In fact, this more basic conception of rhetoric has long been presup­
posed by sociologists and social theorists of action. Already present in
Max Weber’s definition of “social action” is the idea that actors take into
account the behaviour of others.152 For Weber, actors must in some way
represent the behaviour of others to themselves, they must in some way
“construct their audience”. For example, when I drive my car on the right
side of the road (in countries where this is customary), I expect, or “take
into account” the fact that other drivers will generally do the same. I thus
construct other drivers, the “audience” of my action, as people who will
by and large follow the rules and regulations of the road.
The rhetorical dimension of social action becomes more visible in the
work of George Herbert Mead, and the tradition of symbolic interaction­
ism more generally. Though Mead does not appeal to rhetoric in any way,
he cannot help but lean on a rhetorical vocabulary when describing the
social nature of the self.153 More clearly still, rhetoric nearly comes to the
fore in the related work of Erving Goffman. Adopting a dramaturgical
perspective, Goffman seeks to show that individuals choose the way they
present themselves based on their perception of other “characters” and a
presumed “audience”.154 A simple illustration of Goffman’s point is what
is known in linguistics as “code switching”, whereby, depending on one’s
audience, a speaker will modify their manner of speaking (dialect, accent,
vocabulary, etc.). For Goffman, however, this phenomenon is not limited
to speech but extends to every aspect of how one presents one’s “charac­
ter” on the stage of everyday life.155 A similar view was also developed by
Kenneth Burke—arguably the most influential American rhetorical theo­
rist of the 20th century—whose “dramatistic” method analyses human
action in terms of a “hexad” of considerations implied by the term “act”
(act, scene, agent, agency, purpose, attitude).156
The most explicit attempt in this vein, however, is the work of Michel
Meyer, who claims that rhetoric has become the new paradigm of the
152  Max Weber, Economy and Society: A New Translation, trans. Keith Tribe (Cambridge,
MA: Harvard University Press, [1922] 2019), 78–79.
153  George Herbert Mead, Mind, Self, and Society: From the Standpoint of Social Behaviorist,
ed. Charles W. Morris (Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, [1934] 1972), 141–142.
154  Erving Goffman, The Presentation of Self in Everyday Life (New York, NY: Doubleday,
1959), xi.
155  Among others, one could also cite here the work of American sociologist Richard Har­
vey Brown (1940–2003), who explicitly draws on rhetoric in his work (albeit in a looser
way than we would prefer). See Richard Harvey Brown, The Society as Text: Essays on
Rhetoric, Reason, and Reality (Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 1987);
Social Science as Civic Discourse: Essays on the Invention, Legitimation and Uses of
Social Theory (Chicago, IL: The University of Chicago Press, 1989).
156  Kenneth Burke, “Dramatism”, International Encyclopedia of the Social Sciences, Vol­
ume 7, ed. David L. Sills (New York, NY: The Macmillan Company & The Free Press,
1968), 445–446.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  269
human and social sciences.157 By defining rhetoric as the “negotiation of
social distance”, he implies that rhetoric is, to some degree, present in
every form of social interaction. “When dealing with the problem of dis­
tance”, Meyer explains, “what is fundamental is that we always have an
idea of who our audience is”.158 Though there is an unavoidable dialectic
between what we “project” onto the other and who the other “effectively”
is, we always act on the basis of some audience construction.
While each of these authors, and certainly many others, touch upon
the rhetoricity of everyday social interaction, the exercise of our rhetorical
capacity is clearly more pronounced in certain practices. The most immedi­
ate example, and the one most evident to philosophers, is the practice of
arguing—the practice of giving and receiving reasons on an issue pertaining
in one way or another to human action.159 Yet, the fact that our rhetorical
capacity is more engaged in certain practices should not tempt us to return
to a narrow disciplinary view of rhetoric—where rhetoric is only present
in some social contexts or limited to particular kinds of discourse.160 In
every instance of action that can reasonably be called “social”, the actors
involved must reflect on the audience of their actions to some extent. The
trace of this rhetorical reflection in the action is what we have called the
“audience of the text”, the externalization of the actor’s audience con­
struction. However minimal, the work of our rhetorical capacity can be
detected across the full spectrum of human action.
By conceiving rhetoric as a basic human capacity, philosophers no
longer need to think about it as something “other” or “more” than phi­
losophy. Instead, rhetoric can more fruitfully be considered as a prephilo­
sophical competence that—whether appreciated or not—philosophers,
like all social actors, draw upon when reflecting on and constructing their
own audiences. On this view, rhetoric is as much at work in the researcher
attempting to advance a scholarly debate by positioning themselves against
their peers, as in the teacher guiding a new generation of students into the
practice of philosophical questioning, or in the social critic keeping close
to the latest trends in culture to intervene in a debate of broad public con­
cern. If we are willing to give up our conventional philosophical hostility
towards rhetoric and see it at work in each of these cases, we can relieve
ourselves of the awkward posture of the philosopher (or the sophist) who
157  Meyer, What Is Rhetoric?, 1.
158  Meyer, What Is Rhetoric?, 217.
159  Here we follow Tindale’s general characterization of argumentation in The Anthropol­
ogy of Argument, 2.
160  To steer clear of this dead end, we suspect it would be more fruitful to follow a strategy
like that of Johann Michel in Homo Interpretans (2016) and distinguish between some­
thing like “proto-rhetoric”, “rhetoric”, and “meta-rhetoric”. But, of course, this would
require its own dedicated discussion.


270  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
simultaneously exercises their rhetorical capacity while denying that they
are doing anything rhetorical.
Rhetoric in (Critical) Theory and Practice
If rhetoric is a basic human capacity, in this section we explain why rheto­
ric is especially useful for philosophers and social scientists. Simply put, if
human beings exercise their rhetorical capacity by reflecting on and con­
structing audiences, then the audiences we construct become privileged
sites of meaning. For this reason, philosophers and social scientists should
pay more attention to audiences as essential social theoretical data.
We begin again with Aristotle’s On Rhetoric, specifically the first chap­
ter of Book I where he provides four reasons why rhetoric is valuable or
useful.161 While three of the reasons are largely bound up with Aristotle’s
philosophical commitments and broader cultural context, the third reason
stands on its own and best captures the main theoretical advantage of
acknowledging and cultivating our rhetorical capacity:
one should be able to argue persuasively on either side of a question …
not that we may actually do both (for one should not persuade what is
debased) but in order that it may not escape our notice what the real
state of the case is and that we ourselves may be able to refute if another
person uses speech unjustly.162
It is often said that one must hear both sides of an argument before making
up one’s mind. Sound advice, for sure. But Aristotle’s point is more pre­
cise: being able to argue persuasively on both sides of a question permits
us to see something that we would not otherwise be able to see—the “real
state of the case”.163 While not a technical term, Aristotle is suggesting
that our chances of being persuasive (i.e. effectively refuting the unjust use
of speech) are improved in proportion to our grasp of what is “really” at
stake in a debate. Exercising our rhetorical capacity is useful, then, because
the “real state of the case” is often hidden from view. For example, the
distorting effects of ideology or misinformation might be at play. Or, more
innocently, new and better arguments may be out there that have simply
161  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1255a–1255b, 35–36.
162  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355a, 35, our emphasis.
163  Other English translations include: “what the facts are” (Roberts), “how things stand”
(Reeve), “the way things are” (Sachs), “how the matter stands” (Bartlett). Or in French:
“comment se posent les questions” (Dufour), “le procédé” (Chiron) [See Bibliography
for complete references].


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  271
escaped our notice, meaning that the “real state of the case” has moved
on without us.
While we should be cautious not to read too much into this phrase, we
take Aristotle’s suggestive idea to capture the distinctive theoretical contri­
bution of rhetoric. Viewed this way, our rhetorical capacity performs an
important diagnostic function when confronted with what some rhetori­
cians call a “rhetorical situation”.164 Indeed, the trope of comparing rheto­
ric to medicine is telling in this respect: before one can have any chance
of defending a “difficult cause”, as Nietzsche put it, it is first necessary to
have an accurate diagnosis, to have a grasp of the “real state of the case”.
By attempting to see what people actually find persuasive on all sides of a
debate or controversy, we put ourselves in a better position to address its
underlying causes, and not just its symptoms. The specific object of rhetor­
ical diagnosis, then, is audiences: when we exercise our capacity to theorize
the potentially persuasive, we are reflecting on what audiences have found
or do find persuasive, extrapolating this knowledge to the particularities
of new situations. Regardless of context—whether personal, political, or
scientific—audiences are our only means of accessing what a society finds
persuasive (or reasonable, as Perelman might put it). Thus, if we are to
avoid simply imposing our own vision of what is persuasive onto others,
then it is audiences that we must study.
While seldom phrased in the rhetorical vocabulary of “audiences”, the
idea of taking seriously what people find persuasive is not at all foreign
to philosophers and social scientists. For example, take what Anthony
Giddens famously calls the “double hermeneutic” of the social sciences.
As Giddens explains, the double hermeneutic refers to the fact that social
scientists must first step into and grasp “the frames of meaning involved
in the production of social life by lay actors” before reconstituting them
within the technical conceptual schemes required by social science.165
Similarly, as Habermas puts it, the social sciences do not face problems of
interpretation simply because social data are always theory-dependent, but
164  This is not the place to go into the debates surrounding Bitzer’s famous and controver­
sial account of the “rhetorical situation” (Lloyd F. Bitzer, “The Rhetorical Situation”,
Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 1 (1968): 1–14; Richard E. Vatz, “The Myth of the Rhe­
torical Situation”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 6, No. 3 (1973): 154–161). While hesi­
tant about typologizing actions and situations as “rhetorical”, we generally agree with
Kjeldsen, who argues against the false dichotomy posed by the Bitzer-Vatz debate in
favour of a more reciprocal, open-ended understanding of the relationship between situ­
ations and communication (Kjeldsen, “Rhetorical Working Through as Praxis, Process,
and Philosophy”, Revisiting Rosengren’s Doxology, ed. Erik Bengston et al. (Ödåkra:
Retorikförlaget, 2022), 256.
165  Anthony Giddens, New Rules of Sociological Method, 2nd ed. (Cambridge: Polity, [1976]
1993), 86.


272  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
because their subject matter is itself already “symbolically structured”.166
We have seen this issue already in our discussion of Mannheim’s paradox
and the problem of ideology in Ricoeur. As human action is symbolically
constituted all the way down, so to speak, there is no way to step outside
of symbolic mediation and view society from a non-symbolic perspective.
Ricoeur’s way of addressing this problem was to shift from a causal
or mechanistic model to a “motivational model”. Siding with Weber
over Althusser, Ricoeur argues that the philosopher and the social scien­
tist must stay close to the actual meanings through which everyday social
actors understand themselves and the reasons they give to others to justify
their beliefs and actions. Though this approach could appear to some as
a regression into pre-structuralist phenomenology, Ricoeur’s inclusion of
the mediating role of “persuasion” in motivating human action and the
“meaningful procedures” through which systems of authority try to secure
legitimacy is a considerable step forward.167 Already in the mid-1970s,
Ricoeur saw the limits of the structuralist paradigm, laying the ground for
new currents in social theory such as “pragmatic sociology”.168 Clarifying
their concern with processes of justification in social life, sociologists Luc
Boltanski and Laurent Thévenot explain that in their work they want “to
take seriously the question of the legitimacy of the agreement, rather than
set it aside in favour of an explanation exclusively styled in terms of con­
tingency, deceit or force”.169 The point here is not to glorify how people
see and experience things, but to be attentive to the “justifications that
people develop, in speech and in action” as meaningful social data.170 Like
Ricoeur who emphasizes that philosophers must first “hear what is being
said” before being critical, they too emphasize that it is first necessary to
orient oneself to the actual reasons and arguments people provide in situ­
ations of dispute.
