167  Ricoeur, LIU, 134; 154/IU, 185; 209.
168  For a discussion of Ricoeur’s influence on Boltanski, Thévenot and others, as well as
his influence on the human sciences more generally, see Luc Boltanski, François Dosse,
Michaël Fœssel, et al., “L’effet Ricœur dans les sciences humaines”, Esprit, No. 3–4
(2006), 43–67. See also Wolff’s discussion on Ricoeur and social theory in Between
Daily Routine and Violent Protest, 5–9.
169  Luc Boltanski and Laurent Thévenot, “The Sociology of Critical Capacity”, European
Journal of Social Theory, Vol. 2, No. 3 (1999), 364, our emphasis.
170  Boltanski and Thévenot, On Justification: Economies of Worth, trans. Catherine Porter
(Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, [1991] 2006), 37. A similar point is made by
cultural sociologist Jeffrey Alexander: “We need a theory that is less myopically centered
on social structure and power distribution, and more responsive to the idea that people
have in their heads and to what Tocqueville called the habits of their hearts” (Jeffrey C.
Alexander, The Civil Sphere (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2006), 43).


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  273
Yet, what is specifically rhetorical about all this? Do these examples not
rather emphasize the importance of hermeneutics? This of course depends
on one’s definition of rhetoric. If rhetoric is defined as an art concerned
with a particular kind of discourse or action—as most philosophers would
have it—then rhetoric’s place remains, at best, peripheral. However, if one
defines rhetoric more precisely as a capacity at work across the full spec­
trum of human action—as the capacity to reflect on audiences and theorize
what is potentially persuasive—then this capacity is equally at work in the
“double hermeneutic” of the social scientist, or the philosophical “listen­
ing” Ricoeur describes. Understood this way, our rhetorical capacity sup­
plies hermeneutics with its raw material by listening to the actual views
of audiences, by “actively receiving” what and why they think what they
think. Without this important layer of social meaning, the theorist as much
as the ordinary social actor cannot see the “real state of the case” beneath
any conflict of interpretation.
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca were most prescient in recognizing
the social theoretical relevance of audiences. As they observe in §4 of the
Treatise: “Every social circle or milieu is distinguishable in terms of its
dominant opinions and unquestioned convictions, of the premises that it
accepts without hesitation”. This social sense of what is “reasonable”, as
we discussed in Chapter 3, forms “an integral part of [a society’s] culture,
and an orator wishing to persuade a particular audience must of necessity
adapt himself to it”.171 To this sociological observation, they add the fol­
lowing, distinctively rhetorical concern: “the particular culture of a given
audience shows so strongly through the speeches addressed to it that we
feel we can rely on them to a considerable extent for our knowledge of the
character of past civilizations”.172
While left undeveloped, Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are pointing
out that audience constructions disclose an important layer of meaning
that only a rhetorical perspective is equipped to detect—the “audience of
the text”. Since all social actors must adapt themselves to what they expect
others will find acceptable, reasonable, and so forth (even if only to reject
it), the text they leave behind—indeed every cultural object, in the broadest
possible sense—discloses something about its author, the situation which
gave rise to it, and the society more generally. Indeed, the specific way
that audiences are constructed is in many cases the only resource we have
for mapping what a certain social milieu finds acceptable, controversial,
obvious, disrespectful, reasonable, and so forth—key features of the social
171  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 20/TA, 27.
172  Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca, NR, §4, 21/TA, 27.


274  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
imagination, or what Tindale calls the “cognitive environment”.173 By
exercising our rhetorical capacity, that is, by reflecting on audiences, we
are thus able to see what Aristotle calls “the persuasive” (pithanon)—or
“the persuasive as such”, as Ricoeur translates it.174 Not to be confused
with persuasion as a subjective psychological reaction, “the persuasive” is
an objective part of the social imagination visible in the specific way audi­
ences are constructed.
By shifting focus from “persuading” to “the persuasive”, the conception
of rhetoric we are advocating invites a Copernican revolution in the phi­
losopher’s conventional way of thinking about it. For the philosopher, the
immediate questions raised by rhetoric should no longer first be whether or
how others can be persuaded, or when it is right to do so—these are the sec­
ondary questions of the rhetor, posed from within the “practical attitude”
of engaged arguers who will have to risk themselves on certain choices
over others. Instead, the first questions should rather be “What do various
audiences already find persuasive?”, or “What meanings, arguments, and
narratives, have, as a matter of fact, already gained their adherence?”. To
ask these questions is to exercise one’s rhetorical capacity. These are the
questions of the rhetorician, posed from within the “theoretical attitude”
for the purposes of grasping the potentially persuasive.175
Understood this way, our rhetorical capacity performs an important
methodological task for the philosopher and social scientist. By insisting
on starting with what audiences already find persuasive, rhetoric helps us
stay in contact with common sense. Far from a plea for moderation, how­
ever, close contact with common sense is in fact, to paraphrase Adorno,
the basic precondition of its critique.176 However far scientific inquiry and
theoretical reflection might take us, it is from common sense that we will
have travelled and to common sense that we must return if any break is to
be made with it.
Yet paying greater attention to the work of our rhetorical capacity will
not be of equal use to everyone. The methodological contribution of this
capacity will be especially useful to those interested in meeting the challenge
posed by Marx in the eleventh thesis on Feuerbach. This is particularly well
173  Tindale, The Philosophy of Argument and Audience Reception, 222–224; The Anthro­
pology of Argument, 73–74.
174  Aristotle, On Rhetoric, 1355b25, 37; Ricoeur, RM, 33/MV, 44. The noun form “pitha­
non” is the one used in Aristotle’s definition: “Estō dē rhetorikē dunamis peri hekaston
theōrēsai to endekhomenon pithanon”.
175  The distinction between the “theoretical attitude” and the “practical attitude” is dis­
cussed in relation to the NRP throughout Chapter 2.
176  Theodor W. Adorno, “Scientific Experiences of a European Scholar in America”, Criti­
cal Models: Interventions and Catchwords, trans. Henry W. Pickford (New York, NY:
Columbia University Press, 2005), 242.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  275
illustrated by those theorists associated with the Frankfurt School tradition
of critical social theory. Not content with simply interpreting the world,
the “emancipatory interest”177 of critical theories requires a more nuanced
concern with audiences than “traditional theories”, whose aims fall more
neatly within the professional bounds of the academy.178
This concern with audiences is visible already in Max Horkheimer’s
programmatic essay “Traditional and Critical Theory” (1937). Here he
explains that the “real function” of critical theory emerges only when “the
theoretician and his specific object are seen as forming a dynamic unity
with the oppressed class”.179 By keeping close to this class and their actual
views on things, Horkheimer is arguing that the critical theorist’s “pres­
entation of societal contradictions is not merely an expression of the con­
crete historical situation but also a force within it to stimulate change”.180
Critical theories are thus inherently concerned with their audience—with
those whom they seek to form a “dynamic unity” with—as it is this reflex­
ive relationship which, in part, makes a critical theory critical in the first
place.181
177  Axel Honneth, “Is There an Emancipatory Interest? An Attempt to Answer Critical The­
ory’s Most Fundamental Question”, The Poverty of Our Freedom: Essays 2012–2019,
trans. Gabriel Borrud et al. (Cambridge: Polity, 2023): 198–220.
178  It is noteworthy that liberal political philosophers have also picked up on this issue in
recent years—which Hickey, Meijers, Robeyns, and Timmer, following Onora O’Neill,
call the “‘agents of justice’ question” (Colin Hickey et al., “Agents of Justice”, Philoso­
phy Compass, Vol. 16, No. 10 (2016), 2). Where theorists like Rawls and Miller identify
the addressee of political philosophy as “citizens generally”, Ben Laurence and others
working in “nonideal” or “real-world” political philosophy have drawn attention to the
fact that this fails to specify who exactly will enact the changes recommended by the
theory of justice (Ben Laurence, “The Question of the Agent of Change”, The Journal of
Political Philosophy, Vol. 28, No. 4 (2020), 357; Agents of Change: Political Philosophy
in Practice (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2021); Vafa Ghazavi, “Pluralis­
ing (Not Limiting) the Agent of Change: A Task for Real-World Political Philosophy”,
Res Publica, Vol. 29, No. 3 (2023): 445–467). The long history of posing this question
on the Marxist left notwithstanding, this is precisely the kind of oversight that a concern
for the rhetoricity of philosophy would help to avoid.
179  Max Horkheimer, “Traditional and Critical Theory”, Critical Theory: Selected Essays,
trans. Matthew J. O’Connell et al. (New York, NY: Continuum, 2002), 215.
180  Horkheimer, “Traditional and Critical Theory”, 215, our emphasis.
181  Pushing in a similar direction, Gerald Posselt and Andreas Hetzel also argue that con­
temporary critical theory has overlooked the role of rhetoric in critique, and that phi­
losophy should be “rhetorized” by integrating the rhetorical perspective. While we share
their emphasis on the importance of rhetoric for critique, we hesitate on some of their
conclusions, such as: “a rhetorically informed philosophizing … would have to make
the complex tension between philosophy and rhetoric its permanent and productive
reference point, without reducing one to the other” (Gerald Posselt and Andreas Hetzel,
“Rhetoric as Critique: Towards a Rhetorical Philosophy”, Theory, Culture & Society,
Vol. 40, No. 3 (2023), 48). In our view, such a conclusion (and the frequent recourse to


276  Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy
There is thus a specific rhetorical difference between traditional and
critical theories. The “oppressed class” of which Horkheimer speaks is the
audience of critical theory in at least two senses. It is the audience both
upstream and downstream from the activity of the theorist: upstream, in
the sense that the critical theorist must first be responsive to the expres­
sions of discontent raised by this class; but also downstream because the
critical theorist seeks not merely to “express” these discontents, and the
social contradictions and relations of domination they reflect, but to act
as a “force” of emancipatory change with them. Far from a simple mat­
ter of strategic communication, then, this dual concern for its audience
is essential to the very enterprise of critical theory; for, unlike traditional
theories, as Luc Boltanski puts it, their distinctive aim is to “render reality
unacceptable, and thereby engage the people to whom they are addressed
in action”.182
4  Conclusion
Our examination of Ricoeur’s philosophy through the lens of rhetoric
has allowed us to provide a more substantial response to the concerns
of Badiou and Cassin than we could with Perelman alone. To Cassin’s
charge that rhetoric is too philosophical, we responded that the strong
opposition she draws between her sophistic conception of performance
and Perelman’s rhetorical conception of audience is untenable. Drawing
on Ricoeur’s threefold conception of mimesis we showed that the very idea
of a world effect is incoherent without presupposing the “refigurative”
work of an audience. To Badiou’s charge that rhetoric is too sophistical,
we turned to Ricoeur’s practical solution to the problem of ideology to
illustrate the constitutive rhetoricity of philosophy. Interpreting his con­
ception of phronetic judgment through his threefold conception of mime­
sis, we emphasized there is always an audience upstream and downstream
from the philosopher, and, further, that this is true whether one subscribes
to pluralism or not. In a final step, we used Ricoeur’s philosophical anthro­
pology, his hermeneutics of the capable self, to argue that rhetoric should
be understood as a basic capacity of human beings. Moreover, we argued
that this capacity is responsible for bestowing human action with its rhe­
torical dimension. By reflecting on audiences and constructing them in a
chiasmic argumentation) suggests that the typological picture of the conflict between
philosophy and rhetoric has been suppressed rather than transcended. However, deter­
mining the compatibility of our respective proposals would require a closer look than
we can offer here.
182  Luc Boltanski, On Critique: A Sociology of Emancipation, trans. Gregory Elliot (Cam­
bridge: Polity, [2009] 2011), 5.


Ricoeur and the Rhetoricity of Philosophy  277
particular way, all our actions, including those of the philosopher, become
imbued with rhetoricity. This way of conceiving rhetoric allows philoso­
phers to sidestep the problem posed by action typologies and appreciate
the rhetoricity of their own practice. While true of all philosophy on some
level, we concluded by suggesting that the rhetorical considerations we
have introduced here will be of greater importance to those who define
their theoretical orientation in terms of an emancipatory interest.


We are now ready to report the main findings of our study. Here we pre­
sent the results of each chapter, indicate promising directions for future
work, and provide our response to the question raised by Badiou and
Cassin’s criticisms: Is rhetoric a dead end for philosophy?
Our first result concerns the ambiguous status of rhetoric in the Badiou-
Cassin debate, which we examined in Chapter 1. Despite taking opposed
sides in the ancient quarrel between Plato and the sophists, we found that
Badiou and Cassin surprisingly both take rhetoric as a common enemy.
Focusing on this curious point of agreement, we showed that what they
both find problematic about rhetoric is its narrow focus on the audience.
Where Badiou finds that rhetoric’s narrow focus on audiences leaves it
entirely divorced from truths, Cassin finds that it overlooks the broader
world shaping powers of sophistic performance. Until now, these overlap­
ping critiques have gone unnoticed by Badiou and Cassin as well as their
commentators. Future work can thus use their shared critique of rhetoric
as a point of reference for comparing and contrasting their rather different
readings of key figures (e.g. Plato, Aristotle, Kant). Moreover, the opposed
reasons for which they criticize rhetoric magnify the deeper sources of dis­
agreement between them. Their competing views about universalism and
relativism, for example, shed light on the way their respective projects deal
with more pressing socio-political concerns. Finally, by providing a more
defensible view of rhetoric and locating its place within their own practice,
philosophers and rhetorical theorists can explore other aspects of Badiou
and Cassin’s works that would previously have appeared off-limits given
their explicit rejection of all things rhetorical.
The second result is a cluster of findings related to Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca’s NRP. In Chapter 2, we showed that the theory of argu­
mentation developed in the Treatise should not be conflated with either
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s “critical rationalism”, or the philosophi­
cal pluralism Perelman advocates throughout his writings. On our reading,
the audience is the key concept in their theory of argumentation and plays
Conclusion
Is Rhetoric a Dead End for Philosophy?
DOI:  10.4324/9781032686493-8


Conclusion  279
an important role in their methodology. As Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca
do not fully explicate the details of their operative method, we introduced
phenomenological vocabulary to identify what we called their “philosoph-
ical epoché” and “rhetorical reduction”. It is within these methodological
constraints, we argued, that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca are able to
identify and describe various argumentative techniques without presup-
posing a strong conception of rationality in advance. In other words, we
showed how Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca temporarily refrain from tak-
ing any determinate philosophical position when studying argumentation
for the sake of looking at how an argument attempts to induce audience
adherence.
To keep things clear, we then introduced a distinction between two
perspectives or attitudes we detected in the Treatise: the theoretical atti-
tude and the practical attitude. Where the theoretical attitude is the stance
taken when we attempt—in any social context—to describe and interpret
arguments precisely as they are given, the practical attitude refers to the
stance taken as an engaged arguer, when we look to evaluate arguments
on the basis of our personal commitments and values. On our reading,
the Treatise is primarily concerned with the theoretical attitude. With few
exceptions, questions concerning the practical stance are left open for
future elaboration, as are normative questions about the criteria for strong
arguments, which can only be answered in relation to a specific context or
domain. Our reading thus shows that the theory of argumentation is not
yet in a position to prescribe what makes arguments strong or weak. As it
stands in the Treatise, the theory simply (but admirably) aspires to provide
a neutral framework for describing and interpreting the workings of the
already norm-laden practice of argumentation.1
Our third result concerns the rhetorical dimension of Badiou’s philosoph-
ical project. While not rhetorical in the “sophistic” sense that he criticizes,
we showed that Badiou’s own practice—like that of all philosophers—can
be analysed rhetorically in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s sense. Not
only can Badiou’s diverse forms of argumentation be described in terms of
the associative and dissociative techniques laid out in Treatise, but, more
significantly, he even develops a vocabulary for philosophy’s specific form
of address. While he would surely quarrel about calling this “rhetorical”,
Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca would not fail to recognize the family
resemblance between his account of philosophical addressivity and the set
of problems they pose under the name of the “universal audience”. In other
words, Badiou’s aversion to the term “rhetoric” is beside the point: our
1  The implications of this interpretation for contemporary argumentation theory are con-
sidered in more detail in Scott, “The Rhetorical Methodology of Perelman and Olbrechts-
Tyteca’s Theory of Argumentation”, Philosophy & Rhetoric, Vol. 57, No. 1 (forthcoming).


280  Conclusion
distinction between the theory of argumentation developed in the Treatise
and Perelman’s own rhetorical philosophy allows us to retain the NRP’s
conception of audience without necessarily having to adopt a “rhetorical”
philosophy in Badiou’s sense. Our major finding in Chapter 3 was thus that
Badiou is not against rhetoric per se but against rhetoric when it attempts to
pass itself off as philosophy, as a systematic position in its own right (what
he calls the “sophistic position”). By distinguishing our own conception of
rhetoric from what Badiou calls sophistry, we have opened up the rhetorical
dimension of Badiou’s work to further exploration—specifically, his dis­
tinction between “address”, “transmission”, and “inscription”—which has
received little attention from philosophers and rhetorical theorists.
The fourth result concerns Cassin’s attempt to dissociate sophistry
from rhetoric. According to Cassin, philosophers invented rhetoric to con­
tain the dangerous world-building power of sophistic performance. For
her, what philosophers call “rhetoric” is by definition pigeonholed to the
speaker-audience axis of language and indifferent to the speaker-world
axis. By neglecting this axis—the traditional locus of truth—rhetoric over­
looks what Cassin calls world effects. According to this “philosophical”
view, rhetoric is concerned only with effects in the mind of the audience.
The major finding of Chapter 4, however, is that Cassin’s dissociation
of sophistry from rhetoric is less radical than it first appears. By looking
closely at her reading of Austin, we showed that Cassin’s sophistics, which
emphasizes how discourse “performs worlds” beyond the perlocutionary,
cannot break entirely with a rhetorical conception of audience. The strong
opposition she presents between her sophistic conception of performance
and Perelman’s rhetorical conception of audience is thus more a matter of
emphasis than a genuine incompatibility.
At the same time, we were also able to disarm Cassin’s criticism that
Perelman advocates a rhetoric of “intention” as opposed to a rhetoric of
“effect” (i.e. sophistics). This criticism gave us the opportunity to examine
Perelman’s controversial notion of the universal audience, which Cassin
feared contained a hidden ethical injunction. However, on the basis of
our earlier interpretation of the Treatise, we showed that Perelman and
Olbrechts-Tyteca are less interested in imposing an ethics of discourse than
providing a vocabulary for describing the way that arguers construct their
audience, among other features of argumentation. Similar to our response
to Badiou, making a distinction between the theory of argumentation
and Perelman’s own philosophical position allowed us to acknowledge
certain ambiguities and unresolved tension in the NRP—especially those
surrounding the universal audience—without abandoning their rhetorical
conception of audience altogether.
Our fifth result concerns Ricoeur’s philosophy. This set of findings
involved having to read Ricoeur’s relation to rhetoric against the grain.


Conclusion  281
As we showed in Chapter 5, Ricoeur’s conception of rhetoric is often
borrowed from other authors and, in any case, is considerably narrower
than Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s. In particular, we drew attention
to the “missed encounter” between Perelman and Ricoeur in The Rule
of Metaphor. In doing so, we argued that Ricoeur’s own preoccupation
with hermeneutics prevents him from seeing the distinctively rhetorical
dimension of texts—despite his own evident concern with audiences and
readers. Despite the differences between Perelman and Ricoeur, their over­
lapping concern with audiences provides a starting point from which the
later works of both authors could be compared. Indeed, Ricoeur’s later
works on justice and legal interpretation and argumentation refer much
more frequently to Perelman than his writings from the 1970s. However
minor these references might appear, our reconstruction of their missed
encounter suggests that the two contemporaries have more in common
than Ricoeur, at least, seemed to appreciate.
The most notable finding of Chapter 5 concerns the relation between
hermeneutics and rhetoric in Ricoeur and Perelman. Our main conclu­
sion here was that Ricoeur’s hermeneutics could be used to extend the
more “romantic” conception of audience found in the NRP. We called this
extended conception of audience the “audience of the text”. While such
a concept is implicit in Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s work, Ricoeur’s
hermeneutic philosophy of action provides the conceptual resources to
articulate the rhetorical dimension of texts—or, in other words, why all
texts (in Ricoeur’s broad sense of this term) contain an audience construc­
tion embedded within them. To extend Perelman’s rhetorical problematic
with Ricoeur’s hermeneutics, it was therefore not necessary to abandon the
rhetorical perspective altogether: if hermeneutics accounts for the fact that
a text can be read by anyone capable of reading, rhetoric accounts for the
way that a text’s specific configuration of discursive techniques constructs
its reader.
Once we could show that Ricoeur’s hermeneutic theory of action does
not exhaust Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s rhetorical emphasis on audi­
ence construction, it became necessary to challenge Ricoeur’s way of fram­
ing the relationship between rhetoric and hermeneutics. Where Ricoeur
conceives rhetoric and hermeneutics as competing disciplines—seemingly
concerned with different kinds or types of discourse—we argued that the
object of rhetoric is better understood as a particular dimension of dis­
course. To make this argument, we drew on Joas and Wolff who each
emphasize the advantages of adopting an anti-typological approach to
human action. In our case, the main advantage of defining the object of
rhetoric this way is that it avoids reducing every use of language to “mere
rhetoric”, or subsuming every instance of discourse under the disciplinary
presuppositions of rhetoric as Ricoeur understands them. Rather, to speak


282  Conclusion
about the rhetorical dimension of discourse—or “rhetoricity”, as we called
it—is to recognize that all discourse has a determinate addressive charac­
ter; that all discourse constructs its audience by means of specific argumen­
tative techniques. By taking seriously Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s
insistence that all discourse is oriented towards an audience, we were thus
able to break free of the deep-rooted idea that there are rhetorical and non-
rhetorical kinds of discourse.
This brings us to our sixth and final set of results. Looking at Ricoeur’s
philosophy through the lens of rhetoric, we pursued three lines of thought
in his work that helped us deepen our grasp of the rhetorical dimension
of philosophy. Moreover, each line of thought gave us the opportunity to
further develop our responses to Badiou and Cassin.
First, we used Ricoeur’s conception of refiguration to undermine
Cassin’s charge that Perelman and Olbrechts-Tyteca’s way of thinking
about rhetoric precludes any consideration of world effects. As we argued,
if the audience or reader of a text is responsible for the work of refigura­
tion, then even the sophist looking to cast a new Helen into the world
stands to gain from carefully reflecting on their audience. Without first
understanding what image of Helen their audience currently has and why
they find it persuasive, how would Gorgias know how to craft his speech?
On what basis could he make his many argumentative choices? At the
same time, Ricoeur’s philosophy of action—which begins with a phenom­
enological description of the structures of (in)voluntary life before pro­
gressively incorporating more hermeneutic concerns—serves as a broader
philosophical framework for thinking about discourse in a way that avoids
Cassin’s worry about rhetoric “philosophically conceived”. Simply put,
Ricoeur would reject Cassin’s fixed opposition between logology and
ontology in favour of a more dialectical view. Indeed, his threefold concep­
tion of mimesis can accommodate the fact that, in some cases, discourse
does perform being. But, at the same time, his view can also account for
the much more frequent case where discourse seeks to disclose being that
has logical or temporal priority. Yet, either in the case of a world effect
or the disclosure of what already is, Ricoeur’s account has allowed us to
preserve—in the spirit of the NRP—the mediating role of the audience.
Second, we used Ricoeur’s conception of phronetic judgment as a model
for thinking about the constitutive rhetoricity of philosophical practice.
By reading Ricoeur’s reflections on ideology and practical reason over a
longer span of his work, we showed how audiences are implicated both
upstream and downstream from the phronetic judgment of the philoso­
pher. On the one hand, philosophy is always responsive to the world from
which it emerges. Even if the philosopher aims to break with the world
as it is, she must first attune herself to what is upstream: the views and
opinions of others. On the other hand, philosophy never terminates in


Conclusion  283
the mind. Indeed, its terminus is only reached when the audience joins
the philosopher in the shared work of refiguration. In this sense, there
is always an audience downstream as well. Far from a concern only for
the “sophist”, the audience as we have defined it is even important for a
philosopher—and perhaps especially important for one like Badiou who
understands philosophy as the struggle of truth against dominant ideolo­
gies and opinions.
In a final step, we then posed a question presupposed by our previous
discussion of rhetoricity. Where exactly does rhetoricity come from? What
is responsible for this rhetorical dimension of discourse and action that we
have identified? To this we responded that action acquires its rhetoricity
through the exercise of a basic human capacity—our rhetorical capacity.
In support of this view, we showed how this broadly Aristotelian view of
rhetoric can be grafted onto what Ricoeur calls the self’s “capacity for
reception”, a capacity presupposed at every level of his hermeneutics of
the self. Bringing together our interpretations of Perelman and Ricoeur, we
argued that the rhetorical capacity of human beings is that which enables
us to reflect on what audiences find persuasive or reasonable, a capacity
exercised every time we construct an audience. Importantly, this meant
that rhetoric is not only a scholarly capacity but something at work across
the full spectrum of human action. To support this conclusion, we identi­
fied the implicit presence of this capacity in a number of social theorists
whose works touch on the minimal rhetoricity of everyday social interac­
tion. While a full defence and elaboration of this approach was beyond the
scope of this study, we nonetheless provided the groundwork for a more
dedicated treatment of rhetoric’s value for the human and social sciences.
Our most important conclusion, however, is that there is a constitu­
tive rhetoricity to philosophy. By adopting this view, rhetoric no longer
appears as something “more” or “other” than philosophy, but a capacity
philosophers draw upon in their own practice. This is the capacity they
exercise when they reflect on their audience, when they try and see what
is persuasive, and how they might insert their own views into an ongo­
ing debate. But even if rhetoric is essential to philosophy, we still had to
explain why it is useful. Our main conclusion here was that it helps us
get at what Aristotle calls “the real state of the case”. By attempting to
get at the real stakes of a debate, our rhetorical capacity allows us to see
things that might otherwise escape our notice. Drawing our attention back
to audiences is particularly important when conflicts of interpretation
become too deeply entrenched. Above all, a rhetorical perspective serves
to remind us that audiences, and the audience constructions embedded
in texts, are privileged sites of meaning because they disclose the views
of social actors and help us map the contours of the social imaginary.
However, this way of thinking about rhetoric brought us to reverse the


284  Conclusion
way that philosophers traditionally think about rhetoric: rather than first
asking the practical question of whether or how others can be persuaded,
we argued that the theoretical question should be posed first: what do peo­
ple already find persuasive and why? What, as a matter of fact, has already
gained the adherence of audiences? In this way our rhetorical capacity
performs an important methodological task for the philosopher and social
scientist; it reminds them to stay in touch with the actual views of everyday
social actors. Yet the rhetorical considerations we introduced will not be
of equal importance to everyone. Our final suggestion was that those who
will benefit most from our understanding of rhetoric are those who under­
stand their work in terms of an emancipatory interest.
*
With this summary of our main findings, we are now able to provide a
definitive answer to the question of whether rhetoric is a dead end for phi­
losophy. As we have argued, far from a dead end, rhetoric is both essential
and useful to philosophy. Rhetoric is essential to philosophy because its
object of concern is a constitutive dimension of human action—rhetoric­
ity. Whether acting, speaking, or writing, we always operate on the basis
of some construction of our audience. The practice of philosophy is no
exception. At the same time, rhetoric is useful to philosophers because it is
a capacity they rely upon when attempting to interpret and understand the
stakes of a debate. It is only by exercising this capacity, by attempting to
see the “real state of the case”, that apparent contradictions can be recast
and refigured.
While we hope this answer is sufficient to bring Badiou and Cassin over
to our view, an important normative question remains that we have not
addressed—the question of whether the conception of rhetoric we have
proposed here is value-neutral or whether we are suggesting that there is
something inherently good about rhetoric. To put it another way, have we
not overemphasized the positive side of rhetoric to the neglect of the more
obvious harm it can do? If we have at times overemphasized the positive
in this study, it is only because philosophers have long overemphasised the
negative. In some cases, it was therefore necessary to rectify this imbal­
ance by bending the stick in the other direction. In general, however, our
strategy has been to suspend philosophy’s long-standing prejudice against
rhetoric and look at philosophical practice in a more descriptive way.
While our study stops short of asking what constitutes a “good” or
“bad” use of rhetoric, we have at no point forgotten the normative alto­
gether. Indeed, the goal of this study has been to draw attention to a
dimension of action that can enrich the way normative questions are posed
and even help to identify premature claims to universality. To see this


Conclusion  285
dimension, however, it is first necessary to abandon the “oil and water”
view of philosophy and rhetoric with which we began. The endless repeti­
tion of this typological view is not only a problem for philosophers. It also
reappears in the way that pressing social challenges are framed. If philoso­
phers are to play any role in meeting these challenges, the question can
thus no longer be whether to use rhetoric or not, but whether it is already
being used for better or for worse. By drawing attention to the rhetoricity
of philosophical practice itself, this study has taken an important first step
in addressing these larger concerns.
